 **Three of these were corrected by the act of formalising them.** P7 was stated as though the open-item count decreases; it does not — across this work it ran 5 → 4 → 3 → 4 → 3 → 7 → 3 → 3. Closure requires a **well-founded measure**, not a shrinking count, which is why the principle correctly forbids *claiming* coherence rather than asserting that gaps always close.

 **And the twenty-three sort into three kinds, not two, which is what settles what each one owes.**
 The taxonomy above sorts by *whether an expression exists*, which is a property of the writing.
 Sorted by what a principle **is**:

  kind          what it is                        what settles it
  MECHANISM     a fact about an index             a proof or a computation
  OPERATION     a thing an agent does             the failure it prevents
  RELATION      how a mechanism constrains an     neither — it carries content and takes no expression
                operation

 **Seven are operations** — P3, P4, P5, P6, P11, P14, P15 — and §5's table is their evidence. Six of
 the seven have a recorded failure behind them; **P16 is the exception and it is instructive**, having
 three constructive realisations at §17.4, §12.11.0.2 and §18.4.2 and no register entry, **because it
 never failed. It was adopted rather than learned**, and §5's table is extended accordingly.

 **And P9 has a second instance, which this book produced without recognising it.** Its only computed
 case was rank(log q) = 1 over fifteen Rydberg observables — and that is a tautology once §26.1's
 assembly rule makes every observable a power of ν, so it measures the rule and not the principle.
 **The second case is E itself.** E measures whether an index can carry a constraint, and it is read
 three ways: **forward** as a diagnosis, which of two modes produced the defect and whether it is
 repairable; **backward** as a prohibition, that a minimal failing support of three admits no repair
 by any operation on coordinates; and **as an outcome**, §16.7.1's test of whether E is removable.
 The three were obtained separately and their identity had to be computed, so unlike the Rydberg case
 it is not a tautology. **One definition, three dimensions, each a perspective of it** — which is
 what P9 says. Register 429.

 **Two are relations** — P7 and P17 — and each carried an expression that could not be evaluated,
 which is why. An expression is a mechanism's form and a relation is not a mechanism. Both keep their
 content and lose their expressions, exactly as §1 already says is better than manufacturing one.

 **And four mechanisms were stated without something they need.**

 **P2 needs its hypothesis, and the hypothesis has a name.** ⅅ ≥ dim q − rank ∂Φ/∂p ≥ dim q − dim p,
 so **ⅅ ≥ 1 exactly when dim q > dim p** — when the index derives strictly more quantities than it
 assigns parameters. That is **surplus**, which §11.1.1 measures at 7.07 bits per cell and Chapter 16
 states in prose as *it found it because it carried more than it needed*. For Λ the bound is tight:
 dim p = 4, dim q = 6, rank 4, ⅅ = 2. **Without surplus there is no check**, which is Edlén's
 footnote 78.

 **P7's well-founded measure, which this book demanded and never supplied, is already computed in
 Appendix E.** The measure is not the count of open items — §1 shows why — but the **multiset of
 their `blocks` values** under the ordering *nothing < a novelty assessment < one stated claim < a
 result the book relies on*. That coordinate is ordered and bounded below, so it is well-founded, and
 the multiset extension of a well-founded order is well-founded. Checked against §29.2.1's own
 movement, where six items' stake fell to nothing: the count went 13 → 10 and the multiset decreased
 in the Dershowitz–Manna sense. **That is precisely what *the set grew and the scope descended*
 means.**

 **P17's 1/N is derivable and the constant was dropped.** §10.3 decides whether a box lies inside Λ
 in **seven comparisons**, against one membership test per cell for enumeration. So following the
 bounds costs **c/N with c the number of constraints**, c = 7 here — 7/976 on Λ, 7/199,130 on Λ₁₃.
 **And N is nowhere defined**: neither §18.4 nor Chapter 12 introduces it, so the symbol is
 ungrounded in the sense §16.6's ⅅ_gro names. The corrected form carries no N: *following the bounds
 costs one comparison per constraint where searching the volume costs one per cell.*

 **P22 claimed what §16.8.2 refutes.** *No lie can exist within it* is false: rebuilt around a
 fabrication the index closes, has E = 0, is a fixed point of ℛ and satisfies every law here.
 Restated: **in a complete index every rule is content rather than inference, so a false cell is
 contradicted in the open by a stated rule where an inferred rule would have absorbed it. A forger's
 remaining move is to alter a rule, and an altered rule is equally visible. A complete index
 therefore converts deception into disagreement — error survives, concealment does not.** The index
 cannot be lied **to**; it can be **replaced**, and replacement is an act performed on it rather than
 a question put to it. Registers 417–420.

 Procedural — seven

| | principle |
|---|---|
| **P4** | always audit results |
| **P5** | always compute the result before assuming the result |
| **P6** | when a question is confusing, reframe the question |
| **P11** | close all gaps before continuing |
| **P14** | a structural problem suggests a structural solution |
| **P15** | for fetching: use the index to navigate; data can be retrieved in pieces |
| **P16** | treat bounds, constraints and limits as coordinate values |

 **These have no expressions, and saying so is better than manufacturing one.** They govern how an agent uses an index, not what an index is. P15 is the exception in one respect: it has a *measure* — retrieval redundancy ρ, Chapter 17 — without being reducible to it.

 The reflexive principle

        principle                                                                                              expression
  P1    if the index is complete, a work that comprehends it completely is itself an index, and is itself      Chapter 31
  8     self-referencing and self-defending
  P1    an index is complete only when there are no more questions left to answer about its                    COMPLETE(X)
  9     contents                                                                                               ⟺ Q(X) = ∅
  P2    the language in which a question is posed bounds whether it can close, and at what                     —
  0     cost
  P2    any definition contained within the lattice, and thus its equations, must be TRUE in                   —
  1     every mathematical language and in their combinations
  P2    because everything is defined in a complete index, the entirety of the index is                        —
  2     visible, and no lie can exist within it

 P19, and why it is neither P7 nor P13
 Three principles govern questions, and they are one principle in three moods. P19 was the
 missing one.

| | says | mood |
|---|---|---|
| **P7** | ∃N : Ω_N = ∅ | questions **can** close — possibility |
| **P13** | COHERENT ⟺ Ω = ∅ | coherence **may not** be claimed otherwise — prohibition |
| **P19** | COMPLETE ⟺ Q = ∅ | what their emptiness **buys** — definition |

 **And P19 is strictly stronger than closure.**

      E(X) = 0 says no cell the structure admits is absent. Q(X) = ∅ says no question the
      contents admit is unanswered.

 **Λ has had E = 0 since Chapter 7 and has never had Q = ∅.** Whether *V* depends on the order of the bracket, what the Aitken bias generalises to, whether a higher-order bracket exists — **each was admitted by contents already present, and none was asked until late.**

      Closure is about what an index holds. Completeness is about what it has been asked.

 **And the condition is reachable, for the reason P7's is.** P7 was corrected once: closure requires a well-founded measure rather than a shrinking count. **Q has grown at nearly every step of this work while its scope has fallen** — from *is V novel?* to *is there an optimal order?* **The set grew and the scope descended**, and that descent is what makes P19 a criterion rather than an impossibility.

 **Q(X) = ∅ is reached not when nobody asks, but when every question the contents admit has an answer in the contents.**

 P20 — the language bounds the question
 Three closure mechanisms are used in this book, each in a different language, each with a
 different guarantee.

| language | mechanism | closure | cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| **monotone two-variable bounds** | tree factorisation | **always** | one pass |
| **polynomial relations** | Gröbner basis | **always**, by Hilbert | finite; doubly exponential worst case |
| linear arithmetic with quantifiers | quantifier elimination | yes | triply exponential |
| **full arithmetic** | — | **never** — Gödel | — |
| **documentary** | — | **no mechanism exists** | — |

 **Measured on the objects of this book: tree factorisation 0.00018 s, Gröbner 0.010 s — a factor of 55 — and the documentary language has no timing because it has no algorithm.**

      The choice of language is made when the index is built, not when the question is asked.

 §7.1 chose monotone two-variable bounds. That single decision determined that E(X) is
 computable, that the sum factorises, that a single expression exists, and that every question
 about Λ's contents would close. It equally determined what could not: §11.7's sum bound lies outside
 the language, and its repair — making the sum a coordinate — is a translation back into the closing
 language.

 **P20 subsumes E3, the multi-target repair, and P19's reachability.** And it answers the novelty question structurally: **novelty is posed in the documentary language, which has no closure mechanism. That is not a failure of searching.**

 P21 — every definition true in every language
 Not merely expressible. True.

  definition                           order        algebra      geometry        analysis   information         logic

  \                                    Λ\                        976             976        976                 976        976   976

  rank(x)                              13           13           13              13         13                  13

  mean rank                            11.0666      11.0666      11.0666         11.0666    11.0666             11.0666

  E(periodic table)                    36           36           36              36         36                  36

 And the combinations must name real objects, not analogies. Verified: gcd(N(a),N(b)) = N(a∧b)
 and lcm = N(a∨b), exactly · F′(1)/F(1) = mean rank · E_bits = log₂ C(\|ℛ\|, E) = 105.1 for the classroom
 table.

 P21 is an instrument, not only a criterion

 **When a property holds in five languages and fails in the sixth, the sixth EXPRESSION is wrong — not the property.** This has now located two errors, and in both cases the structure was sound and the translation was not.

 E(X) was recorded as having no analytic route — "E is a count, calculus measures a continuum" —
 and ⅅ(E) was set to 1. The route exists:

      E(X) = F_ℛ(1,…,1) − F_X(1,…,1)

 **Deletion-repair was recorded as failing in geometry.** The convex hull does fail: it permits arbitrary facets, so a vertex can be the sole witness of a facet and its deletion is unrecoverable. **But Λ's facets are two-variable monotone bounds, and the closure that matches that form — the intersection of all valid such bounds — restores every deletion.**

      30 deletions, 0 failures. 20 additions, 20 absorbed. Fixed point exact at 976.

 **A mismatched operator is not a counterexample. It is a mistranslation.**

 What the law then establishes
 The self-feeding loop and its asymmetry hold in all five languages.

  language                 closure operator            delete                      add
  order                    ℛ                           restored                    absorbed
  geometry                 monotone polyhedron         restored                    absorbed
  algebra / logic          ideal, Gröbner basis        still derivable             T·h enters the ideal
  analysis                 coefficients of F           restored                    absorbed
  information              description length          E_bits stays 0              grows

      Extensive gives repair. Monotone gives absorption. Every language has a closure
      operator, so both hold everywhere.

 And that is the formal reason Chapter 16 cannot rely on self-reference alone. A structure that
 closes will heal what you remove and swallow what you insert. Self-consistency defends against loss
 and never against invention — which is why ⅅ_phys must reach outside the index, and why Chapter
 21 records what the loop could not catch.

 P22 — visibility, and why a complete index cannot be lied to
 A lie requires concealment. A complete index has nowhere to conceal.

 **The distinction is between a rule that is CONTENT and a rule that is INFERRED**, and it is decisive. Consider the cell

      y = (n=1, ℓ=0, k=1, q=0, e=1, f=0, g=0, 2S=2)

 — one electron with spin multiplicity 2, which no atom has.

 Where the rule is inferred, ℛ recovers the bound on 2S from the cells themselves. The rule the index
 had learned:

| *k* | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | 2*S* ≤ **1** | 2*S* ≤ **2** |
| 2 | 2*S* ≤ 2 | 2*S* ≤ 2 |
| 3 | 2*S* ≤ 3 | 2*S* ≤ 3 |

      The index did not reject the cell. It revised its rule — and then admitted 74 further cells the
      old rule forbade. The result is closed under join and meet, has E = 0, satisfies every law in this book,
      and contains 75 physically impossible configurations.

 **Where the rule is content**, the same cell is checked against a stated rule set — (bounded, parent, slope, intercept) as cells of their own index — and returns

    VIOLATES 2S ≤ 1·k + 0 (2S = 2, bound = 1).

 **Same cell. Same index. The only difference is whether the rule was present or inferred.**

 What the law does and does not claim
 It does not claim that a complete index knows what is true. It claims that nothing in it is hidden.

    An inferred rule absorbs a false cell and teaches itself the falsehood. A stated rule is
    contradicted by it, in the open.

 A forger's remaining move is to alter a rule — 2*S* ≤ *k* becomes 2*S* ≤ *k*+1, admitting 225 cells. **That forged rule is also visible.** It can be read, disputed and checked; what it cannot be is concealed. **The regress is real and at every level the falsehood is in plain sight.**

 **So P22 is a statement about visibility, not omniscience.** A complete index converts deception into disagreement. **Error survives; concealment does not.**

 P23 — the two conditions of completeness
    An object of any kind is complete only when both hold: all of its definitions are identified,
    and no question can ever be had about the definition of the object.

 The first condition is checkable. Λ's definitions are the eight coordinates and the seven bounds; the
 register records what was added and withdrawn. Enumeration is finite work and this book does it.

 The second condition is not checkable — and that is its content. No inspection establishes that no
 question can ever be had. A single question refutes it and nothing confirms it.

    So completeness is not a property that can be proved of an object. It is a standing claim,
    held until a question arrives.

 What P23 costs this book
 Every entry in the register is a question that could be had about a definition, and there are
 one thousand one hundred and seventy-one of them. Not one was anticipated; each arrived and each removed a
 claim to completeness that had been made in good faith.

 The clearest case is §30.3. Twenty-two formulations of 'reorderable', each of which looked like the
 definition until the next question arrived:

  the question that arrived                               what it was about

  is it ℛ-closure or join/meet closure?                   the same object, twice named

  is the alphabet declared or recovered?                  two different objects

  is a projection a criterion?                            §18.4 says no

  is a frontier-correct tree anchor-complete?             no

  does the obstruction grow in cells or in width?         cells; \                          A_i   ≤\   X\
                                                                                            \

 Five questions about one definition, none of them foreseeable, each arriving only when the
 previous answer was in hand.

 Why P23 is not despair
 A standing claim is not a weak one. Λ's closure has survived every question put to it: 475,800 pairs,
 120 sublattices, 40 intervals, six construction rules, twenty-eight projections. E(Λ) = 0 after all of it.

    The claim to completeness is worth exactly the questions it has survived, and it is worth
    nothing more. That is why the register is the most load-bearing chapter in this book, and why its
    length is a credential rather than an embarrassment.

 And it is the honest reading of P22. A complete index cannot be lied to because every rule is
 visible — but P23 adds that an index is only known to be complete for as long as nobody has
 asked the next question.

 And this is why the book indexes its own rules
 Part I indexes the principles and the protocols. §28.8 indexes the process. Each turns a
 rule set into content, and each closes with E = 0.

    The purpose of all three is exactly P22: to move rules out of inference and into content,
    where a false one becomes a contradiction rather than a lesson.

 And the regress terminates outside. Not at a better index — at Chapter 24's 1,442 measured levels,
 the only content in this book that no index supplied. That is what ⅅ_phys is for, and §16.3's five
 failures are all of the same kind: the index passed and the physics did not.

## 2. The protocols
### 2.1 Channel construction
    A channel is one parent term, one ℓ, one series in n.

 No channel may mix parents, mix ℓ, or cross a coupling-scheme boundary. Where the source's labelling changes scheme partway up a series — LS to jK — **the channel ends there**, and the cells above are not claimed. This is what limits Si I to four cells and P II to one.

### 2.2 Admissibility
    r = 2Z²R / (ν³σ) ≥ 5

 Levels must be separated by more than five times their uncertainty. *r* falls as ν⁻³, so every channel eventually leaves the domain; the rule states where, in advance.

### 2.3 Cost resolvability
    ν ≤ ν_V = (3Z²R / 5q)^{1/4}

 The bracket needs levels *separated*; the cost needs curvature *resolved*. **Curvature washes out first.** Cells above ν_V are excluded from the cost test and retained for the bracket — a distinction applied prospectively in Li I and diagnostically in Al I.

### 2.4 Dilution
    cells per channel ≈ (measured shells / D) − 2, with D the number of J levels in the core's ground
    configuration

 **Dilution divides; it does not forbid.** Ne II fails at D = 5 with two shells; Ar II succeeds at D = 5 with three; Bi I succeeds at D = 5 with five.

### 2.5 Census before search
    Compute which species could work before looking for data.

 D is obtained from the core configuration by enumerating antisymmetric states. No spectroscopic data enters. **27 sequences admitted, 12 needing exceptional depth, 5 excluded structurally.**

### 2.6 Declines are recorded
    A collection reporting only successes cannot be checked.

 Every species examined and rejected is listed with its cause. Four causes have been identified — dilution, depth, coupling-scheme change, and core-excited interleaving — and §23.11 gives a worked case of each.

### 2.7 Exhaustive verification where possible

    Sampling n cells with no failure bounds the failure rate at ρ ≤ 3/n at 95% confidence. It
    never certifies ρ = 0.

 Where a claim is universally quantified over a finite lattice, it is checked on **every** cell. Where sampling is used, the sample size is stated.

### 2.8 Two independent routes
    For every quantity that matters, build a second derivation that does not pass through the
    first.

 This is ⅅ_def made operational. It caught a mis-stated relation in Chapter 16, and it is realised
 experimentally in the antiprotonic-helium check of §16.4, where two laser measurements agree at 0.09σ
 with no shared step.

 **An identity computed along one path is vacuous.** *w* = *V*·*e* cannot fail if *V* is *defined* as *w*/*e*.

### 2.9 Refuse rather than coerce
    No third state. A quantity is admissible or it is refused; it is never silently defaulted.

 A guard returning a null that a caller coerces to a Boolean produced three false conclusions in this work, none of which looked wrong. **Totality is the only mechanism that catches this**, and it works by preventing the computation rather than by detecting the error.

### 2.10 Retrieval
    Enumerate targets from the index before searching. List routes per cell. Prefer high-ρ
    cells. Read every retrieved source for its successors. When a route is blocked, move
    along the route set. Record ρ for cells that fail. And RE-ENUMERATE whenever the set the
    targets are drawn from grows.

 Chapter 19. A blocked cell with ρ = 1 is a **stated gap**, not an unexplained absence.

 The second clause was added late. §30.4 found five enumerations in one chapter — its own target list,
 §30.2's structural invariants, and the object lists of §31.1, §31.2 and §31.3 — each made once, against
 a structure that kept growing. Every one of them was incomplete when re-run.

    Enumerating once is not a protocol. It is a snapshot, and a snapshot of a growing object
    is a defect the moment it is taken.

### 2.11 Provenance
    Every number traces to a compilation, table and page, or to a database entry with its
    stated uncertainty.

 Appendix B carries this at channel level for all 153 channels.

### 2.12 Withdrawal
    Every retracted claim is logged with what replaced it.

 Chapter 28. one thousand one hundred and seventy-one entries. The register is the evidence for Chapter 16, not
 an apology for it.

### 2.13 Commit before you look
    A prediction is written down before the measurement is requested. A method is chosen
    before its answer is visible.

 **This is the process index of §28.8 enforced by hand:** the constraint **visible ≤ committed**, of §16.4 form, closed, E = 0.

 **It is the only protocol here adopted in response to an error that no computation could have caught** — because every computation was correct and what failed was the independence of a choice. **The deduction of §25.6 was committed under it.**

### 2.14 Compute, then write
    The result is computed before the sentence describing it is written.

 **This is the protocol most often violated in the production of this book.** The dominant failure recorded in Chapter 27 is prose asserting a conclusion while the output contradicting it stands on the same screen. The protocol is stated here because it is the one that most needed stating.

### 2.15 Apply P8 to every failure
    Every failed attempt is a true answer, and by P8 a true answer is a bound. Record what
    each failure excludes, and collect the exclusions.

 P8 says any true answer, good or bad, is a bound. A failed attempt is a true answer about
 where the target is not. The protocol is to treat it that way rather than discard it.

### 2.15.1 The procedure
 On each failure, write down three things:

  what was attempted                     the claim or construction
  what it excludes                       the region of the answer space now ruled out
  which kind                             upper bound · lower bound · negative · structural

 Then read the collection. Lower bounds say the answer contains something; upper bounds say it is
 contained in something; negatives remove whole methods; structural failures name an object that was
 the wrong one. When the lower and upper bounds meet, the answer is between them.

### 2.15.2 Worked, on §30.3
 Ten consecutive failures on one question, each read as a bound:

  the failure                                              the bound                                               kind
  Γ-free ≠ reorderable                                     reorderable ⊊ totally balanced                          upper
  chain sufficient, not necessary                          chain ⊊ reorderable                                     lower
  depth-2 and depth-3 descent stall, worsening with        no constant-depth local procedure decides it            negati
  size                                                                                                             ve
  linear extensions of the inclusion order incomplete      the inclusion order is not the join-irreducible poset   struct
                                                                                                                   ural
  the slice-chain condition fails at d = 3                 the d = 2 condition does not lift                       upper
  the 3-SAT encoding was refuted                           a reduction must control which pairs arise              metho
                                                                                                                   d
  descent traps at E = 1, 2                                the transposition landscape is not convex               negati
                                                                                                                   ve
  occupancy sort succeeds 53%                              tie-breaking among equal occupancy is not free          lower
  "row chain or column chain" holds at 68%                 neither axis alone decides it; the condition is         struct
                                                           two-sided                                               ural
  the chain criterion characterises the downset            ℛ-closure and downset-closure differ by the direction   struct
  operator, not ℛ                                          of one axis                                             ural

 Two lower bounds, two upper, two negatives, five structural, one methodological. Between
 them: reorderable ⊋ chain and ⊊ totally balanced, and the gap contains at least the diagonals —
 which is what produced the characterisation:

    X is ℛ-closed iff X = {(r,c) : c ≤ M\(r) and r ≤ N\(c)}, with M\, N\ the running maxima of its own
    row and column maxima.

 Ten failures produced one exact statement. None of them was wasted, and none of them would have
 been kept without the protocol.

 And the last two are the ones that located it. The two-sided bound said neither axis decides alone;
 the operator bound said the borrowed condition belonged to a different closure. Together they force
 the form c ≤ M\(r) and r ≤ N\(c), which no single-axis condition can express and which the two-cell
 diagonal {(0,0), (1,1)} realises minimally.

### 2.15.3 Why it belongs here rather than among the principles
 It says nothing about what an index is. It is a way of working, and it is the reason Chapter 27 is the
 longest chapter in this book rather than the most embarrassing.

    A register of withdrawals is a list of bounds. Read it that way and it stops being a
    confession.

### 2.16 The grid — the book's method, stated
 Every open question is a set of requirements, and requirements form an index. Lay them as a
 grid, mark what is occupied, and read the empty cells rather than hunting for them.

    The first empty cell in a column is the whole obstacle. Everything below it is downstream.

### 2.16.1 Why this is the method and not a technique
 It is Chapter 6 applied to the book's own ignorance. A periodic table with 36 unfilled cells does not
 need a chemist to guess which are missing — the layout shows them. An open question with eight
 unfilled requirements does not need twenty-two attempts — the grid shows that four of them are one.

 Worked on §30.3: eight empty cells, four rows deep, at d ≥ 3.

  requirement                     d=2                                d≥3
  characterisation                proved                             ·
  decision procedure              linear                             ·
  YES certificate                 an order                           an order
  NO certificate                  Tucker — unbounded                 unbounded
  obstruction family              Tucker 1972                        unbounded, route closed
  construction rules              six preserving                     six preserving
  hardness                        P, the CSP is binary               ·
  the data structure              PQ-tree                            ·

    Three rows are full at every d, and they are the three that needed no d-specific work —
    the certificate is the order itself, the obstruction family is Tucker's, the construction rules were
    established once. Every empty cell is downstream of one missing object: the d ≥ 3 analogue
    of a PQ-tree.

 Twenty-two formulations were attempts on four cells that depend on one. The grid says so in
 a table; hunting concealed it for a day.

### 2.16.2 And it classifies the whole of Q

  item                             first empty cell                       the obstacle
  Sc VI 6s                         the level is measured                  an instrument
  Paschen & Götze                  the scan is text-searchable            a digitisation artefact
  nine literatures                 each is entered                        labour
  Kreuzer–Skarke                   a readable copy                        a file format
  target-spin axis                 the extended lattice is built          labour — now done
  reorderability at d ≥ 3          characterisation                       a missing object

 Five of six were blocked by something outside the index. One is blocked by something that
 does not exist. §32.2 previously read "four external, one construction, one mathematical" — the grid's
 distinction between retrievable and requires a new object is the one that matters, and it also says
 which to do first.

 The target-spin axis was taken on that basis and it closed. Λ₉ has 1,654 cells, E = 0, is a sublattice,
 is rank-modular on 200,000 pairs, projects exactly onto Λ₈, keeps the constraint graph a tree, extends F
 by one pendant factor, and has F(−1) = 2 unchanged. One unit of labour, six properties confirmed,
 \|Q\| from six to five.

### 2.16.3 A reduced question is still open
    An open question reduced to a smaller or a different question is still open.

 A reduction feels like progress and reads like an answer. §30.3 supplies five in one session, each
 announced as a closure:

  the reduction                                                             what was still open
  reorderability ⟹ C1P + monotone endpoints                                 the endpoint search
  the search ⟹ a PQ-tree frontier question                                  a correct PQ-tree
  the frontier question ⟹ a bottom-up traversal                             the traversal's state
  the traversal ⟹ a row→node anchoring map                                  rows anchoring as runs
  the whole problem ⟹ a CSP on axis orders                                  the ternary constraints

 A reduction transfers the difficulty to a new object, and the new object is unfamiliar, so its
 difficulty is not yet visible. It therefore looks like progress twice: once for what it explains, and once for
 what it has not been tested on.

 State the reduction and the residue in the same sentence. Not "this reduces to a CSP" but "this
 reduces to a CSP whose ternary constraints are 94% of Λ's pairs and are not handled."

### 2.17 Triangulation — three results locate a fourth
    For every three results or bounds that measure the same object, triangulate: they
    identify a related data point that none of them states.

 Three measurements of one thing are not three facts. They are a position.

### 2.17.1 Worked, from §30.3

  the three                              what they                  the fourth, located
                                         measured
  valid orders disconnected ·            connectivity failure in    the disconnection is in the object, not the space —
  E-landscape traps · cell-growth        three different spaces     so no local method in any space can be complete
  peaks
  step 1 at d = 2 · step 2 at d = 3 ·    growth step, three         step = 2^(d−2), then proved by subcube removal
  step 4 at d = 4                        dimensions
  arity 2 bijunctive · arity 3           the constraint             the boundary is arity 3 and it is exact — the
  bijunctive · arity 4 contains          language                   procedure and the hardness proof are one law read at two
  1-in-3-SAT                                                        arities
  three reductions, all                  the encoding               the map X → C(X) is global, non-separable and
  over-constraining                                                 non-monotone — it admits no local editing

 Each fourth point was invisible in any single measurement and forced by the three together.
 The step law is the clearest: 1, 2, 4 fixed the ¾ ratio, and the ratio gave the subcube construction, which
 proved it.

### 2.17.2 And it is a check as well as a method
 Triangulation fails loudly. If three measurements of one object do not locate a consistent fourth, one of
 them is wrong — and that is how three corrections were caught in a single session:

 A map that is global, non-separable and non-monotone should have a SMALL image.
 Measured: 39% at d = 3 and 49.5% at d = 4, rising. The three coordinates were right and the
 inference from them was not — they locate an obstruction to EDITING the map, not to its range.

    Three consistent measurements give a fourth result. Three measurements that give no
    consistent fourth give a correction instead. Both outcomes are useful and the protocol does not
    distinguish them in advance.

### 2.17.3 Why it belongs with the protocols
 It is P8 with a count attached. Every failure is a bound; three bounds on one object are a
 coordinate. §2.15 says to record failures as bounds; this says what to do once three of them are in
 hand.

 And it composes with §2.16. The grid shows which cells are empty; triangulation fills one from three
 of its neighbours.


### 2.18 What is computable, and what is decided
 §3.8 ends by saying a coordinate system answers to two masters and only one of them is
 computable. The word is doing real work and the book has not defined it. It defines it with its own
 operator.

    **A quantity is COMPUTABLE in this book when it is recoverable by ℛ from what the book
    prints** — when two readers running the stated procedure on the printed text return the same
    value, and neither has to decide anything to get there.

 That is not a claim about difficulty. E(Λ) = 0 over 475,800 pairs is computable and took minutes;
 whether A.5's language is order or analysis is not computable and took one sentence.

 **Nineteen quantities in the work of these sessions are computable.** E(Λ) = 0; |J(Λ)| = 17 with its
 twenty covering relations; the Birkhoff down-set count of 976; the alphabet's size Σ(|Aᵢ| − 1) = 17;
 the seventeen-bit encoding with join as OR and meet as AND; the 7.07 bits of surplus; the twenty
 implications cutting 131,072 words to 976; every tower cell count and every density; closure through
 the f shell at 499,246 cells and exhaustively to 47,775,744 ambient ones; the E1 puncture at four
 meets all producing (0,0); the triangle at four caps; lemma 4 over 2,354 interval sets; E(audits) at
 11, 0 and 19; dim(audit hierarchy) = 2; E(G), recomputed at build; E(Q); the tripwire's 150 of 216; the fifty-four
 of one hundred and two. **Each is a function of the printed object and of nothing else.**

 **Ten are decisions, and they are of three kinds.**

 *Frames.* The fibration of Appendix D, the coordinate list for the audits, the caps of §7.4. E is
 computable **given** them and none of them is computed — register 275 is the general statement, that
 a zero is worth the size of the box it was computed in, and the box is chosen.

 *Readings.* Applying a stated criterion to a case it does not settle: whether A.5's proof makes it
 order or analysis, whether the five results are elements under §D.5.3, whether *law* means
 holds-of-every-index or holds-and-forbids. The criterion is printed; the application is a judgement,
 and §D.5.4 marks the one place this work made one and says so.

 *Claims about the outside.* Precedent, novelty, whether a measurement exists. §29.1 proves the first
 of these permanently undecidable in one direction, which is the strongest statement of this kind the
 book contains.

 **And the distinction has a use, not merely a name.** Every computable quantity in this book can be
 checked by a reader with the printed text and no access to its author. Every decision must be
 *stated* rather than checked, and the register is what stating them costs. **The seven prime audits
 of §3.1–7 test computable claims; nothing tests a decision**, which is why the seventeenth
 through twentieth entries of the register are corrections of computation and the twentieth through
 fortieth are reversals of judgement. The book cannot make its decisions checkable. It can make them
 visible, and that is the whole of what the register is for. Register 277.

### 2.18.1 The lattice applied to what it cannot compute
 §2.18 separates nineteen computed quantities from ten decisions and stops there, on the ground
 that nothing tests a decision. **The shape still applies to them, and it says two things.**

 **First: index the decisions and E finds one that has not been made.** Coordinates *scope*, *how it
 is stated* and *reversibility*, fibred by kind — frame, reading, outside claim. Ten decisions,
 E = 1 fibred and 5 unfibred, with a single cell admitted and absent in the *reading* fibre:

    a reading, scoped to one appendix, stated, freely reversible.

 That cell has an occupant the book declined to seat. Register 270 records that Appendices C and G
 are cited from the body only as wholes and never at section level, calls it a navigability defect,
 and leaves the choice open. **It is a reading, it is confined to two appendices, it is stated, and it
 costs nothing to reverse.** E over the decisions located a deferred decision exactly as E over Q
 located item H — which is the first evidence that the operator has any purchase outside the
 computable.

 **Second, and this is the finding: the decisions form a DAG, and two of them have two parents.**
 The order is acyclic, so the decisions can be made in some sequence and none presupposes itself. Six
 of the ten depend on nothing. But two do not have a single parent:

    the fibration of Appendix D — settled only with the caps of §7.4 **and** what counts as an
    element both in view

    not promoting the six theorems — settled only with the meaning of *law* **and** the audit
    coordinate list both in view

 **This is §12.11's dichotomy, arriving in the one place the book had assumed it did not reach.** A
 single-parent constraint propagates down a tree and is carried exactly; a two-parent constraint
 breaks the tree and admits only an envelope. The tight K has two parents and costs the cylinder
 2,475 cells. A two-parent *decision* is the same shape: **no earlier choice determines it, and it
 cannot be delegated to one predecessor or deferred until one is settled.** It must be made with both
 in view or not at all.

 **And with the fourth node of §18.4.1 the count goes to three.** In a triangle every node has degree
 two, so a decision has at most two parents; in K₄ every node has degree three. **Three-parent
 decisions exist and each resisted for the same reason** — none can be delegated to one predecessor
 or deferred until one is settled:

  the decision                        its three parents
  is Λ the clean control?             the object (976 cells, E = 0) · the law (what E = 0 certifies) ·
                                      reference (seven of thirteen letters conflated)
  is the closure rule correct?        the object (Λ closes) · the law (the admissibility criterion) ·
                                      reference (what *monotone* names in the constraint literature)
  is E(audits) meaningful?            the procedure (the set) · the law (the coordinate list, a frame) ·
                                      reference (whether *audit* means one thing across it)

 **Two instances was not a law and three is not much better**, which §18.4.1 says of its own
 prediction and which this section repeats of the extension. What is worth recording is that the
 three were not gathered for the test: each arrived as a difficulty before the shape was drawn.
 Register 407.

 The book has two such decisions and it stumbled on both. Registers 219 to 222 hold three successive
 positions on the element count, which is the fibration decision made once with the caps in view and
 once with the element criterion in view and never with both. Register 232 declines the promotion and
 says the reason is a trade between two things at once. **Two instances is not a law**, and the
 correspondence is offered as what it is — a shape that fits twice, in a set of ten, where the
 prediction it makes is that any third two-parent decision will be hard in the same way. Register 278.

### 2.19 What a safe repair requires
 §3.8 recorded a defect and declined to repair it on the ground that the repair was riskier
 than the defect. That judgement was made without a standard, and this states one.

    A repair is **safe** when five conditions hold. **(i)** Its invariant is written down before
    the edit — what must be true afterwards that was true before. **(ii)** The invariant is
    computable in the sense of §2.18, so it can be checked rather than asserted. **(iii)** The
    edit is a *permutation or an insertion*, never a rewrite: existing text is moved, not retyped,
    so no character can change unnoticed. **(iv)** It is reversible — a copy is taken first and
    restored if the check fails. **(v)** It is checked afterwards on the source **and** the
    artefact, because §3's FIDELITY exists for the gap between them.

 **And a sixth that governs which repair to attempt: prefer the smallest edit that removes the
 defect.** A defect visible in a hundred places is not therefore a hundred-place repair. Applying
 that here changed what the repair was.

### 2.19.1 The register, examined before repairing it
 The recorded defect was direction: entries 165 to 204 ascending, 205 onward descending. Measuring
 the register against its own headings found something larger and cheaper to fix. Each subsection of
 §28.7 states a count in its title — *Eight more*, *Twelve more*, *Fifteen more*, *Forty more* — and
 **§28.7.6 was titled "Three from the tower session" and contained eighty-six entries, 199 to 284**,
 every entry written since that session having been prepended into it. It is now four — the tower's
 three and register 202, which repairs their numbering — and §28.7.7 the eighty-seven written
 afterwards.

 **That is the defect worth repairing**, and it is one heading rather than a hundred and twenty
 entries. The direction is a presentation choice made twice; the heading is a false statement of
 count, which audit 15 ENUMERATION exists to catch and which it missed because it was pointed at
 tables and lists rather than at section titles. Registers 286 and 287.

### 2.20 The prime directive — nothing runs unbounded
 Every action, computation, tabulation, build and verification is executed in a **Zeno structure**: the
 work is divided until each piece fits inside a declared budget, each piece writes its result before
 the next begins, and a rerun costs only what has not already succeeded.

 **One exception, and it is the only one.** **Fetched literature is exempt.** A computation may be
 halved and rerun on half; an external source cannot, because halving what was read changes what was
 read. A source consulted at half length is a different source, and the register would carry a claim
 about a document nobody has seen. So a fetch runs to completion or is not made, and the budget is
 declared before it rather than enforced during it. Register 432.

    **No operation runs unbounded.** A step declares a budget; work that exceeds it is halved and
    retried; a step that has succeeded is never recomputed.

 **Three guarantees, and they are the reason for the rule.** Nothing is lost to a timeout, because the
 last completed step is on disk before the next starts. A rerun costs only the remainder. And a step
 that overruns **reports its overrun** rather than dying silently — which is the part that matters
 most, since a computation quietly growing toward a limit is invisible until the run it kills.

 **Why this book needs it.** Audit 1 tests 475,800 pairs; the exhaustive closure of §12.11 swept
 47,775,744 ambient cells; the f-shell rebuild of §32.1.2 carried 499,246. Each of those was run whole
 and none had a fallback. The harness is *zeno.py*, the state is *.zeno/*, and §2.20's requirement
 that the audits run on every output now runs under it: the twenty write their state, and an
 interrupted pass resumes rather than restarting.

 **What it does not do.** It does not make a slow computation fast, and it does not decide anything —
 a step that fails is retained with its error for retry, not silently skipped. The budget is a
 declaration about what is expected, so an overrun is a finding about the computation and belongs in
 the register like any other. Register 343.

## 3. The twenty-five prime audits, ordered by what a failure costs
 Every session closes with twenty-five audits. They are not spot checks, and they are not equal. They

### 3.0 Ten of the twenty-five test the index; twelve test the book; three test the register

 **For a reader who wants the index rather than the artefact, ten audits carry the whole of it:**

      1·   LATTICE        Λ's cells, closure and defect, recomputed from the construction
      2·   EQUATIONS      the printed identities re-derived — F(1), F(−1), d(x,y), E
      8·   CELL           every cell the text names exists in the index
      9·   DISTINCTNESS   no two index coordinates collapse
      10·  SCOPE          an E value is stated with the index it belongs to
      13·  AGREEMENT      one quantity, one value across the whole work
      14·  ARITHMETIC     the tower's own table against the recomputed tower
      18·  REPRODUCTION   the two headline figures reproduce
      20·  PROJECTION     no claim of predictive power anywhere in the text
      21·  INPUT          every computation names its input set

    **These ten would still have to pass if the book were rewritten from scratch in
    another form.** They are about Λ and the objects around it.

 **The other twelve test whether this book is a usable artefact** — CONSISTENCY, REDUNDANCY,
 ARTEFACT, COHERENCE, ATTRIBUTION, ANTECEDENT, MARKUP, ENUMERATION, FIDELITY, MEASURE, SEQUENCE and
 CENSUS. **Every one has caught something real**, and several caught faults in this chapter's own
 reconstruction. *But a reader assessing the mathematics does not need to know that a § reference
 resolves or that a figure count matches; those protect the reader from the author.*

    **The split is stated rather than enforced. All twenty-five run on every build,
    and the artefact twelve have caught more faults than the index ten** — which is
    the honest reason not to demote them.

**And three test the REGISTER of objects rather than the book or the index** — GRAPH,
CYCLE and NOMENCLATURE, the last three of the table above, added at register 1225. They exist because three faults
reached the Mathematical Compendium unseen by the first twenty-two: no audit read
`mathreg.py` as a graph, and none asked whether an object could be *spoken about* rather
than only cited by key. **A dependency naming an object that does not exist; a cycle, so
that an object is justified by itself; and an object with no name.** *The cycle
Q.delta → Q.exch → Q.delta passed 248 objects and six audit sets before 24 was written,
and 25 failed at ninety-four before the naming pass closed it.*


 are ordered by the cost of the failure each one catches.

    The first two can make the book WRONG. The next two can make it UNREADABLE AS AN
    ARGUMENT. The next two can make it UNUSABLE AS AN OBJECT. The seventh can make it
    DISHONEST, and it is the only one that cannot be run by reading the book.

 1 — LATTICE. Every structural claim recomputed from the construction: closure under join and meet,
 E(X) = 0, rank modularity, the fibre identities, the rank polynomial, F(1) and F(−1), |J(Λ)| and the height.
 Over all 475,800 pairs of Λ, not a sample. First, because if the object is not closed the book has no
 subject.

 2 — EQUATIONS. Every equation the book states, evaluated numerically against the data it claims to
 describe. All of them, including the ones unchanged since the session that wrote them. Second,
 because a wrong equation is a false claim.

 3 — CONSISTENCY. No two statements in the book may contradict. A claim corrected in one chapter
 must not survive uncorrected in another; a number revised in a table must be revised in the prose that
 cites it. Third, because a contradiction leaves the reader unable to tell which statement is the claim.

 4 — REDUNDANCY. No statement may survive its own supersession. Every summary is compared
 against what it summarises; every table against the section it condenses; and any section that only
 restates another is removed rather than maintained. Fourth, because a stale claim is a false claim that
 passed every other check.

 5 — ARTEFACT. Audits 1–4 read the SOURCE. This one reads the built PDF. Missing glyphs, figures
 present and non-blank, the title page and running header read back, no table overflowing its frame, the
 whitespace measured from glyph and image geometry, and every figure checked for ink, tone, resolution,
 duplication and correct numbering. Fifth, because the source can be perfect and the page still wrong.

 6 — COHERENCE. *Source, other places, unusable, presupposes labels are addressable.* Every
 cross-reference resolves; every figure has a caption and every caption a figure;
 every stated count matches its computation; every principle referred to is defined; and the contents
 block is checked against the actual headings. Last, because these failures cost navigation rather
 than truth — and because it is the only audit that can be run before the others.

7 — ATTRIBUTION. Every claim that points outside the book resolves to a source the book lists, and
 every source listed is claimed by something inside it. Five checks: each work cited in the text
 appears in the References; each entry in the References is cited somewhere; §29.2's owner column
 names people the References carry; every Appendix D element recorded as *precedent found* has a
 reachable citation; and every data source in Appendix B is listed. Seventh, because a missing
 attribution makes the book neither wrong nor unreadable nor unusable — it makes it **dishonest**,
 which is a worse failure and a quieter one.

    Audits 1–2 check the book against the world. Audits 3–4 check the book against itself.
    Audits 5–6 check the book against what a reader receives. **Audit 7 checks the book against
    what it owes**, and it is the only one of the seven that cannot be run by reading the book.

### 2.21 A figure removed must have its data represented another way
 §F.3.3 and §E.1.3 remove two counts because a figure computed from the book cannot be printed as
 prose. **Removal is not a repair on its own.** A stale number is wrong; a missing number is not
 therefore right, and deleting one without replacing what it carried loses the reader something the
 book knew.

    **Any figure withdrawn must have its data represented another way** — by the law that generates
    it, by the stable structure beneath it, or by a computation the artefact performs. The
    withdrawal and the replacement are one act.

 **Three representations are available and they are not equivalent.**

 **By the law.** Best where one exists. §F.3.3 replaces a count with the constraint that generates it —
 *definition = stated forbids inputs = enumerated* — which cannot go stale because it is a fact about
 the assignment rules rather than the text.

 **By the stable structure.** Where no law is available but the shape holds still. §F.3.3 names the
 nine combinations admitted and absent in every state of the book, against a count that read five
 different values in one session; §E.1.3 asserts E(Q) = 0 under its fibration, which has held at five
 different item totals.

 **By computation at build.** The weakest, and necessary where the quantity is genuinely a reading. The
 press prints E(G), the open count, E(Q) unfibred, the register's span and the withdrawal ratio on
 every build. **These are readouts and are marked as such** — a number the artefact computes about
 itself is evidence of a state, not a claim about the work.

 **And the rule caught its author immediately.** §E.1.3 removed E(Q) and supplied only the fibred
 statement; the unfibred count had no representation at all until the press was extended to compute it,
 one turn later. **A rule stated after the act it governs is a rule found by breaking it.**
 Register 374.

 **The twenty, with their coordinates.** §3.8 computes E(audits) and the hierarchy's dimension from
 these four values per audit, and until now the book asserted both without printing what they were
 computed from. The table is the input; a reader can rerun either.

   no  audit           reads       compares against   a failure costs   presupposes
   1   LATTICE         object      a computation      wrong             nothing
   2   EQUATIONS       source      a computation      wrong             nothing
   3   CONSISTENCY     source      other places       unreadable        claims individually true
   4   REDUNDANCY      source      other places       unreadable        also mutually consistent
   5   ARTEFACT        artefact    itself             unusable          also mutually consistent
   6   COHERENCE       source      other places       unusable          labels are addressable
   7   ATTRIBUTION     outside     other places       dishonest         claims individually true
   8   CELL            object      itself             wrong             nothing
   9   DISTINCTNESS    object      other places       wrong             nothing
  10   SCOPE           source      itself             wrong             claims individually true
  11   ANTECEDENT      source      itself             unreadable        labels are addressable
  12   MARKUP          source      itself             unusable          nothing
  13   AGREEMENT       source      other places       wrong             claims individually true
  14   ARITHMETIC      source      a computation      unreadable        claims individually true
  15   ENUMERATION     source      a computation      unusable          claims individually true
  16   FIDELITY        artefact    other places       unusable          also mutually consistent
  17   MEASURE         artefact    a computation      unusable          also mutually consistent
  18   REPRODUCTION    outside     a computation      dishonest         claims individually true
  19   SEQUENCE        source      a computation      unusable          nothing
  20   PROJECTION      artefact    other places       unusable          claims individually true
  21   INPUT           source      itself             unusable          claims individually true
  22   CENSUS          source      itself             wrong             claims individually true
  23   GRAPH           register    itself             wrong             nothing                 
  24   CYCLE           register    itself             wrong             nothing                 
  25   NOMENCLATURE    register    itself             unusable          labels                  

 **Twenty-five audits over twenty-five distinct cells, no collision.** The order on each coordinate is
 object < source < artefact < outside; itself < other places < a computation; wrong < unreadable <
 unusable < dishonest; and nothing < claims individually true < also mutually consistent, with
 *labels are addressable* reading as the second. Register 376.

### 2.22 A corroborable entry must carry its object, and the object must carry it


### 2.23 A closed index must be stated by its expression, not only by its verdict

    A closed index is exactly the set its own envelopes admit. §15.2 proves those
    envelopes are recoverable from its cells, so an expression always exists — and
    printing E = 0 without it withholds something the author already has.

 **A reader given the verdict must take closure on trust; a reader given the envelopes can rerun
 it.** The difference is §2.18's, between a computable quantity and a decided one, applied to the
 central claim this book makes about every object it builds.

 **Audited across the twenty closed indices this work and its companion carry: fourteen state an
 expression, one states a fibration and no expression, and five state a verdict and nothing else** —
 the audits at three coordinates, Q fibred by domain, and the geometry, solution and ANEC-coverage
 indices of the companion paper. Λ states F(z₁…z₈); the tower states its five bounds; the register
 index states the envelope ℛ recovered from it; the back-matter Index states its down-set condition.
 The five that do not are not wrong. **They are unrerunnable**, which is the state §2.18 exists to
 make visible and this protocol exists to forbid. Register 439.

    Any register entry that a proof, theorem, equation or expression settles must name that
    object, and the object must name the entry. A reference in one direction is a citation;
    a reference in both is an index.

 **Half the register fails this, and the failure is asymmetric.** Of 219 entry-lines carrying 248
 numbers: 66 carry a mathematical object *and* a § reference; **89 carry an object and no reference**;
 35 are mathematical in subject and carry neither; 59 are narrative, settled by no object at all.
 **107 entries are corroborable and not searchable from what corroborates them.**

 **And the reverse direction barely exists.** Of the 132 mathematical objects this book states, **four
 cite a register entry.** An entry can sometimes be traced to its mathematics; the mathematics can
 almost never be traced to what it corrected.

    A register that cannot be searched from the mathematics is a record of the work and not
    an instrument of it.

 Register 415.

### 2.24 Never one heuristic — §2.8, and the one thing it does not say

    Where a quantity is reached by a heuristic rather than computed, run at least two
    heuristics of different kind and **report the SPREAD**. A single heuristic cannot
    tell you whether it is in the case where it works.

 **The first sentence is §2.8 and this book already had it.** *For every quantity that matters, build
 a second derivation that does not pass through the first* — and the seed is a quantity that matters,
 prune-greedy was one derivation, and no second was ever built. **§2.8's own gloss is what happened:
 an identity computed along one path is vacuous.** The protocol was not missing; it was not applied.

 **What §2.24 adds is the SPREAD.** §2.8 asks for agreement between two routes and is satisfied when
 they agree. **A spread of thirty-three around an exact seven is not disagreement about a value — it
 is information about which case you are in**, and §2.8 has no place to put it. That is the whole of
 what is new here.

 *Found by indexing the protocols: §2.8 and §2.24 occupy the same cell in every coordinate — trigger,
 object, failure and how each was earned. Register 514.*

 **This protocol exists because that failure ran for twelve cycles.** Every seed size in §14.5.7 and
 §14.5.8 came from one algorithm — prune-greedy — and every expression fitted to those sizes was
 checked against numbers from the same source. **The fits could not fail**, which is §4.6's condition
 for a check that is not evidence.

 **What the spread shows.** Five heuristics on Λ₈ give 12, 7, 7, 7 and **40**, around an exact seven.
 On a regular down-set they give 7, 7, 7, 7, 7 — and the lower bound is 7 too, so the answer is
 exact. **The spread is zero where the object is regular and thirty-three where it is not**, and no
 single run distinguishes those cases.

    **A heuristic's output is a bound. Two heuristics' outputs are a bound and a
    warning. Neither is a value.**

 And a lower bound is worth as much as a second heuristic: §14.5.9's disjoint-witness bound is what
 showed the gap between seven and forty was real rather than a tie. Register 506.

### 3.1 Why three of the audits were needed
 CONSISTENCY: twenty-eight cross-references once pointed at a section that had moved. ARTEFACT: a
 rendered page once carried glyphs the source did not. ATTRIBUTION: the three precedents that place
 this book's central theorem in existing literature were cited in the text and absent from the
 References. Each history is in the register — 262, 285, 294 and the grouped mechanisms at §28.7.8.
 **A protocol states what to do; the record states what happened.**

### 3.2 The whitespace clause, and two measurements that were wrong
 A gap a reader sees is a defect the source cannot show. So the audit reads the position of every
 glyph and every image and compares the inked extent with the frame.

 On this book: median foot gap 3.3%, nine pages above 15%, worst 31% — and that one is the
 end of a section, where a gap is correct. Interior gaps cluster at 16–28%, which is exactly the
 CondPageBreak(58mm) guard that stops a heading landing at the foot of a page.

 Tightening the guard makes it worse, not better. At 46mm the count of gaps falls from nine to
 seven and the worst rises from 31% to 43%, because a heading allowed to sit lower pushes the figure
 block after it off the page entirely. 58mm is the optimum, and it was found by measuring rather
 than by preference.

### 3.3 Exhaustive, and what to do when it is impossible
 Check every cell where the set is finite and small enough. Where it is not, state the sampling and its
 size in the same sentence as the result, and never let a sampled figure stand where an exhaustive one
 is affordable — §16.5 stood on 30,000 samples of a 6,912-cell box until register 248.

### 3.4 And two of the audit's own measurements were wrong first
 The first attempt used the PDF text matrix directly and reported a median inked fraction of 103%
 — impossible — with y-coordinates of 5, 6 and −220. The text matrix is not page geometry until it is
 composed with the current transformation matrix.

 The second attempt counted characters and not images, and flagged seven pages as having a
 25–45% gap at the head. All seven begin with a figure. The space was not empty; it was occupied by
 something the measurement could not see.

    Both are the same error as the audits they were written to replace: a check looking at the
    wrong artefact. An audit is not exempt from the discipline it enforces, and this one needed two
    corrections before its first clean reading.

### 3.5 What the figure audit found
 Twenty-eight figures, each checked against its caption and its file.

 Clean: every figure has a caption and every caption a figure; no image is blank or unusually dark; no file
 is duplicated; nothing in fig/ is unused; and after one correction every figure number matches the
 chapter it sits in.

 The one correction. Figures 23.1 and 23.2 appeared in the wrong order — the arity figure sits in §30.3.2
 and the step-law figure in §30.3.4, so the numbering ran backwards against the reading order. Swapped.

 And one defect that is recorded rather than repaired.

  figures at 300 dpi or better                                                  5
  between 200 and 300                                                           8
  under 200 dpi                                                                 15
  median                                                                        164 dpi

 Fifteen figures were produced by an earlier pipeline whose default was figure.dpi = 160, and
 at 163 mm rendered width that gives 140–164 dpi — below print standard. Regenerating them needs
 the source data of the sessions that made them, which this one does not carry. Appendix C.3
 lists them with the rest of what was inherited and not recomputed.

    The audit's value here is not that it fixed the figures. It is that the number is now written
    down instead of unknown.

### 3.6 Why the REDUNDANCY audit was needed
 Because two sections survived their own supersession and passed all five.

 A summary table listed Q's eight items while Appendix E enumerated, indexed and closed them —
 the same content, maintained twice. And a status table still read "nine literatures entered, nine
 named and unentered" and "order recovery beyond trees: open, and known to be hard", both superseded
 hours earlier by §E.5 and §30.3.

  audit                           why it passed
  lattice · equations             neither is a structural or numerical claim
  consistency                     both were internally consistent and cited sections that exist
  artefact                        both rendered correctly
  coherence                       both were well-formed tables with valid references

    A stale statement is not inconsistent with anything. It agrees with the version of the
    book that no longer exists. That is why the check has to compare a summary against what it
    summarises, rather than a document against itself.

 The remedy was to delete a chapter. What survived of it — one paragraph on what the collection
 closed — opens Chapter 32, where it does work instead of restating.

### 3.7 The precedences, and the certificate for the dimension
 §3.8 states nineteen precedences and an order dimension of 2, **and printed neither the precedences nor
 the certificate.** Both are here, so both are checkable.

 **The twenty-five.** CELL before LATTICE, since a set containing an invalid cell has no closure worth
 testing. LATTICE before EQUATIONS. CELL before DISTINCTNESS. EQUATIONS before SCOPE, AGREEMENT,
 ARITHMETIC and ENUMERATION, since a property of a number presupposes the number. CONSISTENCY before
 REDUNDANCY, and both before ARTEFACT. MARKUP before ARTEFACT and FIDELITY. ARTEFACT before MEASURE,
 FIDELITY and PROJECTION. ATTRIBUTION before REPRODUCTION. SEQUENCE before ANTECEDENT, COHERENCE and
 PROJECTION. **And INPUT before SCOPE, ARITHMETIC and ENUMERATION** — all three read a result against
 its inputs, and cannot run where the inputs are not printed.

 **The order falls into three components** — ten audits, nine, and a pair — and the dimension of a
 disjoint sum is the greatest of its parts. **A dimension is certified by exhibiting two linear
 extensions whose intersection is exactly the order**, which is checkable in a page even though finding
 it is not:

  component of nine
   L1  CELL < DISTINCTNESS < LATTICE < EQUATIONS < AGREEMENT < INPUT < SCOPE < ARITHMETIC <
       ENUMERATION
   L2  INPUT < CELL < LATTICE < EQUATIONS < ENUMERATION < ARITHMETIC < SCOPE < AGREEMENT <
       DISTINCTNESS

  component of ten
   L1  CONSISTENCY < REDUNDANCY < MARKUP < ARTEFACT < FIDELITY < MEASURE < SEQUENCE < PROJECTION <
       COHERENCE < ANTECEDENT
   L2  SEQUENCE < ANTECEDENT < COHERENCE < MARKUP < CONSISTENCY < REDUNDANCY < ARTEFACT < PROJECTION <
       MEASURE < FIDELITY

 **dim(hierarchy) = 2, certified.** The ten-element component has 4,140 linear extensions and two of
 them suffice; the nine-element component has 792 and two suffice.

    **A certificate is checkable where a search is not.** Verifying these four lists takes 362
    comparisons; finding them took eight and a half million.

 That is the third companion paper's third finding arriving in the book's own instruments: **an arrow
 is a ranking function and a dimension is a realiser, and both are things an analyst supplies rather
 than derives.** Register 377.

### 3.7.1 The twenty-first audit — INPUT
 Registers 376 and 377 found the same defect twice in two turns: **a result stated from data the book
 does not print.** E(audits), then 17, rested on coordinates that were never printed; dim(hierarchy) = 2
 rested on precedences that were never printed. Both were correct and neither was checkable.

  21 — INPUT. *Source, itself, unusable, presupposes the claims are individually true.* **Every
  stated result must print the data it was computed from.** Not the computation and not the method —
  the input set, in a form a reader can rerun. A result whose inputs are absent is not wrong; it is
  **unusable**, which is a different cost and the one this coordinate names.

 **Its cell was among the seventeen §3.8 leaves admitted and absent**, and adding it takes E(audits)
 from 17 to 16. That is the third of that count confirmed by a defect rather than proposed, after
 SEQUENCE and PROJECTION.

 **Why the other twenty could not catch it.** ARITHMETIC and ENUMERATION check a number against a
 computation the book performs; SCOPE checks that a figure carries its frame. **All three presuppose
 the input exists in the text.** A result whose input was never printed passes every one of them,
 because there is nothing for them to disagree with — the same shape as PROJECTION's finding that
 correctness and completeness are different failures.

 **First run: nine stated results tested, nine pass** — but only because registers 376 and 377 printed
 the two tables an hour earlier. Run against the book as it stood this morning it would have failed
 twice. Register 379.

### 3.8 How the set grew, and the two things that held
 Seven audits became twenty across these sessions, each addition written as its own account of why the
 set had been incomplete. Those accounts are one thing said seven times, and it is said once here.

  when                     audits   E(audits)   what the gap was, and what closed it
  as first stated               7   11 at 3 coords   the set was closed under self-reference: every
                                                     audit checked the book against the book
  ATTRIBUTION added             7   —                the first outward-pointing check; found four
                                                     works cited and unlisted
  the eleven retained          18   0 at 3 coords    every admitted cell occupied
  DEPTH admitted               18   19 at 4 coords   the hierarchy demanded a fourth coordinate; the
                                                     zero had been cheap
  SEQUENCE added               19   18               a numbering is a claim about position and
                                                     nothing tested it
  PROJECTION added             20   17               a derived list is as complete as its rule, and a
                                                     rule that misses a case fails silently

 **Two findings survive the growth and are the reason to keep the record at all.**

 **E is relative to its box.** E(audits) read 11, then 0, then 19, then 17 without the audits changing
 — only the coordinates did. §18.4.1 and register 275 carry the general statement: **a zero is worth
 exactly the size of the box it was computed in**, so every E owes both its fibration and its
 coordinate list.

 **The hierarchy did not tangle as it grew.** Nineteen precedences hold between the twenty; the
 precedence poset falls into three components; its **order dimension is 2, unchanged from seven audits
 to twenty**. Two coordinates recover which audit runs before which; the other two carry what an audit
 *is*. Order dimension answers §10's S2 and says nothing about S1, and Λ has the same division — eight
 coordinates over an order of far lower dimension, the surplus being what makes a cell mean rather
 than sort. Registers 262, 273–276, 285, 294, 295, 309.


### 3.9 The audits are run on every output, and the requirement is executable
 §3 defines twenty audits and left when to run them as a habit. A habit is not a check. From this
 build onward **every output of this work is audited by all twenty before it is delivered**, and the
 requirement exists as a file rather than an intention: *The Method 1.6 audits.py* runs the whole set
 against the source and the rendered artefact together and exits with the number of failures, so a
 build that has not been audited cannot be mistaken for one that has.

 **And three of the twenty-one have never fired, which §4.6 says is not the same as passing.** A check
 must be demonstrated capable of returning a failure before its passing is evidence, and ANTECEDENT,
 ARITHMETIC and REPRODUCTION have caught nothing in this book — no register entry names any of them.
 Each has a constructible failing input and none needs new theory:

  audit          the input that must make it fail
  ANTECEDENT     a label used in Chapter 1 and defined in Chapter 32 — a forward reference the
                 source-against-itself read must flag
  ARITHMETIC     a product stated beside its own printed factors and wrong: *33 × 5 = 166* against
                 §12.6.1's table, which prints 33, 5 and 165
  REPRODUCTION   an altered Condon–Shortley term entry, against the microstate enumeration that
                 reproduces ten tables of ten exactly

 **Running these three is not optional bookkeeping.** §3.6 records two sections surviving their own
 supersession and passing five audits; §3.4 records the whitespace audit's first two measurements
 being wrong. **An audit that has never returned a failure is indistinguishable from one that
 cannot**, and this book has three. Register 422.

 **Why a file and not a rule.** Register 259 records a defect repaired twice and reintroduced in the
 next turn, and §4.4 draws the conclusion: a fix that leaves the mechanism unchanged is an
 instance fix. Every audit in this book was run by hand until now, which made *audited* a claim about
 a session and not a property of an artefact. It is now the second. The script reads the source by
 name, the PDF by name and the press by name, so FIDELITY and PROJECTION compare three files that
 must agree and none of them can be updated alone without the run reporting it.

 **What it does not do.** It decides nothing. Every flag it raises is a hypothesis under §4.7 and
 must be localised before it is recorded — four of the last five flags this book has seen were the
 detectors and not the book. And **it cannot audit itself**: the script is an instrument, its own
 correctness is untested by anything in the twenty, and that is a gap this section admits rather than
 closes. Register 300.

## 4. The failures of the assistant, and the protocol each one earns

 **Ten mechanisms, each earned by a failure that happened.** They are not a taxonomy assembled in
 advance; every one was extracted from the register after the fact, and §28.7.8 groups the entries.

  4.1   **attributed outward before checking inward** — a recomputation must suspect the model
        before it suspects the text
  4.2   **wrote into a structure without reading it** — enumerate the targets before writing
  4.3   **a claim of completion is a claim** — verify completion in the pass that claims it
  4.4   **a count kept by judgement** — read the list; do not carry the number
  4.5   **a conclusion written before the output** — the order matters even when the answer is right
  4.6   **a test that could not fail** — exhibit the failure mode before trusting the pass
  4.7   **a flag is a hypothesis** — localise a detector's output before filing it as a defect
  4.8   **a reading the source never made** — an assumption is the reader's until the source states it
  4.9   **a coordinate collapsed into a count** — ρ, and every scalar standing in for a structure
  4.10  **a stale figure left behind its own correction** — a figure about the book goes stale the
        moment the book is written into

    **And §28.7.9 adds a coordinate this list does not have: the LEVEL a fault landed
    at.** Three of the ten sit at the value, three at the derivation, four at the
    inference — and the axis merges two the list keeps apart, since *a stale figure* is
    a value going stale and *a conclusion written early* is an inference drawn early:
    **the same act at two depths.**

 **This chapter is short because the material is the register.** Each mechanism is a name for a class
 of entry and the entries are the evidence. *An earlier form was a heading with no body at all,
 carrying the word SEVEN while the register held ten — cited as §4.1 and §4.2 throughout a book that
 never defined them. Register 655.*

 Each prevents a specific failure, and each failure occurred:

  §2.2 admissibility                    bracketing unresolved levels                 —
  §2.3 cost resolvability               reading rounding as physics                  Al I nf
  §2.4 dilution                         excluding a viable species                   Ar II, Bi I
  §2.5 census first                     searching without targets                    27 sequences
                                                                                      enumerated
  §2.6 declines recorded                an uncheckable collection                    Ne II, Cu II

# PART II — THE LATTICE

 **And the table stopped where the protocols stopped being about spectroscopy.** Eight are uncited by
 it — §2.4 above, and §2.16 through §2.22, every one written after this section was. They are added
 here, and one of them needs a second column.

  protocol                               what it prevents                             instance
  §2.16 the grid                        hunting where a layout would show             §30.3, 22 formulations
  §2.17 triangulation                   three measurements read as three facts        the step law, 1, 2, 4
  §2.18 computable or decided           a decision checked as though computable        reg. 277
  §2.19 what a safe repair requires     a repair riskier than its defect              reg. 286, 287
  §2.20 the prime directive             a computation lost whole to a timeout          reg. 343
  §2.21 a withdrawn figure's data       a number deleted rather than replaced          reg. 374
  §2.22 a corroborable entry            a defect that cannot be traced to its proof    reg. 415

 **And the second column is P16's, which no failure corroborates.** P16 — treat bounds and limits as
 coordinate values — has three realisations and no register entry, because **it was never learned
 from a failure; it was adopted and then built with**: §17.4 replaces (g₁, g₂) by (g₁, G) and
 conservation holds by construction; §12.11.0.2 carries (g, G) and recovers prior occupancy as a
 derived quantity; §18.4.2 indexes the partial sums of a mass vector and reaches treewidth 1 at every
 n. **An operation may be corroborated by a construction as well as by a failure**, and P16 is the
 only one in this book that is. Register 421.

## 6. The periodic table is not a closed index
 Take the periodic table at its word. Its coordinates are period and group; its cells are the elements. Ask
