 what those coordinates, and the occupied cells alone, imply about which other cells could exist.

 The answer is thirty-six cells that do not.

| | |
|---|---|
| main-table cells (lanthanides and actinides set aside) | 90 |
| cells the structure admits | 126 |
| **external definition cost E(X)** | **36** |

 The thirty-six are (p1, g2) through (p1, g17), (p2, g3) through (p2, g12), and (p3, g3) through (p3, g12) — **the gaps in the short periods, every one of them.**

 This is not a defect in the periodic table. It is a fact about what the periodic table's coordinates can and cannot carry. Period and group locate an element; they do not encode why period 1 has room for two elements and period 4 for eighteen. That information travels alongside the table, in the schooling of whoever reads it.

 Figure 1.1. The external definition cost of seven indices. Four are self-defining. The calendar's seven cells are the
 content of Thirty days hath September; the periodic table's thirty-six are the gaps in the short periods.

### 6.1 What "the structure admits" means
 The reconstruction is mechanical. Given a set of cells X on coordinates (x₁, …, x_d), read off two things:

    the value sets Âᵢ(X) = { xᵢ : x ∈ X } — which values occur the bounds φ̂ᵢⱼ(v) = max{ xᵢ : x ∈ X, xⱼ ≤ v
    } — how far one coordinate reaches given another

 and then form

    ℛ(X) = { x ∈ ∏ Âᵢ(X) : xᵢ ≤ φ̂ᵢⱼ(xⱼ) for all i ≠ j }

 No outside knowledge enters. ℛ(X) is what a reader could reconstruct from the cells alone.

 E(X) = |ℛ(X)| − |X| is the gap: cells the structure implies and the index denies. For the periodic table it is
 36. For Λ, built in the next chapter, it is 0.

 Figure 1.2. Blue: the ninety cells the table holds. Red: the thirty-six its own coordinates admit and it denies. A reader
 given only the occupied cells would reconstruct all one thousand one hundred and seventy-one.

### 6.1.1 And a known layout costs nothing
 E is a property of a layout, not of chemistry. Computed on four conventions over the same elements:

 **And the thirty-six decompose, but not as this book has said, and the missing cell is helium's.**
 Recomputed on the drawn eighteen-column layout with all 118 elements:

  the thirty-six                                       count
  forbidden by ℓ ≤ n−1 — 1d (10), 1p (5), 2d (10)        **25**
  real but deferred by the Madelung order — 3d (10),
  and period 1 group 2                                   **11**

 **1p contributes five and not six, because helium occupies group 18 itself**, and the slot helium
 vacates — period 1, group 2 — is a *deferred* cell rather than a forbidden one, since ℓ = 0 there
 satisfies ℓ ≤ n−1. Two constraints casting a shadow of twenty-five, and eleven cells that could hold
 an element and do not.

 **And E is not a property of the elements. It is a property of where helium is drawn.**

  helium at         cells    E
  group 18          90       **36**
  group 2           90       **20**

 The mechanism is the envelope: φ̂(group | period ≤ 1) is 18 when helium sits at group 18 and 2 when
 it sits at group 2, so the entire first row of gaps exists only under the standard placement.
 **Helium's position is the most argued question in periodic-table design** — IUPAC places it at 18;
 the left-step form and a quantum-chemical case place it at 2, at the cost of the atomic-number triad
 He(2), Ne(10), Ar(18). **E measures what that choice costs to specify, and the difference is
 sixteen cells.** Register 448.

| convention | cells | E(X) |
|---|---|---|
| 18-column, f-block detached | 90 | **36** |
| 32-column | 118 | **106** |
| **left-step (Janet)** | 120 | **0** |

 **The Janet left-step table is closed.** Order the blocks f, d, p, s and define the period by *n* + ℓ, and the same elements cost nothing to specify. **The thirty-six belong to the drawing that hangs in classrooms**, and a known alternative costs none — exactly as §6.3 shows for the calendar, where E = 7 is the price of keeping January first.

 One caution. E(periodic table) = 36 has ⅅ = 0. The order and geometry routes are one computation
 written twice, because the table has no independently stated constraints to check the recovered ones
 against. Only Λ's E = 0 is defended, and only because Λ's constraints are given in §7.1 before
 any cell is enumerated.

### 6.2 It is not a peculiarity of chemistry
 The same computation on other indices:

  index                                                       \       X\                          E(X)   self-defining

  a box ordering, l ≥ w ≥ h                                   56      0           yes

  the drawn nuclide band, between drip lines                  383     0           yes

  a chessboard                                                64      0           yes

  index                                                   \       X\                    E(X)   self-defining

  Λ                                                       976     0      yes

  a subnet with a hole                                    248     8      no

  the calendar, (month, day)                              365     7      no

  the periodic table                                      90      36     no

 **The nuclide row names a drawn band and not a measurement, and the distinction is the whole of
 §6.1.** A chart drawn between two monotone drip lines has E = 0, and it has E = 0 **necessarily**:
 ℛ is idempotent — §32.4.1 proves it — so ℛ(X) satisfies ℛℛ(X) = ℛ(X) for any X whatever. **A band's
 closure is a theorem, not a finding about nuclei.** Reporting it beside the calendar's seven, which
 is a measurement, put a tautology and an observation in the same column.

 **The measurement is the other object, and it does not close.** Taking the particle-bound nuclides
 themselves over Z ≤ 9 — the chart as nature draws it rather than as a textbook does — gives
 **E = 9**, and the nine cells are named:

    He-5, He-7, Li-10, Be-8, Be-13, B-9, B-16, B-18, C-21

 Every one is a known unbound nuclide sitting inside the band its own neighbours define. And the band
 is exactly ℛ of the measurement: **the drawn chart is the closure of the real one**, which is why
 the two were conflated and why the conflation is worth the entry.

 **What the nine are is content the coordinates cannot carry.** Be-8 is unbound because it is two
 alpha particles and nothing else — clustering. He-5, He-7, Li-10 and Be-13 are unbound by one
 neutron against an even-paired core — pairing. (Z, N) knows how many protons and neutrons a nuclide
 has and cannot know that four of them prefer to be an alpha, or that the last one is unpaired.
 **The nine cells are the pairing and clustering terms of the mass formula, counted.** That is the
 same shape as the calendar's seven and the periodic table's thirty-six: a defect that names a real
 mechanism the index has no axis for. Register 298.

 **And the same shape appears a third time, in chemistry, where it can be watched arriving.** An index
 may bracket a quantity only along an axis the quantity is monotone on. Give first ionization energies
 to that test as bare numbers, with no shell model attached:

  period 2   Li 5.392  Be 9.323  B 8.298  C 11.260  N 14.534  O 13.618  F 17.423  Ne 21.565
  period 3   Na 5.139  Mg 7.646  Al 5.986  Si 8.152  P 10.487  S 10.360  Cl 12.968  Ar 15.760

 **Monotonicity fails at Be→B and N→O, and again at Mg→Al and P→S** — the same two positions in both
 periods. Those are the s² → p¹ subshell opening and the p³ → p⁴ first pairing: the two anomalies every
 general chemistry course teaches, located from the numbers alone. Twelve interior cells, four steps
 refused, **67% admissible**.

 It is a retrodiction and not a discovery — both have been explained since the 1920s. What earns it a
 place here is that it is *mechanical*: **where an index refuses, a mechanism is entering that the
 coordinates do not carry**, and the refusal map says where without being told what. The periodic
 table's thirty-six, the measured nuclide chart's nine, and these four are one phenomenon counted in
 three subjects — shell structure, pairing and clustering, subshell opening and pairing again.
 Register 308.

 **And a ninth subject entered this book without a coordinate being added to it: time.** Every subject
 above is a thing indexed. Time is not — §F.4.3 records that a date must never enter Λ, since a
 transition is a type and types are not dated, and §12.11.0 shows what enters instead: **composition.**
 An order between cells rather than a stamp on one.

 What makes it a subject rather than a metaphor is that it is measured. **Occupancy never rises along
 composition** — zero of 739 steps — so the index has a direction it was not given. The object graph
 falls into three eras pure in occupancy, circulation is total inside each and impossible between them,
 and 904 of 1,654 cells are conservative. **The clock is a coordinate the book already had, read as an
 order.**

    Time is the one subject here the index did not have to be extended to reach. It was
    already carrying it, in the constraint that says you cannot place more than you removed.

 And it is the subject that most sharply states the method's limit. §12.11.0.1 shows **definability is
 not reachability** — a cell is its own seventeen-bit description and reaches 24.7% of the object, the
 two orders sharing 0.4% of what either carries. An index can say completely what a thing *is* and
 almost nothing about what it can *become*. Register 348.

 The calendar's seven are (2,29), (2,30), (2,31), (4,31), (6,31), (9,31) and (11,31).

 **That is the content of *Thirty days hath September*.** The rhyme exists because the calendar's coordinates cannot carry the month lengths, and the rhyme is exactly seven cells long.

### 6.2.1 The nuclide chart's defect, recomputed under variation
 §6.2 reports E = 9 on the measured chart at one cutoff. **The measurement is stated here to F.3's
 highest standard** — the method named with its input set, the inputs printed, and the result
 recomputed at four proton-number cutoffs rather than once.

 **The method.** Take the measured nuclides as cells (Z, N), close under the monotone envelopes of
 §6.1, and count what the closure admits and the chart lacks. **The input set is the chart itself**,
 printed at §6.2 for Z ≤ 9, and the variation is the cutoff.

  Z ≤     nuclides   admitted    E    the cells admitted and absent
  5             27         33    6    He-5, He-7, Li-10, Be-8, Be-13, B-9
  6             40         48    8    …and B-16, B-18
  7             52         61    **9**    …and C-21
  9             80         89    **9**    unchanged

    **Every cell named at one cutoff persists at every larger one, and none is added after Z ≤ 7.**
    The defect is not an artefact of where the chart was cut.

 That matters because a closure defect measured once can always be blamed on the boundary. **Here the
 boundary was moved four times and the nine cells did not move**, so they are a property of the
 measurement rather than of the window — and each of the nine is a real nuclide the chart omits because
 it is unbound, which is pairing and clustering, mechanisms the two coordinates cannot carry. Registers
 388, 390.

### 6.3 The calendar can be repaired, and the repair is instructive
 The calendar fails for one reason: days(m) is not monotone in m, because February has 28. Relabel the
 months in order of length — February, April, June, September, November, January, March, … — and E
 drops to 0. The 365 cells are identical; only the labels move.

 **And the repaired calendar is useless.** A calendar whose months run in order of length is self-defining and unusable.

 So the trade is explicit, and it has a number:

      E(X) = 7 is the price of keeping January first.

 Non-closure is not always a fault. It is sometimes a purchase — usability bought with definitional self-sufficiency. What this book supplies is the price tag, not the verdict.

### 6.4 What follows
 Λ is the same 118 elements on different coordinates, with E = 0. It adds no physics. What it removes is
 the thirty-six.

 The rest of Part II builds it and establishes what it is. Part III proves why E = 0 is not an isolated virtue but the source of two others.

## 7. The construction of Λ
 Λ is a set of eight-tuples. Each is a transition cell: a source configuration, a target configuration, and the
 electron count moved between them.

      (n, ℓ, k, q, e, f, g, 2S)

| | |
|---|---|
| *n* | source shell |
| ℓ | source subshell |
| *k* | source occupancy |
| *q* | electrons removed |
| *e* | target shell |
| *f* | target subshell |
| *g* | target occupancy |
| 2*S* | multiplicity |

 Five further coordinates — 2S′, v, 2J_c, K and 2J — extend Λ₈ to Λ₁₃ when target spin, seniority and
 angular-momentum coupling are carried explicitly; §12.11 builds the tower and fixes the order, which is
 J_c, then K, then J. Everything in this book that does not concern coupling is stated for Λ₈.

 One row of the table below is not like the others. Three origins — Pauli, the hydrogenic solution,
 counting — yield exact constraints. The fourth, vector coupling, yields an envelope: 2S ≤ k contains
 every physical multiplicity and admits values no k electrons can carry, at 48.5% density at the caps of
 §7.4. §12.11.3 states the dichotomy and §12.11.2 proves it is forced.

### 7.1 The constraints, and where each comes from
 Λ is not all of the eight-fold product. It is the subset satisfying:

  constraint                      origin
  ℓ≤n−1                           hydrogenic radial solution
  k ≤ 2(2ℓ + 1)                   Pauli exclusion
  q≤k                             counting — cannot remove more than are present
  f≤e−1                           hydrogenic radial solution
  g ≤ 2(2f + 1)                   Pauli exclusion
  g≤q                             counting — cannot place more than were removed
  2S ≤ k                          vector coupling

 **Seven constraints, four origins, and nothing else.** No constraint in Λ comes from anywhere but Pauli, the hydrogenic radial solution, counting, or angular-momentum coupling. That exhaustiveness matters in Part III, where it is the reason the object's properties can be attributed to *indexing* rather than to physics smuggled in through a bound.

### 7.2 Every constraint is of one form
 Each constraint reads xᵢ ≤ φ(xⱼ) — one coordinate bounded by a monotone function of one other. None is
 a sum. None is a difference. That is not an accident of presentation; Chapter 17 shows that a sum could
 not appear here, because a set cut out by a sum bound is not closed.

 **Consequence, developed in Chapter 8:** the graph whose nodes are coordinates and whose edges are constraints is a **tree**.

 Figure 2.1. Every constraint binds one coordinate to one other. Eight nodes, seven edges, connected — a tree, and
 therefore treewidth 1. Three results in this book follow from that fact alone.

### 7.3 Λ is closed
 For any two cells x, y ∈ Λ, both x ∨ y and x ∧ y — the coordinatewise maximum and minimum — are again
 in Λ.

 *Proof.* Each constraint is *xᵢ* ≤ φ(*xⱼ*) with φ non-decreasing. Suppose (*x*∨*y*)ᵢ = *xᵢ*. Then

    (x∨y)ᵢ = xᵢ ≤ φ(xⱼ) ≤ φ(max(xⱼ, yⱼ)) = φ((x∨y)ⱼ)

 by monotonicity. Meets are symmetric. ∎

 And E(Λ) = 0, verified by direct computation at four cap settings — 216, 976, 1,636 and 2,394 cells —
 exact in one step and stable under a second application of ℛ.

### 7.4 A note on caps
 Λ is infinite; every count in this book is stated at explicit caps on n, e, ℓ and k. The counts change with
 the caps. The shape does not — a claim made precise in Chapter 8, where the generator patterns are
 shown identical across a twenty-eight-fold range in cell count.

 Where a number is cap-dependent it is marked. Where a structural claim is made, it has been checked at no fewer than four settings.

## 8. What Λ is — distributive, modular, Sperner


 **The standing convention, stated once so it need not be repeated.** Every figure in this book that
 depends on the caps is computed at **(n_max, e_max, ℓ_max, k_max, f_max) = (3, 3, 1, 3, 1)** unless the
 section names others. Where a figure is recomputed under variation the settings are printed beside it,
 as at §6.2.1, §12.11.1.1, §12.11.1.2 and §22.1.1.1.

    **A cap-dependent figure without a stated cap is not thereby unqualified — it is at these
    caps.** What would be a defect is a figure at *other* caps that does not say so, and audit 10
    checks for that.

 The convention was in force from the first page and had never been written down: 84 paragraphs carry a
 cap-dependent figure and 69 of them name no cap, which is 82% relying on a rule the book did not state.
 **§32.4.2's distinction is why this matters** — every one of those 69 carries a *value* rather than an
 identity, and a value without its setting is a reading whose conditions are missing. Register 395.
### 8.1 Distributive
 Λ is a sublattice of a product of chains, and every such sublattice is distributive. Verified on 4,000
 randomly drawn triples, no failures.

 This is the least surprising fact in the chapter and the most load-bearing: it is what admits Birkhoff's representation, which is the next section.

### 8.2 Rank is conserved
 Λ has a conservation law, and it is exact.

    rank(a ∨ b) + rank(a ∧ b) = rank(a) + rank(b)

 **Verified on all 475,800 pairs: zero violations.**

 **This is modularity, and it is strictly stronger than what most rank functions satisfy.** Matroid rank is *sub*modular — the left side is ≤ the right. Entropy is submodular. **Λ's rank is modular, with equality, and that equality is the signature of distributivity.**

### 8.2.1 What it means
 Join and meet redistribute rank without creating or destroying it. Taking the join of two cells
 raises the rank by exactly what taking the meet lowers it. A quantity is conserved by the lattice's
 operations, in the way energy is conserved by a canonical transformation.

 **The question came from outside the subject.** Celestial mechanics is organised around invariants — the Jacobi integral, angular momentum, energy — and asking *what does Λ conserve* is not a question a spectroscopist would think to put. **This book had not put it.**

### 8.2.2 And it is a cheaper test than §8.1's
 §8.1 verifies distributivity by checking a ∧ (b ∨ c) = (a∧b) ∨ (a∧c) over triples — O(n³).
 Modularity of rank is a pairwise condition, O(n²), and on a graded lattice with this rank function it is
 equivalent:

| test | sample | time |
|---|---|---|
| distributive law, triples | 4,000 | 0.0375 s |
| **modular rank, pairs** | 4,000 | **0.0188 s** |

 **Both true. The second is the one to run first**, because a failure of modularity is a failure of distributivity and it is found in half the work.

### 8.3 Seventeen generators
 By Birkhoff's theorem, a finite distributive lattice is the lattice of down-sets of its poset of join-irreducibles.
 For Λ at caps (3,3,1,3), k ≥ 1:

    976 cells ← 17 join-irreducibles, 20 covering relations

 and the correspondence is exact: **all 976 down-sets of the 17-element poset are precisely the 976 cells.** A fifty-seven-fold compression.

 **The covering relations are the physics made visible:**

| cover | reads |
|---|---|
| *e*=2 ⋖ *e*=2, *f*=1 | a target subshell requires its shell |
| *k*=2 ⋖ *k*=2, *q*=2 | you may remove what you have |
| *n*=2 ⋖ *n*=2, ℓ=1 ⋖ *n*=2, ℓ=1, *k*=3 | shell before subshell before occupancy |

 **And the shape is cap-independent.** Across six settings from 976 to 27,873 cells the generator count grows — 17, 23, 31, 32 — while the *patterns* hold at **fifteen, with zero new and zero lost.** Only multiplicities change.

 That is what licenses stating the poset once.

 Figure 3.1. The seventeen join-irreducibles and their twenty covering relations. Every one of the 976 cells is a down-set
 of this poset, and every down-set is a cell — a fifty-seven-fold compression, exact. The covers are the constraints of §7.1
 made visible: shell before subshell before occupancy, and a target subshell requiring its shell.

### 8.4 Sperner, and not symmetric
 The largest antichain in Λ equals its largest rank level — the Sperner property — verified exactly at five
 cap settings by Dilworth's theorem and bipartite matching, no exceptions. At caps (3,3,1,3), k ≥ 1 the maximum
 is 122 at rank 11.

 The rank sequence is **log-concave**, hence unimodal, which is why Sperner holds rather than merely happening to.

 **But Λ is not rank-symmetric**, so it has **no symmetric chain decomposition** and the classical result for divisor lattices does not transfer. The asymmetry is itself a measurement:

    centre of mass 11.07 against midpoint 11.5 — skew −0.43, computed from the full rank sequence
    at caps (3,3,1,3), k ≥ 1, over all 976 cells

 Low ranks are cut by floors (*n* ≥ 1, *k* ≥ 1, *e* ≥ 1); high ranks by caps (ℓ ≤ *n*−1, *k* ≤ 2(2ℓ+1), *q* ≤ *k*, *g* ≤ *q*). **There are more caps than floors, so the top is pruned harder.** The skew is how much more Pauli constrains than counting does.

 Nor is Λ self-dual. Only 8 of 976 cells survive the reflection x ↦ max − x, coordinate by coordinate —
 reproduced exactly on recomputation. The tower session's competing figure of 112 was computed under
 x ↦ max + min − x, a different map, which the floors n ≥ 1, k ≥ 1, e ≥ 1 separate from this one; under
 that map 112 cells have their image in Λ and none is fixed. Both counts are right about their own
 reflection. The discrepancy was definitional, and it is closed. This reflection is not decoration: restricted to
 one occupancy coordinate it is particle-hole conjugation, terms(ℓᵏ) = terms(ℓ^(4ℓ+2−k)) exactly, and
 §12.11.2 shows it is one of the three machines that price every coupling axis. The
 two sides of the object are genuinely distinguishable, which Chapter 12 develops.

 Figure 3.2. The rank sequence over all 976 cells is log-concave, hence unimodal, hence Sperner. It is not symmetric: the
 centre of mass sits below the midpoint by 0.43, because Λ has more caps than floors.

### 8.5 The constraint graph is a tree
 Eight coordinates, seven constraints, connected:

    e — f — g — q — k — ℓ — n, with 2S attached to k

 **Treewidth 1.** Three consequences run through the rest of the book:

 **The void needs no sieve.** A tree has no cycles, so there is nothing for inclusion–exclusion to correct. Chapter 10 gives the closed-form count.

 **The order is recoverable.** Chapter 15 recovers Λ's coordinate orders from an unlabelled bag of cells by propagation along this tree — 20 of 20.

 **And the constraint graph offers no redundancy.** There is exactly one path between any two nodes. So the two-route protection of Chapter 16 cannot come from the constraints; it must come from derived quantities. **That is a real constraint on how the object defends itself, and it is visible here, three chapters early.**

### 8.6 Order dimension
 Λ₈ has order dimension exactly 8, rising by one per adjoined axis, with zero redundant coordinates. Every
 axis carries information no combination of the others supplies.

 **And it is asserted here rather than derived, which this section now says.** The order dimension of
 a finite distributive lattice is the width of its poset of join-irreducibles — Dilworth — so
 dim(Λ₈) = 8 follows from §8.3's seventeen generators rather than standing on its own. It is the one
 quantity in Part II carried without a derivation, found by building the dependency graph of this
 book's mathematics and looking for objects nothing derives. Register 409.

 Λ occupies **14.1%** of its own bounding box — 976 cells of 6,912.

## 9. The arithmetic encoding
 Assign the ith coordinate the ith prime and read a cell as an integer:

    N(x) = ∏ᵢ pᵢ^{xᵢ}

 The lattice becomes arithmetic, exactly:

  lattice                  arithmetic
  x≤y                      N(x) divides N(y)
  x∨y                      lcm
  x∧y                      gcd
  rank Σxᵢ                 Ω(N), prime factors with multiplicity

    Λ is a sublattice of the divisor lattice of a single integer.

### 9.1 Two classical functions appear as lattice quantities
 rank(x) = Ω(N(x)) — verified on all 976 cells.

 **ω(N(x)) ≤ dim(Λ)** — the count of *distinct* primes is bounded by the order dimension, and the bound is tight. *Proof:* N(x) = ∏pᵢ^{xᵢ} uses at most one prime per coordinate. ∎

 **The two classical prime-counting functions are the lattice's rank and its dimension bound.** Neither was put there.

### 9.2 The occupancy measure
    d(x, y) = τ( lcm(N(x),N(y)) / gcd(N(x),N(y)) )

 **And this is one leg of something the book states three times without naming it once.**

    **Between any two points there is an interval, and the method returns its measure.**

 Not an equation connecting two points — the orders are partial and share 0.3%, so no single order
 relates an arbitrary pair. **An interval between two points is total**, because every pair of cells
 has a meet and a join whether or not either order relates them. That is why d(x,y) works where the
 order does not.

  between                        the interval              its measure
  two **cells**                  [x∧y, x∨y]                d = ∏(\|Δᵢ\|+1) = τ(lcm/gcd), five forms
  two **states** under           [min, max] output rank    17% of 264 inputs single-valued, worst
  composition                                              spread 12, range [3, 23]
  two **measurements**           [T(n−1), T(n+1)]          w, priced at V = 4ν/3

 §12.11.0.12 says the middle row in its own words — *from one coordinate up, a transition is not a
 function of its inputs; it is an interval* — and §22.1 gives the third. §27 already proves the three
 MEASURES are one quantity; what was missing is that the three OBJECTS are one shape.

 **And the form survives what an equation would not.** Theorem 11.2 permits it, because an interval
 carries *less* than the coordinates and not more. §18.6 permits it, because an interval bounds where
 something already is rather than proposing an unlisted cell — §25.6.3's own correction. And the 0.3%
 overlap stops being a defeater and becomes the content: **two intervals per pair, nearly disjoint, so
 what a thing IS and what it can BECOME are separately bounded and rarely both.**

 It is universal over indices whose constraints are pairwise monotone envelopes, per §14.3, and not
 over all mathematics. **And it is why this is a method rather than an equation**: an equation returns
 a value; this returns an interval and a verdict on what the interval is about, which by §18.4.1 is
 whether E measures the drawing or the world. That second half is not expressible as an equation.
 Register 438.

 with τ the divisor-counting function. **d(x,x) = 1**, because τ(1) = 1.

 That is not a convention. A point has no volume, but it is one point, and it counts itself. The measure counts *cells*, and a cell is occupied.

 **Five equivalent forms**, verified 500/500 and 2,000/2,000:

  1. |[x∧y, x∨y]| — interval count
  2. ∏ᵢ(|xᵢ − yᵢ| + 1) — coordinate form
  3. τ(N(x)N(y)/gcd²) — two integers
  4. τ(a·b) where N(x)/N(y) = a/b in lowest terms — one rational input
  5. ∏_p(|v_p(ρ)| + 1) — p-adic
 Properties. Symmetric · d ≥ 1 with equality iff x = y · multiplicative triangle inequality d(x,z) ≤
 d(x,y)·d(y,z), 4,000 of 4,000 · log d is an ordinary ℓ¹ metric · balls are hyperbolic, with boundary
 (1+Δ₁)(1+Δ₂) = D · each axis is a log-distorted chain, first step log 2, tenth step log(11/10).

 Figure 4.1. The occupancy measure. d(x,y) counts cells in the interval between two points, and equals τ(lcm/gcd) of
 their prime encodings. Balls are hyperbolic; log d is an ordinary ℓ¹ metric; and d(x,x) = 1 because a point counts itself.

### 9.3 The Möbius function in closed form
 Since Λ ≅ J(P) with |P| = 17:

    μ(x,y) = (−1)^{|y∖x|} if y∖x is an antichain in P, and 0 otherwise

 Verified against the recursive definition on 47 comparable pairs, values only in {−1, 0, +1}. **No recursion is needed for a 976-cell lattice; the answer is read off a 17-element poset.**

 **And a transfer condition to the arithmetic side:** μ_Λ = μ_arith **iff** the interval is a void-free unit hypercube. Exact — 60 void-free pairs all agree, 56 void-bearing pairs all disagree, no mixed case. Decidable in seven comparisons.

## 10. The void
 Between two cells sits a box. Not all of it is Λ.

    void(x,y) = ∏ᵢ(|Δᵢ| + 1) − |[x∧y, x∨y] ∩ Λ|

### 10.1 What the void is made of
 The excluded cells are exactly those forbidden by Pauli (k > 2(2ℓ+1), g > cap), by the hydrogenic bound (ℓ
 ≥ n, f ≥ e), or by counting (q > k, g > q).

    The void is the shadow of the exclusion principle.

### 10.2 Its size, and its stability
 The void-free fraction is 27.7–30.1% across 776 million pairs and a seventeenfold range in cell count.
 Stable, no trend.

 **And Λ already carries time, in the only form a transition can carry it.** The source end is a
 before, the target end is an after, and q — the transfer — is the amount that changed between them.
 The constraint tree *is* past — change — future, read left to right. A date would add nothing, because
 a transition is a type and types are not dated; **what a transition can have is a predecessor and a
 successor**, and §12.11.0 shows Λ₉ has both.

 **And there is an eighth condition, which is not a constraint.** k ≥ 1: the source subshell must hold
 at least one electron. It is a *definitional restriction* and not a bound — with k = 0 the tree
 collapses, 2S, q and g are all forced to zero, and twenty-five cells survive that are not transitions
 at all. §13.2 already says the cells are transitions and not states; this is that sentence made a
 condition. Excluding them gives 976 and, independently, the bounding box 6,912. **The construction as
 printed said "seven constraints and nothing else" and needed eight things**, which is register 301.

 **And the constraints are correlated.** Satisfied individually 67–94%; their product is 20.19%; the joint figure is **30.13%** — a factor of **1.49** above independence, over the same 776 million pairs. The correlation is not a coincidence: all seven constraints descend from two origins.

 Figure 5.1. The void-free fraction across a hundredfold range in cell count, computed exhaustively over 776 million
 pairs. Stable at 27.7–30.1%, with no trend.

### 10.3 Containment in seven comparisons
 A box is entirely inside Λ iff hi_i ≤ φ(lo_j) for each of the seven constraints. O(1), verified against
 enumeration on 3,168 pairs.

### 10.4 The count, with no sieve
 Because the constraint graph is a tree, the count factorises:

    |box ∩ Λ| = Σ over n, ℓ, k, q, e, f of [ℓ ≤ n−1][1 ≤ k ≤ 2(2ℓ+1)][q ≤ k][f ≤ e−1] · #{2S ≤ k} · #{g ≤
    min(q, 2(2f+1))}

 with the two leaves in closed form:

    #{2S} = max(0, min(hi₇, k) − lo₇ + 1) #{g} = max(0, min(hi₆, q, 2(2f+1)) − lo₆ + 1)

 Verified against direct enumeration on eight random intervals.

 **No Möbius function. No 2⁷-term inclusion–exclusion.** A tree has no cycles for a sieve to correct — and that is a general statement about indices, not about this one. **An index whose constraint graph is a tree has its void in closed form; one with cycles does not.**

## 11. The single expression
 Every cell of Λ is definable in one expression, and the reason it is possible is worth more than
 the expression itself.

### 11.1 In all six languages, and a seventh
 P21 requires every definition to be true in every mathematical language and in their
 combinations. Λ is a definition. Here it is, six ways.

  language          the expression                                                                                      \      Λ
                                                                                                                               \
  order             the fixed point of ℛ: the least set closed under ∨, ∧ satisfying its own recovered bounds           976
  algebra           ℤ⁸ ∩ {x : Ax ≤ b}, A a 7 × 8 matrix with two non-zeros per row                                      976
  geometry          lattice points of a polytope whose constraint graph is a tree; the count factorises                 976
  analysis          F(1,…,1) for the multivariate generating function                                                   976
  information       the set whose description length given its closure is zero bits                                     976
  logic             {x ∈ ℤ⁸ : χ(x) = 1}, χ a product of seven Heaviside steps                                           976

    All six agree exactly.

### 11.1.1 A seventh, in binary, and it earns its place
 Asked whether binary belongs among the languages, the answer is that it does, and the book's own
 admission test is what settles it rather than taste. A candidate is admitted when it distinguishes
 something no existing coordinate distinguishes — §12's condition — and this one does.

    **binary** — Λ is the set of 976 words in {0,1}¹⁷ that are down-sets of the generator poset
    A.19; **join is bitwise OR and meet is bitwise AND**, with zero failures over all 475,800
    pairs. The map cell ↦ word is a bijection.

 The seventeen bits are the seventeen generators. Birkhoff's correspondence, which §3 states and A.19
 now prints, is exactly this statement: a cell is the set of generators beneath it, and that set is a
 bit-string. Nothing here is new mathematics — it is the same theorem in the language a machine reads.

 **What it distinguishes, and why the other six cannot.** *Logic* gives χ, a predicate that tests
 membership. *Information* gives a description length of zero bits, a statement about the closure.
 Neither says the cells **are** bit-vectors, and only that statement makes the lattice operations
 machine primitives rather than definitions. And only in this language is Chapter 16's thesis a
 number:

    bits carried per cell                 17
    bits needed to index 976 cells      9.93
    surplus                             7.07 bits per cell

 **A bit is a generator.** Bit *i* is set in a cell's word exactly when generator *i* of A.19 lies
 beneath that cell, so each bit is one yes-or-no question about a cell — *is this generator under
 you?* — and a cell is the set of its answers and nothing else. The bits are the alphabet S1 requires,
 written as positions.

 **And the bits are not free of one another.** The twenty covering relations of J(Λ) are twenty
 implications between bit positions: if generator *j* covers generator *i*, then bit *j* set forces
 bit *i* set. **Those twenty implications alone cut the ambient space to Λ exactly** — of the 131,072
 seventeen-bit words, precisely 976 satisfy them, with nothing else imposed. So the surplus is not
 spare capacity and no bit is removable, every generator being join-irreducible by construction; the
 seven bits live in the twenty implications, which is where redundancy always lives in a closed index.

 **And in that language Λ is a circuit, which fixes what kind of machine this book is.** The
 seventeen generators are input lines; the twenty implications are gates; join is OR and meet is AND.
 Computed: the twenty gates alone accept **976 words of the 131,072 available, which is exactly Λ**, at
 a depth of five. Available: AND, OR, implication. Absent: NOT, feedback, memory.

    **Λ₈ is a monotone boolean circuit. It decides membership; it does not compute a function of
    successive inputs.**

 **P20 already said this and used another vocabulary.** It states that monotone two-variable bounds
 *close always in one pass*, where full arithmetic never closes by Gödel. Closing in one pass is
 constant depth, and constant depth is combinational — **P20 was a classification of this object's
 computational power before anything had been counted.** §32.4.1 says the same from the third side:
 no negation, so no self-referential contradiction, so no loop to diverge in.

 **And the ninth axis is where it stops being static.** §12.11.0 shows Λ₉ closed under composition —
 41,682 pairs, associative — so Λ₉ has states, transitions and a composition law. **Λ₈ is a circuit;
 Λ₉ is an automaton**, still negation-free and therefore still not general, but no longer a thing that
 answers once. The tower's first step is the step from a decider to a machine, and it was taken for
 seniority. Register 331.

 **Seven bits of surplus per cell.** Λ occupies 976 of the 131,072 available seventeen-bit words — 0.7446% of the space it is written in.
 *It found it because it carried more than it needed* is the claim Chapter 16 makes in prose and §12.11
 makes in envelope densities. **Here it is seven bits.** That is the independence the admission test
 asks for: a quantity no other language on this page produces.

 **One cost, stated.** Appendix D fibres its elements by language, and its language list is not this
 one — order, combinatorics, analysis, complexity, physics, algebraic geometry. Adding *binary* there
 would refine that fibration and, by register 233, make E(Appendix D) = 0 cheaper than it already is.
 So the seventh language is admitted **here**, among the statements of Λ, and is not added to the
 classification in E until someone decides that trade is worth making. Register 258.

 **In logic**, written out:

    χ(x) = H(n−1−ℓ) H(4ℓ+2−k) H(k−q) H(k−2S) H(e−1−f) H(4f+2−g) H(q−g)

 **In algebra**, the seven rows of *A*: ℓ − *n* ≤ −1 · *k* − 4ℓ ≤ 2 · *q* − *k* ≤ 0 · 2*S* − *k* ≤ 0 · *f* − *e* ≤ −1 · *g* − 4*f* ≤ 2 · *g* − *q* ≤ 0. **Every row has exactly two non-zero entries**, and that one property is what makes the constraint graph a tree.

 **In analysis**, the rank polynomial:

    F(z) = z³(z¹⁷ + 4z¹⁶ + 10z¹⁵ + … + 122z⁸ + 121z⁷ + … + 5z + 1)

 with *F*(1) = 976, *F*′(1)/*F*(1) = 11.0666 and *F*(−1) = 2 — **and the asymmetry of §8.3 visible in the coefficients: 122 then 121, the peak one step left of centre.**

 **In algebra again, by a second route:** under *N*(*x*) = ∏ *pᵢ^xᵢ* the lattice is a sublattice of the divisors of *N*(top), with **gcd = meet and lcm = join**, verified exactly on random pairs.

### 11.2 And in their combinations
 P21 requires the combinations to name real objects, not analogies. Ten were tested and ten
 hold.

  combination               statement

  order + algebra           gcd(N(a),N(b)) = N(a∧b), and lcm = N(a∨b)

  order + analysis          F′(1)/F(1) = the mean rank, 11.0666

  order + geometry          \                                                            bo   −\       Λ   = the void, 6,912 − 976 =
                                                                                         x\            \   5,936

  geometry + analysis       F is not palindromic ⟺ the poset is not self-dual

  order + information       E_bits = log₂ C(\                                            ℛ(   , E) =
                                                                                         X)   0
                                                                                         \

  algebra +                 the description is 7 rows of 2 non-zeros — the whole index
  information

  logic + algebra           χ as a product of Heaviside steps selects exactly 976

  logic + analysis          the coefficient function of F is χ

  combination             statement

  analysis +              log₂ F(1) = 9.93 bits to name one cell
  information

  algebra + geometry      the integer points of the polytope are the lattice, exactly

    The eighth is the one that makes "single expression" literal: the coefficient of
    z₁ⁿ…z₈^{2S} in F is 1 if the cell exists and 0 if it does not. F and χ are the same function
    written twice.

### 11.3 The expression
    F = Σₙ z₁ⁿ Σ_{ℓ≤n−1} z₂^ℓ Σ_{k≤4ℓ+2} z₃^k · [Σ_{S≤k} z₈^S] · Σ_{q≤k} z₄^q Σₑ z₅^e
    Σ_{f<e} z₆^f Σ_{g≤min(q,4f+2)} z₇^g

 **Set every *z* = 1 and it gives the count. Set every *z* = *z* and it gives the rank polynomial. And the coefficient of *z*₁ⁿ…*z*₈^S is 1 if the cell exists and 0 if it does not.**

    The coefficient function of F is χ. So F is one expression containing every cell of the
    lattice and nothing else.

 Figure 6.1. The constraint graph of Λ: a path of seven nodes with one pendant at k — a caterpillar, the simplest tree
 that is not a path. That shape is why a single left-to-right nesting exists with one bracketed factor. The dashed edges are
 the two bounds on g; their minimum is the only non-product term in the whole expression, and it is the Pauli principle.

### 11.4 The shape of the tree
    n — ℓ — k — q — g — f — e, with 2S pendant at k

 **Not a generic tree: a path of seven nodes with one pendant — a caterpillar**, the simplest tree that is not a path. **That is why a linear nesting exists with a single bracketed factor.** A branching tree would require nested brackets; a cycle would require inclusion–exclusion.

### 11.5 The one coupling, and it is the Pauli principle
 Every bound is a single inequality except one: g ≤ min(q, 4f+2). g is the only internal node summed
 last, so both its neighbours are fixed when its range is set.

 **Remove the coupling and 976 becomes 1,000.** The excluded 24 cells all have *f* = 0 and *g* = 3 — **three electrons in an s orbital.**

    Every other bound in this lattice is a counting or ordering fact. This one is physics, and it
    is the only non-product term in the whole expression.

 And it is recovered, not assumed. ℛ(Λ) = Λ rebuilds all 976 cells from the cells alone, so a reader
 given only the cell list would deduce g ≤ 2(2f+1) without being told it.

### 11.6 The detachable factor

 **2*S* is a leaf, so its sum closes geometrically and multiplies out:**

    [Σ_{S≤k} z₈^S] = (1 − z₈^(k+1))/(1 − z₈)

 **Spin multiplicity is algebraically inert here.** It scales every cell count by (*k*+1) and changes no structure — **which is why removing it leaves E(X) = 0 unchanged**, a fact §3 verifies numerically and does not explain.

### 11.7 Why a closed expression exists at all
 Three structural facts, proved separately in this book and never combined:

| fact | consequence |
|---|---|
| every constraint is a **monotone bound** | the region is a polytope |
| every bound couples exactly **two variables** | the graph is a tree |
| the tree has **no cycles** | the sum factorises |

 Remove any one and no such expression exists. A sum bound breaks the first — §11.7 measures
 89,864 join failures when one is tried. A three-variable constraint breaks the second. A cycle breaks the
 third, and 2^d inclusion–exclusion terms return.

    "All the information in one expression" is not a curiosity. It is exactly what closure buys,
    and it is the strongest available statement of what E(X) = 0 means: the expression is the index,
    and the cells are its coefficients.

 Figure 6.2. The rank polynomial F(z), read forwards as bars and backwards as the dashed line. They part company at
 rank 1 — 5 against 4 — and never rejoin. A rank polynomial is palindromic if and only if the poset is self-dual, so this
 single picture carries both of §8.3's separately-reported facts: the asymmetric rank sequence, and the 8 of 976 cells that
 survive reflection.

### 11.8 And the rank polynomial says what §8.3 says
 A rank polynomial is palindromic if and only if the poset is self-dual.

    forwards: 1, 5, 15, 34, 59, 87 · backwards: 1, 4, 10, 21, 37, 57

 **§8.3 reports as separate numerical facts that only 8 of 976 cells survive *x* → max − *x*, and that the rank sequence is asymmetric. They are the same statement**, and they part company at rank 1 — **5 against 4.**

### 11.8.1 And F(−1) = 2 has a cause, not a coincidence
 Two things had to be ruled out before the number meant anything.

 **A pairing argument cannot cancel it.** The duality map σ(*x*) = max − *x* gives rank(σ*x*) = *M* − rank(*x*) with **M = 20, which is even**, so σ preserves rank parity. **The eight self-dual survivors are all of even rank and contribute +8, not 0.**

 **And the source is the tree.** A coordinate with an *even* number of consecutive values contributes zero to an alternating sum, and **ℓ and *f* each have exactly two**:

| factor at *z* = −1 | value |
|---|---|
| Σ(−1)ⁿ, *n* = 1…3 | −1 |
| **Σ(−1)^ℓ, ℓ = 0…1** | **0** |
| Σ(−1)^e, *e* = 1…3 | −1 |
| **Σ(−1)^f, *f* = 0…1** | **0** |

    F_box(−1) = 0 exactly. If the coordinates were free the alternating sum would vanish. The
    constraints couple ℓ to n and f to e, the vanishing factors never appear free, and a residue
    of 2 survives.

 **So *F*(−1) = 2 measures the same thing the skew and the eight survivors measure — the tree's asymmetry — by a third route.**

 **And *F*(−1) = 2 is the third face of it.** A self-dual graded poset gives *F*(−1) = 0.

## 12. The shape — a cylinder over the transfer

### 12.1 A cylinder, not a Möbius band
 Λ's derived quantities fall into two classes:

| **ascending** — rise up a series | **descending** — fall up a series |
|---|---|
| *e*, ν, *V* | *T*, *r*, δ, spacing, *w* |

 exchanged under ν ↦ ν⁻³.

 **The sign structure is bipartite**, and a bipartite structure can never produce an odd number of orientation reversals. Verified exhaustively: **zero reversing loops among all cycles of length 3 to 5.**

    Λ is orientable. It is a cylinder.

 That is a *reason*, not an absence. And both alternative routes to a Möbius band close: a non-monotone quantity has **undefined** edges rather than reversing ones; a quantity independent of ν has **no** edge. There is no third option.

 Figure 7.1. The object. Ascending quantities rise up a series, descending ones fall, and the two are exchanged under ν
 ↦ ν⁻³. The sign structure is bipartite, so no traverse can reverse orientation an odd number of times — the surface is a
 cylinder, not a Möbius band, and the reason is structural rather than an absence of evidence.

### 12.2 "Over ν", not "graded by ν"
 ν grades every chain of Λ. It grades Λ itself nowhere — 24 of 70 comparable pairs have ν decreasing.

 **That near-miss is the structure in one sentence.** The bracket of Part IV operates along chains *because that is exactly where ν is a grading.*

 The name follows: **the Lach Cylinder — a hydrogenic cylinder over ν, and over the transfer.** Not *graded by*. *Over.*

### 12.3 One scale, and one exception
 Height goes as ν⁻², the metric as ν⁻³, and the only linearly rising quantity in the entire structure is
 the price of a guarantee. Chapter 23 derives it.

### 12.4 The loop closes
 Λ₈ → derived quantities (V, r) → the bound lattice → dimension 1 = log r = −3 log ν, with residual 8.9 ×
 10⁻¹⁶.

 Λ *decomposes* ν and gains dimension; the bound lattice *recomposes* it and sheds dimension. **Two sides of one object, not two passes over it.**

### 12.5 What Λ is a property of
 Λ is a property of the hydrogenic limit. δ — the quantum defect — is the entire remainder.

    Prediction lives only in the remainder.

 Which is why perfecting the index moves nothing, and why that is a theorem rather than a disappointment. Part III proves it; Part IV measures what is left.

 **And §25.6 will sharpen this into its final form.** The reason Λ is not predictive is not that it is a poor predictor or an incomplete one. **It is that a complete index has nothing to predict** — the cells it could propose are exactly those its structure admits and its extension denies, and for Λ that set is empty. **Completeness and predictiveness are alternatives.**

### 12.6 The cylinder is a fibration, and its fibres are two-body
 §12.1 finds the shape is a cylinder over the transfer coordinate. That is not a metaphor, and the
 reason is structural.

 **The tree has two ends.** One is a parent configuration (*n*, ℓ, *k*) with its spin 2*S*; the other is a target (*e*, *f*) with its occupancy *g*. **They meet at *q*, the number transferred.** That is the shape of a two-body problem: two objects and a coupling.

### 12.6.1 It does not factorise, and then it does
 As a bare product it fails: |A| × |B| × |q| = 2,244 against 976. The two ends are coupled twice over — q
 ≤ k ties the transfer to the parent, g ≤ q ties the target's occupancy to the transfer.

 **Conditioned on the coupling, they separate exactly:**

  q         \         A(q)\                  \               B(q)\                  product         actual

  0         33        5             165      165

  1         33        10            330      330

  2         23        15            345      345

  3         8         17            136      136

      |Λ| = Σ_q \|A(q)\| × \|B(q)\| = 976, exactly.

 **That is how a two-body problem separates** — into centre-of-mass and relative motion — with *q* playing the role of the conserved coupling.

### 12.6.2 Which names the cylinder
      The base is q, the axis along the cylinder. The fibre is A(q) × B(q), the cross-section at
      each q. And the cross-section is itself a product of two lattices.

 **Cross-sections 165, 330, 345, 136 — the cylinder's profile, computed rather than drawn.**

 Figure 7.2. Cross-sections of the tower cylinder at each transfer, √-cell scale — Λ₁₃ solid, Λ₈ dashed inset. The
 sections read 2,294 × 5, 2,294 × 20, 1,794 × 50, 744 × 70 against the Λ₈ profile above, 33 × 5, 33 × 10, 23 × 15,
 8 × 17. Every section is itself closed, per §12.8.4; the tower of §12.11 widens each without breaking one.

### 12.7 The fibres, in closed form
 Each side of the cylinder has its own expression, and they are different shapes.

      A_q(z) = Σ_{n=1}^{3} zⁿ Σ_{ℓ=0}^{min(n−1,1)} z^ℓ Σ_{k=max(q,1)}^{min(4ℓ+2,3)} z^k
      · (1 − z^{min(k,3)+1})/(1 − z) B_q(z) = Σ_{e=1}^{3} z^e Σ_{f=0}^{min(e−1,1)} z^f · (1
      − z^{min(q,4f+2)+1})/(1 − z)

 **Both verified against enumeration at every *q*:** A_q(1) = 33, 33, 23, 8 and B_q(1) = 5, 10, 15, 17.

### 12.7.1 And they are not the same tree

      A_q : n — ℓ — k, with 2S pendant at k — a caterpillar B_q : e — f — g, capped by the base —
      a path

 The whole lattice is a caterpillar because it is these two glued at q, and the pendant sits on
 the A side only. [The tower of §12.11 amends this: with the coupling axes carried, the object is two
 caterpillars glued spine to spine at k–q — A runs n–ℓ–k–J_c–K–J with 2S pendant, B runs e–f–g–v–2S′
 — and the §12.8.3 compression generalises to the glued form.]

### 12.7.2 A tower of fibrations, terminating in boxes

| level | base | fibre |
|---|---|---|
| Λ | *q* | A_q × B_q |
| A_q | *k* | (*n*,ℓ)-set × 2*S*-chain |
| B_q | *f* | *e*-set × *g*-chain |

 **Three levels, and at the bottom every fibre is a product of chains — a box**, with a closed form **Box(*a*,*b*)(*z*) = ∏ᵢ *z*^{aᵢ}(1 − *z*^{bᵢ−aᵢ+1})/(1 − *z*).**

### 12.8 Five things the fibre table says
 The counts 165, 330, 345, 136 carry more than their sum.

### 12.8.1 A Pareto frontier inside the lattice
      \|A_q\| falls 33, 33, 23, 8 while \|B_q\| rises 5, 10, 15, 17.

 **Raising the transfer costs the parent and pays the target, monotonically, and no *q* improves both.** That is the same shape as §23.5's d*w*/d*h* > 0 with d*V*/d*h* < 0 — **which the book presents as a fact about brackets and is here a fact about the index itself.**

 Figure 7.3. The Pareto frontier on a log axis, Λ₁₃ solid against Λ₈ dotted: A falls and B rises at both scales, and no
 q improves both. The section |A × B| peaks at q = 2 with 89,700 cells, and the tower shifts ⟨q⟩ from 1.463 to
 1.887 — coupling multiplicity re-weights the profile toward higher transfer, per §12.11.5.

### 12.8.2 A most-probable transfer
 The fibre peaks at q = 2 with 345 cells — 35.3% of Λ — and the sequence is log-concave, hence
 unimodal.

      ⟨q⟩ = 1.4631, sd 0.930 — the mean number of electrons moved across all admissible
      configurations, and S′(1)/S(1) in the base variable.

### 12.8.3 An 8-to-3 compression that loses no count
 Λ's F has eight variables; the shape has three, and S(1,1,1) = 976. Not a projection that forgets
 cells — one that forgets which coordinate inside each side and keeps how many. A general fact
 about caterpillars: a path with one pendant reduces to (base, left, right) whatever its length.

### 12.8.4 Every cross-section is itself a closed, modular lattice

| *q* | side | closed | E(X) | modular |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0–3 | A and B, all eight | **true** | **0** | **true** |

      The shape is not a cylinder over an arbitrary set. It is a cylinder whose every
      cross-section satisfies this book's own law.

### 12.8.5 Which gives E(X) a local form
      E(Λ) = 0 and E(A_q) = E(B_q) = 0 for every q.

 **The second does not follow from the first.** A closed index could in principle have defective slices that cancel in the total. **Here none do**, and that is a new invariant.

### 12.9 Every other shape the lattice contains
 Intervals. \[x,y\] is a box iff no constraint binds across it:

      I_{x,y} = Box(x,y) ⟺ for every constraint i ≤ f(j), y_i ≤ f(x_j)

 **Exact on 60 of 60 tested**, and only **27%** of random intervals qualify — **the other 73% are where the physics is active.** The binding rates rank the constraints:

  constraint                                                            binds
  g≤q                                                                   35.6%
  q≤k                                                                   33.0%
  g ≤ 4f+2                                                              4.9%

 **The most active constraint is the coupling; the least is the Pauli bound — which is why §16.8 finds it the cheapest to violate.** Two independent measures, one answer.

 **Rank levels.** Eighteen antichains, the largest **122 at rank 11**: the biggest set of configurations no two of which can be reached from each other by adding electrons. **Λ is exactly eight times its widest level.**

 **Maximal chains.** A distributive lattice is J(P); **its maximal chains are the linear extensions of P.** With seventeen join-irreducibles and a chain length of seventeen:

    e(P) = 1,113,045,672

 **The seventeen generators of §8.3 and the chain length of seventeen are the same seventeen**, and the book reports both without connecting them.

 **And one shape absent.** No Möbius band, no cycle, no non-planar minor — **the constraint graph is a tree, so there is nothing to twist and nothing to close.** §12.1's finding follows by construction, not by search.

### 12.10 An axis the index did not carry, and now does
 A_q carries 2S, a spin label on the parent. B_q carried none.

    The index recorded the parent's spin and not the target's.

 That was never a closure defect — ℛ cannot see a coordinate that was never supplied. It was an axis
 E2 admits, and the asymmetry of the two fibres is what made it visible.

### 12.10.1 Λ₉, built by the same construction
 The parent's spin is bounded by the parent's capacity coordinate, 2S ≤ k. So the target's is
 bounded by the target's: 2S′ ≤ g. One pendant, on the other fibre, by the identical rule.

                                 Λ₈                          Λ₉
  cells                          976                         1,654
  box                            6,912                       27,648
  E(X)                           0                           0
  sublattice                     yes                         yes
  rank-modular                   475,800 pairs               200,000 tested, 0 violations
  constraint graph               a tree                      a tree — 2S′ attaches as a leaf
  F(1)                           976                         1,654
  F(−1)                          2                           2
  rank levels                    18, widest 122 at 11        21, widest 185 at 12
  log-concave                    yes                         yes

 And Λ₈ is the exact projection of Λ₉ — 976 cells, identical — with fibres of one to four preimages,
 mean 1.69. §31.2.4's dimension ladder gains a rung, and the extra coordinate carries real
 information.

### 12.10.2 What is still not established

 The bound 2S′ ≤ g was built by analogy and not from spectroscopy. Whether it is the correct
 selection rule for a target's spin is a physical question, and this book has not answered it.

      The lattice closes. That the coordinate means what its name says was untested when this
      section was written. §12.11.1 has since answered it: exact vector coupling confirms 2S′ ≤ g as a
      true bound — zero violations over every fibre — and shows it is not tight, in the two ways §12.11.2
      names. What remains is a data lookup, recorded in Q: whether the measured target terms of the
      153 channels saturate min(g, 4f+2−g).


### 12.11 The tower — Λ₉ to Λ₁₃

 §12.10 added one axis by analogy. This section adds four more by battery, and what the batteries
 return is not five facts about five coordinates. It is one law, met five times, and a shape that survives it.

 Figure 7.4. The constraint tree of the full tower: two caterpillars glued at k–q, counting axes rimmed dark (exact
 bounds), coupling axes in the accent (envelopes) — the Chapter 7 dichotomy drawn — with the forbidden f···K bridge
 dashed and priced at 3.5%.

### 12.11.0 Transitions compose, and the ninth axis is what lets them
 **Λ₈'s two ends are not the same shape.** Its source is (n, ℓ, k, 2S) and its target is (e, f, g) —
 four coordinates against three. A transition's output is therefore not a legal input, and **Λ₈ cannot
 be iterated**: no cascade, no sequence, no second step. The asymmetry is a missing spin.

 **The ninth axis supplies it.** 2S′ ≤ g gives the target its own multiplicity, and the target end
 becomes (e, f, g, 2S′) under exactly the source's constraint forms — f ≤ e−1 against ℓ ≤ n−1,
 g ≤ 4f+2 against k ≤ 4ℓ+2, 2S′ ≤ g against 2S ≤ k. All 1,654 targets of Λ₉ are legal sources.

    Define b ∘ a when a's target equals b's source: the composite runs a's source to b's target,
    transferring min(q_a, q_b) — no more than was taken, no more than arrives.

 **Λ₉ is closed under composition, exhaustively: 41,682 composable pairs, zero failures**, and
 associative on 8,434 sampled triples with none. Λ₉ is therefore not merely a lattice of transitions
 but a **category of them**, and composition is time — not a stamp on a cell but an order between
 cells.

 **And Λ₉ is the only stage of the tower that composes.** Composition needs the target's coordinate
 set to be matched by a source set of the same shape. Every axis above the ninth attaches to one end
 or to the joint object, and the shapes part company:

  stage   target                          source                    composes
  Λ₈      (e, f, g)                       (n, ℓ, k, 2S)             no — 3 against 4
  **Λ₉**  **(e, f, g, 2S′)**              **(n, ℓ, k, 2S)**         **yes — 41,682 pairs, 0 failures**
  Λ₁₀     (e, f, g, 2S′, v)               (n, ℓ, k, 2S)             no — no source seniority exists
  Λ₁₁     (e, f, g, 2S′, v)               (n, ℓ, k, 2S, 2J_c)       no — 2J_c is the source's alone
  Λ₁₂     K binds core to orbit — joint    —                        no — not an end coordinate
  Λ₁₃     2J binds K to spin — joint       —                        no

 **So the tower carries three gradings, not two.** §12.11.3's dichotomy sorts the axes into counting
 and coupling — exact and envelope. Composability sorts them differently and **more strictly**: it is
 lost at axis 10, one step before exactness is, because v is a counting coordinate that closes exactly
 and still has no source counterpart. **An axis can be exact and not composable; none is composable
 and not exact.** Λ₉ sits alone at the top of the stricter grading, and the tower above it is the
 record of what each further coordinate costs — first composition, then exactness.

 **The ninth axis was added for seniority and arrived carrying this.** §12.11.1 takes 2S′ on the grid's
 advice, for the physics of the target's multiplicity; nothing in that argument mentions composability,
 and composability is what it bought. **An index gained a temporal structure as a side effect of a
 spectroscopic coordinate** — the sharpest instance in this book of Chapter 16's thesis that surplus is
 what an index defends itself with. Register 313.

### 12.11.0.1 The clock in the constraint — Λ₉'s composition has a direction
 §12.11.0 shows Λ₉ composes. **The composition is not symmetric**, and what breaks the symmetry is a
 coordinate already in the index. Every figure below is recomputed here from the construction.

 **Occupancy never rises.** Take the source signature (n, ℓ, k, 2S) and the target (e, f, g, 2S′) as
 *objects*, and each cell as a morphism between them. **33 objects, 739 of 1,089 ordered pairs joined
 by a step, and not one step raises k.** The object graph falls into three strongly connected
 components, each pure in occupancy — eight objects at k = 3, fifteen at k = 2, ten at k = 1 — with
 full circulation inside each and no return between them. The quotient is a chain of three.

 **And the stratification is verified under variation, not at one setting.** Λ₉ was rebuilt at four cap
 settings — (3,3,1,3,1), (4,4,1,6,1), (4,4,2,6,2) and (5,5,2,6,2) — by enumerating the constraints of
 §7.1 at each and re-running the composition graph. The inputs are the four tuples printed here; the
 method is the object graph of §12.11.0, its strongly connected components by Tarjan, and a step
 counted as rising when the target's occupancy exceeds the source's:

  caps                |Λ₉|   objects   edges   steps raising k   conservative   all-conservative objects
  (3,3,1,3,1)        1,654        33     739                 0    904 (54.7%)   8 at k = 3
  (4,4,1,6,1)       19,433       101   5,986                 0  6,693 (34.4%)   21 at k = 6
  (4,4,2,6,2)       44,153       155  14,114                 0 15,509 (35.1%)   35 at k = 6
  (5,5,2,6,2)       83,543       214  26,921                 0 29,489 (35.3%)   49 at k = 6

 **Zero steps raise occupancy at any of the four**, the object count and the identity count agree at
 every setting, and the objects into which every morphism is conservative sit at maximal occupancy in
 all four. **The conservative fraction is not stable** — 54.7% at the book's caps against 34–35% at the
 three larger ones — so that figure is cap-dependent and the structural claims are not.

    **The clock is occupancy and the tick is k − g.** Cycles inside an era are closed and cost
    nothing; the downward edges are where the clock advances, and they do not reverse.

 **904 of 1,654 cells are conservative** — 54.7% — and 389 morphisms have a reverse. The eight objects
 at maximal occupancy have a property the others lack: **every morphism into them is conservative**,
 forced by k_max = g ≤ q ≤ k ≤ k_max. Time in this index is a counting phenomenon, not a coupling one.

 **And definability is not reachability.** A cell *is* its own down-set, a seventeen-bit word, and in
 that sense every cell contains its own description. It does not contain a path to every other. Over
 the 2,735,716 ordered cell pairs of Λ₉:

  relation                              pairs        share
  comparable in the **lattice** order    311,180      11.4%
  joined by a **composition** path       675,606      24.7%
  **in both**                              9,720       0.4%

 **Neither order contains the other and they share 0.4% of what either carries.** The lattice says what
 a cell is; composition says what it can become. Running them together is the error this table
 forecloses — a cell can be fully defined and unreachable, and most pairs are exactly that.

 **On the reading that time is nine-dimensional.** As a *dimension* it is false: the composition
 preorder quotients to a chain, order dimension 1. As a *location* it is register 314's result — Λ₈
 cannot iterate and Λ₁₀ loses composition, so **nine is not the dimension of time; it is the unique
 dimension at which time exists.** Register 345.

### 12.11.0.2 The clock is an assumption, and the assumption can be removed
 §12.11.0.1's arrow is real and its hypothesis is narrower than it looks. **One coordinate carries two
 readings and §7.1 does not distinguish them.**

  bound              origin                              what it reads g as
  g ≤ q              counting — cannot place more than
                     were removed                        electrons **placed** by this transition
  g ≤ 2(2f+1)        Pauli exclusion                     electrons **present** in the target subshell

 **They coincide exactly when the target subshell begins empty**, and Λ carries no coordinate for what
 was already there. So composition's matching condition — b's source occupancy equals a's target
 occupancy — identifies *placed* with *present*, which is an assumption about destinations rather than
 a constraint of counting. §7.1 lists seven constraints, four origins and nothing else; register 301
 found it short one condition, and **this is a second — a conflation rather than an omission.**

 **The repair is §17.4's and it works.** A bound on a sum cannot be imposed, but a sum can be indexed:
 carry (g, G) with g the number placed and G the total after arrival, under g ≤ G ≤ 2(2f+1) and g ≤ q,
 with 2S′ ≤ G since a target's multiplicity answers to what is present. All single-parent monotone
 bounds; prior occupancy G − g is recovered as a derived quantity. **13,775 cells, closed.**

    **And the clock does not survive it. Of 2,620,090 composable pairs, 826,950 — 31.6% — raise
    occupancy.**

 **A closed index of transitions has a one-way clock exactly when its destinations begin empty.** The
 arrow is a property of the assumption, not of the object, and this is §18.4.1 arriving at the
 expense of §12.11.0.1: a zero is worth the size of the box it was computed in, and **an arrow is worth
 the size of the assumption it was computed under.**

 **What survives, and it is more than what fell.** The occupancy law as mathematics is untouched — it
 holds of any index of its stated form, and the extended index simply is not of that form. And a
 temporal structure survives as a *restriction*: the maximal sub-index on which a weight never rises is
 X_w = {a : w(tgt a) ≤ w(src a)}, which is closed, and **there is one such arrow per coordinate and no
 others.** Recomputed:

  weight         cells    closed
  shell          9,407    yes
  subshell       9,845    yes
  occupancy      5,165    yes
  spin           7,160    yes
  shell + occupancy  —    **no — a sum breaks both sides**
  max(shell, occupancy) — **no — max breaks meets**
  min(shell, occupancy) — **no — min breaks joins**

 §18.4.1's cap table deciding once again what may be a coordinate, now deciding what may be a clock.
 **An index does not have a clock. It has one per coordinate**, and occupancy — the one this book built
 on — is the most restrictive of the four. Register 352.

### 12.11.0.3 Every second bridge, priced
 §12.11.5 calls the tight two-parent K *the first bridge between the cylinder's two ends other than q*.
 The structural content of q was never stated: computed on the constraint graph, **q is the unique cut
 vertex separating source from target.** Its content as a coordinate is a count; its content as
 structure is the connection.

 **So what does a second bridge cost?** Twenty admissible couplings between the two ends were imposed on
 Λ₈ and priced. Recomputed here, every row reproducing:

  bridge      cells   join   meet   factorisation defect
  none          976      0      0      0
  g ≤ n         953      0      0     23
  2S ≤ e        872      0      0    104
  f ≤ 2S        846      0      0    130
  f ≤ ℓ         832      0      0    144
  g ≤ 2S        769      0      0    207
  e ≤ n         708      0      0    268
  ℓ ≤ g         686      0      0    160
  g ≤ ℓ         685      0      0    120
  e ≤ k         676      0      0    300
  k ≤ e         662      0      0    165
  n ≤ e         636      0      0    316
  ℓ ≤ f         616      0      0    288
  2S ≤ g        526      0      0    225
  2S ≤ f        449      0      0    189
  e ≤ 2S        303      0      0    354
  n ≤ g         141      0      0     70
  k ≤ g         141      0      0      **0**
  e ≤ ℓ         120      0      0      **0**
  k ≤ f          60      0      0      **0**
  n ≤ f          48      0      0      **0**

 **Closure survives all twenty**, as §14.4 guarantees for monotone single-coordinate bounds. **Sixteen
 destroy the factorisation**, defect 23 to 354.

    **The exclusivity is exclusivity of the tree, not of closure. E = 0 is untouched by a second
    bridge; the cylinder is destroyed by it.**

 **And the four survivors are a caution rather than a result.** They are among the five smallest sets,
 48 to 141 cells — **a set cut hard enough factorises trivially.** Register 333's point applies
 directly: every closure test in this book passes on the empty index, so a zero is worth the size of
 the object it was computed in.

 **The mechanism is the spanning-tree identity, |E| = |V| − 1.** Λ₈ has eight nodes and seven edges; to
 add a bridge and remain a tree you must break one. **Two moves exist and only two** — break an edge to
 make an edge, or add a coordinate to buy one, which is P16 and which §17.4 prices. By §18.4.1's
 three-body reading, a second bridge is the three-body problem arriving inside the index: two bodies
 give one edge and a tree, three give a triangle at treewidth 2. Register 353.

### 12.11.0.4 The separation hypothesis, named
 Four families of result in this book hold *because the constraint graph is a tree*, and none of them
 says so. The factorising count, the recoverability of the order, the balance at a cut and the
 propagation of Chapter 15 all use it; §7.1 records treeness as a fact about Λ and never as a
 condition. **A hypothesis that is never stated cannot be checked, and the counterexample has been
 inside this book since the ninth axis.**

 **Λ₉′ is the test, and it separates the results.** Adding 2S′ ≤ 2f+1 closes the cycle f — g — 2S′ — f
 and buys 93 cells of tightness. Measured on both:

  property                                        Λ₉, a tree     Λ₉′, a cycle
  closure, E(X) = 0                               0              **0**
  factorisation over the transfer, defect         0              **0**
  conditional independence, f ⊥ 2S′ given g       0 of 16         **6 of 16**

 **Closure does not need the tree. Nor does the factorisation.** Both survive the cycle intact. What
 fails is conditional independence, and it fails at exactly one cut.

 **Which makes the hypothesis narrower than treeness, and this is the correction.** Remove q from
 either graph and the two ends fall apart — in Λ₉′ as much as in Λ₉ — because the cycle lies wholly on
 the target side and does not cross that cut. Remove g and in the tree f and 2S′ fall apart, while in
 Λ₉′ they stay joined by the new edge.

    **A result conditioned on a coordinate holds exactly when that coordinate separates the things
    being conditioned. Treeness is not the hypothesis; it is the condition under which the
    hypothesis holds at every cut at once.**

 In a tree every internal vertex is an articulation point, so the condition is free and nobody notices
 it is being used. Add one edge and it becomes a fact about *which* cut, which is why §12.11.5's
 factorisation survives Λ₉′ and §12.11.1's tightness costs what it costs. **The 93 cells are not paid
 for in generality across the board** — they are paid for at the cuts the new edge spans, and nowhere
 else. Register 347.

### 12.11.0.5 What the cycle costs propagation — Q item O, closed
 §12.11.0.4 shows the separation hypothesis failing at Λ₉′. **Chapter 10's order recovery by tree
 propagation fails there too, and the failure is measurable.** Appendix E carried this as item O,
 *buildable, days*; it is one computation.

 **In a tree there is one path between any two coordinates, so a bound recovered by propagating along
 it is the bound.** With a cycle there are two, and they need not agree. Recovering the bound on 2S′
 from f, directly and by composing through g:

  index                     direct φ̂(2S′ | f)     composed through g     verdict
  Λ₉, a tree                {0: 2, 1: 3}          {0: 2, 1: 3}           **agree**
  Λ₉′, a cycle              **{0: 1, 1: 3}**      {0: 2, 1: 3}           **disagree at f = 0**

 **The direct edge is strictly tighter.** Composing along the tree path gives 2 where the constraint
 gives 1, so **propagation overstates the bound** — it does not fail loudly, it returns a weaker answer
 that looks like an answer.

    **A tree makes propagation sound by making it path-independent. A cycle does not make it wrong;
    it makes it lossy, and the loss is silent.**

 And the failure is **directional**: recovering f from 2S′ agrees in both objects. Only the direction
 the new edge tightens is affected. **This is the fourth independent result to break at Λ₉′**, with the
 cut law, the two-parent count and the Markov property — and it is the one that explains the others,
 since each of those is a propagation argument wearing different clothes. Register 364.

### 12.11.0.6 The earlier bound on the core, tested — Q item K, closed
 Before φ̂ was defined, the eleventh axis carried the bound **2J_c ≤ k**, and whether it contains the
 physical region was left open as item K, *retrievable, hours*. It is one enumeration, and the answer
 is no.

  occupancy k    largest physical 2J_c    2J_c ≤ k admits    φ̂(k) admits
  1                                  3                  1              **3**
  2                                  4                  2              **4**
  3                                  5                  3              **5**

 **The earlier bound excludes physical states at every occupancy**, not at some. A term of a p² shell
 can reach 2J_c = 4 where k = 2 permits 2. So 2J_c ≤ k was not a loose bound that happened to hold; it
 was the wrong bound, and had it survived, the index would have refused states the physics produces.

 **And φ̂ contains the physical region exactly** — it equals the realised maxima at every k, neither
 admitting a state that cannot occur nor excluding one that can. That is why §12.11.3.1 records it as a
 bound taken from the **extent** rather than from a law: it is the tightest containing bound and it was
 read off the object rather than derived. **The exactness is the reason it cannot be exact** — a bound
 that fits its own extent perfectly has no independent authority, which is what the third value of the
 provenance column names. Register 365.

### 12.11.0.7 Why the arrows agree — a decay theorem
 §12.11.0.2 gives one arrow per coordinate. **They are not independent, and how much they agree is
 governed by their distance in the constraint graph.**

  distance on n — ℓ — k — 2S      pair                        overlap against independence
  1                               occupancy ∩ spin            **1.466**
  1                               subshell ∩ occupancy        1.222
  1                               shell ∩ subshell            1.107
  2                               subshell ∩ spin             1.080
  2                               shell ∩ occupancy           1.070
  3                               shell ∩ spin                **1.025**

 **Strictly ordered by distance, with no overlap between distance classes.** And there is a theorem
 behind it, whose hypothesis is §12.11.0.4's.

    **Theorem.** Let X be an index whose constraint graph is a tree, and let A_u, A_v be the arrows
    of coordinates u and v at tree distance d. Then **(i)** the coordinates form a Markov random
    field on the tree — the indicator of X is a product of one factor per edge, so conditioning on a
    separating vertex gives exact conditional independence; **(ii)** hence by the data-processing
    inequality I(u;v) is non-increasing in d; **(iii)** hence by Pinsker,
    |P(A_u ∩ A_v) − P(A_u)·P(A_v)| ≤ √( I(u;v)·ln2 / 2 ). **The overlap of two arrows is bounded by
    a quantity that decays with their distance in the constraint tree.** ∎

 Mutual information, recomputed: **0.353 · 0.227 · 0.199** at d = 1, **0.094 · 0.070** at d = 2,
 **0.020** at d = 3 — strictly decreasing, as the theorem requires.

 **What is proved and what is evidence.** The decay is a theorem. The *strict* ordering measured above
 is not implied by it — the bound is loose — so the theorem establishes that agreement must fall away
 with distance and the measurement establishes that here it does so strictly. **The first is claimed
 and the second is reported.**

 **And the falsifier fires.** The hypothesis is separation, so the test is an index whose graph carries
 a cycle, and the tower supplies exactly one. At Λ₉′ the Markov property fails — 6 violations of 16
 where the tree has none — the data-processing step has no chain to run along, and the Pinsker bridge
 has nothing to bound. **Λ₉′ is now the third independent result to break at the same stage**, with the
 cut law and the two-parent count, which is why §12.11.1's *the tree or the tightness* is not a choice
 between presentations. Register 356.

### 12.11.0.8 Past, present and future — a path, not a triangle
 §10.2 reads the constraint tree as past — change — future. **That reading has arithmetic, and it is
 not the arithmetic of three free things.**

 **Treated as independent the three parts overcount by 130%**: |A|·|Q|·|B| = 33 · 4 · 17 = 2,244
 against |Λ| = 976, a defect of 1,268. Conditioned on the transfer they separate exactly, defect zero.

 **And the balance is not special to the transfer.** Every two-sided cut of the tree gives it —
 **ℓ, q, f and g, all defect zero.** §12.6.1 presents the factorisation as though the transfer were
 doing the work; the computation says the *tree* is doing it, and q is distinguished only by which two
 things it separates.

 **And the reason it is exactly four is the tree.** Deleting a coordinate splits the constraint graph
 into two components precisely when that coordinate is an internal vertex of degree two. n, e and 2S
 are leaves and deleting one leaves a single component; k has degree three and deleting it leaves
 three. **ℓ, q, f and g are the caterpillar's four internal degree-two vertices, and they are the
 only two-sided cuts that exist.** Recomputed on the tree rather than on coordinate order: defect
 zero at every one of the four, and the other four are not two-sided cuts at all. The list was
 complete when it was written and never said why. Register 408.

 **The shape is a path.** Two edges, past ↔ present and present ↔ future, and no past–future edge —
 treewidth 1. By §18.4.1 treewidth 1 closes exactly and treewidth 2 gives an envelope, so **Λ's
 exactness is a proof that the three parts are not a triangle.** A triangle would be the three-body
 shape and register 316 states its price.

 **The future is never a value.** Of **97** (past, present) pairs, **none determines a single future** —
 futures per pair run 5, median 10, maximum 17. And for each value of the present, **every past gives
 the same future set**:

  present q     distinct pasts    distinct future sets    |future|
  0                        33                       1            5
  1                        33                       1           10
  2                        23                       1           15
  3                         8                       1           17

    **The future is conditionally independent of the past given the present** — a Markov property,
    exact at every value. The past constrains only which presents are reachable; having spent that,
    it has spent everything.

 The dependence is monotone and opposed: the future set nests increasing, 5 ⊂ 10 ⊂ 15 ⊂ 17, while the
 past set nests decreasing, 33 ⊇ 33 ⊇ 23 ⊇ 8. **And the bracket is saturated** — at every present the
