 admissible future is the full interval between its own extremes, nothing inside missing. E(X) = 0 read
 on the target end.

 **The tick, decomposed.** Over the 1,169 composable cells the clock ticks 0, 1 or 2 — 389, 540, 240 —
 and the tick has two sources: k − g = (k − q) + (q − g), *something not removed* and *something
 removed but not placed*. **350 against 350 pure, with 80 cells in both**, and the split table is its own transpose: (0,1) = 270
 against (1,0) = 270, (0,2) = 80 against (2,0) = 80.

    **Irreversibility has two independent sources contributing identically.** One is a choice not
    taken; the other a capacity that could not receive. The index weights them the same.

 **And Pauli is a minority partner in it.** g's ceiling is fixed by counting in 60.2% of composable
 cells, tied in 29.5%, and by shell capacity in only 10.3%. **The arrow of time in this object is
 overwhelmingly a counting phenomenon**, which §12.9 states in advance. Register 357.

### 12.11.0.9 Two indices, and the one that does not exist
 If composition has a direction, the obvious question is what the other direction is. It is not a sign
 change — extending k, q and g below zero leaves the object closed and leaves the clock intact, because
 a negative occupancy is a *lower* one and not a reversed one.

 **The reversal is a different object.** Exchange the two ends of Λ₉ — (n,ℓ,k,2S) ↔ (e,f,g,2S′), the
 transfer unchanged. Recomputed here:

  position                          cells    join fail   meet fail   E(X)
  Λ₉, forward                       1,654            0           0      0
  rev(Λ₉), backward                 1,654            0           0      0
  **Λ₉ ∩ rev(Λ₉)**                    **389**            0           0      0
  Λ₉ ∪ rev(Λ₉)                      2,919      360,000     470,625  **2,857**

 **The reversed index is a lawful closed index of the same size, and it is not Λ₉.** That is §11.8's
 failure of self-duality read temporally: both are lawful and simply not the same object.

 **The intersection is where time has no direction.** Every one of its 389 cells satisfies **g = q = k**
 — total transfer, nothing retained and nothing lost — and that is exactly the set of morphisms
 carrying a reverse. It closes at E = 0, it factorises over its own transfer, and it is a **groupoid**:
 every object carries an identity and every morphism a two-sided inverse. §12.11.0.1's cycles were
 confined to level sets of occupancy with every step conservative; conservative is g = q and the
 level-set condition is k = g. **The intersection is that condition stated as an object rather than as
 a property of paths**, and it is 23.5% of Λ₉.

 **And the union does not exist.** 830,625 failing pairs, and closing it would require admitting
 **2,857 further cells** — §16.8.4's amplification, priced.

    **Three of the four positions exist and the fourth does not. A present is definable from the
    past and the future; the thing containing both is not.**

 A structure holding both directions of time is not available at any price this index recognises. What
 is available is a structure holding their agreement. Register 350.

### 12.11.0.10 Every Λ in one table
 The tower is reported in six places under six aspects. This is all of it, computed in one pass.

  stage   dim   cells      E   ambient box    fill    new coordinate   bounded by            grading
  Λ₈        8       976    0         6,912  14.12%    —                —                     exact · does not compose
  **Λ₉**    9     1,654    0        27,648   5.98%    2S′              2S′ ≤ g               **exact · composes**
  Λ₉′       9     1,561    0        27,648   5.65%    —                + 2S′ ≤ 2f+1          exact · composes
  Λ₁₀      10     2,535    0       110,592   2.29%    v                2S′ ≤ v ≤ g           exact · does not compose
  Λ₁₁      11    13,585    0       663,552   2.05%    2J_c             2J_c ≤ φ̂(k)           envelope · does not compose
  Λ₁₂      12    70,905    0     5,308,416   1.34%    K                2K ≤ 2J_c + 2f_max    envelope · does not compose
  Λ₁₃      13   199,130    0    47,775,744   0.42%    2J               |2J − 2K| ≤ 1         envelope · does not compose

 **Every stage closes.** E = 0 at all seven, tested against every cell of the ambient box and not by
 pairs — forty-seven million cells at Λ₁₃, per register 249. Closure is the one property the tower
 never spends.

 **Everything else falls monotonically.** Fill runs 14.12% down to 0.42%: each coordinate multiplies
 the box faster than it multiplies the object, so **the surplus §11.1.1 measures at seven bits per cell
 in Λ₈ grows at every step**. The tower is not a refinement that sharpens; it is a refinement that
 admits more room and fills less of it.

 **And the two gradings part company at different heights.** Composition is lost at Λ₁₀ and exactness
 at Λ₁₁, so there is a stage — Λ₁₀ — that is exact and does not compose, and no stage that composes
 without being exact. Register 315.

### 12.11.0.11 The fourteenth axis, and what can be said about it from inside
 The tower stops at thirteen because the physics stopped being supplied, not because the construction
 did. **What lies beyond can be bounded without being built**, and the bound is a bracket rather than a
 value.

  D    cells      ambient box     fill     bits carried   needed   surplus
   8      976           6,912   14.12%            12.75     9.93      2.82
   9    1,654          27,648    5.98%            14.75    10.69      4.06
  10    2,535         110,592    2.29%            16.75    11.31      5.45
  11   13,585        663,552     2.05%            19.34    13.73      5.61
  12   70,905       5,308,416    1.34%            22.34    16.11      6.23
  13  199,130      47,775,744    0.42%            25.51    17.60      7.91

 **Fill falls at every step and surplus rises at every step**, and the first of those is a law rather
 than a pattern. A constrained coordinate has mean multiplicity strictly below its own value count — if
 it did not, it would be free and would carry no constraint — so each axis multiplies the ambient box
 by more than it multiplies the object. Fill is therefore monotone decreasing **by construction**, and
 bounded below by zero.

    **The sequence converges. Its limit lies in [0, 0.42%]. That the limit is zero does not
    follow.**

 Monotone and bounded gives convergence, which is a deduction. The ratios stage to stage are 0.424,
 0.383, 0.893, 0.652 and 0.312 — not themselves monotone — so nothing in the construction forbids them
 approaching one and the limit settling above zero. **§22.1.2's rule applies to the tower's own tail:
 the existence of the limit comes from the law and survives; its value would come from the pattern and
 does not.**

 **This is what the index can say about a coordinate it does not have.** It cannot name the fourteenth
 axis — §32.1.4 shows ℛ never produces a coordinate — but it can bound the *direction*: an object that
 occupies less and less of its own ambient box while carrying more and more surplus. In the limit the
 index would be a vanishing fraction of the space it is written in and would defend itself with
 unbounded redundancy. **Whether that limit is a null or merely a small number is exactly the question
 the construction leaves open**, and leaving it open is the correct answer rather than a gap.

 **And a null is definable in this language, which is worth stating where the tower ends.** The empty
 index satisfies every criterion in this book: ℛ(∅) = ∅, so E(∅) = 0 and it is closed; Q(∅) = ∅, so it
 is COMPLETE by P19, which is strictly stronger. It has zero join-irreducibles and takes zero bits.
 **E(X) = 0 is therefore necessary for completeness and not sufficient for content** — every closure
 test in this book passes on nothing at all. What distinguishes Λ from the empty index is not closure
 but |J(Λ)| = 17 and its seven bits of surplus. Register 333.

### 12.11.0.12 How an index enters a larger index, and what it looks like from there
 §12.11.0.9 bounds the direction beyond thirteen. This is the other question: **what happens to Λ when
 it becomes a coordinate of something bigger.** All three answers are computed, and none of them
 requires the larger index to be built.

 **It enters as a chain, and it enters small.** A coordinate must be totally ordered and Λ₁₃ is a
 lattice, so the only chain-valued function ℛ accepts on it is its rank. 199,130 cells become **44
 rank values**: 17.60 bits collapse to 5.46, a compression of 4,526 to 1, with **12.14 bits per cell
 lost.** Thirty-one per cent of the object survives the promotion and the rest is not representable as
 a coordinate at all. Structurally the result is the same kind of thing as dimension 1 of Λ, which is a
 chain of three — so an index can be an axis of another, at that price.

 **A coarse coordinate is a branching node.** At Λ₉ the rank chain has 21 values and the fibres beneath
 them run from 1 cell to **185**. One point above stands for up to a hundred and eighty-five below, and
 nothing in the coordinate distinguishes them.

 **And composition, seen from above, stops being a map and becomes a bracket.** Λ₉ composes: 41,682
 pairs, closed and associative. Reduced to rank there are 264 distinct (rank a, rank b) inputs, and
 **only 17% of them determine a single output rank.** The worst spread is **twelve** possible output
 ranks from one input pair; the composite's rank lies in [3, 23], narrowed by the inputs and not fixed
 by them.

    **From one coordinate up, a transition is not a function of its inputs. It is an interval.**

 That is §22.1's bracket arriving unbidden, one dimension higher, and it is the clearest statement in
 this book of what coarsening costs: not error, but resolution — the deduction stays valid and stops
 being sharp.

 **What this does not show.** The branching is *inside* the index. Those 185 cells are cells of Λ₉,
 states of the same object. What the collapse loses is resolution, not access to anything else, and
 ignorance about this index is not evidence of another. **Every branch terminates in a cell of Λ₉.**
 Register 334.

### 12.11.1 The axes, and what each one sees
  axis     coordinate    bound carried            cells      density    what it distinguishes
  9        2S′           2S′ ≤ g                  1,654      63.7%      the target's multiplicity
  9′       —             + 2S′ ≤ 2f+1             1,561      67.5%      the same, one Pauli cut tighter
  10       v             2S′ ≤ v ≤ g              2,535      44.7%      pairing depth — seniority
  11       2J_c          2J_c ≤ φ̂(k)              13,585     17.0%      the core's fine structure
  12       K             2K ≤ 2J_c + 2f_max       70,905     31.4%      core–orbit orientation
  13       2J            |2J − 2K| ≤ 1            199,130    64.4%      the outer electron's spin bit

 **Two readings the definition requires and a loose reading drops.** The density column was rebuilt
 from the definition above, and four of six values fell out at once; two did not, and localising them
 gives two conditions that are not stated anywhere:

 **First, the sum runs over the STAGE BELOW.** For axis 12 that is Λ₁₁, so the cells are Λ₁₀'s
 weighted by their own 2S′ and v fibres — not Λ₈'s. The same quantity computes to 30.8% over Λ₈,
 31.1% over Λ₉ and **31.4% over Λ₁₀**, which is the stated value. The weighting is a third of a
 percentage point per stage and it converges on the answer exactly.

 **Second, each envelope is priced once.** Filtering axis 12's numerator on whether J_c is physically
 realised charges axis 11's price a second time — 12.7% against 30.8%, an eighteen-point gap that is
 entirely the double charge. §12.11.3's dichotomy says each coupling axis pays once, and the density
 column is where that has to be enforced rather than stated. Register 434.

 **The table's own vocabulary, defined.** Four terms in the rows above are used to define the
 coordinates and were never themselves defined, which is the failure S1 exists to prevent. They are
 defined here.

    **terms(ℓᵏ)** — the multiset of LS terms (2S, 2L) of the configuration ℓᵏ, obtained by
    **microstate enumeration**: list every way of placing k electrons in the 2(2ℓ+1) spin-orbitals,
    accumulate (2M_L, 2M_S), and strip complete (2S, 2L) blocks from the largest M_L down until the
    list is empty. This is the standard construction and the origin Chapter 7 already cites for
    2S ≤ k.

    **f_max** — the cap on f under §7.4, **not** the cell's own f. The distinction is the whole of
    §12.11.5: the bound 2K ≤ 2J_c + 2f_max has one parent and preserves the tree and the cylinder;
    the same bound written with the cell's f has two parents and breaks the factorisation by 3.5%.
    Writing f_max without defining it left the book's title resting on an undefined subscript.

    **φ̂(k)** — the monotone envelope of k ↦ max 2J over the parent shells admitted by the caps, so
    φ̂ = {1: 3, 2: 4, 3: 5} at §7.4's. **This is not §6.1's φ̂ᵢⱼ**, which is ℛ's pairwise envelope
    max{xᵢ : x ∈ X, xⱼ ≤ v}. Both are monotone envelopes and that is the whole of the resemblance;
    one takes two coordinate indices and the other takes an occupancy. The collision is recorded
    rather than renamed, because renaming a symbol used 213 times is a larger risk than naming the
    two senses once. Register 250.

 **Density, stated so it can be rechecked.** For each axis, over every cell of the stage below that
 the lattice admits, count the values of the new coordinate that exact vector coupling realises and
 divide by the values the admissible bound allows. Three of the six exact sets are not the obvious
 ones and the column cannot be reproduced without them: seniority at axis 10 means the terms *new*
 at occupancy v — terms(f^v) less terms(f^(v−2)) as multisets, not merely a term of spin 2S′
 existing at v; the core's J at axis 11 is restricted to terms carrying the cell's own multiplicity 2S,
 not to all terms of ℓᵏ; and the target spin at axis 9 is the spin set of f^g by microstate
 enumeration. Under that definition all six entries reproduce exactly — 63.7, 67.5, 44.7, 17.0, 31.4,
 64.4 — at the caps of §7.4 and cap-dependent as always. Every bound is §14.4-admissible; every stage is closed — exhaustively to
 Λ₁₀, by the §7.3 proof beyond it — and every stage projects exactly onto the one below.
 Independence is demonstrated, not assumed, at every axis: p²'s ¹S and ¹D share a cell below axis
 10 and split at v; p¹'s ²P shares a cell below axis 11 and splits at J_c; and every K > 0 cell splits
 at J. Each coordinate is a physical degree of freedom that no function of the coordinates below it
 can express — the same door through which every legitimate axis must enter, by Chapter 17's theorem.

 **The ninth axis, checked against the textbook and tightened once.** Its exact set is the seniority
 ladder read edge-on, {g mod 2, g mod 2 + 2, …, min(g, 4f+2−g)} — the ninth fibre was indexing v
 before the tenth axis named it. The textbook anchors agree exactly: p² gives {¹S, ¹D, ³P},
 2S′ ∈ {0, 2}; p³ gives {⁴S, ²D, ²P}, 2S′ ∈ {1, 3}. And Λ₉ is not the tightest admissible object:
 2S′ ≤ 2f+1 is §14.4-admissible and provably containing — min(g, 4f+2−g) ≤ 2f+1 by averaging — and
 Λ₉′ closes at 1,561 cells. The 93 cells it cuts are exactly (f = 0, g = 2, 2S′ = 2), an s² target
 carrying triplet spin. The price is structural: the constraint graph gains its first cycle, f–g–2S′,
 so Chapter 15's tree-propagation guarantee lapses and must be retested rather than inherited. **The
 tree or the tightness** — the language offers both only up to 1,561 against 1,654 cells.

 **Every stage closed exhaustively, by the book's own theorem.** The upper tower was never
 pair-tested: Λ₁₂ has 2.5 billion pairs and Λ₁₃ has 19.8 billion, so closure above Λ₁₀ rested on the
 form argument with sampling beside it. A.2 removes the need — *X is closed iff ℛ(X) = X* — and ℛ is
 a sweep of the ambient box, not of the pairs. Every stage now tested against **every cell of its
 ambient box**:

  stage      |Λ|      |ℛ(Λ)|      E     ambient box swept
  Λ₈           976        976     0             6,912
  Λ₉         1,654      1,654     0            27,648
  Λ₁₀        2,535      2,535     0           110,592
  Λ₁₁       13,585     13,585     0           663,552
  Λ₁₂       70,905     70,905     0         5,308,416
  Λ₁₃      199,130    199,130     0        47,775,744

 Forty-seven million ambient cells at the top stage, every one decided, and no stage admits a cell it
 does not contain. **The instrument was in the book from Chapter 14 onward and was never turned on the
 book's own tower** — the sampling caveat existed because the theorem was proved and then not used.
 Register 249.

 **The four-cap sweep, now run.** Across caps (3,3,1,3,1), (4,4,2,4,1), (4,4,2,6,2) and (5,5,2,6,2):
 φ̂ stays monotone, every stage still projects exactly onto the one below, and the cylinder's
 factorisation Σ_q |A(q)|·|B(q)| stays exact at Λ₁₃ — 199,130, 4,731,790, 40,310,170 and 77,083,771
 cells, defect zero at every cap. The shape is not an artefact of the book's caps.

 **The tower at the shells the book had been discussing from outside it.** Referee flag 1 asked how
 an s-and-p object licenses conclusions about d and f electrons. The tower was rebuilt at caps
 carrying both, by multiplicity weighting rather than enumeration, and every density recomputed:

  caps                    shells      axis 9   9′     10     11     12     13
  (3,3,1,3,1)             s, p         63.7%  67.5%  44.7%  17.0%  31.4%  64.4%
  (4,4,1,6,1)             s, p         54.2%  60.3%  33.2%   7.0%  36.7%  64.5%
  (5,5,2,10,2)            + d          49.4%  55.6%  28.4%   4.9%  28.2%  65.7%
  (5,5,3,14,3)            + f          46.6%  53.0%  25.9%   4.2%  22.4%  66.1%

 **The dichotomy survives every enlargement**: the counting axes stay exact and all three coupling
 axes stay envelopes, at the d shell and at the f. Two things move, and both are informative. Every
 density except the thirteenth **falls** as the object grows, so the envelope gap widens with scale —
 the price of certainty is not a constant but an increasing function of the index's size, which no
 part of this book had predicted. And the thirteenth **rises**, 64.4 to 64.5 to 65.7 to 66.1,
 converging on the ⅔ that §12.11.1 states as its nominal value. The gap between ⅔ and the measured
 figure was attributed there to the K = 0 singlet; as the caps grow those cells thin out and the
 density approaches the nominal, which confirms the attribution by a route the book did not have when
 it made it. Registers 240 and 241.

 **And two facts of the tenth's battery worth the print.** Its 10-box appears at caps (5,5,2,6) and
 not at the book's — the known small-cap effect, caught by §7.4's own discipline, as it caught the
 same absence for Λ₈ and Λ₉. And the obstruction that broke closure at the ninth axis does not
 dissolve at the tenth; it moves up one level, onto v itself — v ≡ g mod 2 and the conjugation
 ceiling, both still outside the language. **Extension conserves the obstruction.**

 The order matters and is not the order first written down. K precedes J: K's parents are J_c and
 f, so axis 13 is reachable from axis 11 without axis 12, and taking J first costs 25 points of density
 on its fibre. §12.11.4 gives the reason. And carrying both with all admissible bounds closes the
 11–12–13 cycle in the constraint graph — a triangle in both senses at once: each coupling rule is a
 triangle inequality, and the axes themselves form a 3-cycle.

 **What the twelfth axis measures, and when it is real.** On every f = 0 channel the K-set is a
 singleton — 2K = 2J_c, the triangle collapses, and axis 12 degenerates into a copy of axis 11. Where
 it does not collapse, K measures the one thing no occupancy or magnitude coordinate can: the relative
 orientation of the core's angular momentum against the Rydberg electron's orbital plane — the
 coordinate anisotropic core–electron interactions resolve, and the one that separates series
 converging to the same core limit with different quantum defects. Its validity is conditional in
 exactly the right direction: K is a good quantum number when core splitting dominates exchange —
 high n, the bracket's domain — and degrades toward low n, outside it.

 **And the thirteenth is one bit, at the apex where the index and the atom converge.** Given K, the
 J-fibre is a doublet everywhere except the K = 0 singlet: axis 13 sees precisely the outer electron's
 spin orientation, the weakest and last coupling, and nothing else. But it is the only coordinate in
 the entire tower that nature conserves exactly. Everything below it is bookkeeping that mixing
 breaks — configurations at 40–50% in the book's own exclusion (iii), LS terms in intermediate
 coupling, J_c and K outside the jK regime. J survives all of it, by rotational invariance. So the
 tower ascends from coordinates the language expresses exactly but the atom keeps only approximately,
 to a coordinate the atom keeps exactly and the language expresses up to one parity point. **The two
 ends of the tower trade places.**

### 12.11.1.1 The eleventh axis's density, computed under variation
 §12.11.1 reports the coupling axes' densities at the book's caps and does not say what they do when the
 caps move. **This is that measurement, and it is stated to the standard F.3 defines and no number in
 this book had reached** — the method named with its input set, the inputs printed, and the result
 recomputed under variation rather than once.

 **The method.** For each cell of Λ₁₀, enumerate the terms of the source shell by vector coupling and
 take the largest 2J_c that occurs; sum those over the cells to get the exact count. Divide by |Λ₁₁|,
 which is the same enumeration with 2J_c bounded by φ̂(k) instead. **The input set is the caps tuple**
 (n_max, e_max, ℓ_max, k_max, f_max), and every value used is printed below rather than named.

  caps               \|Λ₁₁\| envelope        exact     density
  (3, 3, 1, 3, 1)               13,585       10,585      77.9%
  (4, 4, 1, 6, 1)              226,609      106,421      47.0%
  (4, 4, 2, 6, 2)            1,225,415      616,725      50.3%
  (5, 5, 2, 6, 2)            2,341,841    1,228,816      52.5%

    **The density is not monotone in the caps.** It falls from 77.9% to 47.0% when the d shell
    opens, then rises to 50.3% and 52.5% as the shells widen further.

 **The fall and the recovery have different causes.** Opening the d shell admits many k values whose
 φ̂ is set by the largest term of the widest shell, so the envelope grows faster than the exact set.
 Widening further adds cells whose exact 2J_c is near that bound, so the ratio recovers. **Neither
 movement is available from one cap setting**, which is why §12.11.1's single figure could not show it
 and why the fourth column of F.3 exists.

 Registers 387, 388.

### 12.11.1.2 The conservative share, recomputed under variation
 §12.11.0.1 reports 904 of 1,654 cells conservative — 54.7% **of every cell**. This is the share over
 a different population: **the composable cells only**, those whose target is a legal source, which is
 the set the clock actually runs on. At the book's caps that is 739 of 1,169, and the two figures are
 not in competition — they answer different questions and the denominators say which. **Recomputed at
 four cap settings**, with the method named, its input set named, and every input printed.

 **The method.** Build Λ₉ at a caps tuple, keep the cells whose target is a legal source, and count
 those with g = q — the transfer total and nothing left behind. **The input set is the caps tuple**
 (n_max, e_max, ℓ_max, k_max, f_max), printed in the first column.

  caps                  \|Λ₉\|    composable    conservative     share
  (3, 3, 1, 3, 1)        1,654         1,169             739     63.2%
  (4, 4, 1, 6, 1)       19,433        16,150           5,986     37.1%
  (4, 4, 2, 6, 2)       44,153        37,430          14,114     37.7%
  (5, 5, 2, 6, 2)       83,543        71,087          26,921     37.9%

    **The share is not a property of the object. It nearly halves when the shells open, then
    settles** — and the same is true of the all-cells share, which runs 54.7% at the book's caps
    against 34–35% above them.

 63.2% at the book's caps is the smallest object's number, and it is the outlier. **Once the d shell is
 open the share sits at 37–38% and moves by less than a point across a fiftyfold growth in cells** — so
 the honest statement is not a single percentage but that **reversibility settles near three cells in
 eight among the composable, and the book's own caps overstate it by two thirds.** Both denominators
 behave the same way, which is the point: the instability is in the caps and not in the choice of
 population. Registers 389, 392.

### 12.11.1.3 An index has a time column exactly when its cells are moves

 §12.11.0 shows Λ₉ composes and register 313 records that **Λ already carries time — source,
 transfer, target.** The companion's threshold table prints the same pair. **Read together they give
 a law about which indexes can carry time at all.**

  index                      kind          cells    composable    fraction
  **Λ₈**                     transition       976          **0**     0.000
  **Λ₉**                     transition     1,654      **1,169**     0.707
  **the violation index**    transition     2,370      **1,410**     0.595
  the periodic table         state             90          **—**         —
  the calendar               state            365          **—**         —

    **A transition cell has two ends — a source and a target — so it can be asked
    whether its target is another cell's source. A state cell has one position and
    the question does not arise: there is nothing to compose.**

 **The second column exists exactly when the first column's cells are moves.** That is why Λ₉ and the
 companion's index print it and the drawn tables cannot — not because nobody measured them, but
 because the measurement is undefined there.

 **Space is what the index holds; time is what it can compose.** Not an added coordinate and not a
 metaphor: **a property of the same cells, available only because they have two ends.**

    **And it separates two objects the book has been comparing as though they were
    alike.** The periodic table's E = 36 and Λ's E = 0 are not one quantity measured
    on two objects of the same kind. **One has a time column and one cannot have one.**

 **The twenty-point jump.** Λ₈ composes **not at all** — zero of 976, four source coordinates
 against three target — and adding the single axis 2S′ ≤ g takes Λ₉ to **70.7%.** §12.11.0's
 finding that Λ₉ composes and Λ₈ does not now carries a magnitude, and it is the whole distance
 from nothing to seven-tenths. *An earlier form of this paragraph said 50.3%, computed on a
 signature chosen for convenience rather than the book's own. Register 625.*

 Registers 622–623.

### 12.11.1.4 Composability up the tower, and what stops it

 §12.11.1.3 gives the law. **The tower measured stage by stage gives its shape, and the shape has a
 peak:**

  stage    cells    composable   fraction   axis added        kind
  **Λ₈**       976         **0**   **0.0000**   —                 four-against-three: nothing composes
  Λ₉        1,654       1,169     0.7068   2S′ ≤ g           **COUNTING**
  **Λ₁₀**    2,535       2,050   **0.8087**   2S′ ≤ v ≤ g       **COUNTING** — the peak
  Λ₁₁       13,585      9,450     0.6956   2J_c ≤ φ̂(k)       **COUPLING**
  Λ₁₂       22,275     14,242     0.6394   2K ≤ 2J_c + 2f    **COUPLING**
  Λ₁₃       64,290     39,772     0.6186   \|2J − 2K\| ≤ 1     **COUPLING**

    **Counting axes raise the composable fraction. Coupling axes lower it. Not one
    exception in either direction**, and the boundary is §12.11.3's own dichotomy —
    stated there for how each axis is PRICED, and it turns out to predict the SIGN
    of the composability change as well.

 **And the reason is in what each kind of axis says.** A counting axis says how much of something is
 present, and *how much* is a property a target can hand to a source. **A coupling axis says how two
 momenta are oriented relative to each other, and an orientation belongs to a pair, not to an end.**
 The target of one transition carries a coupling the next transition's source has no slot for.

 **Λ₈ is zero, not half.** An earlier form of this section said 50.3%, computed on a three-against-three
 signature chosen for convenience. **On the book's own — source (n, ℓ, k, 2S) against target (e, f, g),
 four against three — nothing composes at all**, which is register 313 read literally.

### 12.11.1.5 The non-composable cells, defined

 **At Λ₁₀'s peak, the missing 19.13% is one thing.** All 485 non-composable cells have **g = 0**, and
 every g = 0 cell is non-composable — 485 for 485. Five target signatures fail, at 97 cells each, and
 the coordinate that fails is **k**. **L.c8 is k ≥ 1**: a source must hold at least one electron, and a
 target ending with g = 0 holds none.

    **The non-composable share at the peak is exactly the set of transitions that
    empty their target subshell. It is k ≥ 1 seen from the other end.**

 **At Λ₁₃ there are three kinds**, and together they are exhaustive:

  g = 0                          11,188   the target ends empty — L.c8 again
  **2J ∈ {6, 7, 8}**              **5,116**   **the outcome exceeds any 2J_c a core can carry**
  combination only                8,214   no single coordinate fails; the five-tuple is not a source
  **total**                      **24,518**   of 64,290 — 38.14%

 **The second kind is structural.** 2J reaches 8 and 2J_c reaches 5. **Three values of total angular
 momentum exist as outcomes and cannot exist as starting points**, because 2J inherits 2K's range after
 2K has absorbed 2J_c + 2f, while 2J_c is bounded by φ̂(k) alone. *The outcome inherits more than the
 core can carry.* Registers 625–627.

### 12.11.1.6 The periodic table's own numbers, and where a transition may be continued

 Λ has no Z. Every constraint is internal to one atom, and **the question of what changes when the real
 atomic numbers enter is answerable from the 118 configurations already used for the 18,288 constraint
 tests.** Building cells (Z, n, ℓ, k, q, e, f, g) from real occupied subshells gives 4,325:

  composable WITHIN one element              982 of 4,325     **22.71%**
  composable ACROSS the table              3,686 of 4,325     **85.23%**
  gained by allowing another element       **+2,704**         **+62.5 points**

    **Within one atom fewer than a quarter of real transitions can be followed by
    another. Allow the whole table and it is six-sevenths.** A target configuration
    that no transition in element Z can start from is an ordinary source in Z′.

 **And closure runs the other way.** E over these cells with Z as a coordinate is **972,862**; strip Z
 and it is **28,503**. *A prediction that E would stay 0 because Z is free is refuted, and refuted by
 the freedom itself: 118 values times everything else, with the envelopes admitting nearly all of it.*
 **Λ closes because it has no Z — which is why E(Λ) = 0 is a statement about transition structure and
 not about chemistry.** *Composability wants the whole table; closure wants one atom.*

### 12.11.1.7 And the selection rules decide which side of the atom a transition can continue on

 The electromagnetic quotient is a filter on the same cells. **If charge or frequency carries the
 exchange, allowed and forbidden transitions should compose differently. They do, and they cross:**

  population                     cells    within     rate    across     rate
  all                            4,325       982    0.227     3,686    0.852
  **EM-allowed \|Δℓ\| = 1**      2,673       309   **0.116**     2,399   **0.897**
  **EM-forbidden**               1,652       673   **0.407**     1,287   **0.779**
  parity-conserving                606       233    0.384       544    0.898
  parity-changing                3,719       749    0.201     3,142    0.845

    **Within one atom a dipole-allowed transition is the LEAST likely to be
    followable — 11.6% against the forbidden 40.7%. Across the table the order
    reverses — 89.7% against 77.9%.**

 *The committed prediction was that EM-allowed cells compose at a higher rate. It is refuted within an
 atom and confirmed across the table, and the crossing is the result.*

 **The selection rule that forbids composition within an atom is the same rule that enables it between
 atoms.** A dipole transition changes ℓ by one, moving the target into a configuration that atom rarely
 holds as a source — while some element does hold it, and the elements that do are exactly the ones
 ℓ-adjacency reaches. **Parity says it twice over**: parity-conserving cells compose within at 38.4%
 and parity-changing at 20.1%. Registers 628–629.

### 12.11.2 Three excluded forms, and the fold has a name
 Chapter 17 excluded two constraint forms — sums and differences. The tower requires a third:
 SYMMETRIC BOUNDS. The exact ceiling of every coupling axis is symmetric under the reflection of
 §3 — particle-hole conjugation, terms(ℓᵏ) = terms(ℓ^(4ℓ+2−k)), verified exactly for every k on the p
 and d shells — and a symmetric non-constant function is not monotone, so §14.4 cannot carry it.
 min(g, 4f+2−g) for spin; the conjugation ceiling for seniority; max2J(ℓ,k), unimodal with its peak at
 half filling, 25 at f⁷, zero at closure, for the core's J. One reflection, three appearances.

 The envelope gap decomposes, and the three parts are distinct. At the caps of §7.4: axis 9's
 gap is parity 5, fold 1, both 1; axis 11's is ceiling 16, parity 24, triangle 17. Three machines —
 the REFLECTION (conjugation), the CONGRUENCE (fermion parity), the TRIANGLE (coupling
 additivity, one sum and one difference, precisely Chapter 17's two exclusions wearing physics) —
 and no coupling axis escapes all three. The exact coupling region is not a lattice in any
 presentation tried: as 2S′ (50,592 failures), as parity alone (52,080), as the min-cap (17,856), as
 pair count (7,254), as (2S,2L,2J) (2,443 meets) — and the triangle's first meet failure manufactures
 J = ½ from S = L = 0. The componentwise minimum forgets that J is built from L and S.

 And the law, three instances deep before the triangle made it four: **every coupling coordinate's
 exact physical bound requires either two parents or a congruence, and the tree carries only one.**
 Spin needed min(g, 4f+2−g); seniority needs the conjugation ceiling; the core's J needs a
 non-monotone φ. Each admissible extension is the monotone envelope of its physics.

 One half of the triangle survives, and the four-cap check of §7.4 has now been run: the region
 {|2L−2S| ≤ 2J ≤ 2L+2S} is JOIN-closed — zero failures at caps 6, 8, 10 and 12 — and meet-broken at
 every one, by 2,862, 12,489, 40,887 and 110,229 failing meets. Certainty survives upward and dies
 downward, which is §3's skew stated as an inequality. The check also sharpens the statement: impose
 the parity congruence as well and the join closure dies too — 1,848 failures at cap 6 — so the
 surviving half belongs to the parity-free triangle and to nothing larger.

### 12.11.3 The dichotomy
    Counting coordinates close exactly. Coupling coordinates close as envelopes. There is no
    third kind in this object, and the first coupling constraint was already in §7.1.

 The origins table of Chapter 7 has four rows. Pauli, the hydrogenic solution and counting give
 single-coordinate exact bounds — that is why E(Λ) = 0 was available at all. Vector coupling gives
 envelopes, provably and permanently, because its exact form is made of the three excluded shapes.
 The price of certainty is not paid once at the door; it is paid per coupling axis, and §12.11.1's density
 column is the receipt.

 And the surplus is not waste. Every envelope cell is a tripwire: a measured term assigned a
 nonphysical (v, 2S′) or (2S, 2J_c) pair lands in the gap and is caught before any energy is read — a
 D1 check of Chapter 16's kind, mechanical from the exact tables, available for all 153 channels.
 The price of certainty and the budget of self-defence are the same cells, counted twice.

### 12.11.3.1 Where each bound comes from, and why 9, 10 and 11 differ
 The dichotomy sorts axes into counting and coupling. **The sharper statement is about where each
 axis's bound is taken from**, and it is the same distinction §15 draws between a deduction and a fit.

  D   coordinate   bound                    taken from                              provenance
   1  n            1 ≤ n ≤ cap              the hydrogenic radial solution          law
   2  ℓ            ℓ ≤ n − 1                the hydrogenic radial solution          law
   3  k            1 ≤ k ≤ 4ℓ + 2           Pauli exclusion                         law
   4  q            q ≤ k                    counting                                law
   5  e            1 ≤ e ≤ cap              the hydrogenic radial solution          law
   6  f            f ≤ e − 1                the hydrogenic radial solution          law
   7  g            g ≤ min(4f + 2, q)       Pauli and counting                      law
   8  2S           2S ≤ k                   vector coupling on the source           law
   9  2S′          2S′ ≤ g                  vector coupling on the target           law
  10  v            2S′ ≤ v ≤ g, parity      seniority, Racah 1943                   law
  11  2J_c         2J_c ≤ φ̂(k)              the envelope of the realised maxima      **extent**
  12  K            2K ≤ 2J_c + 2f_max       vector coupling, **weakened to one parent**   law, weakened
  13  2J           |2J − 2K| ≤ 1            coupling K with spin ½                  law

 All thirteen bounds are verified against the construction, cell by cell. **The provenance column has
 three values, not two**, and the third is where the tower spends its exactness.

 **Dimension 12 is the third kind, and it is the one the book chose.** The law for K is
 2K ≤ 2J_c + 2f — vector coupling of the core with the target's orbital momentum — and *f* is the
 cell's own. That bound has **two parents**, which breaks the tree; §A.15 and register 230 record what
 it costs, 2,475 cells of the cylinder. The book substitutes f_max, the cap, giving one parent and a
 surviving factorisation. **The law is not abandoned but weakened**, and 39,375 of Λ₁₂'s 70,905 cells
 sit at an f below the cap, so for those the bound admits more than the law does. Dimension 13 inherits
 the weakening through K without adding to it.

 **φ̂ is not derived; it is observed.** Computed here: φ̂ = {1: 3, 2: 4, 3: 5}, and the largest 2J
 actually realised at each occupancy is {1: 3, 2: 4, 3: 5}. They are the same numbers because φ̂ *is*
 the envelope of the set's own extent. Λ₉ and Λ₁₀ take their bounds from laws that exist independently
 of Λ; Λ₁₁ takes its bound from Λ.

    **A bound taken from the law closes exactly. A bound taken from the extent closes only to an
    envelope.**

 That is why exactness is lost at the eleventh axis and not before it, and it is the same rule §15
 states for brackets: presume the next bracket from the law that generates the widths, never from the
 pattern the widths make.

 **And it says what E(X) measures.** ℛ builds φ_ij from X's own projections — ℛ is an extent-bound
 operator throughout. So **E(X) = |ℛ(X)| − |X| is the price of describing a set by its extent rather
 than by its law**: the count of what the pattern admits and the law does not. E(X) = 0 says the two
 coincide — the extent *is* the law for that set, which is P1's self-reference stated as an arithmetic
 rather than a property. Register 325.

### 12.11.4 Precision is path-dependent; physics is not
 The exact J-set of a channel is the same whether J is reached through K or directly — recoupling,
 12 of 12 (J_c, f) pairs — but the envelopes differ: 64.4% through K, 39.4% direct. The envelope does
 not commute with recoupling. The index remembers the route; the atom does not.

### 12.11.5 The shape survives, and it has a boundary
 Every stage of the tower factorises exactly over the transfer: sections 815 · 3,260 · 6,150 · 3,360 at
 Λ₁₁, 11,470 · 45,880 · 89,700 · 52,080 at Λ₁₃, log-concave, unimodal, peak at q = 2, Σ_q |A(q)|·|B(q)|
 equal to the direct count at every stage with zero defect. The object is still a cylinder — because
 every coupling axis hangs off one side of the tree. The mean transfer rises, ⟨q⟩ = 1.4631 → 1.887,
 as coupling multiplicity re-weights the profile.

 Figure 7.5. The solid in silhouette: plan (parent) and side (target) views at ±√-cell scale, Λ₁₃ filled against Λ₈
 dashed. The parent side thins toward q = 3 while the target side thickens — §12.8.1's trade, seen as shape, at both
 scales of the tower.

 The boundary: the tight two-parent K — bounded by J_c on the A side and f on the B side — is the
 first bridge between the cylinder's two ends other than q, and it breaks the factorisation by **15,150 cells at Λ₁₂ and
 45,450 at Λ₁₃**, 21.4% and 22.8% of the product — recomputed at register 358; the 2,475 previously
 printed here is withdrawn. The tree is the cylinder; tightening K is the move that spends it. The
 title of this book is a property of envelope precision. [And the two-body reading: sections separate
 while the coupling is isotropic; the tight K is the anisotropy; interpretation, not theorem.]

### 12.11.6 An index whose one prediction is false, and why that is a result
 The E1 selection rule on (2J, 2J′) — |Δ2J| ≤ 2 — is a closed index, by the mutual-bound lemma.
 Remove the one cell nature forbids, J = 0 ↛ 0, and closure fails by exactly four meets at every cap
 tested, and all four failing meets are the forbidden cell: (0,1) ∧ (1,0) = (0,0). The defect is E = 1,
 and the one cell ℛ restores is the one the atom vetoes — the photon's unit of angular momentum,
 with the parity half of the rule a congruence that breaks joins, the photon's odd parity.
 [The photon reading is interpretation; the E = 1, the four meets and the congruence are computed.]

 This is the calibration §31.3.4 owes its reader. E(X) counts proposals, not guarantees: here is an
 index whose single proposal is known false, vetoed by structure the index does not carry. The 540
 of the Kreuzer–Skarke slice are proposals of the same kind, and are stated as such.

### 12.11.7 What the tower leaves open
 Recorded in Q, and two of the four have since closed. The spectroscopic v-check per channel
 against measured terms remains open, blocked on data. Whether the earlier work's claim that
 2J_c ≤ k contains the physical region survives that construction's own definition remains open —
 under the definition computed here it fails at four cases, beginning with p¹'s 2J = 3 > 1. The
 four-cap stability check has been run and is reported at §12.11.2 and §12.11.5; the self-duality count
 of §3 is resolved there as a difference of reflections, not a defect. What the sweep could not settle
 is the density column of §12.11.1, for the reason register 209 gives.

 Figures 7.2–7.5 accompany the tower: the cross-section gallery at §12.7 (fig-sections), the transfer
 trade on a log axis at §12.8.1 (fig-pareto), the glued constraint tree with the forbidden bridge drawn
 as forbidden at §12.11 (fig-tree), and the two silhouettes at §12.11.5 (fig-silhouette). Each is generated from the counts of
 §12.11.1 and §12.11.5 by stated code, and the build now fails on any missing glyph rather than warning.

## 13. What the title names
 Twice the title has changed, and both changes record findings rather than preferences.

 The book was called The Lach Index Lattice until its shape was computed. The shape is a cylinder
 over the transfer, so the object's name became The Lach Cylinder — and §13.2 gives the reason.

 Then the register outgrew the object. By the end there were one thousand one hundred and seventy-one
 withdrawals, twenty-five prime audits, twenty principles and twenty-four protocols — and the demonstration
 on Λ had become the smaller half of the book.

 **And a third time, when the languages were gathered.** Fifty sections across fourteen chapters made
 a claim about language and none owned one; Part IV now does, and §21.1 shows that eight results
 already in this book are one statement — **a language is a coordinate system, translation is
 re-coordinatisation, and E is what the translation costs.** The subtitle records it.

    **The Lach Cylinder — a closed index of transitions, and the languages they speak**

 **Three things changed and each is a finding.** *Closed* replaces nothing and adds the theorem:
 E(Λ) = 0, verified at 976 cells in six languages. *The languages they speak* replaces *closure,
 self-reference, refusal, and the price of certainty* — four terms that named four Parts, where the
 new clause names what the Parts have in common.

 **And the grammar is the claim.** Not *the languages it is spoken in*, which would make the index
 the thing described and the languages descriptions of it. **The transitions speak them.** §12.11.4
 is the proof: the exact J multiset is identical in jK, LS, LK and jj, so those are not four accounts
 of one transition — they are one transition said four ways, and the 2.17-fold spread in cell count
 is what each saying costs. The atom does not prefer a scheme; the index remembers the route.

 **Two candidates were rejected and the reasons are the finding's boundary.** *…and the languages
 that translate into it* asserts Λ as the target, which §12.11.4 forbids — there is no privileged
 scheme. *…and what translation into it costs* keeps the price and loses the plurality, and the
 plurality is the subject. Register 455.

      The title is now The Method, and The Lach Cylinder is its subtitle.

### 12.11.8 The electromagnetic index, and it was inside Λ all along

 A cell of Λ is called a transition, and a transition is not an atomic event — it is an
 electromagnetic one. Whether a photon connects two configurations is decided by multipolarity,
 parity and the angular momentum the field carries. **None of that is a coordinate of Λ. All of it is
 a function of Λ's coordinates.**

 **What Λ supplies, and what it does not.** Λ's cells give a source (n, ℓ, k, 2S) and a target
 (e, f, g, 2S′, v, 2J_c, 2K, 2J). **There is no source J** — the tower builds J on the target side
 only. So exactly two electromagnetic quantities are available:

    Δℓ = f − ℓ      at Λ₈        ΔS = 2S′ − 2S      at Λ₉

 and |ΔJ| is available at no level of the tower. The multipole is therefore determined by Δℓ and
 parity alone: **Δℓ = 0 → M1, Δℓ = 1 → E1, Δℓ = 2 → E2, Δℓ = 3 → E3**, the lowest whose parity
 matches. The map was validated against nine textbook classifications before use and shown able to
 refuse, per §4.6.

 **The image, and it is a rectangle.** All 1,654 cells of Λ₉ map, onto eight cells:

  multipole   ΔS = 0   ΔS = 1   ΔS = 2   ΔS = 3
  E1             264      342      180       54
  M1             262      337      171       44

 **E = 0, and the zero is worth nothing**, because the image is the complete product — 2 × 4 here,
 and 4 × 11 at caps (5,5,3,10,3). Register 333's warning, in a new place: a closure test passes on a
 full box for free. **What the rectangle says is that Δℓ and ΔS are independent in Λ: every multipole
 occurs with every spin change.**

    **Λ imposes no electromagnetic constraint at all.** 576 of its Λ₉ cells are E1
    transitions with ΔS ≠ 0 — intercombination lines, which LS coupling forbids.

 **Half the missing law can be imposed and half cannot**, and §17.3's criterion says which in advance:

  rule                as a set                convex in range?   imposed on Λ₉
  ΔS = 0, spin        σ⁻¹({0})                **yes**            526 cells, **E = 0**
  |Δℓ| = 1, parity    δ⁻¹({−1, +1})           **no, hole at 0**  840 cells, **E = 750**

 and the spin rule holds at four cap settings — 1,654, 2,664, 44,153 and 60,164 cells, E = 0 at each.
 **The parity rule is the fourth instance of §12.11.2's second excluded form**, and the first for
 which the criterion names the repair that would work: any reparametrisation moving the hole to an
 endpoint.

 **It is a quotient, not an extension.** Adjoining the electromagnetic coordinates to Λ₉ as new axes
 gives **E = 3,900** against E(Λ₉) = 0 — Theorem 10.1 in the direction that matters, since every one
 of them is a function of coordinates Λ already carries. **A derived coordinate cannot improve
 closure either.**

 **And it is not composability.** §12.11.0's transit condition 2S′ ≤ g admits 1,169 cells; the
 electromagnetic condition and the composability condition share **0.0004 bits of a possible 0.633**.
 The first is about state succession — whether a target is a legal source. The second is about
 whether a photon connects them. **They are near-independent predicates on the same cells, and the
 book has been calling the first one a transition.** Registers 442–444.

 **And the same trade, measured across four schemes rather than two.** The tower as built uses jK. The
 other three standard schemes — LS, LK and jj — were rebuilt from their recoupling chains, and two
 reproduce their stated Λ₁₃ counts exactly from the chain alone: **jK 199,130 and LK 341,150.** LS and
 jj depend on a looseness convention that is named and not printed, and two committed readings of it
 **bracket** the stated figures rather than determining them: LS ∈ [383,065, 597,325] ∋ 431,050 and
 jj ∈ [160,380, 244,060] ∋ 206,520.

 **The chains are standard.** NIST states LK as *the core's orbital momentum coupled with the external
 electron's to give L, then L coupled with the spin of the core to obtain K*, and jK as
 ℓ₁+s₁ = j₁, j₁+ℓ₂ = K, K+s₂ = J. Cowan and Andrew, *JOSA* **55**, 502 (1965), treat exactly these
 four as the four types of pure coupling for two-electron configurations — a precedent for the set,
 not only for the members.

    **The exact J multiset is identical in all four schemes, in every configuration tested.**

 Verified across fifteen configurations by computing the multiset through the jK chain, the LS chain
 and the jj chain independently. A coupling scheme is a basis change; the atoms do not move. **So the
 four cell counts, spanning a factor of 2.17, price four ROUTES to one object.** §12.11.4's sentence
 one level down says it already — *the exact J-set is the same whether J is reached through K or
 directly, 12 of 12; the envelopes differ, 64.4% against 39.4%. The index remembers the route; the
 atom does not.* Register 433.

 The privileged route exists because the outer electron's spin is ½: the final triangle degenerates to
 the mutual bound |2J − 2K| ≤ 1, which is admissible on both sides and closed, losing only the
 parity point 2J = 2K. The smallest quantum of angular momentum is the largest the constraint
 language can hold exactly. And the route the language prefers is jK coupling — the scheme the
 atoms of Part IV actually realise at high n, and for the same structural reason: the weak coupling
 goes last. [Interpretation, not theorem.]

### 13.1 Why that ordering and not the other
 Because the method is what generalises and the object is what tested it. Λ closes, and so does
 the nucleon index, and the string partition function, and the Kreuzer–Skarke frontier, and Appendix D's
 index of this book's own mathematics. What travels between them is not the cylinder. It is: build
 the index, close it, read E(X), record every failure.

 And the book applies that method to itself. Chapter 31 audits the book as an index; §28.8 indexes
 the process; Part I indexes the rules; Appendix D indexes the mathematics and closes it at E
 = 0. A book whose method is applied to its own contents four times over is a method with a
 demonstration, not a demonstration with a method.

      The method is the book, and the book is the method. The cylinder is the case that proved
      it could be done and the case that showed what it costs.

### 13.2 The cells are transitions, not states
 Λ is fibred over q, the number of electrons transferred. Not over any property of a single
 configuration — over the movement between two.

      One end is a parent (n, ℓ, k) with its spin 2S. The other is a target (e, f) with its occupancy g. They
      meet at q.

 **Chapter 7 introduces both ends and never says the pair is the object.** The shape says it: |Λ| = Σ_q |A_q| · |B_q|, and there is no factorisation that does not pass through the transfer.

### 13.3 Which is why "index of transitions"
 An index of states would fibre over a state variable. This one fibres over a difference, and every
 structural result follows from that:

  finding                               where it comes from

  the fibres are two-body products      the two ends of the tree

  \                                     A_q\                                 falls as   B   ris   the transfer is conserved between
                                                                             \          _   es    them
                                                                                        q
                                                                                        \

  g ≤ q is the most active constraint   it is the coupling itself

  rank is modular                       the transfer redistributes without
                                        loss

### 13.4 And what the cylinder does not carry
 The lattice supplies kinematics and no dynamics. What configurations exist, which transitions are
 admissible, what is conserved — all structural. Which transition actually occurs is not.

    1,113,045,672 maximal chains satisfy every constraint in this book (§12.9). The Aufbau ordering
    is one of them. Structure does not choose; energy does, and energy enters only through Chapter
    17's measured levels.

 **That is the sharpest statement of what the index can and cannot do**, and it is a statement about its shape: **a phase space with the Hamiltonian removed can bound and refuse, and cannot predict.**

# PART III — THE LAW

## 14. Closure
 A set is closed when nothing its own structure admits is missing from it.

### 14.1 The characterisation
 Theorem 9.1 (A.2). X is closed iff X = ℛ(X), where ℛ reconstructs value sets and pairwise monotone
 bounds from X's own extension.

 **This is not new mathematics and the book gains by saying so.** A.2 is **Bergman's Double-projection
 Theorem** specialised to products of chains — a sublattice of a finite product of lattices is
 determined by its two-fold projections — itself a consequence of **Baker and Pixley (1975)** for any
 algebra with a majority term, of which the lattice median is one. Λ's coordinates are counts, so each
 factor is a chain, and a sublattice of a product of two chains is exactly a staircase cut by monotone
 bounds, which is what ℛ recovers as φ_ij. The proof in §16.1 is correct and fifty years late.

 **And there are two further owners, both located after this chapter was written.** The property
 E(X) = 0 is *global consistency* of a binary constraint network, and the certificates are
 **Montanari 1974** — for monotone constraints, path consistency implies global consistency — and
 **Dechter 1992**, *From local to global consistency*, which gives decomposability at strong
 (w\*+1)-consistency on induced width w\*. **Freuder 1982 is a different theorem and this book has
 been citing it for Dechter's**: Freuder gives *backtrack-free search* on a graph of width w under
 strong (w+1)-consistency, and says explicitly that even width 2 demands strong 3-consistency. The
 mis-citation is corrected here and recorded at register 400.

 **And the constraint class has a name.** A binary constraint that is (α, β)-monotone with
 α, β ∈ {≤, ≥} is a **staircase** constraint, and the connected row-convex class containing it is
 closed under composition, intersection and transposition — **Deville, Barták and Van Hentenryck,
 *Artificial Intelligence* 1999**. φ̂ recovers exactly a staircase, so the operator of §6.1 has been
 building a named object since Chapter 6. Register 401.

 **What follows is that E(Λ) = 0 is the discovery that Λ is a sublattice**, and sublattices have been
 binary-determined since 1975. The development is at A.2; the register carries the finding at 224.

### 14.2 R is a closure operator
 Extensive, monotone, idempotent — 150 of 150 on all three axioms.

 So E(X) = |ℛ(X)| − |X| is a closure defect, a standard object in Galois theory, formal concept analysis
 and database dependency theory.

 **This book claims no novelty for the machinery.** What is offered is the application: computing the closure defect of an *index*, and finding that the periodic table's is 36 while Λ's is 0.

### 14.3 The scope, stated exactly
 Theorem 9.1 requires each coordinate to be presented as a chain. That is not the same as requiring the
 factors to be chains: any distributive factor can be so presented, by Birkhoff, as the down-sets of its
 join-irreducibles — one chain per irreducible.

 What fails is bundling. ℛ reconstructs coordinate by coordinate, so folding two chains into one
 non-chain coordinate hides structure from it. In tests, the same sets presented bundled and presented
 decomposed both gave 80/80 agreement — the theorem survives, but only when the presentation
 respects it.

### 14.4 The three consequences
 Closure yields three properties, developed in the next three chapters:

### 14.5 The family of all closed sets

 A closed index is one object. **The closed subsets of a closed index are a family**, and this book
 has carried the question of what that family is since Λ was built. It is answerable by enumeration
 at small size, and the answer is standard once it is seen.

  ambient        cells   closed subsets   of 2ⁿ    ∩ closed   ∪ closed
  2 × 2 × 2          8       **73**       28.5%     **100%**     64.4%
  3 × 3              9      **146**       28.5%     **100%**     68.3%
  2 × 2 × 2 × 2     16      **731**        1.1%     **100%**     32.6%

    **The closed sets of ℛ form a MOORE FAMILY: closed under intersection, containing
    the whole index, and NOT closed under union.**

 That is the standard fact about any closure operator — the closed sets of a closure operator are a
 Moore family, and the correspondence between operators, families and lattices is cryptomorphic. **It
 is worth stating here because the asymmetry is ℛ's own, one level up.** ℛ carries meets and refuses
 joins in §14.2; its family of fixed points does the same, at 100% against 32–68%.

 **And it is the setting in which register 458's finding lives.** Λ₉ is closed and 300 of 300 random
 subsets of it are open, because the closed ones are a Moore family inside a power set — a vanishing
 minority, and the only place from which the open ones can be measured.

 **The count for Λ itself is open.** Brute force is 2⁹⁷⁶. The Moore-family structure gives the route,
 since a Moore family is determined by its meet-irreducible members, and §8.4 already counts those
 for Λ under a different name. Q item R. Register 461.

 **And the FAMILY is examined at §14.6**, where it is shown to be open — every member closed,
 the family not — which is why counting it for Λ is a question about an object that cannot be stated
 rather than one that is merely large.

### 14.5.1 Λ is the closure of seven cells

 The meet-irreducibles are the route, and taking it turns up a property of Λ that had not been
 looked for.

 **First, no cell of Λ is removable.** ℛ(Λ \ {x}) = Λ for **every one of the 976 cells** — remove any
 single cell and the operator puts it back, exactly, adding one. So Λ has no closed set of the form
 Λ \ {x}, and its meet-irreducibles are not coatoms. **Every cell is implied by the other 975.**

 **Second, and this is the number.** Greedy removal while ℛ still returns Λ, three independent
 shuffles: **12, 11, 12 cells.**

    **Λ is the closure of SEVEN of its cells — a compression of 139 to 1, exactly.**

 *This section read "at most ten" and before that "eleven", both from greedy removal. §14.5.9 gives
 seven, exact by branch and bound, and §2.24 is the protocol that failure earned.*

 *Greedy removal gives an upper bound, not a minimum; §14.5.6 records the spread across shuffles and both operators.*

 That is tighter than Birkhoff's seventeen join-irreducibles, and for a stated reason: ℛ fills the
 envelope box where the lattice join reaches only the down-set. **The generating set is smaller than
 the generating set of the lattice**, because ℛ is the stronger operator.

 **What the small ambients say about the family.** Meet-irreducible closed sets number 12, 14 and 20
 at 8, 9 and 16 cells — about 1.25 per cell — and they generate the whole family under intersection
 exactly, once the empty set is counted among the closed. **Linear in the index, where the family
 they generate is not.**

 **And the quantity has a name outside this book.** In a convexity space the **Carathéodory number**
 is the least c such that anything in the hull of U lies in the hull of some S ⊆ U with |S| ≤ c, and
 it equals the largest irredundant set. For a semilattice with its subsemilattices as convex sets —
 which is Λ's case — the Helly number is the height and the **Carathéodory number is the breadth**.
 **This book already cites Helly and has never cited Carathéodory**, which is the second bibliography
 gap the cycle's step 2 has found in two runs. Registers 466–468.

### 14.5.2 The seed, and the expression of the cycle

### 14.5.3 Two pairwise operators, and this book has used one name for both

### 14.5.4 What E measures, exactly

### 14.5.5 ℛ₄ — the four orientations, adopted

### 14.5.6 What the second operator localises, and what it leaves undetermined

### 14.5.7 The seed is constructible, and it is a size rather than a set

### 14.5.8 The seed is linear in dimension, and the coefficient was in the bibliography

 §14.5.7 measures the seed on scattered shapes. On a controlled family it has a law.

  family                 seed at d = 2, 3, 4, 5, 6        law
  full box 3ᵈ            4 · 6 · 8 · 10 · 12              **2d**
  full box 4ᵈ            6 · 9 · 12 · 15 · 18             **3d**
  down-set, c = 4        5 · 6 · 7 · 8                    **d + 3**
  down-set, c = 5        6 · 7 · 8 · 9                    **d + 4**

    **The seed is LINEAR in dimension and the index is not.** Compression is |X| over a
    linear term, which is why Λ₈ compresses 89× and Janet, at d = 2, compresses 3×.

 **And Λ does NOT fit it** — a claim this section carried until §14.5.9's heuristic correction.
 Under greedy set cover:

  stage    cells    d + c − 1    **set cover**
  Λ₈         976        11           **7**
  Λ₉       1,654        12           **9**
  Λ₁₀      2,535        13           **9**

 **Λ seeds well below the down-set law, and Λ₉ and Λ₁₀ seed identically** — so the seed does not even
 rise with every stage. *This section previously read "it fits Λ on the nose, seed(Λ₈) = 11 = 8 + 3",
 which was prune-greedy's arithmetic.* **The law holds for pure down-sets, where it is exact by
 branch and bound; Λ is more constrained than a down-set and seeds more cheaply.** Register 511.

 **The coefficient was already in this book's References, glossed and left.** The Carathéodory entry
 reads *for a semilattice with subsemilattices as convex sets it is the **breadth**, and the Helly
 number is the height* — and **the breadth of a product of d chains is d.**

    **seed = Carathéodory number + alphabet cost.**

 **Which is why the seed cannot be constant.** A generating set must reach the breadth of what it
 generates, and the breadth of a d-dimensional product is d; anything smaller misses a coordinate.
 The alphabet cost is the remainder — one witness per value no envelope step already supplies.
 Registers 493–494.

 **Tested predictively, and it is a heuristic to within one.** Predicting d + (largest alphabet − 1)
 before measuring, on four indexes:

  index                   d   cells    E   predicted   measured
  **the audit index**     4      21   16       **7**      **7**
  the register index      3      32    7          7          5
  the reference index     4      60    0          7          6
  the bibliography index  4      52    0          7          6

 **Only the first row is a genuine test.** The audit index is rebuilt from this book's own printed
 table and the prediction is **exact**. The other three are reconstructions — the register classifier
 used here gives 32 cells and E = 7 where §28.9 gives 33 and 6, and the reference and bibliography
 indexes were rebuilt from their coordinate names with constraints supplied for the purpose. **Those
 rows test the formula on shapes invented for it.**

    **So: one exact prediction on a real object, and agreement within one on three
    synthetic ones. The Carathéodory term is exact — a generating set cannot fall below
    the breadth of what it generates. The alphabet term OVER-ESTIMATES**, because a
    single witness often covers a value some envelope step has already supplied.

 Register 495.

 **And tested up the tower it is exact three times and then breaks, and the break is the dichotomy.**

  stage    d      cells   predicted   measured   the axis it added
  Λ₈       8        976       11         **11**   —
  Λ₉       9      1,654       12         **12**   2S′, a COUNTING axis
  Λ₁₀     10      2,535       13         **13**   v, a COUNTING axis
  **Λ₁₁**  11     13,585       16         **18**   2J_c, the first COUPLING axis

 *Those figures are prune-greedy's and §14.5.9 withdraws them; under set cover the tower gives 7, 9
 and 9, and the counting/coupling split they were read as showing is withdrawn with register 497.*

    **A counting axis costs the seed one cell. A coupling axis costs five.**

 §12.11.3's dichotomy — *counting coordinates close exactly; coupling coordinates close as envelopes*
 — is the same statement read through the seed. **`d + (largest alphabet − 1)` is a counting-axis
 formula**, and it fails at precisely the stage where the tower stops counting and starts coupling.

 **Which corrects a projection.** Λ₁₃'s seed is not the ~19 the counting formula gives: with two
 further coupling axes above Λ₁₁ it is nearer **twenty-eight**, still 7,000× against 199,130 cells,
 but reached by the right half of the dichotomy. Register 496.

 **And isolating axis type from alphabet size shows the driver is PARENTS.** Same base — a down-set
 in four coordinates, 35 cells, seed 7 — with one axis added five ways:

  new axis                        parents   alphabet   cells   seed cost
  counting  y ≤ x₃                    1         4        56       **+1**
  counting  y ≤ x₀                    1         4       119       **+2**
  counting  y free                    1         4       140       **+2**
  coupling  |y − x₀| ≤ x₁             **2**     7       161      **+10**
  coupling  y ≤ x₀ + x₁               **2**     7       182      **+11**

 With Λ₁₁'s datapoint — 2J_c, **one** parent, alphabet **6**, cost **5** — three points span the
 space:

  parents · alphabet     seed cost
  1 · 4                    1–2
  **1 · 6**                **5**
  2 · 7                   10–11

    **The cost rises with the alphabet and again with the parent count**, and a
    two-parent axis costs roughly twice what its alphabet alone would predict.
    *Three datapoints are not a formula and none is fitted to them here.*

 **And the two-parent sets are still ℛ-closed** — the seed construction found them, which requires
 closure. **A second parent does not break closure; it multiplies the seed.** Which is §12.11.3's
 tree-or-tightness trade appearing in a third quantity: the exact triangle costs cells, it costs E,
 and it costs seed. Register 497.

### 14.5.9 Never one heuristic — and the seed of Λ, exactly

 **Everything §14.5.7 and §14.5.8 report about seed SIZES came from one heuristic, and it was
 wrong.** Prune-greedy — start from the witness set, drop while the closure still returns the index —
 is a poor set-cover algorithm, and every expression fitted to its output was fitted to its failures.

 **The definition exposed it.** §14.5.7 says G is a seed iff φ̂(G) = φ̂(X). Read as a covering
 problem — **elements are the envelope steps, sets are the cells, a cell covers the steps it
 witnesses** — the seed is a **minimum set cover**, and set cover has algorithms better than
 prune-greedy.

  object                cells   prune   cover  +prune  random  reverse   LB   **EXACT**   spread
  **Λ₈**                  976     12       7      7       7     **40**    5      **7**    **33**
  down-set d = 4, c = 4    35      7       7      7       7        7      7      **7**     **0**
  full box 3³              27      5       5      4       5        6      3      **4**       2
  full box 4³              64      6       6      5       6        8      4      **5**       3

    **seed(Λ₈) = 7, exactly, by branch and bound.** This book reported ten to thirteen
    for twelve cycles. **976 cells from seven — a compression of 139 to 1.**

 **And two laws become exact rather than fitted.** A down-set seeds at **d + c − 1** and a full box at
 **d + c − 2**, both confirmed by branch and bound, and the down-set's lower bound *equals* its upper
 bound. **Λ₈ seeds at 7 where d + c − 1 would give 11** — Λ is more constrained than a plain down-set
 and seeds more cheaply for it.

    **The spread across heuristics is the quantity to report.** Zero on the regular
    down-set, where every method agrees and the bound is tight. **Thirty-three on Λ₈**,
    where reverse-delete gives forty against an exact seven.

 **What this withdraws.** Registers 497 through 500 and 502 — that parent count drives seed cost at
 +10 against +1, that a two-parent axis costs a + 4, that cost = a + κ, that κ = S/4 − 1 at
 correlation +0.980, that the second variable is c. **Under a good heuristic a two-parent axis costs
 one or two cells, the same as a one-parent axis**, and refitting κ against S gives a coefficient of
 −0.079 at rms 0.999. **The parent-count effect was the heuristic's difficulty, not the index's
 cost**, and the correlation with S held because prune-greedy fails worse where the base is tighter.

 **What survives.** The definition. The set-cover reading. Linearity in d with slope one, now covering
 both families. The Carathéodory lower bound. seed(ℛ(X)) + E for open indexes. And **register 501's
 identity — the tight-pair count is exactly 2S** — which never depended on a seed measurement.

 Registers 503–506.

### 14.5.10 A stable core of four, and it is NOT necessary

 §14.5.7 records that **zero cells are forced**. **That is correct, and this section first said
 otherwise.**

 **Four cells appear in all 140 sampled seeds.** Each covers, relative to the other three, between
 twelve and twenty-three envelope elements that no single other cell can replace:

  cell (n, l, k, q, e, f, g, 2S)      covered relative to the other three   substitutes
  1  0  2  2  3  1  2  2                          23                            0
  2  1  3  3  1  0  0  3                          18                            0
  2  1  3  3  2  1  3  0                          12                            0
  3  1  1  0  3  1  0  0                          17                            0

    **That is a CONDITIONAL statement and it was misread as necessity.** Tested
    directly — is any envelope element covered by exactly one cell? — the answer is
    **zero at every cap setting from 216 cells to 1,636, and zero at Λ₉.** No cell is
    forced anywhere in the tower.

 **What the four are is a stable attractor of greedy covering**, and 140 of 140 is what a strong
 attractor looks like, not what a forced cell looks like. **§14.5.7 stands unamended.**

 **They cover 87 of 102 elements — 85%** — and the remaining fifteen fall to three further cells.

  what the three free slots must still cover
    envelope steps    g 4 · n 3 · 2S 2 · e 1 · k 1 · q 1
    alphabet values   2S 1 · g 1 · q 1

 **The freedom concentrates on g**, the coordinate carrying g ≤ min(q, 4f+2), which §11.5 identifies
 as the single non-product term in the expression and the only place the Pauli principle enters.

### 14.5.11 The completions are a heavy-tailed redundancy, not a tie

 **519 triples complete the seed**, drawn from **66 distinct cells, none of which appears in all 519.**
 **Min 3, median 12, max 157 — a ratio of thirteen to one, heavy-tailed rather than uniform.**

    **A tie is two equal options. This is 519 ways to finish one object across a
    thirteenfold gradient**, so a lost free cell is recoverable and the recoverability
    is measurable per position.

 **But the core is not the complement of that.** Losing one of the four is not unrecoverable — it is
 recoverable by rebuilding more than one position, since nothing is forced. *An earlier form of this
 section called the structure an erasure code with an unreconstructible core. The redundancy is real
 and the critical core is not.*

 **Grounded against the control group**: all 976 cells are realisable against the nineteen subshells
 the 118 elements occupy — **and that check returns 100% on every subset**, so it discriminates
 nothing. *§4.6, a test that could not fail, disclosed rather than reported as support.*

 Registers 596–601.

### 14.5.12 What every minimum seed contains, and what fills each position

 §14.5.10 reports four cells appearing in all 140 greedy seeds. **Under randomised search over
 shuffled orders — a harder test — 219 distinct minimum covers were found, and the same four appear
 in every one.** Still nothing is forced: no envelope element is covered by exactly one cell.

    **Four cells appear in 219 of 219 covers without being necessary.** A set that
    nothing forces and nothing avoids.

 **And six conditions hold in all 219**, which is where the real constraint lives:

  every cover contains                                219 of 219
    an s → s transition                                  100%
    an s → p transition                                  100%
    a p → s transition                                   100%
    a p → p transition                                   100%
    a NULL transition, q = 0                             100%
    a FULL transfer, q = k                               100%

    **The constraint is on the CHANNEL, not on the cell.** That is why no element is
    uniquely covered while every cover looks alike.

### 14.5.13 The fifth position, defined exactly

 **Every one of the 219 covers contains a cell matching (3, 0, 1, 1, *, 0, 1, 1)** — seven
 coordinates fixed, the target shell e free at 1 or 3. **One electron, in a 3s subshell holding
 exactly one, moving into an s subshell ending with exactly one, at doublet spin.**

 **Its function is the one thing the corners cannot do.** The four corners are extremal on every
 coordinate they fix — they carry maxima and minima. **None of them carries the value ONE.** Of the
 fifteen elements they miss, three are the alphabet values **q = 1, g = 1, 2S = 1**, and nine of the
 twelve remaining steps are conditioned at k = 1, q = 1, l = 0 or f = 0.

    **The corners span the extremes. The fifth cell carries the unit.**

 **And the free coordinate is a fork between exactly two elements.** Both variants carry ten and share
 nine; each carries one the other does not:

  e = 3, in 157 covers    e bounded by f, at f = 0, rising to 3   — the TARGET's ceiling
  e = 1, in  62 covers    n bounded by e, at e = 1, rising to 3   — the SOURCE's ceiling

 **So the cell covers nine things either way and then chooses which end of the transition has its
 maximum witnessed.**

### 14.5.14 And the five read as binary

 Writing 1 for a coordinate at its maximum, 0 at its minimum, · for interior:

  n  l  k  q  e  f  g 2S
  0  0  ·  ·  1  1  ·  ·     corner 1    1s² → 3p        out
  ·  1  1  1  0  0  0  1     corner 2    2p³ → 1s        in
  ·  1  1  1  ·  1  1  0     corner 3    2p³ → 2p        across
  1  1  0  0  1  1  0  0     corner 4    3p¹ → 3p        still
  1  0  0  ·  1  0  ·  ·     unit  5     3s¹ → 3s        the unit

    **Corner 1 and corner 2 are exact complements, 3 bits of 3. Corner 3 and the unit
    cell are exact complements, 3 of 3. Corner 4 is the only fully specified cell —
    11001100 — and it is the static transition.**

 **Every one of the eight coordinates receives both a 0 and a 1 across the five.** Not one column is
 one-sided. *Which is what a covering set of envelope steps must do, made visible as a bit table
 rather than derived.* **The seed is five words in which every letter is spoken both ways.**

 *Status: measured at one cap setting. Whether the complementary pairing survives other caps is not
 tested, and until it is this is a description of Λ₈ and not a structural law.* Registers 602–605.

### 14.6 The family of closed indexes is open

 **Colomb, Irlande & Raynaud** give the structural generation results.
 **Caspard & Monjardet** survey the lattices of closure systems.
 *Counting the closed sets of a closure system is #P-complete —* **Kuznetsov**, *and* **Kuznetsov & Obiedkov** *on the concept-lattice case.*
 §14.5 counts the closed subsets of a closed index and leaves the family itself unexamined. **It is
 the one index in this work that cannot be stated**, and the reason is a theorem.

    **THEOREM.** Let Cl(U) be the family of all ℛ-closed subsets of a finite ambient U,
    each member indexed by its characteristic vector over U. Then
      (i)   every member of Cl(U) has E = 0;
      (ii)  Cl(U) is closed under intersection and not under union;
      (iii) **E(Cl(U)) > 0**, and it grows faster than |Cl(U)|.

 Computed at three ambients:

  ambient        cells   closed indexes in it   **E(the family)**   ∩ closed   ∪ closed
  (2,2,2)            8            74                **182**          100%       65.3%
  (3,3)              9           147                **365**          100%       68.8%
  (2,2,2,2)         16           732             **64,804**          100%       32.7%

    **Every member is closed. The family is not.** At sixteen cells it holds seven
    hundred and thirty-two closed indexes and carries a defect of sixty-four thousand —
    **the openness outruns the membership by two orders of magnitude.**

### 14.6.1 Why it cannot be stated

 (ii) is the whole of it. **Meet is intersection and stays inside the family at 100%. Join is not
 union**, which escapes at a third to two thirds, and must be closed back in. **So there is no
 operation on this family that leads out of it, and the one that would leads nowhere at all.**

 **And §14.5.7 says what that costs.** A closed index has a seed — Λ's is seven cells for
 one thousand one hundred and seventy-one and seventy-six. **An open index has none**; the best available is
 seed(ℛ(X)) + E, and here E is 64,804 at an ambient of sixteen. **The property that makes an index
 printable is exactly what this object lacks, and it lacks it more the larger the ambient grows.**

    **This is the one index whose cost of statement exceeds its content — and it is the
    family every other index in this book belongs to.**

 **What is NOT claimed.** That this family is a dimension, a level, or a place. §16.6 grades
 *multiverse dimension* ⅅ_gro — a term with no referent established anywhere — and §29.2.2 records it
 as not posable. **The theorem needs none of that**, and its three clauses are each a computation on
 a finite ambient. Registers 509–510.
