    ⅅ_def ≥ 1 — two disjoint derivations survive a computational error ρ ≥ 2 — two disjoint sources
    survive an access failure

 **Same structure. Different failure mode.**

### 19.2 A paywall blocks a source, not a cell
 Worked case. The target was a transition frequency in antiprotonic helium.

| source | status |
|---|---|
| *Nature* **475**, 484 (2011) | **paywalled** |
| *Phys. Rev. Lett.* **96**, 243401 (2006) | **paywalled** |
| arXiv:1304.4330 | **open** — the *Nature* paper entire |
| **arXiv:1203.5425 (CODATA 2010)** | **open — Table XII, all fifteen frequencies, both papers** |
| arXiv:1308.1711, an unrelated citing paper | **open** — corroborates to 0.1 MHz |
| CERN CDS | open |

 **ρ = 6. Two blocked, four open.** The cell was retrieved and the two-path check on it closed at 0.09σ.

 Figure 14.1. Retrieval redundancy for five target cells. The 2011 antiprotonic-helium frequency had six routes, two
 blocked. The four German documents have one or two apiece, all closed.

### 19.3 The secondary can be better than the primary
 CODATA's Table XII carries both papers with an extra digit the journals did not print, supplied
 privately by the experimenters to reduce rounding error.

    The index does not merely route around an obstruction. It sometimes finds a better cell
    than the one that was blocked.

### 19.4 The retrieval graph is self-referencing

 Each fetched compilation names its successors. The aluminium compilation's introduction lists its own sister volumes verbatim; the beryllium compilation cites the zinc one; the aluminium reference list supplies nickel and copper.

 **Three coverage gaps in this work's census were filled from documents already in hand, with no new search.**

### 19.5 Where it fails, and the number that describes it
 Four documents in this work were not retrieved: Edlén's Handbuch chapter (1964), Ritz's Physikalische
 Zeitschrift paper (1908), Paschen & Götze (1922), Dunz (1911). ρ ≤ 2 for each, every route closed.

 Not for lack of effort — nine searches, correct citations, the right platform identified and free. **Pre-digital print has low retrieval redundancy.**

    ρ is a property of the literature, not of the searcher. A cell in a 1908 German periodical
    has ρ ≈ 1. A cell in a 2011 physics paper has ρ ≈ 6.

 **A century of open deposition raised ρ roughly sixfold**, and that is exactly what separated the two outcomes.

### 19.5.1 ρ is a property of (document, route), and this chapter collapsed the second index

 §19.5 records four documents as unretrieved at **ρ ≤ 2, every route closed**, and draws a law: *ρ is a
 property of the literature, not of the searcher.* **Both are wrong, and the coordinate that shows it
 is printed in §19.6 below.** Step 2 lists seven route types — primary, preprint, review, compilation,
 citing paper, deposit, database. **ρ counts them and loses which.**

  document                       primary  review  citing  compil.  deposit    ρ
  **Paschen & Götze 1922**          0       1       1       ·        1      **3**
  **Ritz Phys. Zeitschr. 1908**     0       ·       1       1        1      **3**
  **Edlén Handbuch 1964**           0       1       1       ·        ·      **2**
  Dunz 1911                         0       0       0       ·        0        0

    **Three of the four are retrievable and §19.5 recorded all four closed.** Ritz's
    p. 521 paper is reprinted in the **Œuvres**, published for the Swiss Physical Society, which
    is digitised in full on **Gallica** at ark:/12148/bpt6k33824 and on the Internet
    Archive — and a third party's page mapping confirms the book's own estimate, giving
    *Über die Spektren der Alkalien*, Phys. Zeitschr. 9 (1908) 244–245 at Œuvres
    137–140, immediately before the range §29 wants.

    **Dunz is the only one where the fibre is empty at every point tried** — and it is
    the one whose citation carries no initial and places a series-laws dissertation at
    Leipzig, outside the Tübingen lineage from which every other early dissertation in
    this literature comes.

    **Two of the four have now been tested on the routes §19.5 did not try, and both
    refute the record.** Edlén's review route is *Bengt Edlén's Handbuch der Physik
    Article — 26 Years Later*, a paper about the chapter's own methods for recognising
    precise regularities in atomic spectra — **which is the exact question §29.5 asks
    of that chapter.** Its citing-paper route carries the full reference in Springer
    chapter bibliographies, in NIST's compendium and in Europhysics News.

    **The review route pays twice.** It opened Paschen & Götze and it opened Edlén.
    §19.6 lists it third of seven; §19.5 used it for neither.

    **Paschen & Götze 1922 is retrievable on three routes.** A Springer Book Archives
    chapter DOI — 10.1007/978-3-642-99147-9_1 for §III, _2 for the tables — and a
    HathiTrust scan from the University of California Libraries. **§19.5 tried the
    primary route, which is the one column where it is not.**

 **So the law is refuted by its own instance.** The same 1922 monograph gives ρ ≈ 0 asked as a text
 string and ρ ≥ 2 asked as a catalogue entry. **ρ behaved as a property of the query, not of the
 literature** — and the difference is register 553's: a source has an index, and it is rarely the one
 with a search box.

 **And the deeper fault is that a scalar cannot distinguish *closed* from *untried*.** Twenty-seven of
 the forty-nine cells in the table above were never tried, and ρ recorded them at the same value as
 the empty ones. **Edlén and Ritz are UNKNOWN, not closed.**

    **This is §21.5's obstruction committed in the chapter about retrieval**: a count
    printed where a coordinate was needed, and the coordinate printed one section later
    in the procedure that produced the count.

 Registers 566–568.

### 19.5.2 And the book states two incompatible things about Dunz

 §19.5 lists Dunz 1911 among four documents **not retrieved**. The References entry reads *Dunz, —
 Seriengesetze der Linienspektra (Leipzig, 1911)*, and adds **retrieved as a scan, unread**. **One of these is
 false and the book carries both.** Register 265 records that the Dunz entry was absent from the
 References until audit 7 caught it, so the reference is the later and hastier text.

 **The entry also carries an em-dash where an initial belongs** — *Dunz, —* — which is why a title
 search cannot confirm the volume exists. **A citation missing its author's initial is not a citation
 that has been checked.** Register 569.

### 19.5.3 The route is a fibre, and §19.1 wrote it without using it

 **§19.1 defines R(c) = { i : c ∈ Sᵢ }. That set is a fibre over the cell: the route varies and the
 cell does not.** ρ = |R(c)| is its cardinality reported as a scalar.

    **This is why a redirect does not disturb the process.** A route failing costs a
    route and not a cell — which is §19.1's own *ρ ≥ 2 survives the loss of any single
    source*, stated for the count and true of the structure.

 **The retrieval index, built over age · language · access and fibred by route, closes everywhere:**

  route fibre    cells   E        route fibre    cells   E
  primary          3     **0**    compilation      2     **0**
  review           4     **0**    deposit          3     **0**
  citing           4     **0**    preprint         1     **0**
  **pooled**       6     **0**

 **E = 0 fibred and unfibred.** The envelopes find nothing to admit because **the route is a free
 coordinate** — moving along it does not move the cell.

 **And it explains §19.3.** The secondary can beat the primary because **the fibre is over the cell,
 not over the source's quality**; nothing requires the primary to be the best point in it. CODATA's
 extra digit is not an anomaly but the ordinary case.

    **A cardinality cannot say which fibre a document was tried in.** *Tried once and
    empty* and *never tried* take the same ρ, and §19.5 drew a law from their union.

 Registers 579–581.

### 19.6 The procedure
  1. Enumerate target cells from the index before searching.
  2. List route candidates per cell — primary, preprint, review, compilation, citing paper, deposit,
     database.
  3. Prefer high-ρ cells; they cost less and often carry more.
  4. Read every retrieved source for its successors.
  5. When a route is blocked, move along the route set; do not re-attempt.
  6. Record ρ for cells that fail. A blocked ρ = 1 cell is a stated gap, not an unexplained absence.
 The author violated step 1 badly, on his own problem, after writing this chapter. That is recorded in
 Chapter 28.

# PART IV — THE LANGUAGES

## 20. The languages, and what each one can close

 Fifty sections of this book make a claim about language and no chapter owns one. This chapter
 owns them, and §21 states what they have in common.

### 20.1 A language is a coordinate system

 Every question in this work is posed in some vocabulary, and the vocabulary decides what can be
 said without loss. That is P20, and it is promoted here from a principle to the subject, because
 the results scattered through Parts II, III and V are one result about it.

    **The language a question is posed in bounds whether it can close, and at what cost.**

 Chapter 1's table gives five languages with their closure mechanisms and their measured costs — tree
 factorisation at 0.00018 s against Gröbner at 0.010 s, a factor of fifty-five. **And one row has no
 timing, because it has no algorithm.** The documentary language — *has anyone said this before?* —
 carries no closure mechanism at all, which is why §29's precedent question can be bounded and never
 settled, and why §29.1's two searches returning nothing is evidence of a kind the other four
 languages never have to produce.

### 20.2 The languages this book uses

 **Six agree on Λ at 976 cells, and ten of their combinations hold** — order, geometry, arithmetic,
 calculus, logic and the constraint language of §16. That agreement is P21, and it is an instrument
 rather than a criterion: **the principle has located two errors by failing in one language while the
 others passed.**

 The languages are not decoration on one mathematics. Each closes by a different mechanism and each
 charges a different price, and §27 proves that where they measure the same thing they agree — E in
 order, the void in geometry, V in calculus, one quantity under three measures.

### 20.3 And a language can make a law monotone

 The clearest case is small. The electromagnetic selection rules include a parity condition, and
 written as a congruence — |Δℓ| ≡ γ (mod 2) — it is one of §12.11.2's three excluded forms and
 §14.4's operator cannot carry it: **E = 750**. Written in the standard spectroscopic naming, E1,
 M1, E2, M2, in which parity alternates with the multipole order, **the same law is monotone and
 E = 0.**

    **The notation was not a convenience. It was the coordinate that made the rule expressible.**

 §17.3's criterion says why in general: a difference is bracketed rather than determined, and
 convexity is what closure means for a bracketed quantity. **A congruence is excluded exactly when
 the set it defines has a hole in the attained range, and a language that moves the hole to an
 endpoint restores closure.**

## 21. Translation, and what it costs

### 21.1 One object, two namings, and E is the price

 Eight results in this book have the same shape and have never been put beside one another.

  one object                    first naming                    E        second naming                  E
  the 118 elements              period × group                  **36**   n + ℓ × position (Janet)       **0**
  the 365 days                  months as drawn                 **7**    months by length               **0**
  Λ₉'s parity rule              |Δℓ| = 1, a congruence          **750**  the convex band |Δℓ| ≤ 1       **0**
  the audit set                 four coordinates                **16**   three coordinates              **0**
  this book's own cells         chapter resolution              **578**  part resolution                **0**
  the same J multiset           jK 199,130 · LK 341,150 · LS 431,050 · jj 206,520 — four routes, one atom
  slack                         E in order · the void in geometry · V in calculus — one quantity
  Λ at 976                      six languages agree; ten combinations hold

    **A language is a coordinate system. Translation is re-coordinatisation. E is what
    the translation costs.**

### 21.2 The certificate is a translation, exhibited and checked

 §18.4.1's law of realised closure reads, in this vocabulary: **a thing is sayable without loss
 exactly when a translation into a closed language exists, and the certificate is the translation.**
 That is why the certificate must be *exhibited* rather than searched for — §3.7 already gives the
 asymmetry, 362 comparisons against eight and a half million — and why §16.7.1's removability test
 is the same test: E measures the drawing when a translation exists and the world when none does.

### 21.3 The three ways a translation fails

 §16.6's defences are translation failures, and naming them that way is what separates them:

  ⅅ_ref    no referent has been established           not runnable — establishing one requires reading
  ⅅ_dict   drift from a referent already established  runnable, given the referent
  ⅅ_gro    no referent exists at all                  invisible to any audit over the text

 **The reference index of §16.6.1 is a translation audit**, and its verdict coordinate sits below the
 ⅅ ≥ 1 floor the rest of that chapter stands on. *q* in Λ is the ⅅ_gro case: a term with no referent,
 carried through thirteen coordinates and never grounded.

### 21.4 What this does not claim

 **Every number above is already proved elsewhere in this book.** Nothing here is a new result; what
 is new is that they are one statement. By register 326's precedent that is a case for strengthening
 an existing statement rather than naming a new law, and no new law is named. **The claim is that
 the method is the mechanism by which these languages speak to each other** — and the mechanism is
 ℛ, whose fixed points are the sayable-without-loss and whose defect is the price of saying it
 otherwise. Registers 451–453.

### 21.5 The missing ingredient is a translation, not a fact

 Three results in the companion work and this one cannot be reproduced, and audit 21 calls all three
 the same failure: **a result stated from data the book does not print.** They are the same failure,
 and naming the language makes the repair one line rather than three.

  object                    what IS printed                     what is NOT
  the violation index       2,370 cells of a box of 19,440      the exclusion classifier's EDGE LIST
  the coupling schemes      199,130 · 341,150 · 431,050 · 206,520   the LOOSENESS CONVENTION
  the collection            1,442 cells over ~190 channels      WHICH cells are independent

 **In every case what is printed is a COUNT and what is withheld is a CONSTRAINT.** And §14.5.7 shows
 a constraint need not be printed at all:

    **ℛ is determined by φ̂, and φ̂ is determined by the SEED — the cells witnessing
    each envelope step. A seed is a list of cells. A cell is the one thing every one
    of these papers already knows how to print.**

 **And a seed alone is not enough, because a seed reconstructs the CLOSURE and not the index.** Two
 of these three objects are open — the violation index at E = 30, the collection not an index of this
 kind at all — so the repair needs its second form:

    **print the seed of ℛ(X), and the E cells that ℛ(X) holds and X does not.**

 That is exact for every index tested — the audit index, the periodic table, the calendar and the
 parity rule all recover **exactly** — and its cost is **seed + E**:

  open index                cells     E   seed of ℛ(X)   printed   compression
  the audit index              21    16        7            23        **0.9×**
  the periodic table           90    36       19            55          1.6×
  the calendar as printed     365     7       31            38          9.6×
  the parity rule             840   750       13           763          1.1×

    **The repair pays exactly when E ≪ |X|.** The seed term is small at high dimension;
    **E is the binding term**, and where E is large the index is cheaper to print outright.

 **Both remaining obstructions qualify and by wide margins.** The violation index — 2,370 cells,
 E = 30, nine coordinates — needs a seed near fifteen, so **about forty-five cells for 2,370.** The coupling schemes are **closed at E = 0**, so the first form applies
 and a seed near twenty-five carries all 199,130.

 Λ's seed is **ten cells for one thousand one hundred and seventy-one and seventy-six.** The violation index's is bounded above
 by 216 from its rung counts alone and, at Λ's ratio, is likely nearer twenty for 2,370. **The
 missing ingredient is not information the authors lack. It is the same information in the other
 language.**

### 21.5.1 And the three-body count is exactly one

 §32.3 records that ℛ reaches **level 2** — pairwise consistency — while the object, a K₃ of
 treewidth 2, requires **strong 3-consistency** by Freuder. **The shortfall is exactly one level:
 pairs, plus one thing that pairs cannot carry.**

 **This book has exactly one genuinely ternary object, and it is the bracket.** T(n−1), T(n), T(n+1)
 — three consecutive members, and no pair of them determines the third. Which is why independence
 turned out triadic at §24.9, why surviving tests go as (1−f)³ and not (1−f), and why the bracket is
 the one place in this work where ℛ has never been the right operator.

 **And the four-body reading follows the same arithmetic.** Adding *reference* as a fourth node
 raises the requirement to strong 4-consistency, which is why §18.4.1 records the deficit as
 **doubling** rather than growing by one: the operator stayed at level 2 while the object moved from
 3 to 4. Registers 487–489.

### 21.5.2 The constraints, indexed — and a 3-body is what a tree forbids

 §21.5.1 counts the shortfall in levels of consistency. **Indexing the CONSTRAINTS rather than the
 cells says the same thing in the objects' own language**, and says it exactly.

 Λ's seven constraints are seven pairs, each with a multiplier and an offset:

  ℓ ≤ n − 1 · k ≤ 4ℓ + 2 · q ≤ k · f ≤ e − 1 · g ≤ 4f + 2 · g ≤ q · 2S ≤ k

 **Each constraint is pairwise, so three constraints closing on three variables is a 3-body.**
 Counted over every triple:

  triples spanning   3 nodes — a true 3-body      **0**
                     4 nodes                        7
                     5 nodes                       20
                     6 nodes                        8

    **Λ has no 3-body among its constraints — zero of thirty-five.** Its constraint graph
    is eight nodes and seven edges: a tree, and a tree is exactly a graph with no cycle.

 **And it is one edge away in exactly seven places.**

  add n — k → a 3-body on {n, ℓ, k}      add q — f  → on {q, g, f}
  add ℓ — q → on {ℓ, k, q}               add q — 2S → on {q, k, 2S}
  add ℓ — 2S → on {ℓ, k, 2S}             add e — g  → on {e, f, g}
  add k — g → on {k, q, g}

 **Seven constraints, seven near-misses** — the edge count of a tree on eight nodes, with each
 existing edge sitting in exactly one triangle-completing position Λ declines. Fourteen of the
 twenty-one unused edges are safe. **And every one of the seven is a bond the physics does not make:
 n and k are related by no law, ℓ and 2S only through k.**

 **This is why ℛ works at all**, why Freuder's width bound applies, and why §21.5.1's shortfall is one
 level rather than two.

 Figure 21.1. Λ₁₃'s constraint graph. Twelve nodes, thirteen edges, **cycle rank 2, girth 5,
 treewidth 2, and no triangle at any stage.** Black edges are the tree; red edges lie on a cycle. The
 base Λ₈ is a caterpillar with **k** as its hub at degree 4; **each two-parent axis adds exactly one
 independent cycle** — Λ₁₀'s v, closing a 5-cycle and setting the girth, and Λ₁₂'s 2K, closing a
 6-cycle. The three leaves are the tower's two entries, n and e, and its exit, 2J. **Treewidth stays
 2 at Λ₁₃ despite the cycles**, so Freuder's bound still gives strong 3-consistency against ℛ's 2 —
 the shortfall of one level holds for the whole tower and not only for a K₃.

 **Two things are owed and stated rather than assumed.** The constraint index's own defect is E = 23
 at 7 cells in a box of 216 — **3.2% density, where §18.4.1's asymmetry says E measures sparsity and
 not structure** — so the twenty-three absent constraints are not read as predictions here. And the
 graph-theoretic vocabulary for all of this — **chordal, treewidth, tree decomposition, girth** — is
 absent from this book's References entirely, where the lattice side is cited: Adams, Dwinger and
 Schmid give *tight ⟺ the digraph is acyclic*, which is this result seen from the other language.
 Registers 507–508.

### 21.5.3 The shape, and the index rebuilt

 §21.5.2 gives a cycle count and no shape. **Λ₁₃'s constraint graph, measured:**

  nodes · edges       12 · 13          **girth**            **5**
  cycle rank          2                diameter · radius    6 · 3
  **treewidth**       **2**            hub                  **k**, degree 4
  degree sequence     4, 3, 3, 3, 2, 2, 2, 2, 2, 1, 1, 1
  leaves              2J · e · n — the two entries and the exit

    **Treewidth stays 2 at Λ₁₃ despite the two cycles**, so Freuder's bound still gives
    strong 3-consistency against ℛ's 2: **§21.5.1's shortfall of one level holds for the
    whole tower and not only for a K₃.**

 **And the index is rebuilt on coordinates that carry the shape** — stage, parent count, multiplier —
 at 38% density where the first attempt sat at 3.2%:

  operator                        cells   box   E
  **ℛ**, the (≤,≤) corner            9    24   **15** — it admits the entire box
  **ℛ₄**, four orientations          9    24    **4**
  orientation cost                                **11**

 **ℛ sees nothing here**, because the multiplier and the parent count both peak at stage 0 and an
 upward-only envelope cannot express *×4 occurs only at the base*. **ℛ₄ cuts the defect from fifteen
 to four — the largest orientation cost anywhere in this book, and the first object on which the
 second operator is the only one that works.**

 **The four remaining cells are the alternation's complement.** Λ₉ two parents · Λ₁₀ one · Λ₁₁ two ·
 Λ₁₂ one — **the stages where the tower could have taken the other kind and did not.**

 **And the distribution is a shape statement.** Only two of thirteen constraints carry a multiplier —
 k ≤ 4ℓ+2 and g ≤ 4f+2, both Pauli — and eleven are ×1. Seven take one parent, six take two. **Three
 collisions**, each correct: the base's three plain one-parent bounds are indistinguishable in these
 coordinates, and so are the two-parent pairs at Λ₁₀ and Λ₁₂, **each of which is one two-sided bound
 written as two.** Registers 523–524.

### 21.5.4 Shape decides the seed, and neither extreme is cheapest

 §14.5.7 makes the seed a covering problem and §21.5.3 measures the graph. **The two meet here.** Six
 constraint graphs on six nodes over the same alphabet, differing only in shape:

  shape                  edges   cells   S steps   **seed**   diameter   max degree
  **path**                  5      28       30       **8**       5           2
  star                      5     276       10       **6**       2           5
  caterpillar               5      65       22       **6**       3           3
  binary tree               5     100       16       **6**       4           3
  double star               5     127       16       **6**       3           4
  forest, two components    4     196        8       **5**       2           2

    *All six seeds exact by branch and bound. Greedy set cover gave the star **7** and
    was wrong there and nowhere else — §2.24, and register 526.*

    **Both extremes cost more.** The balanced trees all seed at six; the path and the
    star at eight and seven. *The prediction under test was that a star seeds cheapest,
    one hub cell witnessing many steps at once. It is refuted: the hub does, and the five
    leaves then need their own witnesses.*

 **And no simple graph statistic decides it.** Diameter runs 5, 2, 3, 4, 3, 2 against seeds 8, 7, 6,
 6, 6, 5; maximum degree runs 2, 5, 3, 3, 4, 2. **Cell count does not decide it either** — the path
 has twenty-eight cells and the worst seed, the star two hundred and seventy-six and a better one.

 **What does the work is S, the envelope-step count**, and on exact seeds the relation is
 monotone with nothing set aside. **The alphabet decides nothing** — it is eighteen for every shape
 and correlates at exactly zero.

    **And the seed is MONOTONE in S with no exception**: 8 → 5, 10 → 6, 16 → 6, 16 → 6,
    22 → 6, 30 → 8. The covering reading of §14.5.9 holds on all six.

 *This section first reported the star at seven and named it as the residual where the covering
 reading fails. It does not fail: **greedy set cover fails there**, and exact search gives six. The
 anomaly was the heuristic, and it was caught by §2.24 one cycle after that protocol was written.*

 Register 525.

### 21.5.5 Every constraint in the book, indexed

 §21.5.3 indexes Λ's thirteen. **This indexes every constraint that defines an index anywhere in the
 work** — the tower, the electromagnetic quotient, the drawn objects, and the book's own record —
 over five coordinates: parent count, form, which operator carries it, where it came from, and
 whether it **defines a quantity or constrains a product.**

  24 constraints · 15 distinct cells · box 96 · density 16%
  **E under ℛ 21 · E₄ under ℛ₄ 6 · orientation cost 15**

  carried by ℛ                22
  carried by ℛ₄ and not ℛ      2
  **carried by neither**       **0**

    **Every genuine constraint in this book is carried by one of the two operators.**

 **A first attempt gave two that neither carried — and both were miscategorised.** *Period × group*
 and *month × day* are axis declarations, not relations: the periodic table's thirty-six and the
 calendar's seven come from having **no** constraint the operator can find, which is a different thing
 from having one it cannot carry. **Eight rows of twenty-eight were coordinate systems entered as
 constraints**, and removing them emptied the column.

 **And the fifth axis was earned rather than chosen.** Half the first table collided; the groups
 showed that four of eight collisions were the category error above, and of the rest only one was
 real — *g ≤ q*, a conservation bound, sharing a cell with *Δℓ = f − ℓ*, a definition. **Defines
 against constrains is the coordinate the data asked for**, and §17.1 admits no other here.

 **Nine collisions remain and eight are correct.** ℓ ≤ n−1 with f ≤ e−1 is one law on parent and
 target; q ≤ k with 2S ≤ k are two plain bounds by the same parent; the two bands are both
 angular-momentum couplings; d(x,y) with E(X) are both this book's own definitions. **The ninth is a
 miscoding of mine**: 2J_c ≤ φ̂(k) is a *tabulated envelope*, not an affine bound, and the form axis
 has no value for it.

  parents  one 14 · two 10        form    plain 11 · affine 7 · band 3 · congruence 3
  source   physics 16 · a drawing 3 · this book's own record 5
  role     defines 4 · constrains 20

 Registers 531–532.

### 21.6 E = 0 is relative to coordinates, and the coordinates must be given

    **THEOREM.** Let X be finite with |X| = a·b, a, b > 1. Relabel X bijectively onto
    the a × b rectangle. The result is a full box, hence ℛ-closed. **So every index
    whose cell count is composite has a coordinate system in which E = 0.**

  object                      E as given    re-coordinatised    E after
  the periodic table              **36**         9 × 10          **0**
  the calendar                     **7**         5 × 73          **0**
  a random 26-cell set            **38**         2 × 13          **0**
  V3 geometry                        0           5 × 5              0

    **E = 0 is not a property of a set of cells. It is a property of cells TOGETHER
    WITH coordinates**, and a relabelling into a rectangle is always available.

 **This touches no result in this book, and it changes what every one of them asserts.** Every
 coordinate used here is fixed by its subject — n, ℓ, k and q are quantum numbers, period and group
 are how chemists draw the table, month and day are the calendar. **None was chosen to make E come
 out.** But E(Λ) = 0 does not say *Λ has a special shape*; it says **Λ has a special shape in the
 coordinates physics supplies**, and that those coordinates, fixed for other reasons a century ago,
 happen to close. **That is the stronger claim and the more falsifiable one.**

 **And it supplies the check a reader must make before believing any E here.** §21.1's table shows one
 object under two namings with different E. This shows one object has a naming with **E = 0** whenever
 its count is composite — so the question is never *what is E* but **who fixed the coordinates**.

 **Which is why V3 geometry is dark.** Its 25 cells are 5 × 5, so its E = 0 may be vacuous in exactly
 the sense §12.11.8 already names for the electromagnetic image — *a complete rectangle, E = 0
 vacuously.* **Without knowing whether V3's five letters are subject-fixed or convenience, its closure
 carries no information.** Register 546.

### 21.6.1 The letter that defines rather than constrains

 The companion's ANEC-proofs index prints **four letters and a box of twelve**, and four rungs each at
 least two give at least sixteen. **One letter has rung 1.**

 A rung-1 coordinate takes one value, so it contributes no envelope. Tested against everything this
 book measures — same cells, same box, **same E, same envelope-step count, same seed.** *It is
 invisible to ℛ entirely, and it still counts as a letter.*

    **A rung-1 letter is the index's own name carried as a coordinate. It takes one
    value because the object is the thing for which that value never varies.**

 **And only that index carries one.** V6's box 24 = 2·2·2·3 uses all four letters; the null surface's
 64 = 2⁶ uses all six; V3's 48 = 2⁴·3 uses all five. **Three name their object outside the alphabet
 and one names it inside**, which is why its letter count and its box disagree by exactly one — not an
 error but the defining letter, counted.

 **This is §21.5.5's *role* axis at its extreme**: defines and constrains nothing. Register 547.

### 21.6.2 Two of the companion's five, read from three numbers each

 **The local null surface is a FACE.** Six letters and a box of 64 force every rung to 2; 32 is exactly
 half the cube; and of the two natural halves, **a parity class gives E = 32 and a face gives E = 0.**
 The printed E is 0. *Deducible with no access to any cell.* And §10.4 nests it further — THETA = 0 at
 16 cells and STAT = 0 at 8, so Killing horizons sit inside non-expanding ones, **one cell from where
 half-sided modular inclusion is proved.**

 **The charger index's ambiguity is one cell wide and the companion names it.** §8.1 prints five
 chargers and §10.1 counts six; the missing one is stated: *the empty JUR = 0 direction is exactly the
 charger shape that would close the law index* — and **the missing cell in the charger index and the
 defect in the law index are one absence at two levels.** Register 548.

# PART V — THE METHOD

## 22. The bracket
 Everything so far has been about structure. This part asks what such a structure can do with
 measurements, and the answer is deliberately modest.

### 22.1 The rule
 For a Rydberg channel — fixed parent, fixed ℓ, varying n — the binding energies T = I − E are monotone in
 n. So for any interior member:

    T(n) lies between T(n−1) and T(n+1)

 That is the bracket. It uses **only measured neighbours**. It fits nothing, assumes no functional form, and returns an interval rather than a value.

 **It is a deduction, not a prediction.** The distinction is the whole method: a fit says where a level probably is; the bracket says where it cannot fail to be.

### 22.1.1 A bracket whose admissible set is a singleton
 The bracket of §22.1 bounds a *measured value*: where a level cannot fail to be. A companion
 paper — *Muon-Catalysed Fusion*, Lach 2026 — uses the same machinery on a **parameter**, and the
 admissible set comes out with one element. That is a use this book had not stated.

 The parameter is the mass of a binder that must hold two nuclei close enough to fuse. Two bounds, each
 monotone in that mass and each with a stated physical origin:

  bound                                    scales as        gives          origin
  fusion must outrun the cycle             R = a_H/m        m > 119 mₑ     geometry
  at least two molecular bound states      N ≈ √(M/m)       m < 918 mₑ     index survival

 **The window [119, 918] mₑ holds two known particles and one filter removes the second.** The pion at
 273 is absorbed by the nucleus before it can catalyse; the muon at 207 remains, interior by 1.74×
 below and 4.44× above, so the conclusion survives reasonable movement of either edge. Recomputed
 here: N_states of 60.6, 4.21 and 1.03 for electron, muon and tau against the paper's 61, 4.2 and 1.03.

 **A bracket with one occupant is a derivation.** §22.1's bracket says where a value must lie and
 leaves the interval populated; this one says where a parameter must lie and the interval contains a
 single realisable object, so the reaction is not selected but forced. **The same operation reads as a
 deduction about a quantity or as a uniqueness proof about a kind, and which it is depends only on how
 many admissible cells the interval contains.**

 **And it refines §18.4.1's criterion in one direction.** Both bounds *decrease* in m, and a decreasing
 cap breaks joins in a product — the coordination-number result. On a single axis it does not matter:
 **for a bracket on one chain, monotone in either direction suffices; only closure in a product
 requires increasing.** The criterion was stated for products and this is the one-dimensional case of
 it. Register 319.

### 22.1.1.1 Containment on level energies, recomputed under variation
 §22.1 brackets a level from its two measured neighbours and reports the result once. **This states it
 to F.3's highest standard**: the method named with its input set, every input printed, and the test
 recomputed at four quantum defects rather than one.

 **The method.** Take a Rydberg series T(n) = Z²R/(n − δ)² with R = 109,737.31568 cm⁻¹ and Z = 1, and
 for each interior level n test whether its energy lies between the energies of n − 1 and n + 1.
 **The input set is printed**: n from 4 to 14, giving nine interior levels, at δ = 0.00, 0.35, 1.35 and
 2.65 — a hydrogenic series and three penetrating ones.

  δ        interior levels    contained    energies, cm⁻¹
  0.00                   9            9    559.9 to 6,858.6
  0.35                   9            9    589.0 to 8,237.0
  1.35                   9            9    685.8 to 15,626.5
  2.65                   9            9    851.8 to 60,212.5

    **Thirty-six of thirty-six, at every defect, across two orders of magnitude in energy.**

 **What varying δ tests is exactly the assumption the bracket rests on.** The defect shifts every level
 and stretches the series by a factor of nine between the first row and the last, and containment does
 not notice — because it needs only monotonicity in n, which no value of δ disturbs. **A result that
 survives its own input being varied by an order of magnitude is a result about the shape and not about
 the numbers**, which is what §22.1's single run could not distinguish. Register 391.

### 22.1.2 The bracket's own boundary — interpolation and extrapolation
 §22.1 brackets an interior point from its two measured neighbours. **Two questions sit at the edges of
 that, and they have different answers.**

 **Inward, the bracket is a deduction and does not fail.** Tested on a Rydberg-like binding series
 T(n) = Z²R/(n − δ)² across n = 4 to 12: **seven interior cells bracketed, seven held, zero failures.**
 That is §22.1 doing what it claims, and it is the same operation as bracketing an intermediate state
 between two observed times — a composable index makes before and after an order, per §12.11.0, so an
 interior bracket bounds a state the index never saw.

 **Outward, past the last measurement, it is not a bracket at all.** Bracket widths in that series fall
 4,799 · 2,594 · 1,562 · 1,015 · 697 · 499 · 370, with successive ratios 0.540 · 0.602 · 0.650 · 0.687
 · 0.716 · 0.741 — a clean pattern. Presuming the next width from it gives **274.1 against a true
 281.7, an error of 2.72%.** A good estimate, and not a bound: a bracket says where a value *cannot
 fail* to be, and this one can fail — it happened not to by much.

 **The legitimate form is to take the next width from the law, not from the pattern.** The widths fall
 as ν⁻³, which gives the ratio 0.764 against an observed 0.741 — derived rather than fitted. The
 companion paper's first observed r_ℓ exit was predicted in advance from that same ν⁻³ scaling and
 then occurred, which is a presumed next bracket that is a deduction.

    **Presume the next bracket from the law that generates the widths, never from the pattern the
    widths make.**

 That rule is the one §12.11.3.1 shows governs the tower: dimensions bounded by laws close exactly,
 dimension 11 bounded by the extent closes to an envelope. **The asymmetry is structural.** An index
 can say where something must have been between two observations; it cannot say where it will be after
 the last one, and that is the same limit §18.6 states when it calls E(X) a prediction budget rather
 than a prediction. Register 328.

### 22.2 The four rules

 The bracket is stated in one line and applied under four.

    Rule 1 — Interiority. Bracket only cells belonging to the same Rydberg sequence as their
    neighbours and sharing a term set with them, and either interior — a measured neighbour on
    each side — or above the highest measured member. Extrapolation below the lowest
    measured member is forbidden. Rule 2 — Variable. Read the interpolation variable off T =
    Z²R/ν² for the axis in question: E along n within a series; interval ratios along J; geometric in T along
    the core shell. Rule 3 — Separation. Report the deductive bracket separately from the inferential
    point estimate. Given monotonicity, the true value cannot lie outside [max below, min above]. Rule
    4 — Uncertainty. Fit δ locally by a Ritz expansion and take σ = 2R Z_eff² · SE_pred / ν³ from the fit's
    prediction standard error. Use δ = δ₀ + δ₂/n² where three or more neighbours exist, δ = δ₀ where
    only two do — subject to the admissibility clause below. Rule 4a — Admissibility of the
    constant form. The constant-defect form may be used only if the constant does not exceed the last
    measured defect in the direction of extrapolation. On a decreasing channel it must satisfy δ̄ ≤
    δ(last measured); on an increasing one, δ̄ ≥ δ(last measured). Where it does not, the
    two-point Ritz solve is the admissible form.

### 22.2.1 Each rule justified by ablation
 A rule that costs nothing to break is not a rule. Each was tested by violating it:

| rule violated | cost |
|---|---|
| extrapolate **below** the series | median error 0.46 → 206 cm⁻¹ — **446×** |
| extrapolate **above** the series | median error 0.46 → 0.13 cm⁻¹ — **0.28×** |
| wrong interpolation variable | rms 200 → 588 cm⁻¹ — 2.9× |
| pooled σ instead of per-cell | ±1σ coverage 96.2% instead of 69.6% |
| include a collapsed orbital | median error 12.8 → 41.3 cm⁻¹ — 3.2× |

 **The extrapolation cost is directional, and a single figure conceals it.** An earlier version of this work quoted 3.7× on pooled rms over twelve extrapolated cells. **That statistic is not reproducible and is unstable**: on the same data it is 18.8× on rms, 4.6× on median, and **0.5× if the two worst cells are removed.** An rms over twelve points containing a 12,942 cm⁻¹ outlier is not a summary that should carry a rule.

 **Split by direction the instability disappears.** Extrapolating *below* a series costs 446× on the median, because δ varies fastest at low *n* and a Ritz fit run backwards diverges. Extrapolating *above* costs 0.28×, because δ has already flattened. **The asymmetry is a factor of 1,577**, and Rule 1 is stated accordingly.

### 22.2.2 Rule 1 is not about n
 The rule is stated for a series in n, and that is where it was found. It is not a rule about n.

 The isoelectronic axis — fix the electron count, vary the nuclear charge — carries the same defect and shows the same asymmetry:

| axis | toward the loose end | interpolate | toward the tight end |
|---|---|---|---|
| ***n***, within a series | **446×** | 1× | **0.28×** |
| ***Z***, along a sequence | **17.3%** | 1.3% | **2.1%** |

 **Same sign, same magnitude of asymmetry, same cause.** δ varies fastest where the electron is loosely bound — low *n*, low *Z* — and flattens where it is tightly bound. **Extrapolating toward the flattening is cheap; away from it is not.**

    Rule 1 is a rule about direction, and it holds on whichever axis carries the defect.

 **Measured on two sequences, holding out each member in turn.** The lithium-like neutral is predicted from its three ions at 11.1% error; the sodium-like neutral at 23.4%. **The tight ends — C IV and Si IV — come back at 1.8% and 2.5%.**

### 22.2.3 And one step beyond the fitted range costs more again
 A hold-out is bracketed by the points that remain. A genuine prediction is not. Tested on the
 sodium-like sequence with P V — a fifth member — held out entirely:

| fit form | predicted δ(P V) | true | error |
|---|---|---|---|
| δ = *a* + *b*/*Z* | 0.8189 | 0.7129 | **14.9%** |
| **δ = *a* + *b*/*Z* + *c*/*Z*²** | 0.7376 | 0.7129 | **3.47%** |
| δ·*Z* linear in *Z* | 0.7835 | 0.7129 | 9.9% |

 **Only the quadratic form is usable, and it costs 3.5% rather than the 2.1% a hold-out suggests** — a factor of 1.7 for stepping outside the fitted range.

 **This matters for §25.6** The Sc VI prediction has a second, independent route through its isoelectronic sequence, and **3.5% is the figure that route must carry** — not 2.1%, and not the 1.3% of pure interpolation.

### 22.2.4 Rule 1's second clause is not redundant
 Sc III 3d is interior in n within ℓ = 2, and including it inflates the median error by 3.2× — because it is the
 collapsed ground state rather than a sequence member.

 **The operational test is the defect.** Its δ departs from its neighbours' by 0.14 where they depart from each other by 0.008 — **a factor of 17.** A cell can be interior by index and outside the sequence in fact, and the defect says which.

### 22.2.5 Rule 4 at the edge of a channel
 The two-parameter Ritz form needs three neighbours to leave a residual, and a channel with exactly three
 members has an interior cell with only two.

 Why Rule 4a exists
 The constant-defect form takes the mean of the available defects. On a monotone channel
 that mean lies between them — and therefore on the wrong side of the last one.

 **Sc VI's ⁴S°*n*s channel is the case that forced the clause.** With δ(4s) = 1.0057 and δ(5s) = 0.9812, the constant form gives **δ̄ = 0.9934 — above δ(5s)**, which a decreasing channel forbids. The resulting estimate falls **outside the deductive bracket its own monotonicity implies** (§25.6).

    A rule that produces an estimate its own bracket excludes is not a rule about uncertainty.
    It is an error.

 **With two neighbours the Ritz form is exactly determined** — two equations, two unknowns, no residual and therefore no fitted σ — but it is *admissible*, and Rule 3's separation means the bracket carries the guarantee while the point estimate carries none.

 **One further case in this work:** the Kr I (²P°₁⁄₂) *n*p series — 5p, 6p, 7p, with 6p interior. Under the constant-defect form the cell returns an estimate in error by −70.9 cm⁻¹ against σ = 602, or **0.12σ**.

    It is a valid cell and an almost worthless one. Its bracket spans 14,602 cm⁻¹ because its
    neighbours are two principal quantum numbers away.

 **That is the validity/value distinction**, and it is the clearest instance of it in the collection: a cell may satisfy every rule and still tell you almost nothing. **The rules govern correctness. They do not govern usefulness**, and a method that conflated the two would report this cell as a success.

### 22.3 The bracket is limit-free
 Since T = I − E for a constant I:

    T(n) between T(n±1) ⟺ E(n) between E(n±1)

 Containment is invariant under the affine map. **ν, δ and *V* all need the ionisation limit; containment does not.**

 **Consequence:** the method applies wherever levels are known, even when the threshold is not. This is not a technicality — it is what allowed the antiprotonic-helium cells of §25.6, where the relevant threshold is ambiguous.

### 22.4 The price of limit-freedom, computed
 §32.3 presents limit-freedom as pure gain. It is a trade, and the terms are computable.

 The alternative is to bracket **δ** rather than *T* — which is what the field does. And *V* is identical either way:

      δ = δ₀ + δ₂/n² has L = \|δ′/δ″\| = n/3, the same as T's, and δ₀ is an additive constant that
      cancels from both w and e. V(δ) = V(T) = 4n/(3h)

 **The single most standard move in Rydberg spectroscopy — interpolate δ, not *T* — improved estimates enormously and left the price of a guarantee exactly where it was.** A century of practice raised numerator and denominator by the same factor.

### 22.4.1 What δ buys, and what it costs
 In δ the bracket is narrower by exactly n³/(2δ₂) — measured against predicted: 4,329 vs 4,267 at n
 = 8; 1.387 × 10⁶ vs 1.386 × 10⁶ at n = 55. Agreement to four figures.

 **But that factor is the size of a Ritz coefficient, not a property of bracketing** — δ barely moves because δ₂ is small.

 **And a bracket on δ does not bound *T*.** Converting requires the ionisation limit and inherits its uncertainty:

| *n* | *w_T* direct | via δ | **+ limit σ = 0.01** |
|---|---|---|---|
| 20 | 68.06 | 0.00102 | **0.0210** |
| 40 | 7.613 | 1.43 × 10⁻⁵ | **0.0200** |

 **At *n* = 40 the δ-route's own width is three orders below the limit's uncertainty. The limit dominates entirely.**

      Bracket T: bounds the measured level, needs nothing. Bracket δ: bounds a derived
      quantity, needs the limit, and the energy bound is never better than the limit is known.

 **The choice is conditional, and the condition is computable per channel.** This book's tightest bracket is 1.398 cm⁻¹; limit uncertainties in the collection run 0.001 to 0.4 cm⁻¹. **The δ route wins where the limit is known better than the bracket — true for some species, false for others.**

### 22.5 Admissibility
 A channel is admissible when its levels are separated by more than their uncertainty:

      r = 2Z²R / (ν³σ) ≥ 5

 *r* falls as ν⁻³. Every channel eventually leaves the domain; the rule states where.

### 22.6 What the bracket costs
 A guarantee is wider than an estimate. Chapter 23 prices it exactly, and the price is the only linearly rising
 quantity in the structure.

## 23. The cost surface

### 23.1 The ratio
 Let w be the bracket width |T(n+1) − T(n−1)| and e the interpolation error |T(n) − ½(T(n−1) + T(n+1))|.

    V = w / e = 4ν/3

 for a Rydberg series. And in general, for *y* = *x^p* sampled at step *h*:

    V(x, p) = 4x / ( h · |p − 1| )

 Verified on power laws from *p* = −3 to +11, agreement under 1%.

 Figure 16.1. The exact cost against its asymptote. The floor 32/11 = 2.909 belongs to the exact expression; the
 asymptotic form gives 26/9 and is low by 0.69% at ν = 2. They converge by ν = 10.

### 23.2 The floor is algebraic before it is physical
 The cost of a guarantee has a floor that owes nothing to atomic physics.

    Proposition 14.1. Let three consecutive cells of a channel carry values y₀ < y₁ < y₂ at equally
    spaced index. Let w = y₂ − y₀ be the bracket width at the middle cell and e = |y₁ − (y₀+y₂)/2| the
    error of two-point linear interpolation from the same neighbours. Then V = w/e > 2, with V → 2 only
    as one of the two steps vanishes. Proof. Write d₀ = y₁ − y₀ and d₁ = y₂ − y₁, both positive by
    monotonicity. Then w = d₀ + d₁ and e = |d₀ − d₁|/2, so V = 2(d₀+d₁)/|d₀−d₁|. Since d₀, d₁ > 0 we
    have |d₀ − d₁| < d₀ + d₁, giving V > 2. As min/max → 0 the ratio → 2; as d₀ → d₁ it diverges. ∎

 **This holds for any monotone sequence whatever.** Not for some observables, not with better data:

    The deductive interval is always more than twice the error of the crudest possible point
    estimate built from the same two numbers.

 **Every empirical *V* in this book is an instance of this bound**, not an independent fact. And against a better estimator — the Ritz expansion of Rule 4, or a mass model — the gap is wider still, so *V* as defined **understates** the cost.

 **The Rydberg floor of 32/11 = 2.909 is therefore the tighter of two.** The algebraic floor at 2 constrains every monotone sequence; the physical floor at 32/11 constrains Rydberg series specifically, and the difference between them is what the hydrogenic form contributes.

### 23.2.1 When the floor is approached, and what it means
    Observation 14.2. V → 2 requires min(d₀,d₁)/max(d₀,d₁) → 0 — one step of the channel vanishing
    relative to its neighbour.

 **In every sequence examined here where that occurs, the small step is produced by two channels interleaving**, which Rule 1's second clause excludes. So the algebraic floor is approached exactly where the method declines to operate: **the cheapest guarantee is available only on a channel that is not one.**

### 23.3 V is a bound, not a tool
 V contains no σ. Improving measurement precision by six orders of magnitude leaves V = 26.689
 unchanged at ν = 20 — computed exactly at three precisions spanning σ = 10⁻² to 10⁻⁸ cm⁻¹.

    No spectrometer can buy a narrower bracket. The price of certainty in a Rydberg series is
    set by the series.

 So *V* is not an instrument. It is an impossibility statement, and it makes four:

| | forbids |
|---|---|
| *V* = 4ν/3 | a guarantee costs 4ν/3 × an estimate, and no measurement reduces it |
| *V* ≥ **32/11** | no guarantee is ever cheaper than 2.909 × |
| pole at *p* = 1 | no guarantee exists for a linear observable |
| ν_V ceiling | above it the cost cannot be *measured*, though it stays defined |

 **The exact *V* is rational at every ν** — 32/11, 54/13, 256/47, 250/37, 4000/299 — and 4ν/3 + 4/(9ν) is its asymptotic form, low by 0.69% at ν = 2 and exact to four decimals by ν = 10. **The floor 32/11 belongs to the exact expression, not to the asymptote.**

### 23.4 V is a function of ν/h alone
 The cost has one free parameter, and it is not where the book first looked.

 *V* is defined from three levels at *n* − *h*, *n*, *n* + *h*. Nothing forces *h* = 1: a cell may be bracketed from neighbours two or three principal quantum numbers away. Carrying *h* through the algebra gives an exact result.

    V = 4ν³ / ( h(3ν² − h²) ), and with r = ν/h, V = 4r³/(3r² − 1)

 **One variable. *Z* and *R* cancel identically**, not approximately, and 4*r*/3 is the large-*r* expansion of this expression rather than the expression itself.

### 23.4.1 Which makes depth and step interchangeable

| (ν, *h*) | ν/*h* | *V* exact |
|---|---|---|
| (20, 1) | 20 | **26.688907** |
| (40, 2) | 20 | **26.688907** |
| (60, 3) | 20 | **26.688907** |
| (80, 4) | 20 | **26.688907** |
| (10, 1) | 10 | **13.377926** |
| (40, 4) | 10 | **13.377926** |

 **Identical to six figures, and identical across *Z* = 1, 2 and 6.** That last point is worth dwelling on: *Z*_eff is precisely what the wrong-charge error of §16.3 got wrong and the bracket could not see. **The similarity law is blind to it too — by cancellation, provably.**

### 23.4.2 Tested on the collection

 **1,033 (ν/*h*, *V*) pairs from measured levels**, at both steps:

                    pairs             median deviation from 4(ν/h)/3
  h=1               560               0.49%
  h=2               473               1.15%
  all               1,033             0.69%

 **The *h* = 2 pairs bracket from neighbours two principal quantum numbers away and obey the same law.** And the outliers identify themselves: Al I at *n* = 51 and Ga I at *n* = 53 both sit above the ν_V ceiling of §23.11, where quotation granularity destroys the second difference. **The method's own admissibility bound predicts which pairs fail it.**

### 23.5 The trade-off, and a conservation law that is not one
 Since h is free, ask whether it can be used to buy a cheaper guarantee.

 **It can.** *V* falls from 53.3 to 13.4 as *h* goes 1 → 4. **But the bracket widens in proportion**, because

    w = 2y′h + O(h³) — linear in h e = y″h²/2 + O(h⁴) — quadratic in h

### 23.5.1 A withdrawn proposition
 An earlier version of this section claimed w·V = w²/e = 8y′²/y″ independent of h, and called it a
 conservation law. It is not. Exact symbolic evaluation for y = x⁻² gives

    w²/e = 16x⁴/(h⁶ − 5h⁴x² + 7h²x⁴ − 3x⁶)

 which depends on *h* explicitly and equals 8*y*′²/*y*″ only as *h* → 0. **The numerical table on which the claim was made showed 366.3, 367.9, 370.6, 374.5 across *h* = 1…4 — a 2.2% rise, read as a constant.** Withdrawn, and recorded in Chapter 28.

 **And no other candidate exists.** Since *w* ~ *h* and *e* ~ *h*², any *w^a e^b* scales as *h*^(*a*+2*b*), so **the only *h*-free combination is *a* = 2, *b* = −1** — the one tried. The leading-order invariant is unique and merely asymptotic.

### 23.5.2 What is exactly true
    dw/dh > 0 and dV/dh < 0, for every monotone convex y and every h.

 **Exact, with no expansion and no limit.** Verified over 172 monotone steps with **zero violations**. Width and cost move in opposite directions under the only free choice available, so **the family {(*w*, *V*) : *h*} is a Pareto frontier: no *h* gives both a narrower and a cheaper guarantee.**

 **The withdrawn quantity is the frontier's equation**, not a conserved number. A conservation law says a value cannot change; a frontier says which pairs are attainable. **The algebra is the same and the claim is weaker.**

### 23.5.3 And the frontier has an optimum
 Opposite signs give any stated preference αw + βV an interior minimum:

    h\ = √( 2β / (α y″) )

 Checked against direct minimisation: 0.0551 against 0.0550, 0.1743 against 0.1750, 2.7886 against 2.7450 — **and 8.82 against 7.83 at ν = 40, β/α = 10**, an 11% miss where *h*\* approaches ν/4 and the leading-order derivation fails. **The formula holds for *h*\* ≪ ν and not beyond.**

### 23.6 The fractional forms
 The relations of §23.4 close, and the closure states two quantities the book had not.

    w/T = 4(h/ν) + 8(h/ν)³ — the bracket width as a fraction of the term e/T = 3(h/ν)² — the interpolation
    error as a fraction of the term

  ν          h         w/T                  4h/ν                  e/T                           3(h/ν)²

  20         1         0.201004             0.200000              7.531 × 10⁻³                  7.500 × 10⁻³

  40         1         0.100125             0.100000              1.877 × 10⁻³                  1.875 × 10⁻³

  40         2         0.201004             0.200000              7.531 × 10⁻³                  7.500 × 10⁻³

 **Both are pure functions of *h*/ν**, and their ratio returns *V* = (4/3)(ν/*h*).

 **The second is the more useful and the more exposed.** *e*/*T* = 3(*h*/ν)² is the relative error of linear interpolation on a Rydberg series — a statement available to any spectroscopist since 1925. **It is not in the literature, and §25.6 explains why: the field abandoned the variable.**

### 23.7 The exponent axis
 Two involutions act on p: cost, p ↦ 2 − p (paired values multiply to x²); and virial, p ↦ −p (paired values
 multiply to a constant). Composed, they generate translation by 2, so exponents fall into two orbits by
 parity — and the parity is b, the number of energy denominators.

 **The pole at *p* = 1 sits in the odd orbit.** Nothing built purely from powers of ⟨*r*⟩ can reach it.

 Figure 16.2. The cost surface. Every observable in this book is a slice at fixed p. The pole at p = 1 is the method's only
 hard singularity: a linear observable has no curvature to price.

### 23.8 The cost has a name in another field
 And the name explains two things this book could only observe.

### 23.8.1 The quantity is the Newton decrement
 The asymptotic form of §23.5 is not an accident of spectroscopy:

      8y′²/y″ = 8λ², where λ² = ∇f ᵀ[∇²f]⁻¹∇f

 **λ is the Newton decrement**, introduced by Nesterov and Nemirovskii (1994) for interior-point methods and standard in convex optimisation since. In one dimension λ² = *f*′²/*f*″, and ½λ² is *exactly the decrease in the quadratic approximation obtained by a full Newton step*. Verified symbolically at *p* = −2, −3, 2, 11: **the ratio is 8 in every case.**

 **For a Rydberg series it takes a physical value:**

      λ² = (2Z²R/ν³)² / (6Z²R/ν⁴) = (2/3)·Z²R/ν² = (2/3)·T

 **The Newton decrement of a Rydberg series is two-thirds of its binding energy** — 731.5820 against 731.5820 at ν = 10, 7.3158 against 7.3158 at ν = 100. **And the optimum whose distance it measures is the series limit**, where *T* → 0.

### 23.8.2 The term function is self-concordant
 The Newton decrement carries its meaning only for self-concordant functions, |f‴| ≤
 2(f″)^{3/2}. Nobody has asked whether T(ν) qualifies. It does:

      24Z²R/ν⁵ ≤ 2·6^{3/2}(Z²R)^{3/2}/ν⁶ ⟺ ν ≤ (√6/2)·Z√R

| *Z* | self-concordant for |
|---|---|
| 1 | ν ≤ **406** |
| 2 | ν ≤ 811 |
| 6 | ν ≤ 2,434 |

 **The deepest channel in this book reaches ν = 55.** The ratio \|*f*‴\|/2(*f*″)^{3/2} runs 0.0049 at ν = 2, **0.1356 at ν = 55**, and crosses unity between ν = 405 and 406.

      T(ν) is self-concordant across the entire measured range, with sevenfold margin.

### 23.8.3 Which explains the cancellation
 §23.4 proved that Z and R vanish from V and gave no reason. Self-concordance is the reason.

 The class is *defined* to be affinely invariant — that is its purpose — and the Newton decrement inherits the invariance. ***Z*²*R* enters *T* as a multiplicative constant, a multiplicative constant is an affine map, and under an affine map the decrement cannot change.**

      The book observed a cancellation. This says why it had to happen.

### 23.8.4 And the attribution the book owes
 Interval analysis has asked this book's question since 1966, and named it. Excess width appears
 in Moore's own index; IEEE 1788-2015 requires enclosures with "bounded excess width to control
 overestimation"; practitioners quote enclosures at "114% of optimal width" with an "excess cost"
 attached. And the field states the trade in one sentence:

      "We use interval arithmetic when we want global solutions with rigorous bounds, and we are willing
      to pay more for that."

 **That is Chapter 23's thesis, published sixty years earlier, about function ranges and rounding rather than interpolation from measurements.** The framing is theirs. **What is not theirs is the ratio computed for a measured monotone sequence, the value 4ν/3, the floor 32/11, and the identification λ² = (2/3)*T*.**

### 23.9 Which variable to bracket
 Chapter 23 has treated V as a property of the method. It is a property of the observable, and
 the observable is chosen by the person.

### 23.9.1 The cost ranks by exponent
 Since V = 4x/(h|p−1|), the cost falls as the power law steepens. Evaluated at ν = 20, h = 1:

  observable                                                                 p          V
  C₆                                                                         11         8.18
  polarisability α                                                           7          13.44
  spacing                                                                    −3         20.04

  observable                                                            p            V
  T, the term value                                                     −2           26.69
  C₃, geometric cross-section                                           4            26.72
  radiative lifetime                                                    3            40.03
  ⟨r⟩, dipole moment                                                    2            80.00
  blockade radius                                                       11/6         95.99

 **A guarantee on C₆ costs a third of a guarantee on *T*, and a twelfth of one on the dipole moment.**

### 23.9.2 Two branches, and where they cross
 The asymptotic form appears to drive V below the floor of Prop. 14.1 as p grows. It does not —
 it stops being valid.

| *p* | *V* exact | 4*x*/*h*\|*p*−1\| |
|---|---|---|
| 4 | 26.72 | 26.67 |
| 11 | 8.18 | 8.00 |
| 50 | 2.39 | 1.63 |
| 300 | **2.0000** | 0.27 |
| 10,000 | **2.0000** | 0.008 |

 As *p* → ∞, (*x*+*h*)^*p* swamps everything: *w* → (*x*+*h*)^*p* and *e* → (*x*+*h*)^*p*/2, giving ***V* → 2 exactly.** The two branches cross where 4*x*/(*h*\|*p*−1\|) = 2, that is **\|*p*−1\| = 2*x*/*h***.

      The absolute floor is reached only by power laws steeper than 2ν/h. At ν = 20 that requires
      p > 41. C₆ at p = 11 is nowhere near it.

### 23.9.3 And the pole sits on the field's favourite variable
 ν = n − δ is nearly linear in n, and p = 1 is the pole.

| *n* | *V* on ν | *V* on *T* | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10 | 1.09 × 10⁵ | 11.58 | 9.4 × 10³ |
| 20 | 1.77 × 10⁶ | 24.89 | 7.1 × 10⁴ |
| 40 | 2.84 × 10⁷ | 51.54 | 5.5 × 10⁵ |

      The effective quantum number is the most natural variable in the field and the one
      variable in which a bracket is worthless. §32.5's pole is not a curiosity at the edge of the
      method; it sits on the coordinate everyone uses.

### 23.10 The order of the bracket
 Monotonicity is not the only order property available, and using more of them changes what
 the bracket is — but not, in the end, what it costs.

### 23.10.1 A deductive bracket exists at every order
 For T = Z²R/ν² every derivative has known sign, sign(f^(j)) = (−1)^j — checkable on data as the sign
 of the j-th finite difference, with no fit and no smoothness assumption beyond a sign. With k+1
 nodes and the classical interpolation error,

      sign( f(n) − p(n) ) = (−1)^(k+1) · (−1)^m, with m nodes above n k+1+m even → a lower bound.
      Odd → an upper bound.

 **A two-sided deductive bracket at every order, by choosing *m*.** Tested on ν = 8…119 and *k* = 1…5:

      560 containments, zero failures.

 **And the widths fall fast** — at ν = 40 from 0.362 cm⁻¹ at order 1 to **8.9 × 10⁻⁶ at order 5.**

### 23.10.2 And the cost still grows
 An earlier draft claimed V stays near 2 at matched order and that Q9's conclusion — bounds
 becoming relatively worthless as data accumulates — was an artefact. That was wrong twice
 over, and the correction is recorded at 96–98.

 **The order-*k* bracket draws on a wider node set than the estimate it was compared against.** At ν = 40, *k* = 3 the bracket uses **eight** levels and the estimate used **four**. On the same nodes:

  ν          k=1                 2                    3                         4                    5

  20         157                 495                  1,212                     2,582                3,629

  40         673                 4,037                2.2 × 10⁴                 8.5 × 10⁴            3.2 × 10⁶

  80         2,785               3.2 × 10⁴            3.7 × 10⁵                 —                    —

      V grows with order, and steeply. Both quantities improve as nodes are added; the estimate
      improves faster, because a bracket must span the value from both sides while an estimate need
      only approach it.

 **So the trade is the one Chapter 23 has argued throughout, and the order axis does not escape it:**

      A deductive bound is worth most when data is scarce. Its relative value falls
      monotonically as data accumulates — not because the bound gets worse, but because its rival
      gets better faster.

 **And Proposition 15.2's floor applies to the classical bracket only** — first-order, against the linear interpolant on the same two neighbours, where the two objects are locked together. **At higher order they are computed from different node sets and no inequality between them is implied.**

 Figure 16.3. Left: V on the same node set at each order — it grows steeply, from 157 to 3.2 × 10⁶. Right: at ν = 40 both
 the bracket and the estimate improve as nodes are added, and the estimate improves faster; the shaded gap is the price
 of certainty, and it widens. An earlier version of this figure drew a floor at V = 2 through data reaching 0.24, and is
 withdrawn at correction 97.

### 23.10.3 What the order axis does buy
 Not a cheaper guarantee. A narrower one, and a finer detector.

| | order 1 | order 5 |
|---|---|---|
| bracket width at ν = 40 | 0.362 cm⁻¹ | **8.9 × 10⁻⁶** |
| tolerates a displacement of | **0.129 cm⁻¹** | 4.3 × 10⁻⁶ |
| cells from a 10-member channel | 8 | 4 |

      Higher order buys absolute precision and pays in robustness, domain and resolution. A
      collection assembled from real, perturbed, coarsely quoted spectra is exactly where order 1 belongs
      — and V = 4ν/3 is the price of certainty that survives a perturbation of half the local
      spacing.

### 23.10.4 And the admissibility rule generalises
 §23.13 gives r ≥ 5 for the spacing and ν_V for the curvature. Those are two instances of one rule:

      order k is admissible only while \|Δ^(k+1)T\| > 5·2^(k+1)·σ

 **Applied to the collection it separates arithmetic from physics.** A cell whose (*k*+1)-th difference falls below the quotation floor is **unresolved**; one whose difference has the wrong *sign* is **refused**, and that refusal is a perturbation detected deductively.

| order | admitted | held | refused | median bound |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 499 | **499** | 19 | 25.96 cm⁻¹ |
| 3 | 187 | **187** | 101 | 0.715 |
| 6 | 34 | **34** | 145 | **0.0164** |

      Containment is 100% at every order once the premise is checked, the bound tightens
      1,585-fold, and the refusals rise from 3.7% to 81% — the higher the order, the finer the
      perturbation it sees.

 **Which supersedes §25.5's 1,061 order-1 bounds:** the same data at matched order yields 34 bounds at 0.0164 cm⁻¹, **and 619 refusals that are themselves perturbation detections this book did not count.**

### 23.11 The refusal pattern carries four verdicts, not one
 §23.10.4 counts refusals and reads them as "a perturbation". The count is one number; the
 PATTERN is four.

  refusals                       V          what the family is doing
  none                           infinite   exact — linear in the parameter, two points suffice
  none                           finite     a smooth channel
  one                            finite     a bifurcation — the family changes character and does not return
  regularly spaced               finite     an oscillation — the family returns
  dense and irregular            undefine   chaotic
                                 d

 **An index does not see *a* refusal. It sees a family**, and the spacing of the refusals across it is what separates these. §16.7.1 makes the same move for a different pair: the discriminating question is asked of the whole structure, never of one cell.

### 23.11.1 On the collection

  channel                                               membe       refusals               verdict
                                                        rs
  Na I, K I, Al I — eleven channels                     13–52       0                      smooth
  Ga I 4s²np ²P°                                        15          1                      a bifurcation
  Li I np ²P°                                           41          6, irregular           repeated structure

 **Li I *n*p is the channel with the largest quantum-defect spread in the collection, 0.6779** — and the refusal pattern says something the spread does not: **not one perturbation but several, irregularly placed.**

### 23.11.2 And a caution about the test itself
 The classifier requires a tolerance relative to the sequence's own scale. Applied to a
 near-constant sequence with an exact-zero test it reports the float noise as a periodic signal — verified by
 failing on the logistic map at r = 2.5, a fixed point, which it called an oscillation. The admissibility rule
 of §23.10.4 is the tolerance, and it must be applied to the pattern test as well as to the count.

### 23.12 The optimal order is a boundary, not a peak
 Information per cell rises monotonically in k, so no interior optimum exists. What stops the
 climb is σ.

      |Δ^(k+1)T| > 5 · 2^(k+1) · σ — the (k+1)-th difference is a signed sum of 2^(k+1) measured
      levels, so its accumulated quotation error is at most 2^(k+1)σ.

  ν         σ         k\                    \                           Δ^(k+1)T\                                  floor

  20        10⁻²      2                     9.43 × 10⁻¹                 4.0 × 10⁻¹

  20        10⁻³      3                     2.09 × 10⁻¹                 8.0 × 10⁻²

  20        10⁻⁴      4                     7.60 × 10⁻²                 1.6 × 10⁻²

  40        10⁻⁴      2                     2.75 × 10⁻²                 4.0 × 10⁻³

      Better data raises k\. It never creates an interior peak. The optimum is the admissibility cap,
      computable per cell from the data alone.

### 23.13 Inversion
 |p − 1| = 4x/(hV), and the sign is fixed by sign(y′) and sign(y″). Controls recover p = −1 and −3 correctly;
 9 of 10 within 5%.

 **On real data**, each recovered from its own measured series: Na I −1.993, K I −1.998, C₃ 3.999, α 6.990, **C₆ 10.956.**

 Figure 16.4. Left: the fate of every cell in the collection at each order, once the admissibility rule \|Δ^(k+1)T\| >
 5·2^(k+1)·σ separates arithmetic from physics. Right: the refusal rate. A refusal is a measured sequence whose k-th
 difference has the wrong sign — a perturbation, detected deductively, with no fit and no limit. The higher the order, the
 finer the perturbation it sees.

### 23.14 Two kinds of bound, and the book has been calling them
 one
 Every constraint in Λ is a CAPACITY: ℓ ≤ n−1, k ≤ 2(2ℓ+1), g ≤ q. Every admissibility rule is a
 RESOLUTION: r ≥ 5, ν ≤ ν_V, \|Δ^(k+1)T\| > 5·2^(k+1)·σ.

 **They look alike — both are monotone inequalities — and they are not the same kind of thing:**

      A capacity bound is about the world. Violating it means the configuration does not exist. A
      resolution bound is about the measurement. Violating it means the configuration cannot be
      distinguished, and may exist perfectly well.

 **E(X) counts violations of the first. The refusal rate counts violations of the second.** §16.7's whole distinction between a defect of the index and a feature of the world runs along this seam.

### 23.14.1 And celestial mechanics is what made it visible
 Gravity has resolution floors everywhere and a capacity bound in only one place. A planet holds
 satellites within its Hill sphere, r_H = a(m/3M)^{1/3} — a monotone cap of exactly §15.4's form, and
 log r_H against log satellite count runs at +0.86 across six planets.

 **The Kirkwood order cut is the other kind.** Resonance strength enters at *e*^\|*p*−*q*\|; with a belt eccentricity of 0.15 the strength crosses the detection floor at **order 7 exactly**, which is where occupancy stops. **That is not a capacity and there are no magic numbers — only a resolution boundary, the same form as ν_V.**

      Λ and the nucleus have capacity bounds and therefore magic numbers. The belt has
      none, and therefore has none. Atoms carry both kinds in the same place, which is why the
      difference was invisible from inside spectroscopy.

### 23.15 The second admission bound
      ν_V = (3Z²R / 5q)^{1/4}

 for quotation granularity *q*. The bracket needs levels **separated** (ν⁻³, governed by *r*); the cost needs curvature **resolved** (ν⁻⁴, governed by ν_V).

 **Curvature washes out before separation, in every channel in this work.**

| channel | *q* | ν_V | ν reached |
|---|---|---|---|
| K I *n*d | 0.0001 | 160 | 45.7 |
| Na I *n*s | 0.001 | 90 | 20.0 |
| **Al I *n*f** | **0.01** | **50.7** | **55.0** |

 **Al I *n*f is the first channel to reach its own ceiling.** Predicted 50.7; the four failing cells are *n* = 48, 51, 53, 54 — **per cell, not a range cutoff**, since 49, 50 and 52 pass. The bracket is unaffected in all 94: containment needs only monotonicity, which rounding preserves.

 **And in Li I, ν_V was applied prospectively** — eight of fifty-five cells excluded from the cost test *before it ran*, because the ASD's quotation coarsens above *n* = 33. That is the difference between a bound derived and a bound noticed.

## 24. The collection
 **Two collections, and only one of them is bracket-tested.**

 **The bracket has now been run, and it passes 546 of 789 interior cells — 69.2%.** *The test takes the defect each neighbour implies, forms the range they span, and asks whether the middle member's measured energy falls inside. It fails on 243 cells across 62 channels.*

**On the same 789 cells the TRIVIAL bracket — E(n−1) ≤ E(n) ≤ E(n+1) — passes 789 of 789.** *A Rydberg series rises monotonically toward its limit by construction, so that form cannot fail. An earlier version of this chapter read "the bracket holds in every one", and 100% is what the trivial form produces; the implementation behind Appendix B is not in hand, so this is a signature rather than a proof.* **Register 797.**

*The 243 failures are not bad cells. They are places where the series is not locally smooth — which is what P.perturb and P.lens predict, and both were registered before this test was written.*

 *An earlier version of this chapter read "1,442 interior cells across 35 atomic systems, the bracket holds in every one." That figure matches neither Appendix B's totals line (1,105) nor its table (869) nor the present tested count (930), and register 673 already recorded that the totals line counts twenty rows the table does not list. **Four numbers for one collection is a disclosure, not a reconciliation** — reconciling needs the twenty rows, which are owed. Register 783.*

### 24.1 What it spans
  axis                                    span
  ℓ                                       0 through 6
  Z                                       1 through 83
  Z_eff                                   1 through 3
  core types                              23, all characterised

  axis                                          span
  isoelectronic pairs                           5, each at two charge states

 **Cores tested:** bare · 1s ²S · 1s² ¹S₀ · 1s²2s ²S · 2s² ¹S₀ · 2p⁵ ²P° · 2p⁶ ¹S₀ · 3s ²S · 3s² ¹S₀ · 3s²3p ²P° · 3p⁴ · 3p⁵ ²P° · 3p⁶ ¹S₀ · 3p⁶4s ²S · 3d¹⁰ ¹S₀ · 3d⁹ ²D (declined) · 3d¹⁰4s² ¹S₀ · 4p⁵ ²P° · 4p⁶5s ²S · 4d¹⁰ ¹S₀ · 5p⁶ ¹S₀ · 5p⁶6s ²S · 5d¹⁰ ¹S₀ · 3d4s ³D · 6p² ³P.

 Figure 17.1. Every species in the collection, defect against angular momentum. The fall is monotone in all of them, and
 spans six orders of magnitude — from 4.9 in bismuth to 1.6 × 10⁻⁶ in He II.

### 24.2 The largest contributors
  species                 core                                                  channels                cells
  He I                    1s ²S                                                 9                       189
  Ne I                    2p⁵ ²P°, two limits                                   16                      131
  KI                      3p⁶ ¹S₀                                               4                       105
  Al I                    3s² ¹S₀                                               4                       94
  Na I                    2p⁶ ¹S₀                                               6                       80
  Ga I                    3d¹⁰4s² ¹S₀                                           5                       61
  Li I                    1s² ¹S₀                                               3                       55

 Figure 17.2. What the collection covers. Marker area is proportional to cells. The gaps are not omissions: the census of
 §24.12 excludes them structurally.

### 24.3 He I — the angular span
 Nine channels, ℓ = 0 through 6, singlets and triplets, n to 35. And the defect falls monotonically with ℓ:

    0.2965 (s³) · 0.1392 (s¹) · −0.0133 (p) · +0.0014 (d) · −0.0010 (f) · −0.0014 (g) · −0.0016 (i) —
    each a mean over its full channel, twenty members for ni and twenty-three for the two ns channels

 **An electron with six units of angular momentum never reaches the 1s core.**

 **The ordering itself is not new, and this book does not claim it.** Edlén stated it in the 1960 manuscript of his *Handbuch* chapter, and Curtis restates the criterion in his 1987 review:

    "classical arguments imply that, for a given Rydberg series, penetration contributions should
    decrease and polarisation contributions should increase with increasing n … this criterion classifies
    the S and P levels as penetrating and the D and F levels as nonpenetrating."

 What is new is the bound the ordering licenses. Edlén used it to classify levels; §32.5 uses it to place
 a deductive upper limit on the local perturbation at 1,061 cells. The observation is sixty-five years
 old. The inference from it is not.

 He I's levels are all theoretical — **but they are ab initio QED calculations, not Ritz series formulas.** That distinction matters and §24.7 develops it.

### 24.4 He II — the δ = 0 edge itself
 A hydrogenic ion at Z = 2. Its defects run 7.6 × 10⁻⁵ at ℓ = 0 down to 1.6 × 10⁻⁶ at ℓ = 6, monotone
 across six orders of magnitude, and never reach zero.

 **There is no core to penetrate.** The residual is relativistic and QED structure, and it falls with ℓ exactly as those corrections do.

 **And the ionisation limit came out of the data.** Minimising the δ spread over the *n*g channel — six members, scanned at 0.001 cm⁻¹ — gives **438,908.871 cm⁻¹** against the tabulated 438,908.885 — **agreement to 0.014 cm⁻¹, three parts in 10⁸**, from a criterion that never used the tabulated value.

### 24.5 Bi I — the charge span
 Atomic number 83, more than double anything previously tested. The 6p³ ground term is strongly j-j
 coupled and the levels carry jK labels throughout. δ̄ = 4.90 for the ns channel, over five members —
 the largest defect in this work, as expected when a 6s-like electron penetrates a filled 5d¹⁰6s² core.

 **And Bi I settles a rule.** Its core carries five J levels — the same dilution that made neon II fail entirely — but its ³P₀ parent reaches *n* = 11.

    Dilution divides. It does not forbid. cells per channel ≈ (measured shells / D) − 2

 Three species establish it: Ne II fails at five J levels and two shells; Ar II succeeds at five and three; Bi I succeeds at five and five.

### 24.6 The isoelectronic pairs
  sequence                                    Z_eff = 1                         Z_eff = 2
  helium-like                                 He I                              Li II
  lithium-like                                Li I                              Be II
  sodium-like                                 Na I                              Mg II
  aluminium-like                              Al I                              Si II
  potassium-like                              KI                                Ca II
