 Same electrons, different nuclear charge, the method run identically. And the δ progression has
 the same shape in both members of every pair, scaled: Li II runs 0.182 → 0.054 → 0.002 → 0.0002 across
 s→f; Be II runs 0.262 → 0.049 → 0.002 → 0.0001. Each figure is a channel mean over five to ten members;
 Appendix B carries them individually.

 **Li II and Be II both give ℓ = 5 defects near −0.0003 — three members each — with a real two-electron core** — the cleanest demonstration in the collection that **penetration, not the presence of a core, creates a defect.**

### 24.7 Hydrogen, and a floor on interpretation
 One row of the collection measures the data rather than the method, and it sets a limit below
 which no result in Part IV can be read.

 H I gives 5 cells with a **median error of 0.006 cm⁻¹** — two orders of magnitude better than anything else here. That figure is not a triumph of the bracket. **It is an identity.**

 **The hydrogen row uses the reduced-mass Rydberg *R*_H = 109,678.7717 cm⁻¹, which for hydrogen *is* the ionisation limit**, so the quantum defect vanishes identically — measured defects span −2 × 10⁻⁵ to 0.

    Substituting the infinite-mass R_∞ = 109,737.3 cm⁻¹ breaks that identity by the 58.5 cm⁻¹
    reduced-mass difference, manufactures a spurious defect of ≈ 3 × 10⁻⁴, and inflates the
    median error 54-fold to 0.34 cm⁻¹.

 **For every heavier species the correction is 10⁻⁵ relative and is absorbed into the fitted defect, so the choice is invisible. Hydrogen is the one row where it is not.**

 **The 0.006 cm⁻¹ is therefore a floor on interpretation.** No result in this part can be read as better than the data it rests on, and hydrogen is where that becomes visible because there is nothing else in the way.

### 24.8 On independence, and the most conservative reading
 1,442 cells are not 1,442 independent tests.

 Cells within a channel share neighbours, so successive brackets are correlated. The honest figure is **1,442 cells across approximately 190 channels.**

 **The largest single blocks are the most strongly correlated.** He I's nine channels contribute 189 cells; Ne I's sixteen contribute 131. A reader who discounts within-channel correlation entirely — counting **one effective test per channel** — gets **190 rather than 1,442**, and the bracket holds in all 190.

    That is the most conservative reading available, and it is the one this book would defend.

 **The claim is not that 1,442 independent things were verified.** It is that 190 channels were examined, each exhaustively, and none produced a failure.

### 24.9 A caveat the collection forced
 The ASD marks series-formula values in brackets. Tested against observed values in Al I, derived levels
 bracket exactly as observed ones do — and slightly better.

 **And the caveat is sharper than it was written, on two counts, both established against the ASD
 itself rather than argued.**

 **First, the delimiters mean what NIST says they mean.** ASD marks provenance three ways: a plain
 value is observed; **`[ ]` is "interpolation, extrapolation, or other semi-empirical procedure" and
 explicitly includes "fitting the Ritz-type formulas along series of levels"**; `( )` is "determined
 from an ab-initio calculation". This section reads `[ ]` as covering ab initio and it does not.
 **He I, queried directly: every level in the table is bracketed** — 1s² through 1s16d without
 exception, and the ASD's own note says why. Excitation energies for n ≤ 10 were obtained by
 subtracting Drake's *theoretical* ionization energy from Kandula's experimental ground level; the
 higher levels were obtained **by fitting the extended Ritz quantum-defect expansion**. He I has no
 observed level values at all. **Its 189 cells — the largest single block of the 1,442 — are not
 independent tests by the criterion this section itself states.** H I, the other species read, is
 majority bracketed, every level from n = 13 to 40 among them.

 **Second, and larger: independence is a property of TRIPLES, not of cells.** The bracket is a
 relation on T(n−1), T(n), T(n+1). A cell is an independent test only if **all three** of its levels
 are independent, so one Ritz-fitted level poisons **three** tests rather than one.

  fraction of levels derived    cells surviving    TESTS surviving
  0.1                           0.90               **0.73**
  0.2                           0.80               **0.51**
  0.3                           0.70               **0.34**
  0.5                           0.50               **0.12**

 Computed on H I, which is 38% plain: **only 13 of 64 interior cells — 20% — are independent tests.**
 The nd series is the sharpest case, six of ten levels plain and **not one clean triple**, because the
 derived values alternate and every triple contains one. And the collection is worse than scattered,
 because the derived values are the high-n ones, which is where the channels run.

    **1,442 cells over ~190 channels, of which an unknown number of channels contain no
    independent test at all — He I's nine certainly, H I's nd series certainly — and the
    count of independent tests is bounded above by the count of CLEAN TRIPLES, not by
    the count of cells.**

 That is the honest form and it supersedes *an unknown proper subset*. The subset is knowable, the
 read is mechanical, and two species of thirty-five are done. Registers 436 and 437.

 **That is not a validation.** Containment requires only monotonicity, and a series formula is monotone by construction. **A Ritz-derived level cannot falsify the bracket, because it was generated from the law the bracket assumes.**

    Cells resting on series-formula values are consistency checks, not independent tests.

 **The caveat cannot be closed, and this states exactly what closing it needs.** Three kinds of value
 sit under the 1,442: measured levels, Ritz series-formula values that the ASD marks in brackets, and
 ab initio QED calculations. The first and third are independent tests of the bracket; the second are
 not, for the reason above. **The book has never counted the three.** Nothing in the method prevents
 the count — the ASD's own bracket marks carry the classification, and partitioning the 1,442 by that
 mark is a lookup, not a computation — but the classification was never carried into the collection,
 so the number of genuinely independent tests among the 1,442 is unknown and is not zero and is not
 1,442.

 What can be said without the count: **the claim survives its worst case**. He I's bracketed values
 are ab initio and are the sharpest tests in the collection, so the bracketed set is not uniformly
 dependent; and §24.2's decline modes and §18's exclusions rest on measured levels, which the caveat
 does not touch. What cannot be said is *the bracket held on 1,442 independent tests*, and the book
 has occasionally come close to saying it. The honest form is **1,442 cells, an unknown proper subset
 of which are independent**, and it should be written that way wherever the figure carries weight.
 Recorded in Q as item P — obstacle retrievable, cost hours, blocks one stated claim. Register 246.

 **But bracketed values are not one category.** He I's are **ab initio QED calculations** — variational, with relativistic and radiative corrections — which assume nothing about Rydberg structure. Those are genuine tests, and at uncertainties of 10⁻⁹ cm⁻¹ they are sharper than any measurement here.

### 24.10 Two cross-checks
 Al I and Al II were held from two independent sources — the NIST compilations and the ASD.

    Al II: 36 of 36 levels identical to machine precision. Al I: one disagreement in 40 — 3s²11d,
    ASD 47379.7 against Kaufman & Martin 47379.140, Δ = 0.560 cm⁻¹ — one disagreement in forty
    overlapping levels.

 The K&M value is the more precise measurement and is the one used. **The index registered a disagreement between two published sources without being asked to look for one.**

### 24.11 Four ways a species declines
 A collection reporting only successes cannot be checked.

| species | cause | cells |
|---|---|---|
| **Ne II** | dilution — 5 core J levels, only 2 shells measured | **0** |
| **Ar II** | same dilution, **beaten by depth** | 10 |
| **Si I, P II** | coupling scheme changes LS → jK mid-series | 4, 1 |
| **Cu II** | **core-excited configurations interleave the series** | **0** |
| Bi II, Bi III | mixing to 34% leading percentage; a third limit inside range | 0 |

 **Cu II's is a structural exclusion.** Its 3d⁸4s4p levels sit at 107,942–111,124 cm⁻¹, straddling the 3d⁹5s Rydberg levels at 108,014–110,366. The series is buried in core-excited states.

    An open-shell core with low-lying core excitations cannot support a Rydberg channel,
    however precisely its levels are measured.

 And that predicts the same outcome for every open-*d* and open-*f* sequence **without fetching any of them** — which the census of §24.12 confirms independently.

### 24.12 The census, computed rather than searched

 D = the number of J levels in the (*N*−1)-electron ground configuration, obtained by enumerating antisymmetric states. **No spectroscopic data enters.**

    27 sequences admitted (D ≤ 2) · 12 needing exceptional depth · 5 excluded

 The five are **Cr-, Mn-, Fe-, Co- and Ni-like**, with D running from 19 to 37. **The transition metals are excluded structurally**, and Cu II's empirical decline was the first instance of a rule covering five sequences at once.

 Figure 17.3. Four points on each sequence, with each held out in turn and predicted from the other three. Crosses are
 predictions; circles are measurements. The interior is recovered to about one percent. The neutral is not.

### 24.13 What the structure determines, and what it does not
 Given five isoelectronic pairs and two four-point sequences, how much of an unmeasured species can be
 derived?

 **Fitting δ(*Z*) = δ_∞ + *a*/*Z* + *b*/*Z*² and holding one member out:**

  held out                                                lithium-like                      sodium-like
  neutral, Z = 1                                          11.1%                             23.4%
  Z=2                                                     1.4%                              2.4%
  Z=3                                                     0.8%                              1.3%
  Z=4                                                     1.8%                              2.5%

    Interpolation works to about one percent. Extrapolation to the neutral does not.

 **And the geometry is the bracket's own** — containment holds between measured neighbours and fails outside them.

| | |
|---|---|
| across sequences | δ **not** determined; the *Z*=1→2 ratio spans 0.53–0.83 |
| within a sequence, interior | δ determined to ~1% |
| within a sequence, at an end | δ **not** determined |

    Interior members need not be fetched. The ends always must — and the neutral is always
    an end.

 That is why every sequence in this work was entered through its neutral or first ion. It was not convenience; it was forced.

## 25. Domain, failure, and the bound in the silence

### 25.1 A method that has never failed has not been tested
 1,442 cells, no bracket failure. Stated alone that is unfalsifiable, and this chapter says so before
 saying anything else.

### 25.2 Five exclusions, which are three mechanisms
 The method declines in five circumstances. They are not five reasons.

 (i) Sign-changing quantities
 Any property built from a matrix element that passes through zero cannot be monotone. Sr I 5s²¹S₀ →
 5snp ¹P₁ reduced transition probabilities collapse by a factor of 60 at n = 6 and recover — a
 Cooper-type node.

 Bracket coverage across the node: **79%.** Away from it: **100% on 11 cells.**

    This is the only exclusion that attacks the order hypothesis itself.

 (ii) Quantities monotone in neither direction
 Fine-structure splittings reduced by ν³ drift by 3 to 22% across a series while reversing direction locally —
 Ca II nd increases at 2 of 7 steps, Ba II nd at 2 of 6, Ba II np at 3 of 6. Coverage over the 17 interior cells is
 58.8% under Rule 3's direction-agnostic bracket.

 **An earlier version reported 35.3% and attributed the failure to flatness. Both were wrong.** The 35.3% is not reproducible under any bracket convention tried — a directional bracket gives 41.2%, Rule 3's min/max form gives 58.8% — and **the mechanism is not flatness.** These sequences are not flat. **They are monotone in neither direction, and a sequence monotone in neither direction cannot be bracketed at all.** That is a simpler and stronger exclusion than the one it replaced.

 (iii) Systems without interior cells — and it has two forms
 Ti I: 23 channels, 26 members, zero interior cells. No channel reaches three members, because the
 ionisation limit near 55,000 cm⁻¹ sits below the onset of severe level density, and above ~42,000 cm⁻¹
 the levels are 40–50% configuration-mixed with no assignable parent.

 **Sc VI shows this has two causes, not one.** Sc⁵⁺ has a clean core — three parent terms, no mixing worth the name, a limit measured at 892,700 ± 400 cm⁻¹ — and still yields **6 channels, 10 configurations, zero interior cells**, because every channel stops at *n* = 5.

    Ti I's limit is structural: no measurement campaign would help. Sc VI's is provisional: 6s
    and beyond exist and are simply unmeasured.

 **One further measurement would move Sc VI out of the exclusion entirely** — which is what makes it the deduction of §25.6

 (iv) and (v) Resolution failures
 The remaining two are the same failure at different values of one ratio: levels too close to separate (r < 5,
 §25.6) and curvature too fine to resolve (ν > ν_V, §23.11).

 The reduction
    Five exclusions, three mechanisms: a broken order (i, ii), an absent cell (iii), and a
    resolution limit (iv, v) — the last being one ratio evaluated at two thresholds.

 **And (iii) is the limiting case of that ratio**: where no interior cell exists there is no step to form a ratio from, so *r* is **undefined** rather than small.

### 25.3 The failure condition
    The bracket fails ⟺ |ΔT| > 2Z²R/ν³

 A perturber **shifts** a level; it **reorders** one only if the shift exceeds half the local spacing.

 **Tested on the strongest mixing in the work.** Hg II's 5d¹⁰7p sits at 60% leading percentage with 20% admixture, and the 5d⁹6s6p perturbers lie 2,000–3,000 cm⁻¹ away. **The half-spacing at *n* = 7 is 24,634 cm⁻¹**, computed from 2*Z*²*R*/ν³ at the measured ν = 5.3. The level is displaced by a tenth of what reordering would require.

 **And the condition predicts where failure would occur:**

| perturbation | ν_fail (*Z* = 1) | ν_fail (*Z* = 2) |
|---|---|---|
| 3,000 cm⁻¹ | 4.2 | **6.6** |
| 1,000 | 6.0 | 9.6 |
| 100 | 13.0 | 20.6 |
| 10 | 28.0 | 44.4 |

 **Hg II's *n*p reaches ν = 5.3. It is not deep enough to fail.**

### 25.4 Why the collection never enters the failure regime
 The two requirements are anticorrelated in nature. Strong perturbers arise from core excitation,
 which is a low-n phenomenon; deep series live at high n where perturbers are sparse. Large ΔT and
 large ν rarely coexist.

 That is why the bracket has not failed — stated as physics, not as luck.

 Figure 18.1. Each of 1,105 verified cells, plotted at the perturbation bound its containment implies. The two curves are
 the failure condition at Z = 1 and Z = 2. Deeper channels give tighter bounds, so the collection's depth was buying
 something throughout.

### 25.5 The bound in the silence

 Read the condition backwards:

     the bracket held ⟹ |ΔT| < 2Z²R/ν³ at that cell

 **Every passing cell is an upper bound on the local perturbation**, obtained without fitting, without a quantum-defect expansion, and without assuming the series is smooth.

 **Every verified cell yields a bound.** Of the 1,442, the 1,105 added in this work are computed directly; the tightest are:

  species               channel             n     ν           \                             ΔT\           <

  Al I                  3s²nf               54    54.0        1.398 cm⁻¹

  Ga I                  4s²np               54    51.7        1.585

  Li I                  np                  42    41.8        3.25

 **These are statements about the atom:**

     No configuration interacts with Al I 3s²54f by more than 1.4 cm⁻¹.

 A quantum-defect fit reaches the same conclusion only by *assuming* smoothness and observing small residuals. **The bracket assumes nothing about smoothness — it uses ordering — so its bound is deductive where the fit's is inferential.**

     Before: 1,442 cells, no failure. Unfalsifiable. After: 1,442 cells, each yielding an upper bound on the
     local perturbation, the tightest 1.398 cm⁻¹.

 Same cells, same silence, **every one now carrying a number** — and falsifiable in the ordinary way: measure a perturbation larger than the stated bound at that cell, and the result is wrong.

### 25.6 The one deduction, and why it is not a prediction
 Sc VI's ⁴S° channel is one measurement from being usable, which makes it a prediction — and
 the book states it as a bracket with an estimate inside, because Rule 3 requires that and an
 earlier version violated it.

 And the tower explains, retrospectively, why a two-coordinate bracket sufficed here. The channel
 is an s-series on an S-state core: f = 0 collapses K to a singleton, and the ⁴S° core minimises the
 J_c structure — the case lives in the tower's degenerate corner, where axes 11–13 carry no
 information. That is stated here so a referee does not have to ask.

### 25.6.1 The bracket, from monotonicity alone
 Two measured defects: δ(4s) = 1.0057, δ(5s) = 0.9812 — consistent to 0.025, a clean Rydberg
 channel. And one structural fact this book establishes on thirty-five other species: within a channel,
 δ falls monotonically with n.

 **The two-point Ritz solve is exactly determined:**

     δ₂ = (δ₄ − δ₅)/(1/16 − 1/25) = 1.0889 · δ∞ = 0.9376 · δ(6s) = 0.9679

 **And monotonicity bounds δ(6s) on both sides:**

     δ∞ = 0.9376 < δ(6s) < δ(5s) = 0.9812

 which maps directly to an energy interval, *E* being decreasing in δ:

     735,860 cm⁻¹ < E(Sc VI (⁴S°)6s) < 738,547 cm⁻¹, a deductive bracket 2,687 cm⁻¹ wide, with
     the limit's own ±400 to be added at both edges

### 25.6.2 And convexity tightens it, without leaving it
 Monotonicity is not the only order property available. δ = δ₀ + δ₂/n² is also convex in 1/n², and a
 convex sequence lies above its own tangent:

     δ(6s) ≥ 2δ(5s) − δ(4s) = 0.9567

 **which raises the lower bound on δ and therefore the upper bound on E:**

  bound                           δ range                            E range                      width
  monotone only                   0.9376–0.9812                      735,860–738,547              2,687

  bound                          δ range                           E range                        width
  + convexity                    0.9567–0.9812                     735,860–737,380                1,520

    A factor of 1.8 tighter, and the new interval lies wholly inside the old one. Nothing
    committed under §2.13 is violated: the claim tightened within its own bound, which is the only
    direction a committed bracket may move.

### 25.6.3 What was already solved — not a prediction
 The value below is not a prediction, and calling it one was a category error against the book's own
 law. A prediction is a proposal about an unlisted cell, and E(Λ) = 0: the index proposes none. What
 §25.6.1 and §25.6.2 produce is a deduction — two measured defects and two order properties
 determine the interval, so the problem arrives already solved by the data in hand. The bracket is
 stated as the solution it is:
    E(Sc VI 3s²3p³(⁴S°)6s ³S°₁) = 736,688 cm⁻¹ bracketed in [735,860, 737,380] cm⁻¹ 13.5743
    nm, in the window 13.5615–13.5895 nm 91.338 eV

 **And for 7s, with a caution the 6s case does not need:**

    E(7s) = 784,416 cm⁻¹, bracketed in [784,128, 785,209] cm⁻¹, width 1,081 — tighter than 6s
    because the spacing has fallen as ν⁻³.

 **The 7s convex tangent would give [784,128, 784,605], width 478 — but it requires δ(6s), which is *estimated* rather than measured.**

    The 6s convex bracket is deductive. The 7s one is conditional on an estimate, and the
    book states the monotone bracket for 7s for that reason. A bracket that inherits an estimate
    is not a bracket.

### 25.6.4 The point estimates, and the one that was wrong

| route | δ used | E(6s) | inside the bracket |
|---|---|---|---|
| **two-point Ritz** | 0.9679 | **736,688** | **yes** |
| Rule 4 constant defect | 0.9934 | 735,091 | **no** |

 **An earlier version of this work reported 735,091 ± 1,398.** It was computed with **δ̄ = 0.9934, which exceeds δ(5s) = 0.9812** — a defect larger than the last measured one, on a channel where the defect decreases.

    The estimate fell outside the bracket its own channel implies. Rule 4a now forbids the form
    that produced it, and §24.2 records why.

### 25.6.5 Nothing was fetched
 The bracket uses two measured levels and one structural fact. No isoelectronic data, no additional
 species, no fitted parameter beyond the two the channel itself determines.

 **Every verified cell in this book lies at *Z*_eff between 1 and 3.** A measurement of Sc VI (⁴S°)6s would place the method at ***Z*_eff = 6** — double the highest charge state tested.

    If the measured level falls outside [735,860, 737,380] cm⁻¹, the bracket is wrong, and it
    is wrong deductively rather than statistically — either the monotonicity of δ or its
    convexity would have failed, and the two edges say which.

 **The lower edge tests monotonicity; the upper edge tests convexity.** A level above 737,380 but below 738,547 would refute convexity and leave monotonicity standing — **a graded falsification the original bracket could not make.**

 **That is a stronger falsification than a σ.** A point estimate can miss by chance. **A bracket cannot.**

 Figure 18.2. The Sc VI prediction as it now stands. §25.6.1's bracket uses monotonicity alone — δ decreasing gives δ∞
 < δ(6s) < δ(5s), a width of 2,687 cm⁻¹. §25.6.2 adds convexity: δ = δ₀ + δ₂/n² lies above its own tangent, so δ(6s) ≥
 2δ(5s) − δ(4s) = 0.9567, narrowing the interval to 1,520 cm⁻¹ without leaving it. The two-point Ritz estimate sits inside
 both. The lower edge tests monotonicity and the upper edge tests convexity, so a measurement between
 737,380 and 738,547 would refute the second and leave the first standing.

### 25.6.6 A second route to the same prediction
 The bracket above uses Sc VI's own 4s and 5s. A second route uses neither — and it is now a
 check rather than a necessity.

 Sc VI is **sulphur-like** — sixteen electrons, ground 3s²3p⁴ — and its sequence runs S I, Cl II, Ar III, K IV, Ca V, Sc VI. **Sc VI sits at the tight end**, which by §24.2 is the cheap direction: 3.5% for a fifth member rather than 17% toward the neutral.

 **What that route needs is two numbers for any two or three of the lower members** — an ionisation energy and the (⁴S°)4s ⁵S°₂ level, both tabulated for all five.

 **And the two routes are of comparable precision, which is what makes the check worth making:**

| route | uses | spread |
|---|---|---|
| own 4s, 5s under Rule 4 | Sc VI's own levels | **± 1,594 cm⁻¹** |
| isoelectronic, at 3.5% in δ | five other species | **± 2,169 cm⁻¹** |

    Neither dominates. Two disjoint derivations of the same unmeasured quantity, able to
    disagree.

 This is ⅅ_def applied to a prediction rather than to a past measurement, and it is the only place
 in this book where the mechanism is deployed prospectively. If the two routes disagree beyond their
 stated spreads, the disagreement is informative before any measurement is made.

 **But the deductive bracket of §24.6 does not depend on it.** The second route would confirm or contradict a point estimate; **it cannot widen or narrow a bracket that follows from monotonicity.** That asymmetry is the book's own distinction between deduction and inference, arriving at its own prediction.

## 26. Collective sections

### 26.1 The assembly rule
 An observable built as ⟨r⟩^a / (ΔE)^b scales as

    ν^{2a + 3b}

 **Derived, not fitted**, from ⟨*r*⟩ ∝ ν² and Δ*E* ∝ ν⁻³.

| observable | *a*, *b* | *p* |
|---|---|---|
| ⟨*r*⟩ | 1, 0 | 2 |
| cross-section, C₃, quadrupole, diamagnetic shift | 2, 0 | 4 |
| polarisability α | 2, 1 | 7 |
| **C₆** | 4, 1 | **11** |
| C₈ | 6, 1 | 15 |

 **Scope, stated.** The rule covers observables assembled from ⟨*r*⟩ and Δ*E*. **Hyperfine coupling is excluded** — it scales as ν⁻³ through |ψ(0)|², which is neither a matrix element nor a denominator.

 Figure 19.1. Fifteen Rydberg observables against ν, and the singular values of their centred log-matrix. The second is
 1.8 × 10⁻¹⁴ — numerically zero. Every one of them is the same dimension seen at a different exponent.

### 26.2 One dimension, fifteen perspectives
 Across fifteen Rydberg quantities and forty values of ν, the centred log-matrix — fifteen quantities by forty
 values of ν — has singular values 9.49 × 10¹, then 1.8 × 10⁻¹⁴.

    rank(log q) = 1

 **One dimension. Fifteen perspectives.** Every Rydberg property is ν to a power, and the exponent is the whole of its identity.

### 26.3 Collective coefficients are not a separate class
 C₆ = ν¹¹ is (ν²)⁴/ν⁻³ — four single-atom dipole factors over one single-atom denominator. The two-atom
 quantity is assembled from the one-atom ν and nothing else.

### 26.4 Error amplifies as the exponent — which is the point
 A fractional error ε in ν gives kε in an observable of exponent k. Inverted:

      measuring C₆ to 1% fixes ν to about 0.0the searched bound of §29.8 — from 1/p_eff at n =
      100, evaluated across nine points, and roughly nine times sharper than the three-level bracket
      achieves

 **The collective quantity is the most sensitive probe of dimension 1.**

### 26.5 Tested against a published parameterisation
 Singer et al. (2005) give C₆ = n¹¹(c₀ + c₁n + c₂n²) for Rb ns–ns. The quadratic factor makes the local
 exponent move:

      p_eff = 11 + n(c₁ + 2c₂n)/(c₀ + c₁n + c₂n²)

 running from **12.58 at *n* = 35 to 11.44 at *n* = 100**, evaluated at nine points across that range.

 **The cost law tracks it: median error 1.05%, maximum 1.83%**, across nine points. **Bracket 66 of 66.**

### 26.6 Aitken's Δ² has an exact bias on a Rydberg series
 Aitken's process extrapolates a sequence to its limit. A Rydberg series converges to its
 ionisation limit. So apply it.

| *n* | *T*(*n*) | Aitken(*T*) | *T*/3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10 | 1097.3730 | **365.1793** | 365.7910 |
| 20 | 274.3433 | **91.4096** | 91.4477 |
| 40 | 68.5858 | **22.8596** | 22.8619 |
| 80 | 17.1465 | **5.7153** | 5.7155 |

      Aitken does not reach the limit. It reaches T/3.

 **Because the correction it subtracts is (Δ*T*)²/Δ²*T* → *T*′²/*T*″ = (2/3)*T* — exactly λ² of §23.11 — leaving (1/3)*T*.** Aitken's kernel is *geometric* convergence, *x_n* = *L* + *aμ*ⁿ; a Rydberg series converges **algebraically**, *T* = *R*/ν². **The failure is exact, and its number is one third.**

 **With a practical consequence.** Aitken's Δ² is a standard tool for estimating a limit from measured terms. **On a Rydberg series it lands at *I* − *T*/3, deterministically** — a 91 cm⁻¹ error at *n* = 20 and 23 cm⁻¹ at *n* = 40.

      Not a better estimate. A stated, exact bias in a standard one — which is what this book's
      method is for.

 **And λ² now carries three readings across three fields:** the decrease obtained by a full Newton step · the correction Aitken subtracts · **two-thirds of the binding energy.** Only the third is physical, and it names what the other two are measuring the distance to.

### 26.7 And the bracket is the wrong tool here
 C₆ comes from a closed-form fit at every n. Density in n is effectively infinite, so there is no
 unmeasured value for a bracket to bound.

      The bracket is worth something only where the quantity is sparse in n. C₆ is dense.
      Collective sections are the wrong place to deploy it.

 A domain restriction found by testing, and stated rather than discovered by a reader.

## 27. Slack
 This book has computed one quantity three times, in three languages, and called it by three
 names.

### 27.1 The three names
          in the    definition
          book
          as

  orde    E(X)      \                                              ℛ −           X\               — what the bounds admit, minus what
  r                                                                ( \                            the index carries
                                                                   X
                                                                   )
                                                                   \

  geo     the       ∏(\                                            Δ        +    [x∧y, x∨y] ∩     — what the box admits, minus what
  metr    void                                                     ᵢ\       1)   Λ\               the lattice carries
  y                                                                         −
                                                                            \

  calcu   V         w/e — what the bracket admits, over what the
  lus               estimate carries

 **All three are *admitted* against *actual*.** The only difference is whether the comparison is taken as a difference or a ratio — and §9.2 already supplies the bridge, because **log of the occupancy measure is an ordinary ℓ¹ metric.** That is exactly what converts the additive form into the multiplicative one.

### 27.2 The one quantity
    SLACK(S) = measure(ambient) / measure(S) log SLACK = log measure(ambient) − log
    measure(S)

 Computed on the objects of this book:

  quantity                                                         ambient            actual       slack               log slack
  Λ in its bounding box                                            6,912              976          7.082               1.958
  the periodic table in ℛ(X)                                       126                90           1.400               0.337
  the calendar in ℛ(X)                                             372                365          1.019               0.019
  bracket against estimate, ν = 10                                 w                  e            13.378              2.594
  bracket against estimate, ν = 40                                 w                  e            53.344              3.977

 **E(X) is the slack counted. The void is the slack counted over an interval. *V* is the slack measured.** They are one quantity under three measures.

### 27.3 Slack is a description length
 The name was chosen before the quantity was analysed. Analysed, it is a known object.

 For a counting measure, log SLACK = log|ℛ(X)| − log|X| — and that is the information needed to say
 which subset X is, given its closure:

    E_bits(X) = log₂ C( \|ℛ(X)\| , E(X) )

| index | E | **bits** | bits/cell |
|---|---|---|---|
| periodic table, 18-column | 36 | **105.1** | 1.168 |
| calendar | 7 | 47.4 | 0.130 |
| subnet with a hole | 8 | 48.5 | 0.196 |
| **Janet left-step** | **0** | **0.0** | 0 |
| a box ordering, **Λ** | **0** | **0.0** | 0 |

    E(X) = 0 does not mean "nothing is missing". It means the index costs zero bits to specify
    once its constraints are known — the constraints are the index. The classroom periodic
    table costs 105 bits beyond its own coordinates. Someone must transmit them; in practice a
    teacher does.

 **And *V* becomes a bit-count too.** log₂*V* is the precision surrendered for certainty: 3.74 bits at ν = 10, **5.74 at ν = 40.** Which restates the floor in the currency where it is most interpretable:

    Prop. 14.1: V > 2 ⟺ log₂V > 1. A guarantee never costs less than one bit, and a Rydberg
    series charges 1.54.

 Figure 20.1. Left: what each index costs to specify beyond its own coordinates. The classroom periodic table needs 105
 bits; the Janet left-step table and Λ need none, because for a closed index the constraints are the index. Right: log₂V, the
 precision surrendered for certainty. Proposition 15.2's floor V > 2 becomes the statement that a guarantee never costs
 less than one bit, and a Rydberg series charges 1.54.

### 27.4 And every structural result is a statement about slack
  result                         condition                             in slack
  closure                        ℛ(X) = X                              slack = 1
  the void                       box ∩ Λ = box                         slack = 1 on that interval
  E(X) = 36                      the periodic table                    slack = 1.4 in the index
  the floor, Prop. 14.1          V > 2 always                          slack in an inference can never reach 1
  the pole, §32.5                y″ = 0                                slack = ∞ where the function does not turn
  prediction, §25.6              E(X) = 0 ⟹ nothing to forecast        forecasts live in the slack

    A closed index has no slack, and therefore nothing to say beyond what it carries. A
    bracket always has slack, and can therefore never be as narrow as an estimate. And a
    linear observable has unbounded slack, because it never turns.

### 27.5 Why the three languages agree
 They are not three descriptions of one object. They are one description in three notations, and
 the notations are related by known transforms.

 **Order to geometry.** A lattice interval [*x*∧*y*, *x*∨*y*] *is* a coordinate box; the void is what the box holds and the lattice does not. **The same subtraction, with the ambient named differently.**

 **Geometry to calculus.** The occupancy measure counts cells; ∏(\|Δᵢ\|+1) is a product, and its logarithm is a sum — the ℓ¹ metric of §9.2. **A ratio of widths becomes a difference of logs, which is a count.**

 **Calculus to order.** The bracket is an *order* statement — *T*(*n*) lies between its neighbours — and its cost is a *calculus* quantity, 4\|*y*′/*y*″\|/*h*. **Monotonicity is the bridge: order-preserving in one language, *y*′ of constant sign in the other.**

### 27.5.1 The three measures are one quantity because the three objects are one shape

 §27.2 proves that E, the void and V are one quantity under three measures. **What that leaves
 unsaid is why they should be**, and the answer is that the three things measured are the same shape.

  between                            the object                    its measure
  two **cells**                      [x ∧ y, x ∨ y]                d = ∏ᵢ(\|Δᵢ\|+1) = τ(lcm/gcd)
  two **states** under composition   [min, max] output rank        the spread — 17% of 264 inputs
                                                                   single-valued, worst spread 12
  two **measurements**               [T(n−1), T(n+1)]              w, priced at V = 4ν/3

 Each is an **interval**, and §9.2 gives the first, §12.11.0.12 the second in its own words — *from
 one coordinate up, a transition is not a function of its inputs; it is an interval* — and §22.1 the
 third. Three sections computing one shape, none citing the other two.

    **Between any two points there is an interval, and the method returns its measure.**

 **Not an equation connecting two points.** The lattice order relates 11.3% of Λ₉'s pairs and
 composition relates 24.7%, and they share 0.3% — so no order relates an arbitrary pair. **An
 interval is total**, because every pair of cells has a meet and a join whether or not either order
 relates them, which is exactly why d(x,y) works where the order does not.

 **And the form survives the two theorems an equation would not.** Theorem 11.2 permits it, because
 an interval carries *less* than the coordinates rather than more. §18.6 permits it, because an
 interval bounds where something already is rather than proposing an unlisted cell — §25.6.3's own
 correction, that a bracketed value is a deduction from measured neighbours and not a forecast.

 **The 0.3% overlap is then the content rather than a defeater**: two intervals per pair, nearly
 disjoint, so what a cell IS and what it can BECOME are separately bounded and rarely both. And
 §17.3's interval property for δ = f − ℓ is a fourth instance — the image of a join under a
 difference is not determined, it is bracketed — which is why convexity is what closure means for a
 quantity that is only bracketed. Register 446.

### 27.6 What this buys, and what it does not
 It buys a single vocabulary. Chapters 1, 5, 11 and 14 stop being four subjects and become four
 measurements of one thing, and the reader who understands slack in one language has it in all three.

 **And it buys nothing infinite, which is worth stating because the shape invites the suggestion.**
 This mathematics is finitary throughout. ℛ takes a finite set; the envelopes are maxima; Birkhoff
 needs finiteness; the largest transfer case is a catalogue of 473 million and finite. **There is
 exactly one infinity in the whole construction and it is a boundary convention** — max ∅ = −∞ in
 §6.1's definition of φ̂, which exists so that an empty fibre has a bound rather than none.

 **The extension is available and it is standard.** ℛ acts on a complete lattice with suprema in
 place of maxima, and a Moore family needs no finite carrier. **What it does not do is close
 anything here**, because no finite question is answered by an infinite object, and every question
 this book has is finite: which species, which cells, which references.

    **And an infinity on the multiverse dimension would be an ungrounded term.** §29.2.2
    records that reading as not posable — §E.1.4 shows the open set cannot express an
    unclosable question, because every value of its obstacle column names a route and
    that one names none. A coordinate whose values no route reaches is *q*'s failure
    exactly, and §16.6's ⅅ_gro is the mechanism that catches it.

 **The sentence above is the whole of it, and it has no infinity in it.** An interval is a statement
 about what lies between two bounds; ∞ is a claim about what lies beyond every bound, and that is the
 one thing an interval cannot carry. Register 447.

 **It does not buy a new result.** Every number in §24.2 was computed elsewhere in this book. **What is new here is only that they are the same number**, which is a statement about the book's own organisation rather than about atoms.

    The three languages were never in competition. Algebra says what a structure permits;
    geometry says how much room that leaves; calculus says what the room costs. Slack is
    the quantity all three are measuring, and naming it is the last thing this book had left to
    do.

# PART VI — THE RECORD AND THE REACH

## 28. Withdrawals
 one thousand one hundred and seventy-one claims made in the course of this work were wrong. They are
 listed here because a book asserting 1,442 verified cells and no failures is unfalsifiable without them.
 Twenty-six were made after the register was first closed — six in the literature search of Chapter
 22 and eight in the structural work of §24.8 and §25.6 They are listed at §31.2.

 **The entries themselves are not printed here.** Every correction, withdrawal and finding this
 work has made — **273 kept in an earlier form of this chapter, 493 in all** — lives in
 **THE METHOD 1.6 — THE REGISTER**, generated by `register_gen.py` from this book's own source and
 its archive, so it cannot drift and cannot be thinned by an edit here.

    **The book cites a register entry where the entry carries an argument. It does not
    reproduce the record, because a record reproduced in two places is a record with
    two versions.**

 *What remains in this chapter is what the register is FOR — the patterns, the mechanisms, the
 level a fault lands at, and what the whole set shows about where errors are caught.* Register 658.



### 28.1 The dominant pattern
    Prose asserting a conclusion while the output contradicting it sits on the same screen.

 This accounts for the majority. A computation was run, its result printed, and a summary written beneath it that the result did not support. In several cases the contradicting numbers appeared on the same printed line.

 **That pattern is the reason the mechanisms of Chapter 16 matter.** An index that only holds what its method consumes cannot catch it. One that holds redundant quantities can, and did.

### 28.2 The five that earned their place
 The wrong nuclear charge. Al II and K II computed at Z_eff = 1 when both have doubly charged cores.
 The bracket held 56 of 56 and could not notice, because Z cancels from containment. δ came out at
 4.9 with a spread of several units — impossible — and V departed 50% where a clean channel gives 0.2%.
 Two derived quantities, neither needed by the method, both correct.

 **The wrong limit.** A neon-like sodium spectrum read as neutral neon. *T* = *I* − *E* went negative and **every channel was refused at entry.** Totality did not detect the error; it prevented the computation.

 **"*w* = 3ν·*e*."** Relation (b) of Chapter 16 written down incorrectly, with 540.0 and 1215.0 printed side by side. **The check caught the check** — which is the strongest single argument in the book for building redundancy into the object rather than into the discipline of the person using it.

 The silent discard. A guard returned None above an alphabet cap; the caller coerced it to False. That
 produced "3 of 85," which produced "almost never reorderable," which produced a false dismissal of a
 whole line of evidence. One discard, three conclusions, none of which looked wrong — because the

 arithmetic stayed consistent. ⅅ ≥ 1 cannot catch this. Only totality can, and totality had not yet been
 stated.

 **The retrieval failure.** Chapter 19 argues that an index should enumerate its targets and navigate before searching. Having written it, the author spent several hours proposing and refuting five frameworks for a problem that **one literature search resolved** — the problem was known, named, and settled in a 2004 survey.

### 28.3 Others worth listing
  • Claimed a species integrated that had never been written to the source; found by auditing the built
     document rather than the draft.
  • Fitted a curve through non-monotone data and extrapolated it to zero.
  • Stated the ℓ-ordering law from a hand-typed subset rather than from the files; the real figure was 10 of
     16, not 10 of 10.
  • Asserted "almost never reorderable" from a sampler that silently discarded most trials.
  • Ran a circular test — a prefix loop whose last step was the thing being tested — and reported 560/560
     as evidence.
  • Three successive wrong statements of the E3 criterion before reaching the q-dependent form.
  • Four failed compositions of a reduction gadget, each refuted by its own output.
  • Proposed a hardness route, a width parameterisation, a lattice-invariant parameterisation, an arity
     parameterisation and an arc-consistency algorithm; all five refuted by their own data.

### 28.4 From from the earlier work, and how each was caught — retained in the compendium
 The mechanism matters more than the error, so each is recorded with what found it.

 **Ba I 6s11f mis-keyed as 6s8f.** Caught by a **derived ordinal coordinate** producing four order violations, all involving one cell. As *n* = 8 the datum implies δ = −2.84, which is impossible; as *n* = 11 it implies δ = +0.16, which is correct for an f orbital. Corrected, the coordinate is **2,163/2,163 monotone.**

    A quantity the method never used located a typing error in a level table.

 **Ca I 3d² ¹D₂ position.** Caught by the **d² term-ordering constraint** (¹D < ³P iff *C*/*B* < 5), which three fitted analyses had not applied.

 **A regex parsing failure.** An early configuration parser read `3d4s` as 3d⁴, **silently dropping the second subshell** and reporting 0% of configurations as multi-target. Caught by manual inspection of a case known to be two-target.

    This is D3 again — a silent drop, not a wrong value. No relation could have flagged it,
    because everything downstream was consistent.

 **Bracket direction for decreasing sequences.** For a decreasing property the tightest bracket takes the maximum over cells *above* in *n* and the minimum over cells *below*; the reverse returns the global extremes. **The error inflated a reported width from 11% to 114%.**

 **Pooled σ.** Quoting one σ across series with a **370× range** in defect scatter produced 96.2% coverage at nominal ±1σ, which was misread as heavy tails. Per-cell σ gives **68.1%** — the expected figure. **The error was in the aggregation, not the data**, and it is why Rule 4 is per-cell.

 **Anchoring on a published estimate.** A point prediction for Ba I 5d² ¹G₄ was developed with a literature value visible from the first computation. Three methods were tried, and **the selection among them was not independent of that value.** The finally reported range was **38% narrower than the inputs supported.**

    This is the only error in the register that no computational check could have caught,
    because every computation was correct. What failed was the independence of a choice.

 **It produced two standing policies:** §25.6 reports a bracket and no point estimate, and any prediction is **committed in writing before the measurement is requested.**

### 28.5 What the register is for
 Two things.

 **It makes the rest checkable.** A reader who wants to know whether the 1,442 is trustworthy can see what the same process got wrong and how it was caught.

 **And it is the evidence for Chapter 16.** The three defence mechanisms are not proposed on theoretical grounds. **Each was demonstrated on a real error made in the course of this work**, and in two cases the object caught what the author did not.

### 28.6 What the register shows about where errors are caught
 Sorting the register by what caught each error gives a distribution, and the distribution is the
 argument of Chapter 16 in empirical form.

  caught by                                           co   character
                                                      un
                                                      t
  a derived quantity (δ, V, an ordinal coordinate)    4    the method was blind; a redundant quantity was not
  totality — a refusal at entry                       3    the computation never ran
  two routes disagreeing                              2    one an identity, one an apparatus
  an audit against the built artefact                 3    claims checked against output, not against draft
  a collaborator's refusal of a conclusion            4    correct reasoning from corrupted or incomplete input
  the author, on re-reading                           ~3   the dominant pattern of §32.1
  a reader                                            0    —
  a process constraint, adopted after the fact        1    the anchoring failure of §25.6

 Zero were caught by a reader, because there has not been one. That is not a claim about the work's
 correctness; it is a statement about what has and has not been tested, and it is why §32.5 states
 falsification conditions.

 **The last row was originally written as *no available mechanism*, and that was wrong.**


### 28.7 From made after the register was closed, two printed here — retained in the compendium
 These arose in the literature search of Chapter 29 and follow the same pattern as the first
 forty-eight.

 **49. The Sc VI prediction.** Computed with a constant defect δ̄ = 0.9934 exceeding δ(5s) = 0.9812 — larger than the last measured defect on a channel where the defect decreases. **The estimate fell outside the bracket its own channel implies.** Rule 4a now forbids the form; §25.6 states the bracket.

 50. A conservation law that is not one. w·V = 8y′²/y″ claimed independent of h. Exact evaluation
 gives w²/e = 16x⁴/(h⁶ − 5h⁴x² + 7h²x⁴ − 3x⁶), which depends on h explicitly. The table showed 366.3,
 367.9, 370.6, 374.5 across h = 1…4 — a 2.2% rise, read as a constant. What survives is a Pareto
 frontier, §32.5

 51. "Checkable" for "checked." §32.3's four clauses were described as checkable when two were
 checked, one was unchecked by construction, and the calibration was post hoc. The word did the
 concealing.

 **52. The framing claimed as new.** *V* read as the price of a guarantee was listed as *not found* through nine literatures. **Interval analysis has owned it since 1966, Manski since 1989, and Shannon's zero-error capacity since 1956.** Withdrawn in §25.6

 **53. A unit artefact.** The δ-bracket was reported as 10⁶ times narrower than the *T*-bracket. **δ and *T* are in different units**; the comparison was meaningless as stated.

 **54. And its correction was also wrong.** The repair claimed "2–3× wider in comparable units" **while the table beside it showed 8 × 10³ to 5 × 10⁵.** The correct statement is §25.6: the ratio is *n*³/(2δ₂), and the choice is conditional on how well the limit is known.

    Two of these six are corrections of corrections. That is the register's most useful property and
    its least comfortable one: the rate did not fall as the work got better, because the claims got
    sharper at the same time.

### 28.7.1 From more from the structural work — retained in the compendium
 55. "Bounds become worthless as data accumulates." Asserted from a table comparing a first-order
 bracket against a k-th order estimate — the book's own comparison, and unfavourable to the bracket by
 construction. A matched-order bracket holds V at 2 across k = 1…5 and ν = 20…80.

 **56. *V* = 4ν/3 as "the price of certainty."** It is the price of *first-order* certainty. **What 4ν/3 buys is robustness**: order 1 tolerates a perturbation of half the local spacing, order 5 tolerates five orders less. §25.6 states the correction.

 **57. A cells-lost table** giving the same figure for orders 1 and 2, which cannot be right since order 2 needs an extra node.

 **58. A negative bracket width at order 5.** Reported as a median without remark. **The sign rule had been applied without checking its premise**; the (*k*+1)-th difference had the wrong sign and the bounds crossed. Checking the premise first gives **100% containment at every order**.

 **59. Purity as a perturbation measure.** It measured **numerical resolution**: the deep, clean channels scored lowest because their high differences fall below the quotation floor.

 60. σ from decimal places. Inferred granularity from how many digits a compiler printed, which is
 notation rather than accuracy. Appendix B carries the stated uncertainties and they were not
 used.

 **61. "Six relations reduce to eight generators, so −2 were redundant."** A Gröbner basis is routinely *larger* than its input. The redundancy measure is the reduction test, not the count.

 **62. "The survivors account for 8 of the 2."** Eight cannot account for two. The eight self-dual cells contribute **+8** to F(−1) = **+2**, so the remaining 968 contribute **−6** — the opposite of what was written, and why the residue is 2 rather than 8 is now open.

    Two of these eight are corrections of corrections, and one — 58 — was caught by a
    number that could not exist. A negative width is the same class of signal as a negative T: the
    arithmetic refusing rather than the author noticing.

### 28.7.2 From more from the structural and audit work — retained in the compendium

  59. The cheapest undetectable forgery in Λ is 60 cells, not one.
    What ⅅ_phys actually buys is protection against systematic error, where the whole induced
    closure is wrong together and the amplification signature vanishes. Every one of §16.3's five
    recorded failures is of that kind.

 63–64. Two audit-harness errors. A sign convention that failed a true identity, and a matched-order
 test that compared a 2k-point estimate against a (k+1)-point bracket. The harness, not the book.

 65. The closed form of V. §23.4 printed V = 4ν³/(h(h²−3ν²)), whose denominator is negative for ν > h.
 Correct: 4ν³/(h(3ν²−h²)). Magnitude right everywhere, sign inverted.

 **66. And the verdict on 65.** Asserted a discrepancy that the same output showed to be zero — the error is in the *definition* against the absolute widths, not in the algebra.

 **67. The Shanks exponent, asserted before proof.** Claimed (−1)^*m*/(*p*−1)^*m* from a composition argument while the finite-*h* numbers were a factor of two out. **Proved afterwards: the law holds, the discrepancy was finite-*h* error plus a misaligned index.**

 68. "E(X) has no analytic route." It has one: E(X) = F_ℛ(1,…,1) − F_X(1,…,1). ⅅ(E) rises from 1 to 2.

 **69. *V*(δ) = *V*(*T*) taken too far.** True for the *ratio* under interpolation; **false for the width under extrapolation** — bracketing *T* for Sc VI is 129× worse than bracketing δ.

 **70–71. A conclusion written past its evidence.** "The ideal collapses" under output showing it did not, and two table rows never computed.

 **72–73. Language-independence, denied then restored.** Deletion-repair was recorded as failing in geometry. **The convex hull was the wrong operator**; the monotone polyhedron restores 30 of 30. **A mismatched operator is a mistranslation, not a counterexample.**

 74. "Self-consistency never defends against invention." It does. 220 of 220 single fabrications
 amplify — median 340 induced cells, minimum
### 28.7.3 From more from the audit work of the final session, all printed — retained in the compendium here
 75–79. Four hypotheses for one anomaly, each fitted to the single point it was invented to
 explain and each failing to predict it. The leaf rule, the load rule, the marginal rule, the activity rule —
 out-of-sample errors of 652%, 560% and 1,156%. Seven constraints, six of them clustered, one
 extreme: any measure is a relabelling of the outlier.

 **80–83. The closed form for amplification, wrong three times.** Global slackening (1 of 7), one-sided slackening (13 of 140), and a verdict line asserting a discrepancy its own output showed to be zero. **The parent values printed beside the "fix" were all already at their minimum, which made the fix arithmetically identical to what it repaired.**

 **84–86. Three claims about closure, each contradicted by the table beside it.** "Deletion-repair fails in geometry" — the convex hull was the wrong operator. "Self-consistency never defends against invention" — it defends against every invention below the amplification threshold. **"Still undone before the check runs" — written about a test that had deleted all 75 added cells and returned to Λ, testing nothing.**

 **87–88. The index was never deceived.** Two passages described a *replaced* index as though it were the original being lied to. **Asked, Λ refuses 200 of 200. Rebuilt around them, it admits 200 of 200. Those are different operations and only one is a question.**

 **89. A tree-factorisation harness that dropped two guards**, giving 1,548 against 976. **The book's formula is correct; the transcription of it was not.**

    Of the last fifteen corrections, eleven are in test harnesses and four in the book. The
    book's stated equations now audit at 35 of 35. The apparatus has become more reliable than
    the person operating it, which is what it was built to do.

 **90–92. Three more, from the closing pass.** An admissibility test that compared the bracket *width* to the noise floor when the rule is about the *difference* — the correct comparison gives *k*\* rising with better data, which the wrong one hid. **A verdict line reading "the load rule holds" from *r* = 0.849 while the held-out table beside it showed a 652% miss.** And a tree-factorisation harness that dropped two guards, giving 1,548 against 976.

    The book's stated equations audit at 35 of 35. Of the last twenty corrections, fifteen are
    in test harnesses.

 93–98. Six on the order axis, in one hour. §23.10 and §31.1 both said matched-order V oscillates
 about Proposition 15.2's floor. It does not: 241 of 570 ratios fell below 1, which should have been
 impossible and was the signal. Figure 15.3 drew a line labelled "floor V = 2" through data
 reaching 0.24.

 **The cause was a rigged comparison.** The order-*k* bracket draws on a wider node set than the estimate it was measured against — **eight levels against four at ν = 40, *k* = 3.** On the same nodes *V* runs from 157 to 3.2 × 10⁶ and **grows steeply with order.**

 **Which means Q9's original conclusion was right and its withdrawal was the error.** A deductive bound's relative value does fall as data accumulates, because an estimate improves faster than a bracket that must span both sides. **The "artefact" was the correction, not the finding.**

    Three claims, each withdrawn, each replaced by its own negation, and the negation of
    the negation is where it started. The register's value is that this is visible rather than quietly
    settled.

 **106–108. Three from the transfer tests.** Order made a coordinate when it is *p* − *q*, derived — the index then admitted arithmetically impossible cells, and §11's E2 forbids exactly that. A prose line reading "monotone and convex" above output printing **convex: False**. And **"an index cannot tell a defect of the index from a feature of the world"** — it can, by asking whether E is removable, and §6.2's Janet table is that test.

 **109. The Rydberg sign pattern, assumed rather than read.** Applied to L2, whose differences alternate the other way, it produced brackets with the bounds crossed. **§23.10.1 says read the sign from the data; I hardcoded it.**

 **110–111. Two on the nuclear index.** A commentary reading "E(X) > 0 means it is NOT closed" printed directly beneath **E(X) = 0**, and a re-coordinatisation offered as a repair that **broke a closure which had not been broken** — 0 became 27.

    Six of these are the same failure: a sentence written past the number beside it. The
    register's first entry says so, and the hundred-and-tenth says so again.

 **112–118. Seven from the transfer work.** "The one coupling has no gravitational analogue" — **the Hill sphere is exactly that analogue**, a monotone cap; what has none is *exclusion*, not the cap. A prediction of *V* ≈ 8*N* printed beside a measured 71.8. **"Celestial mechanics and string theory are included in the index"** — accepted too readily and withdrawn: **2 of 8 objects indexed in each**, and P20 forbids the rest.

 **And three on the same distinction.** "That is a question about physics, not about the index" — **the line E2 forbids**, since where the coordinates are physical the physics *is* the index. **"The index sees the sign change and cannot tell which it is"** — it can, from the pattern across the family. And **"three-body trajectories are not indexable"** — they are not *bracketable*, which is a different verdict.

 **118. A classifier that reported a fixed point as an oscillation**, by testing signs against exact zero instead of against the sequence's own scale. **The admissibility rule was written in §23.10.4 and not applied to the test built from it.**

    One of these was caught before it was written down — §16.7.2's description of a coherent
    fabrication is what stopped a claim that the transfer was total. The first time in the register that
    a section prevented an entry rather than recording one.

 **119–122. Four from the shape work.** "min(*q*, 4*f*+2) is the only non-product term" — **a minimum of two bounds IS a product, of their indicators**, and that error blocked the shape's expression for an hour. A commentary reading "E(X) = 0. The index admits combinations the five points do not realise" — **two sentences contradicting each other on one line.** A nuclear-index commentary reading "E(X) > 0 means it is NOT closed" printed directly beneath **E(X) = 0**. And a re-coordinatisation offered as a repair that **broke a closure which was not broken** — 0 became 27.

    The register's hundred-and-nineteenth entry is the same failure as its third: a sentence
    written past the number beside it.

 123–139. Seventeen from one question. §30.3's reorderability problem produced more corrections
 than any other line in this work, and §2.15 tabulates the ten that became bounds. The other seven
 were the same failure repeating: a sort direction guessed rather than derived, four times; a

 criterion proved against the wrong closure operator; a heuristic test that enumerated
 everything and proved nothing; and a table column read as if it were a different column.

    Ten of the seventeen produced the characterisation in §30.3.1. Seven produced nothing

 And the worst of them is 125. A test was written, run on 593 instances, reported as a stress test of a
 published claim — and it was exhaustive search wearing the heuristic's name. It could not have

 145–149. Five from re-auditing Chapter 30 against its own §30.4. "The difficulty is not structural"
 — distinct row supports correlates 0.606 with backtracking steps against the ten-invariant
 list's best of 0.31. §30.4's own audit, run once against a four-relation set that has since grown to eight
 polynomial and nine non-polynomial. §31.1's object list, missing six — including the Hill-radius
 correlation measured in §23.14.1 and never carried across. §31.2's, missing five, two of them computed
 in the same session and not written down. And §31.3's Pareto statistic stated against 473.8 million

    Five lists in one chapter, each enumerated once, and the section that diagnoses the
    defect is one of the five.

### 28.7.4 From more, from the session that closed §30.3 and §32.2 — retained in the compendium
 The largest single block in the register, and its shape is one thing repeated: an interpretation
 written before the number above it was read.

 150–151. Two cross-references to §24.4 for a theorem in §18.4. Recorded as two. They were
 twenty-eight — see 186 below.

 152–164. Thirteen implementation errors across nine attempts at a d ≥ 3 PQ-tree analogue: a
 Q-node using sorted columns instead of computing an order; four traversal implementations;
 frontier-correct trees that were not anchor-complete. The finding each attempt rediscovered was
 the same: a row can span consecutive Q-node children.


 **This chapter holds the entries the book's chapters cite, together with every entry those
 cite in turn. The complete record — all 436 — is the REGISTER compendium**, generated by
 `register_gen.py` from this source so it cannot drift. *An entry earns a place here by carrying
 an argument somewhere in the text; the rest are the record of how the work went, which is the
 material §4's ten mechanisms were built from.* **242 of 436 kept.**


### 28.7.5 What the block shows
 Thirty-one of the forty are one error class: a claim asserted before the output supporting it
 was read. Not arithmetic, not method — narration outrunning verification.

    The countermeasure already existed. §2.14 says state the number before the interpretation. It
    was written into this book and then broken thirty-one times in one session.

 And two entries are about the audits themselves — 186 and 189. An audit that checks whether a
 reference resolves will pass a reference that resolves to the wrong thing; a test that compares a list to
 itself will pass anything. §3's CONSISTENCY audit exists because of 186. Its exhaustiveness
 clause exists because of 189.


### 28.7.6 Two from the tower session and its repair, in the collaborator's hand
### 28.7.7 The results of the sessions after the tower — 119, printed individually
 Entries 203 to 288, written after §28.7.6's three and prepended into it until the count in
 that heading was wrong by a factor of twenty-eight. They are given newest first, which is the
 direction the register has been maintained in since 205 and is not the direction of the blocks
 above; §2.19.1 states the inconsistency and why it is left. Register 287.
### 28.7.8 The recurring mechanisms, grouped
 40 of the entries above are instances of 10 mechanisms, each stated in full at §4.
 When N entries share one mechanism the mechanism is the finding and the instances are a count, which
 is §28.7.4's own form. Grouped here; the results they sit among stay individual.

### 28.7.9 The level a fault landed at, which is a coordinate §4 does not have

 §4 classifies faults by **act** — wrote without reading, attributed outward, a test that could not
 fail. **It has no coordinate for where in the chain the act landed**, and the ten mechanisms
 distribute across three levels:

  level          mechanisms   entries    the mechanisms
  **value**           3          7       detector artifact · stale figure · a count kept by judgement
  **derivation**      3         15       wrote without reading · a test that could not fail ·
                                         a coordinate collapsed into a count
  **inference**       4         18       attributed outward · claimed completion · a conclusion
                                         written before the output · a reading the source never made

    **§32.6.1 exhibits one of each in a single paragraph** — a stale value, a sum that
    never closed, and an inference drawn from a ratio that has since inverted. Read
    without the level axis they are three unrelated faults; with it they are one
    section failing at three depths.

 **And the axis merges two mechanisms §4 keeps apart.** *Stale figures left behind their own
 correction* is a **value** going stale; *a conclusion written before the output* is an **inference**
 drawn early. **The same act at two depths, recorded as unrelated mechanisms with separate entry
 lists** — because act was the only coordinate available. The pairing predicts a third, *a derivation
 written before the output*, which is §4.6's *test that could not fail*, already recorded and never
 connected to either.

    **This book's characteristic defect is dimensional rather than arithmetical. It
    keeps recording an index as a scalar** — ρ collapsed route into a number, §32.6.1
    collapsed three levels into forty-three cells, §4 collapses level into mechanism —
    **and every time the coordinate is restored the population re-sorts.**

 Registers 571–572.

### 28.8 A dependent choice is indexable, and naming the
 dependency names the coordinate
 The anchoring error of §25.6 was not a computational failure. Every computation was correct.
 What failed was the independence of a choice: the selection among three methods was not independent
 of a literature value visible from the first computation.

 **An earlier draft concluded that no index could catch it. That conclusion does not survive the obvious question — what did the choice depend on?**

    The choice of method depended on the answer.

 **Λ cannot see it because Λ indexes cells** — configurations and levels — **not the process**: which method, chosen when, with what visible. The dependency runs through a coordinate Λ does not carry.

 **So carry it.**

 The process index
 Cells are (step, visible, alternatives, committed), and one constraint makes a prediction valid:

    visible ≤ committed — nothing of the answer may be visible before the choice is written down

 **That is §16.4 form** — one coordinate bounded by a monotone function of one other. **36 cells, closed, E = 0.**

 **And the anchoring error is a cell outside it:** (step 2, visible 2, alternatives 3, committed 0). **χ over this index refuses it** — not detects, refuses, exactly as the wrong ionisation limit was refused in §16.5. **The computation does not proceed.**

 The policy was already this index
 The rule adopted after the anchoring failure —

    any prediction is committed in writing before the measurement is requested

 — **is visible ≤ committed, enforced by a person rather than by a structure.** The book had built the index and had not noticed it was one.

 The four error classes are one mechanism on four coordinates

  error                                     index that carries it                    mechanism
  a wrong value                             the data index                           ⅅ_phys

  error                                    index that carries it                   mechanism
  a wrong derivation                       the derivation graph                    ⅅ_def
  a missing value                          the ambient set                         χ refuses
  a dependent choice                       the process index                       χ refuses

    What varies is which index you have built, not whether the error is catchable.

 The revised boundary
 Chapter 16's limit is not a limit on what structures can catch. It is a limit on which coordinates
 you happen to be carrying.

    An error is uncatchable only if no index carries the coordinate its dependency runs
    through — and naming the dependency is the same act as naming the coordinate.

 **This is the strongest form the law takes in this book**, and it arrived last, from a question about a single row of a table.

### 28.9 The register, indexed and closed

### 28.9.1 From registers, and only one of them is a backlog — retained in the compendium

 This book keeps two registers and has spoken of them as one thing. **They are different objects and
 they behave oppositely.**

  **the mathematics register** (Appendix D)   152 objects · **47 unfinished, 105 settled**
  **the withdrawals register** (this chapter) 319 entries · **33 cells, E = 6**

 **The mathematics register is a backlog. It shrinks.** Twenty cited-and-unverified, nineteen
 computed-and-unchecked, six unnamed roots, one asserted, one open. Closing it means verifying
 forty-seven things, of which thirty-nine are figures nothing has independently reproduced. Across
 this session its settled count rose by twenty while its unfinished count did not move — **work went
 in and the backlog held**, which is what a backlog does when the work also creates objects.

 **The withdrawals register is not a backlog.** It is a closed index with a stable defect, measured
 three times as it grew:

  entries   cells   density   E
    248       33     68.8%     6
    265       33     68.8%     6
    **319**   33     68.8%     6

    **Seventy-one entries and the shape has not moved.** Same thirty-three cells, same
    density, the same six admitted-and-absent.

 **That is §14.5.7's seed in a second place.** The register's *shape* is its seed and it is
 invariant; its *content* is the closure and it grows. E = 6 is therefore **not a to-do list** — it
 is the six kinds of correction this work has not yet had.

 **And what blocks E = 0 is stated by the register's own recovered bound.** Of 319 entries, **210 are
 recorded and not repaired, 93 are instance fixes, 10 reach class level and 7 produce a protocol.**
 Four of the six missing cells require class-level or protocol-level repair — **the two rarest values
 in the index** — and §28.9's envelope says why: *repair ≤ φ(corroboration)*, while **116 entries
 carry a mathematical object and no reference at all**, which caps them at instance level.

    **The withdrawals register closes through §2.22, not through effort.** The
    hundred and sixteen unreferenced entries are the constraint; the count is not.

 Register 486.

 §28.8 indexes the process by which a choice was made. This indexes the record itself, on
 coordinates assigned from the entry text rather than by hand.

  coordinate      values
  corroboration   narrative < owed < an object carried, unreferenced < an object carried with a §
  caught          by hand < by an instrument < from outside the book
  repair          recorded, not repaired < an instance fixed < a class fixed < a protocol or audit made

 fibred by the four-body layer of §18.4.1 — object, law, procedure, reference.

 **248 entries over 33 distinct cells in a box of 48 — density 68.8%, the densest object in this book
 after the periodic table's 71.4% — and E(register) = 6.** At that density E is informative by the
 asymmetry §18.4.1 records, so the six are structure rather than sparsity.

 **And ℛ recovers a bound this book has never stated.** The operator was given the cells and not the
 constraints; what it returned is a single monotone envelope:

    repair ≤ φ(corroboration) — narrative → an instance · owed → a class · an object carried → a protocol

 **No entry without a mathematical object beneath it has ever produced a protocol.** Every protocol
 and every audit in this book descends from a defect that some equation, theorem or computation
 settled. That is why §2.22 is a requirement and not a tidiness: **corroboration is what bounds how
 far a defect can be repaired**, and an entry with nothing beneath it can be fixed and cannot be
 generalised.

 **Fibred, the layers separate sharply.** LAW closes almost exactly — 44 entries, 11 cells, E = 1.
 REFERENCE has 23 entries over 11 cells at E = 8, and it is the newest and thinnest layer. OBJECT
 carries 80 entries at E = 19, which is where the admitted-and-absent cells concentrate.

 **The six cells the register admits and lacks**, each a kind of entry that could exist and does not:
 an owed entry caught by an instrument or from outside and repaired at class level; an unreferenced
 entry that nonetheless produced a protocol; a fully referenced entry repaired only to class. **They
 are not defects. They are the shapes of correction this work has not yet had**, and the third is the
 one to watch: a protocol built on an object the entry never names would be a protocol whose warrant
 cannot be found.

 **One coordinate is a reading and is marked as one.** *Repair* is inferred from the wording of each
 entry, so its distribution — 67.5% recorded but not repaired — is a property of the classifier as
 much as of the register, and is stated here as a reading whose conditions are the regex. The other
 two are recoverable from the text by anyone. §2.18's division, applied to this book's own record.
 Register 416.

## 29. Precedent, and what a search can bound
 This chapter does not claim novelty, and an earlier draft did.

### 29.1 Why novelty is not the author's to claim
 Novelty is an award, conferred by the community a work serves. It is not a property an author
 can establish about their own work, and the reason is structural rather than modest.

 **By Chapter 16's criterion a claim is defended when two disjoint routes reach it. Novelty has one route — search — and can never have two.** More searching is more of the same route.

    ⅅ(novelty) = 0, necessarily and permanently. Not undefended: undefendable.

 **And Chapter 27 supplies the second prohibition.** E(search) > 0 always: the question admits more literatures than any search enters. Nine were entered here; **nine more are named and unentered**, and that list is bounded only by what could be written down in one sitting.

 **A book that forbids claiming coherence while Ω ≠ ∅ cannot claim novelty while E(search) > 0. They are the same prohibition**, and P20 explains why: **novelty is posed in the documentary language, which has no closure mechanism at all.** That is not a failure of searching. No amount of it would help.

### 29.2 What can be claimed instead
 Three things, and each has a truth value.

 **THE SEARCH, WITH ITS SLACK STATED.** Nine literatures entered, one deeply and most by one or two queries; nine further named and unentered; six targets not found; seven prior results found incidentally and reported as incidental rather than as controls.

 **THE ATTRIBUTIONS, WHICH ARE POSITIVE CLAIMS.** Every component of this work has an owner, and naming them is checkable:

  component                                            owner
  ratio of first to second central differences         Boole · Nörlund · Steffensen
  y′/y″ as a Newton step                               optimisation
  λ² = y′²/y″                                          Aitken 1926 · Seki Kōwa c.1680 · Nesterov & Nemirovskii
                                                       1994
  the cost-of-rigour framing                           Moore 1966 · IEEE 1788 · Manski 1989 · Shannon 1956
  ⅅ = 0 when parameters equal                          Edlén, footnote 78, 1960
  observations
  δ falls with ℓ; S and P penetrate                    Edlén 1960
  E(X) as a closure defect                             Galois · formal concept analysis
  solution density; reorderability                     CSP phase transition; Stahl & Wille 1984

    Four independent owners of the cost-of-rigour framing, quoted and cited at §23.8.4: Moore
    states the trade plainly in 1966; Manski names it the law of decreasing credibility; Shannon's
    zero-error capacity is the same trade in bits.

 **THE FRAME, WHICH IS VERIFIABLE BY INSPECTION.** Not a negative existential about the world but a positive property of this object:

  does it state its falsification conditions?                         yes, §32.5
  does it run them?                                                   yes — two failed
  does it report the failures?                                        yes
  does its index satisfy its own law?                                 yes, E = 0
  does it print its withdrawal register as evidence?                  yes, one thousand one hundred and seventy-one entries

 **Those five are checkable by opening the book.** Whether another work has all five is a separate question — **and unlike novelty, a reader can settle it for any candidate they name.**

### 29.2.1 The question closed by the author
 §29.2 asks what component this work owns, and the author has answered by declining the frame: **no
 novelty is claimed, and there is no question of it.** That is not a concession forced by §29.11's
 precedent or §29.12's locations. It is the position §29.3 below already states — *E(X) is
 deliberately not claimed as novel* — made general and made final.

 Two consequences, both computed. **E(Q) does not move under its fibration**: nought by domain, before the
 decision and after, so the index of open questions is not destabilised by it. And **six of the eleven
 items in Q had novelty as their only stake** — B, C, F, G, K and N: the Paschen and Götze scan, the
 nine literatures, the reorderability search, the complexity literature, the 2J_c containment claim
 and the seniority-lattice question. Those six now block nothing. They remain open, because a question
 does not stop existing when it stops mattering, but their *blocks* coordinate falls to nothing and Q
 loses more than half its stake while losing none of its length. **This is the shape §29.4 already
 describes** — Q growing at nearly every step while its scope falls — arriving at its end state.

 Referee flag 3 asked what remains after the precedents. The answer is that the question was
 withdrawn, not answered, and the book was built to permit exactly that. Register 238.

### 29.2.2 A reading the language cannot close
 The promotion arithmetic of §12.11.0.10 invites a reading in which the branches leave the object and the
 lattice continues into indices we cannot observe. **It is recorded here and not argued**, because P20
 says what kind of claim it is.

 A question closes at a cost set by the language it is posed in: monotone two-variable bounds close in
 one pass; full arithmetic never closes, by Gödel; **the documentary language has no closure mechanism
 at all.** A claim about indices outside every measurement is posed in the third. It cannot be settled
 in either direction by any search, which is the same status §29.1 proves for novelty — **not false,
