 not true, unclosable** — and the discipline is identical: name it, decline to argue it, and do not let
 it lean on results that were computed inside.

 **What was computed stands and does not support it.** Λ₁₃ enters a larger index at a cost of 12.14
 bits per cell; a coarse coordinate branches up to 185 ways; composition seen from above is an interval
 and not a map. All three are statements about *this* object viewed at lower resolution. **None of them
 reaches outside it, and §32.1.4 proves none of them can**: ℛ(X) ⊆ ∏ᵢ Aᵢ(X), so the operator cannot
 produce a value the index lacks, let alone an axis, let alone another index. Register 335.

### 29.3 The word for it
 If a word is wanted, it should be unprecedented rather than novel, and even that should be said
 carefully.

    Novel asserts that nothing like this exists. Unclaimable. Unprecedented asserts that no
    precedent was found by a stated search. Claimable, with the search attached.

 **And the second is the weaker word doing the honest work.** It carries its own qualification: a precedent found tomorrow does not make the sentence false, only superseded — **which is exactly the status Principle 8 assigns to every bound in this book.**

### 29.4 On priority for V = 4ν/3
 We have not established that this ratio is new, and we state the limits of the search rather than the
 strength of the claim.

 *V* = 4ν/3 is **two lines of algebra** from *T* = *Z*²*R*/ν², in a field worked continuously since 1885. The prior expectation that nobody wrote it down should be **low**.

 **Ritz (1908) does not contain it.** We read the *Astrophysical Journal* version in full: the series formula, the combination principle, the derivation of new series, and the magnetic-atom model. **No error bound of any kind.**

 **But it contains the qualitative ancestor.** Ritz observes that computing differences and sums of observed wave-numbers is more accurate than working through fitted constants — **the principle the bracket formalises, left unquantified.**

 **Martin (1980) does not contain it.** Its deliverable is a set of formulas predicting one-electron levels to a stated ±0.03 cm⁻¹ — **flat, not ν-dependent.**

### 29.5 Edlén 1964, examined at one remove
 The chapter itself is paywalled. Its principal expert review is not, and we have read it in full.

    Curtis, L. J., "Bengt Edlén's Handbuch der Physik Article — 26 Years Later," Physica Scripta 35,
    805–810 (1987).

 Curtis was Edlén's collaborator, built a forty-year research programme on the chapter's methods, and wrote this specifically to assess what it established and how it had held up.

### 29.5.1 Every equation in the review is a fit

| | equation | status |
|---|---|---|
| (1) | *T*_{*n*ℓ} = *TH*_{*n*ℓ} + *A*⟨*r*⁻⁴⟩ + *B*⟨*r*⁻⁶⟩ | *A*, *B* **fitted** |
| (2) | *TP*_{*n*ℓ} = *TH*_{*n*ℓ} + α_d⟨*r*⁻⁴⟩ + (α_q−6β)⟨*r*⁻⁶⟩ | theoretical α's |
| (3) | *T*_{*n*ℓ} = *TP*_{*n*ℓ} + *f*_ℓ⟨*r*⁻⁶⟩ | *f*_ℓ **fitted** |
| (4)–(5) | penetration-energy difference forms | definitions |
| (6) | Δσ = Ry α²(*Z*−*S*)⁴/*n*³ℓ(ℓ+1) | *S* **fitted** |
| (7) | *S* = *S*₀ + *S*₁/(*Z*−*S*) + … | *S*₀, *S*₁ **fitted** |

 **Seven equations. Not one is a bound.** There is no ratio of a bracket width to an interpolation error, no statement of how much wider a guarantee is than an estimate, and no constraint of any kind on where an unmeasured term must lie.

### 29.5.2 And the paradigm is stated twice, explicitly
 Curtis opens by giving Edlén's purpose:

    "The article sought to recognise regularities among atomic systems that could help to predict the
    position of unknown levels, or to interpret newly found levels."

 and closes:

    "These methods can be used with confidence to make very precise interpolative and extrapolative
    predictions."

 **Predictions. Not bounds.**

    §23.3 argues that V has no use in a fitting paradigm. This review is that paradigm,
    documented in full by its principal modern practitioner — and the ratio is not in it.

 **That converts the structural argument from a conjecture into an observation.**

### 29.5.3 One near miss, and it is not ours
 Curtis reports that Edlén did compute a rational fraction:

    "A screening parametrisation of the exchange energy … yielding a linear dependence upon effective
    central charge with a proportionality constant that (in Rydberg units) is a calculable rational fraction.
    In his Handbuch article (cf. Fig. 54, pp. 170–2), Professor Edlén computed this rational fraction
    theoretically for n = 2, and evaluated its effective value from empirical data for n = 3, 4, 5 and 6."

 **It is the proportionality constant of the exchange energy** — a Slater-integral quantity — **not a ratio of differences and not a measure of interpolation accuracy.** The exact-to-empirical agreements Curtis quotes (1.04, 0.93, 1.05, 0.87) are for that constant.

 **We record it because it is the closest thing in the chapter's expert summary to what we are looking for, and it is not the same object.**

### 29.5.4 A bibliographic correction
 Curtis's reference [1] carries a note that should be reproduced:

    "Edlén, B., in Handbuch der Physik, Vol. 27 … pp. 80–220, Springer-Verlag, Berlin (1964). [This
    publication date is misleading, since the manuscript was delivered in final form in October 1960.]"

 **The chapter is a 1960 manuscript**, and this book cites it accordingly. The page range 80–220 is likewise confirmed from this source, having previously been stated from recollection.

### 29.5.5 What this establishes, and what it does not
 Establishes. A six-page expert review, written to assess the chapter's methods for predicting unknown
 levels, contains no accuracy ratio, no bound and no bracket — and states the programme's purpose as
 prediction twice.

 **Does not establish.** Absence from 140 pages. A review summarises; an aside can survive summarising.

    But the aside would have to have escaped the one person most likely to have used it.

### 29.6 The three documents we could not reach
 Named so that a reader with library access can settle it:

| document | why it matters |
|---|---|
| **Edlén, *Handbuch der Physik* XXVII (1964)**, on series formulas | Edlén wrote the standard account; an elementary ratio would plausibly appear as an unremarked aside. In English despite the German series title. |
| **Ritz, *Physikalische Zeitschrift* **9**, 521 (1908)** | Two pages longer than the ApJ version, carrying the "further particulars" Ritz promises in a footnote. Reprinted in *Œuvres* (1911), ~pp. 142–150. |
| **Paschen & Götze, *Seriengesetze der Linienspektren* (Springer, 1922)** | Book-length treatment by the two people who measured most of the underlying spectra. |
| **Dunz, *Seriengesetze der Linienspektra* (Leipzig, 1911)** | Earliest complete compilation, chronologically closest to Ritz. |

 **ρ ≤ 2 for each; every route closed.** By the analysis of Chapter 19, that is a property of pre-digital print, not of the search.

### 29.7 What the entered literatures returned
 Nine literatures have now been entered. Each supplied prior art for a component; none
 supplied the assembly; and the ones sharing this book's mathematics also supplied new
 derivations, while the ones sharing its philosophy did not.

  literature                     what it owns                                 for this book
  spectroscopy                   Edlén fn. 78; the ℓ-ordering                 two components
  finite differences             V = 4·μδ/δ²                                  a second derivation
  sequence acceleration          (Δx)²/Δ²x — Aitken 1926, Seki Kōwa           λ², and a new result
                                 c.1680
  Richardson extrapolation       step-ratio elimination                       attribution only
  optimisation                   λ² = f′²/f″, Nesterov 1994                   w·V = 8λ²;
                                                                              self-concordance
  interval arithmetic            excess width; IEEE 1788                      the cost framing

  literature                     what it owns                             for this book
  econometrics                   Manski's law of decreasing               the framing again
                                 credibility
  zero-error information         Shannon 1956; Körner & Orlitsky          the framing, a third time
  theory                         1998
  regression curvature;          ‖f″‖/‖f′‖²; \                            k′/k²\                           different
  WKB                                                                                                      quantities

 **Four owners of the cost-of-rigour framing**, set out with the quotation at §23.8.4: Moore 1966, Manski's **law of decreasing credibility**, and Shannon's zero-error capacity — the same trade stated in bits.

    This book is the fourth to make the argument and the first to compute the ratio for a
    measured monotone sequence.

### 29.7.1 The calibration was post hoc
 §29.7 reported 7/7 on controls. That was not a calibration.

 Controls were never chosen in advance. The search sought targets, noted what else appeared, and afterwards called those findings controls. **Nothing was recorded as *looked for and not found* among them, because nothing was looked for.**

 **And the filtration was not uniform.** Spectroscopy received nine approaches; four literatures received one search each; **two were entered only after the audit exposed that they had not been.**

    The corrected statement: nine literatures entered, one deeply, most by one or two
    searches; seven prior results found incidentally and reported as such; six targets not
    found; nine further literatures named and unentered.

 §2.13 — commit before you look — was adopted in this book after the anchoring failure of §28.6,
 and the calibration violated it.

### 29.8 The other claims
  claim                            assessed                basis
  V = 4ν/3                         not claimable           nine literatures entered; four owners of the framing found; see
                                                           §23.8.4
  ν_V ceiling                      ~75%                    very specific form, not found
  cost surface, pole at p = 1      ~50%                    the framing is new; the content elementary
  bracketing rather than           the searched bound of   literature uniformly predictive
  predicting                       §29.8
  E(X) as closure defect           not claimed             see below
  reorderability complexity        not claimed             see below

 **E(X) is deliberately not claimed as novel.** Closure operators, Galois connections and formal concept analysis are established mathematics. **What is offered is the application** — computing the closure defect of an index, and finding 36 for the periodic table and 0 for Λ.

 **And the reorderability material claims nothing.** The problem is known: Stahl & Wille proved 2-dimension NP-complete in 1984; Yannakakis proved dimension ≤ 3 NP-complete in 1982; Habib, Nourine, Raynaud & Thierry surveyed the computational aspects in *Theoretical Computer Science* **312** (2004), 401–431. **The work reported in Chapter 29 was done before that literature was located**, and is retained only for what it records about method.

### 29.9 Why state it this way

 **A book that names its own unchecked prior art is in a stronger position than one that claims priority and is corrected.** The three documents and one section are a finite, specified task. Whoever completes it either confirms the result or improves the historical record, and both outcomes are better than an unexamined claim.

### 29.9.1 Attributions entered by the tower
 The tower borrows, and the debts are named: seniority is Racah 1943; jK coupling is Racah 1942;
 particle-hole conjugation is standard shell theory; the term-counting is textbook. And E(search)
 grows by one item: whether anyone has lattice-ordered the seniority scheme is a literature this
 work has not entered — named and unentered, per this chapter's own discipline.

### 29.10 Two searches run, and an obstruction worth naming
 Two searches were run against the tower's order-theoretic results — one for lattice or order
 structure on angular-momentum selection rules, one for distributive-lattice or poset treatments of
 electron configurations. Neither returned a precedent. **That establishes nothing**, by §29.1: a
 search settles novelty in one direction only, and two queries are not a literature.

 What the searches did establish is an obstruction, and it is specific enough to record. **The word
 lattice is doubly occupied in physics** — the crystal lattice and lattice gauge theory — and both
 outrank order theory in every index a physicist's search will reach. A query pairing *lattice* with
 any physical term returns those two subjects and nothing else, so an order-theoretic precedent, if one
 exists, is not merely unfound but systematically unreachable by the obvious query. E(search) is
 therefore larger here than the chapter's general argument already makes it: the vocabulary itself
 hides the shelf. Anyone entering this literature should search in *poset*, *join*, *meet* and
 *Birkhoff*, and should expect the physics terms to be useless.

### 29.11 A precedent found, and it is for the central theorem
 Searched in the vocabulary §29.10 recommends, the literature answers at once, and it answers about
 A.2.

    **Bergman's Double-projection Theorem.** A sublattice S of a finite product of lattices
    L₁ × ⋯ × Lₙ is uniquely determined by its two-fold projections in Lᵢ × Lⱼ. It is a
    consequence of Baker and Pixley (1975), which states the same for any algebra with a
    majority term; in a lattice the median m(x,y,z) = (x∧y) ∨ (x∧z) ∨ (y∧z) is one.

 **A.2 is that theorem specialised to products of chains.** Λ's coordinates are counts, so each
 factor is a chain; a sublattice of a product of two chains is exactly a staircase region cut by
 monotone bounds, which is what ℛ recovers as φ_ij. *X closed ⟺ ℛ(X) = X* and *a sublattice is
 determined by its binary projections* are one statement. The proof in §16.1 is correct and it is
 not first. Fifty years of universal algebra stand behind it, and Appendix D's precedent coordinate
 for A.2 changes from none found to found.

 This is the outcome §29.1 says a search can produce and the only one it can: precedent found, in the
 one direction the method settles. It costs the book nothing it was entitled to — the book never
 claimed novelty — and it gains the book a foundation, since a result with a fifty-year pedigree is
 stronger than a result proved once in an appendix. **What it does cost is the reading that E(X) = 0
 is a discovery about Λ.** It is a discovery that Λ is a sublattice, and sublattices have been known
 to be binary-determined since 1975.

 **What the search did not find** is any precedent for E(X) as a *measure*. Baker–Pixley is a
 dichotomy: determined or not. E(X) = |ℛ(X)| − |X| counts how far a set that is not a sublattice
 lies from the one its own projections generate, and no order-theoretic source returned by these
 searches quantifies that gap or names it. That absence is not evidence — §29.1 again — and §29.12
 gives the tests that would settle it.

### 29.11.1 Two categories entered: quantum mechanics and astrophysics
 Both were searched in the order-theoretic vocabulary §29.10 recommends. They returned different
 things, and the second returned a target.

 **Quantum mechanics: one adjacent literature, unentered.** No source was found ordering electron
 configurations, term symbols or coupling schemes as a poset. What exists and this book has not
 entered is **partial order in chemistry** — Hasse-diagram methods applied to chemical series, QSAR
 and property rankings, with its own software and review chapters. It orders *substances by
 properties*, not configurations by structure, so it is adjacent and not the same; but it is a
 literature in which someone would already know the tools, and Chapter 29's discipline is to name it.
 E(search) grows by one.

 **Astrophysics: the tripwire has targets, and they are named.** Stellar spectroscopy runs on atomic
 line lists — NIST ASD, VALD, Kurucz, and cross-matched compilations such as BRASS — and the
 literature reports two failure modes by name. *Missing lines* are observed features with no atomic
 data. **Unobserved lines** are the inverse: atomic data producing features that no spectrum shows,
 arising from inaccurate wavelengths, predicted energy levels, or misassignments. BRASS states that
 such lines are **typically removed by hand**, and separately reports duplicated transitions found by
 cross-matching VALD against Kurucz and NIST. Kurucz's own estimate is that half the known lines in
 stellar spectra lack wavelengths of good accuracy.

 **That is the D1 tripwire's job description, written by someone else.** §16.3.1 rejects 150 of 216
 offered (subshell, occupancy, 2S′) assignments — 69.4% — from the index alone, needing no spectrum
 and no model atmosphere. A line list carrying a term designation outside its subshell's admissible
 set is nonphysical before any wavelength is compared. The check is mechanical where the current
 practice is manual, and it is prior where the current practice is posterior.

### 29.11.2 The run, attempted
 It was attempted rather than deferred, and the attempt is reported with its size.

 **What could not be reached.** Kurucz's line files are the right target — computed lines, many never
 laboratory-verified, carrying level labels of the form (³F)5s e²F that name a parent term, an outer
 orbital and a level term. The format specification was retrieved and the references file with it.
 The data files themselves could not be: gfall is of the order of a gigabyte and the per-species files
 could not be addressed through the interface available. VALD requires registration. So the run
 against uncurated astrophysical data did not happen, and that is the run that would matter.

 **What was reached.** Appendix B's own 153 channels, sourced from NIST ASD, Kaufman and Martin,
 Kramida and Martin, and Sansonetti. Of 103 designations parsed, **ten carry an explicit parent term**
 — the (³P)ns ⁴P form — and only those can be checked by the coupling rule, which is that adding one
 electron of spin ½ to a parent core must change 2S by exactly one. **Ten tested, ten pass, none
 flagged.**

 **Then the null was defined instead of discarded, and it stopped being ten.** Ten was the count of
 designations carrying an *explicit* parent in parentheses. Ninety-two carried none — and forty-four
 of those have a parent that is implicit and completely determinate: 3s²nf ²F° has a closed 3s² core,
 which is ¹S, so the level must be a doublet, and it is. Resolving closed and single-electron cores
 gives **fifty-four checkable designations of one hundred and two, and fifty-four pass.** The test
 grew by a factor of five and a half by defining what the first pass had recorded as absent.

 **What the null is, stated as the positive it is.** Fifty-four passes on curated data measure
 **specificity, not sensitivity**: zero false positives in fifty-four. That is the necessary condition
 and not the sufficient one — a check that rejected correct physics would be worthless, and this one
 does not. It remains untested against material that contains errors, and Kurucz's predicted lines are
 where that test lives.

 **And the reach is a second result the run was not looking for.** Fifty-four of one hundred and two
 is 53%, and the forty-eight left out are not left out because the data is poor. They carry open
 subshell cores whose parent term the designation does not determine. **Published channel notation
 fixes the coupling parent for about half of these channels and leaves it indeterminate for the rest**
 — a property of the notation, not of the physics or of the compilers, and a hard ceiling on any
 mechanical check applied to designations as published. Registers 253 and 254.

 **This changes referee flag 5's status and does not answer it.** The flag said the tripwire has never
 run on anyone else's data. It still has not. What has changed is that *anyone else's data* is no
 longer hypothetical: VALD, Kurucz and the BRASS compilation are public, they carry term
 designations, and their maintainers document removing spurious entries by hand. The flag moves from
 *no target identified* to *targets identified, run not attempted*, and the run is the obvious next
 piece of work this book could do that would matter to someone outside it. Registers 251 and 252.

### 28.10 What removing the register cost, and what it caught

 **Eight references were orphaned the moment the entries left.** Lubiw, Anstee, Kuznetsov, Caspard,
 Colomb, Hoffman, Van Isacker and Chandrasekaran & Flanagan were cited **only** inside register
 entries — so the book carried eight sources nothing in it pointed at. *Each is now cited where its
 finding is: the Γ-free literature at §30.3.9, the #P-completeness of counting closed sets at §14.6,
 seniority's reduction chains at §30.3, and the modular interval at §12.11.*

    **A reference belongs to the argument that uses it, not to the record of when it
    was found.** The record's citations were carrying references the book itself never
    made — which is only visible once the record is removed.

 **And part 2 of the audit now checks the References as a set**: every entry must be cited somewhere
 in the book. *Nothing had ever looked.* Registers 658–659.


### 29.12 Five unlocated results, defined, with the test that would settle each
 What follows is not a claim of novelty, which §29.1 forbids the author to make. It is a list of
 statements this work computed, for which these searches returned no source, written so that a reader
 who knows the source can recognise it in one reading and a reader who does not can attack it. Each
 carries a definition, the search already run, the test that would confirm it, and the observation
 that would destroy it. **A result with no stated falsifier is not on this list.**

 **U1 — E(X) as a defect measure. LOCATED, and the test that found it was the one printed here.**
 *Definition.* For finite X ⊆ ∏ᵢ Aᵢ let ℛ(X) = {y : yᵢ ≤ φ_ij(y_j) for all i ≠ j}, where φ_ij is the
 monotone envelope of X's projection onto (i, j). Then E(X) = |ℛ(X)| − |X| ≥ 0.
 *Located.* The property E(X) = 0 is called **2-decomposability** in constraint satisfaction: a set S
 of integer vectors is 2-decomposable when any vector whose every two-dimensional projection lies in
 the corresponding projection of S lies in S. That is ℛ(X) = X verbatim. The class is studied for its
 tractability, characterised by closedness under operations — min and max among them, which is the
 same closure Chapter 14 imposes — and the connection is live research, not settled history: Kimura,
 Makino, Yamada and Yoshizumi, *Characterizing the integer points in 2-decomposable polyhedra by
 closedness under operations*, 2024. Adjacent and older: row-convex constraints, where path
 consistency yields a globally consistent and decomposable network, and the Helly property for
 ordered domains.
 *What remains unlocated* is narrow and should be stated narrowly: **the count**. 2-decomposability
 is a predicate and the literature found treats it as one. E(X) = |ℛ(X)| − |X| as a graded measure of
 how far a set is from the class, and E(X) as the *specification* of what may be added to an index,
 were not returned. That is not evidence of absence.
 *Test that remains.* Search *degree of decomposability*, *decomposability defect*, *minimal network
 size gap*. Ask whether the CSP literature measures the excess of the path-consistent closure over
 the solution set, rather than only detecting it.
 *Falsifier.* One paper defining |closure| − |set| for a monotone-bound system, in any field.

 **U2 — the counting/coupling dichotomy.**
 *Definition.* In an index whose admissible constraints are monotone bounds of one coordinate by one
 other, a coordinate whose exact physical range is a monotone function of a single parent is carried
 exactly; a coordinate whose exact range is a sum, a difference, or a function symmetric under
 half-filling is carried only as an envelope, and the shortfall is not removable by re-presentation.
 *Searched.* Not found; the physics-vocabulary searches of §29.10 cannot reach it.
 *Test.* Attempt the construction the theorem forbids: exhibit any angular-momentum coupling
 coordinate with an exact single-parent monotone bound at some cap setting. Five presentations were
 tried here and all failed — 2S′, parity alone, the min-cap, pair count, and (2S,2L,2J).
 *Falsifier.* A sixth presentation that closes exactly. One suffices.

 **U3 — the E1 puncture.**
 *Definition.* The E1-allowed set T = {(2J, 2J′) : |2J − 2J′| ≤ 2} is closed under componentwise max
 and min. T ∖ {(0,0)}, the set with the physically forbidden J = 0 ↛ 0 transition removed, fails
 meet-closure by exactly four pairs, and all four meets produce (0,0).
 *Searched.* Not found. Selection rules are universally known; their closure properties are not a
 subject these searches reached.
 *Test.* Recompute at caps beyond 20 and confirm the count stays four — done to 20 here. Then repeat
 for M1 (|ΔJ| ≤ 1, no parity change) and E2 (|ΔJ| ≤ 2 with J + J′ ≥ 2): do their allowed sets close,
 and does each forbidden cell cost a constant number of meets?
 *Falsifier.* A cap at which the count is not four, or a second cell produced by a failing meet.

 **U4 — the join/meet asymmetry of the triangle.**
 *Definition.* T = {(2L, 2S, 2J) : |2L − 2S| ≤ 2J ≤ 2L + 2S} is join-closed and meet-broken. Verified
 at caps 6, 8, 10, 12 with 0 join failures and 2,862 / 12,489 / 40,887 / 110,229 failing meets.
 Imposing the parity congruence breaks join as well.
 *Searched.* Not found.
 *Proved.* A.18 gives it, and the proof corrects the guess that preceded it. Joins are closed
 because both inequalities survive coordinatewise max. Meets fail on **both** inequalities, not on
 one: the upper bound fails at (0,1,1) ∧ (1,0,1) = (0,0,1), which manufactures J = ½ from L = S = 0,
 and the lower bound fails at (4,0,4) ∧ (2,2,0) = (2,0,0). At every cap tested the lower bound fails
 exactly twice as often as the upper — 8,326 against 4,163 at cap 8, 73,486 against 36,743 at cap 12,
 363,384 against 181,692 at cap 16 — a ratio the proof does not explain and which is left open.
 *Falsifier.* A join failure at any cap; none in 369, 1,105 and 2,465 cells.

 **U5 — the cylinder as a cap-stable fibration.**
 *Definition.* Λ₁₃ factorises over the transfer q as Σ_q |A(q)| · |B(q)| = |Λ₁₃| with zero defect,
 at every cap setting tested; the tight two-parent form of K destroys the factorisation.
 *Searched.* Not found, and this one is least likely to be general — it is a property of one
 constructed object.
 *Test.* Caps beyond (5,5,2,6,2), and the same construction on a different shell system.
 *Falsifier.* Any cap at which the defect is non-zero on the tree path.

 **Verification common to all five.** Every computation above is reproducible from the definitions
 printed in this book and nothing else: the constraint system of §7.1, the caps of §7.4, and the
 bounds of §12.11.1 with the three exact sets §12.11.1 now states. A reader who rebuilds Λ from those
 and does not reproduce the cell counts 976, 1,654, 1,561, 2,535, 13,585, 70,905 and 199,130 has
 found an error in the book and should say so. That reproduction is the entry test; the five results
 above are what lies past it.

## 30. Order recovery, and the reorderability law
 Chapter 15 recovers Λ's order from its own cells. This chapter asks what happens when the
 index is harder than Λ — and the answer is a law, a procedure, and a residue that belongs to
 complexity theory rather than to this book.

### 30.1 Order recovery above the tree
 Chapter 15 recovers Λ's coordinate order because its constraint graph is a tree. For an index whose
 graph has cycles, the question is open, and the following is what is known.

 Complete under a targeted attack. Fixing one pair of axes jointly and extending one axis at a time
 with backtracking found a valid ordering in 384 of 384 reorderable instances, sampled deliberately by
 solution density rather than at random. No proof; no counterexample.

    The earlier figure of 139 was a random sample. §30.2 says the search is hardest when the solution is
    nearly unique, so the instances were re-drawn to concentrate there — 101 of the 384 sit in the
    lowest density bucket, and none of them failed.

 Not backtrack-free, and the cost tracks the basin exactly:

  solution density                                         mean backtracks
  0.00–0.05                                                23.40
  0.05–0.20                                                6.90
  0.20–0.50                                                1.05
  0.50–1.00                                                0.00

 A twenty-three-fold rise as the solution becomes unique, with completeness unbroken
 throughout.

 And the worst case cannot be bounded by a local test, because of §18.4: closure is not determined
 by its proper projections.

### 30.2 What governs the difficulty
 Ten structural invariants were tested against backtracking — dimension, alphabet size, cell count,
 occupancy, join-irreducibles, poset height, poset width, covers, antichain, rank range, over 211
 reorderable instances. The best correlation was 0.31.

 Solution density governs it, at −0.68 in log over the same instances:

| solution fraction | backtrack-free | mean backtracks |
|---|---|---|
| 0.009 – 0.25 | **24%** | 2.9 |
| 0.25 – 0.50 | 47% | 1.0 |
| 0.50 – 1.00 | 82% | 0.2 |
| 1.00 | **100%** | **0.0** |

    The search is hard exactly when the solution is nearly unique.

### 30.2.1 And that list was enumerated once

 An earlier draft concluded from the 0.31 that the difficulty is not structural. That was a
 conclusion about ten invariants, and the list predates the containment forest, overlap components,
 C1P and the interval structure. Re-run over 928 instances with six invariants discovered since:

  invariant                                   corr. with steps            corr. with density
  best of the original ten                    0.31                        —
  overlap pairs                               0.438                       −0.423
  nesting pairs                               0.375                       −0.640
  distinct row supports                       0.606                       −0.883
  log solution density                        −0.628                      —

 A structural invariant does beat 0.31, by twofold, and it predicts the density better than the density
 predicts the difficulty.

      Structure determines density; density determines difficulty. The earlier reading had the
      second link and called it the whole chain.

### 30.2.2 Which gives §30.3 a parameter
 Search steps against distinct row supports, over 1,480 instances:

  distinct rows                        mean steps                 max         max / d²
  2                                    1.05                       8           2.00
  3                                    2.63                       10          1.11
  4                                    5.41                       36          2.25
  5                                    8.68                       39          1.56

 The ratio does not climb; the log–log slope is 2.29. That is a measured candidate exponent for
 §30.3's complexity question — on five points, with R² between 0.37 and 0.42. Evidence, not a bound.

### 30.2.3 And it is a landscape statement
 E is a potential on the space of orderings and adjacent transposition is a move. Solution density
 is the size of the basin: a rare solution means a longer descent with more places to stall. §30.3.3
 completes this reading.

### 30.3 Reorderability — the law, the procedure, and what is left
 The question this section asked was: given an index whose constraint graph has cycles, can
 **Van Isacker**'s survey gives the seniority reduction chains.
 its coordinate order be recovered? The answer has a law, a procedure, and a residue, and none of the
 three is where the section began.

### 30.3.1 The object, correctly identified
      ℛ-closure and closure under join and meet are the SAME CONDITION — 1,487 instances,
      zero disagreements, and confirmed on all 475,800 pairs of Λ. So reorderability is: does some
      relabelling of each axis make X a SUBLATTICE of a product of chains?

 That is lattice embedding, and it is the subject of Rival 1973/74, Larson 1998 and Siggers 2016.
 §30.3's original citations — Stahl & Wille, Yannakakis — were correct; a draft withdrew them on the
 grounds that this was an interval problem, and it is not. The withdrawal is withdrawn.

 And the recovered bound of Chapter 15 is Rival's closed family of irreducible intervals. Siggers'
 Lemma 3.5(ii) states φ_ij exactly.

### 30.3.2 The law
 A relabelling decides, for each axis and each pair of values, which comes first. So the problem
 is a constraint system on those decisions, and every constraint is closed under reversing all of

 its own orientations — 3,781 constraints checked, 100%.                           A   function     invariant     under
 complementation depends only on XOR-differences, so:

  arity — the axes on which two        the constraint      cost
  cells differ                         becomes
  2                                    a single XOR        signed-graph 2-colouring — LINEAR
  3                                    two XORs            2-SAT, and all 14 constraints that arise are 2-SAT
                                                           expressible — LINEAR
  ≥4                                   three or more       contains exactly-one-of-three; no Schaefer class covers
                                       XORs                the language

      THE LAW. Reorderability is a constraint system whose arity is the number of axes on
      which two cells differ. At arity ≤ 3 the language is entirely bijunctive, so the problem is
      linear. At arity ≥ 4 it contains 1-in-3-SAT and admits no Schaefer class.

 The procedure is the law read at low arity; the hardness is the same law read at high arity.
 One statement, two regimes, boundary at arity 3 — and since arity is bounded by d and reaches
 it, at d = 3.

 Measured: at d = 2 every constraint has arity 2 at every alphabet size, and 2-colouring plus
 transitivity decides exactly — 300/300 at \|A\| = 3, 300/300 at \|A\| = 4. At d = 3 with binary axes,
 396 of 396.

 Figure 23.1. Left: the fraction of each arity's constraint language that is 2-SAT expressible — 14 of 14 at arity 3, and
 114 of 141 at arity 4. The boundary is exact. Right: the share of constraints by arity as the dimension rises — arity 2
 falls from 0.62 to 0.26 while arity ≥ 4 grows from nothing to 0.38. The tractable part shrinks with d, and the law
 says why.

### 30.3.3 What the interval machinery was
 Chapter-length work went into C1P, monotone endpoints and PQ-trees, and the d = 2
 characterisation is proved there: X is reorderable iff every row is an interval with both endpoints
 monotone, iff no interval nests strictly inside another. Four lemmas, three verified independently —
 2,047, 3,751 and 20,000 instances — with Booth & Lueker's linear PQ-tree construction cited
 for the fourth.

 **The fourth lemma, verified.** The three lemmas above were checked at 2,047, 3,751 and 20,000
 instances; the fourth stood on Booth and Lueker's construction and on nothing of this book's own.
 It is now discharged, and it did not need a linear-time algorithm:

    A set of intervals admits an ordering in which both endpoints are non-decreasing **iff** no
    interval nests strictly inside another. Sort by left endpoint; if a right endpoint then
    decreases, the later interval has the weakly greater left end and the strictly smaller right
    end, so it nests strictly. Conversely a strictly nesting pair admits no such order. ∎

 Verified exhaustively over all 2,354 interval sets of two to four intervals on ground sets of two to
 five points: zero counterexamples. **Strictly** is load-bearing — an interval sharing an endpoint
 with its container does not nest strictly, and reading it as ordinary containment refutes the lemma
 on 1,098 of those 2,354 sets. That reading was tried first here and was wrong, which is the whole
 reason the qualifier is in the statement. Register 247.

      That apparatus is a REPRESENTATION of a 2-colouring. It is correct and it is not the reason d =
      2 is easy. The reason is that two cells in two dimensions differ on at most two axes.

### 30.3.4 The step law
 Growth from the minimal reorderable sets, adding cells, reaches every reorderable set — with
 a step size measured exhaustively across twelve boxes:

  d                     step                      boxes
  2                     1                         eight, all 2-D

  d                     step                      boxes
  3                     2                         three
  4                     4                         one

 The over-cap cases collapse to a single object: the full box. Its largest reorderable proper subset
 has 3·2^(d−2) cells — three quarters — and the removed cells are a subcube. Verified by construction
 at d = 2, 3, 4 and 5, with one-fewer failing at all four.

      step f(d) = 2^(d−2).

 Figure 23.2. Left: the growth step against dimension — 1, 2, 4 measured exhaustively over twelve boxes, and 8 at d = 5
 by the subcube construction, against 2^(d−2). Right: the box against its largest reorderable proper subset — 3, 6, 12, 24
 cells, exactly three quarters at every dimension. Rival's 1973 bound on maximal sublattices is \|K\| ≤ (3/2)\|L\|; the
 Boolean case is tighter.

 And this is Rival's theorem. His 1973 bound is \|K\| ≤ (3/2)\|L\| for a maximal sublattice;
 Chen–Koh–Tan give the interval form; the large sublattices of 2^[r] are catalogued, and the
 maximal ones studied in finite topology. §E.5 records the precedence.

### 30.3.5 The procedure, and its cost
      DECIDE(X). Build the constraint system on orientations. Arity ≤ 3: 2-colour the signed graph
      and check transitivity — linear. Arity ≥ 4: grow from the minimal reorderable sets with step
      2^(d−2), collect signatures, decide by lookup.

 Verified end to end at 2×3×3 by growth against exhaustion: 18,736 = 18,736, in 47.8 s against
 86, converging in nine rounds. And NextClosure enumerates the sublattices with polynomial
 delay — 1,834 sets in 0.12 s — giving 401 signatures against 401 exhaustive.

 Cost: \|YES\| · \|X\|^{2^(d−2)}. Polynomial in \|X\| for fixed d; exponential in d. Fixed-parameter
 tractable, and not polynomial in both.

 For Λ: d = 8, so ∏\|A_i\|! = 11,943,936 — and Λ needs none of it. Its structure is constructed in
 Chapter 7, its embedding is tight, |J(Λ)| = 17 = height, width 7 ≤ 8, and by Dilworth and
 Larson the chain decomposition of J(Λ) IS the axis system.

### 30.3.6 The signature, exhaustively verified
 A cell-space invariant whose FORM does not change with d: the multiset of canonical forms of the
 3-subsets of X. O(\|X\|³) structure, 2^d labels — the two factor, where every order-space method
 cost ∏\|A_i\|! and coupled them.

  box                 subsets                                                  classes                    mixed
  2×2×2               all 256                                                  30                         0
  2×2×3               all 4,096                                                210                        0
  2×3×3               all 262,144                                              4,168                      0

 Three complete boxes, 267,000 subsets, no signature containing both answers. After the duality
 merge the 19 canonical forms reduce to 12 with no loss.

### 30.3.7 Three method classes ruled out
 Each with its measurement, and each a result rather than a failure:

 Projection-based criteria. §18.4: closure is a d-dimensional condition and no projection serves as a
 criterion. Thirteen of seventeen attempted criteria were seed-independent invariants — all
 necessary, none sufficient, with the gap widening as nesting deepened.

 Local search through valid orders. The valid set is DISCONNECTED — 1.66 to 2.19 components,
 and the components are orbits of the identical-fibre swap group, exact on 493 of 493. So descent
 plateaus: 89.8%, and 96.7% with bounded uphill moves.

 Forbidden-substructure procedures. Tucker's families are unbounded, so no finite census decides
 — and the census is finite within any box, which is what reopened the question. The infinite antichain
 lives across indices, not in one.

### 30.3.8 The residue
 The language is NP-complete — it contains exactly-one-of-three and no Schaefer class covers it, with
 the smallest obstruction exhibited on three XOR variables.

 The realisability is blocked. Three reduction attempts, all over-constraining in the same direction:
 satisfiable formulas encoding as non-reorderable, never the reverse. The map X → C(X) is global,
 non-separable and non-monotone, so it admits no local editing.

 And the restriction is not exploitable by sparsity — the constraint hypergraph has d vertices and up
 to 2^d − 1 hyperedges. It is complete, so treewidth gives back the 2^d bound already known.

    Three walls: hardness blocked at realisability, tractability blocked at the completeness of
    the hypergraph, and the FPT bound between them as the best known.

### 30.3.9 And it is not this book's problem
 Λ's constraint graph is a tree. Its nine recovered extra edges are implied. Every sublattice and
 **Anstee & Farber 1984** and **Hoffman, Kolen & Sakarovitch 1985** give the recognition results.
 **Lubiw 1982** and the Γ-free matrix literature are the nearest neighbours; §2.15.2's derivation settles which predicate this book means.
 interval of Λ has E = 0. Its own case is decidable by enumeration, and it arrives with a
 derivation — box → interval → sublattice → product, three preserving rules, no search.

    The open problem concerns cell sets arriving with no construction. This book never has
    one.

 §E.6 records that the complexity question may already be settled in Ryter & Schmid 1994 or
 Adams–Dwinger–Schmid 1996, both unread.

### 30.4 The lists this work enumerated once
 The target list for the novelty search was audited by closing the relation set, and it was
 incomplete. From {V = 4ν/3h, λ² = (2/3)T, w·V = 8λ², T = Z²R/ν²} the closure yields w/T = 4(h/ν), e/T =
 3(h/ν)², and bits(estimate) ≈ 2·bits(bracket) + constant — none of them on the list.

    That is Chapter 6's defect committed by the searcher rather than by a chemist — a
    structure admitting cells its keeper never enumerated.

### 30.4.1 And the audit itself was run once
 The relation set has grown since. Closing the eight polynomial relations the book now states gives a
 Gröbner basis of 18 where the original four gave 6, and seven further relations reduce to zero that
 did not before — including V·e = w, w² = (16/3)·T·e and T·h² = eν²/3.

 Worse, the closure is over polynomial relations only. The book now states at least nine that are not:
 A^m(x^p) = (−1)^m x^p/(p−1)^m, V = 2/tanh(kh/2), self-concordance, the modular rank law,

 Σ_q\|A_q\|·\|B_q\| = |Λ|, F(−1) = 2, E(X) as non-pairwise content, and the reorderability theorem. None
 was ever audited against any list.

    E(target list) is not 3. It was 3 for the relation set of one moment.

### 30.4.2 Which is a defect of five lists, not one
 This chapter contains five enumerations, and every one was made once:

  the list                                    what it omitted
  §30.4's search targets                      7 polynomial relations, 9 non-polynomial
  §30.2's structural invariants               six, one of which beats its stated best twofold
  §31.1's celestial objects                   six the method plainly admits
  §31.2's string objects                      five, two of them measured in this work
  §31.3's statistic                           stated against the wrong denominator

 §2.10 reads enumerate targets before searching. It should read enumerate before searching, and
 re-enumerate whenever the set grows — which is this section's own content, applied to itself.

## 31. The method outside spectroscopy
 The method was built on one index and tested on one collection. If it is a method rather than a
 description, it must apply where neither was available. This chapter runs it on four subjects it
 was not built for.

### 31.1 Celestial mechanics and the nucleus
 Objects tested against the index, none of them in the collection. A method that transfers is a
 different claim from one that fits.

### 31.1.1 Celestial mechanics — families, not trajectories
 What does not transfer. Sensitive dependence destroys monotonicity along a chaotic trajectory.
 Nothing here touches Poincaré.

  tested                                   result
  Lagrange L1, L2, L3 in μ (Euler's        monotone, convex, 3-monotone; 18 of 18 containments at orders 1–3
  collinear solutions)
  the same at order 3                      widths 6.9 × 10⁻⁴ (L1), 3.6 × 10⁻⁹ (L3)
  Hill stability of hierarchical triples   monotone and convex in the mass ratio; V ≈ 100
  (Mardling & Aarseth)
  Hill radius (μ/3)^{1/3}                  monotone, convex, 3-monotone; V = 47.9
  Roche limit ∝ ρ^{−1/3}                   monotone, convex, 3-monotone; V = 17.9
  planetary semi-major axes                monotone, not convex — the third difference changes sign between
                                           Jupiter and Saturn, where Titius–Bode fails worst
  mean-motion resonances                   §16.7.1: E(X) recovers the order cut at 7/8 from occupancy alone
  satellite counts vs Hill radius          log–log +0.86 across six planets — §23.14.1's capacity bound

 The pole appears at the first point one looks. At μ = 0.030 with a step of h = 0.010, V is 24.6 at
 L1, 12.4 at L2 and 16,423 at L3 — the last because L3 moves almost linearly in μ. L4 and L5 sit on it
 exactly: (1/2 − μ, ±√3/2), the first linear and the second constant.

    Which corrects §18.5. The pole is where a bracket becomes unnecessary, not worthless — a
    vanishing second difference makes linear interpolation exact.

 And one object the method cannot touch. L4/L5 are linearly stable for μ < 0.0385209; Earth–Moon
 and Sun–Jupiter both qualify. That is a threshold, not a family — a single number, with nothing for
 a bracket to act on. The method applies to sequences and is silent on thresholds.

 Signs must be read from the data. L1 and L3 alternate −, +, −, +; L2 alternates +, −, +, −, and
 assuming the Rydberg pattern produces bounds that cross.

### 31.1.2 The nucleus — a second index of the same construction
 An atom's mass sits in its nucleus, and nucleons are fermions. Indexed by (N, L, 2j, occupancy)
 with occ ≤ 2j+1, the shell model is closed under join and meet with E(X) = 0, and shell-order filling
 gives 2, 8, 20, 28, 50, 82.

    Λ gives chemistry. The nucleon index gives mass. This book indexes one of them; Z is a label
    on a channel, never a coordinate.

### 31.2 String theory — the pole, the generating function, and a
 disanalogy
### 31.2.1 Three objects on the pole
 α′m² = N − 1 has second difference zero. So do two more:

  object                                   form                                  V
  string mass spectrum                     m² linear in N                        infinite
  Regge trajectory                         J = α′m² + α₀                         infinite
  central charge                           c=D                                   infinite

 All three join L4 and L5. A bracket is unnecessary on any of them: two levels determine every other,
 exactly.

### 31.2.2 The degeneracy is where the content is
 log d(N) is monotone, convex and 3-monotone, with V ≈ 147 at N ≈ 14 — an ordinary bracketable
 channel, steeper than a Rydberg series.

### 31.2.3 The partition function is Λ's expression with counts
 ∏(1 − q^n)^{−24} has counts where Chapter 11's F has booleans. An index, and an index with
 multiplicity. Both close, for dual reasons: the string's modes are independent; Λ's coordinates couple
 only along a tree. §11.7's three conditions have independence as their degenerate case.

### 31.2.4 No critical dimension, and a ladder
 Λ closes at every dimension 2–8 with E = 0. D = 26 falls out of anomaly cancellation; Λ's 8 is a
 modelling choice.

 And each Λ_d is the exact projection of Λ_{d+1}, closure preserved both ways, with fibres of 1 to 4
 preimages — the extra coordinate carries real information. That is the relation M-theory's eleventh
 dimension has to the ten-dimensional string. What is missing is any reason for the ladder to stop.

### 31.2.5 And no Hagedorn point, for a stated reason
 d(N) ~ exp(4π√N) diverges above a limiting temperature. Λ's rank sequence is finite and
 log-concave, peak 122 at rank 11.

    Capped coordinates give a finite index and a Sperner bound. Uncapped modes give
    exponential degeneracy and a limiting temperature. Λ's finiteness is what capping buys.

### 31.3 The Calabi–Yau catalogue

### 31.3.1 Self-duality, measured
 Batyrev's polar duality swaps the Hodge numbers and the Kreuzer–Skarke list is complete
 over reflexive 4-polytopes — 473,800,776 of them, 30,108 distinct pairs. So the set is closed under
 the involution by construction, and three published counts measure it:

    248,305 with h¹¹ ≥ 140 · 248,305 with h²¹ ≥ 140 · 495,515 with at least one · and 248,305 +
    248,305 − 495,515 = 1,095, as reported.

                                                                 Λ                       KS
  self-dual cells                                                8 of 976                all
  rank polynomial palindromic                                    false                   true

### 31.3.2 The thin tip is a defect of the construction
 h¹¹ + h²¹ < 22 is sparse in the KS list and populated by complete intersections, Klemm–Kreuzer toric
 CICYs and conifold transitions. By §16.7.1's test the absence is removable — not by
 re-coordinatising but by re-constructing — so it is a defect of the index, like the periodic table's 36
 and unlike the belt's six.

### 31.3.3 The Pareto strength, against the right denominator
 1,095 of the 495,515 polytopes with one large Hodge number have both: 0.221%. Stated
 against the full 473.8 million it reads 0.0002%, a factor of 956 — true and uninformative, because the
 denominator includes polytopes with no large Hodge number at all.

    §12.8.1 finds the same trade inside Λ: \|A_q\| falls as \|B_q\| rises. A catalogue with a bounded
    coordinate difference and a large total must be Pareto-shaped, and 0.221% is how strongly.

### 31.3.4 E(X) on a specifiable slice, and 540 predictions
 The 30,108 pairs are a gzipped file this session could not read. But one slice of the catalogue is
 specified exactly in print, and a slice is enough for the operator.

 Candelas, de la Ossa, He & Szendroi (Triadophilia, ATMP 12 (2008) 429) state that the KS list's χ = ±6
 points have Hodge numbers (h, h+3) and (h+3, h) for 13 ≤ h ≤ 128, and name the exclusions: h = 102,
 103, 115, 117 and 119–126. That is a complete cell set — 208 cells — and ℛ applies to it.

    E(X) = 540. 498 join failures and 498 meet failures of 21,528 pairs.

 24.3.4.1 Which is 540 predictions, not a defect
 Chapter 6 reads E(X) > 0 as what a structure admits and lacks. The periodic table admits 36 such
 cells and they are the elements. Here the slice admits 540 and they are Hodge pairs the
 catalogue must contain if the pair set is closed.

 The slice cannot contain them, and the reason is geometric. Two parallel lines have no fixed point:
 the join of (h, h+3) and (h+3, h) is (h+3, h+3). So ℛ immediately names the diagonal.

  the predictions
  cells                                     540
  distinct χ values                         5 — only 0, ±2, ±4
  on the diagonal, χ = 0                    112, from (13,13) to (131,131)
  h¹¹ + h²¹ range                           26 to 262

 The slice was cut at χ = ±6 and the closure predicts inward. It says the set the slice came from fills
 the gap between the two lines — which is where the rest of the Kreuzer–Skarke list lives.

 24.3.4.2 The fourth falsification test, run

 The file is missing, so the predictions cannot be looked up. They can still be refuted, because
 the literature states where the catalogue's contents lie.

  test                                                                     result
  h¹¹ ≥ 1 and h²¹ ≥ 1                                                      540 / 540
  h¹¹ + h²¹ ≤ 502, the published maximum                                   540 / 540
  mirror partner present                                                   540 / 540
  falling where Candelas et al. record "essentially every site occupied"   540 / 540
  falling in the "thinly populated" tip, where a prediction would be       0 / 540
  refuted
  falsified                                                                0
  individually named in print                                              X₁₉,₁₉ (split bicubic), X₂₀,₂₀ (the 24-cell)

    Zero falsifications, two named confirmations, and every prediction inside the region
    published as fully populated.

 This is weaker than a lookup and it is not nothing. A prediction landing in the sparse tip would have
 been refuted, and none does. The test is against the literature's characterisation of the file
 rather than against the file.

 24.3.4.3 And the void reads the same way

                                                                                    cells        predictions
  all h from 13 to 128                                                              232          589
  with the documented exclusions                                                    208          540

 The 24 excluded values — 102, 103, 115, 117 and the run 119–126 — reduce the predictions by
 49. The gaps in the published list are not noise with respect to closure: removing them moves the set
 toward its own normal form, which is what §5 says a void should do when it is structural rather than
 accidental.

 What remains of item D is the lookup. 540 named cells, a file, and an afternoon.

## 32. The law applied to this book
 Everything the collection was built to settle is settled. The census is complete at 23 core types; the
 four decline modes each have a worked case; and the parked questions of earlier drafts — C₆ density,
 antiprotonic helium, the isoelectronic law — are resolved in Chapters 15 and 17. What remains open is
 enumerated and closed as an index in Appendix E: eight items, not one of which blocks a
 result this book relies on.

 So the last question is about the book itself. It has made a claim about indices. This chapter asks
 whether the book satisfies it, because a work asserting that complete indices are self-referencing and
 self-defending has no standing if it is not one.

### 32.1 The book is an index
 Its cells are (claim, location, support). Its coordinates are the part, the chapter and the section. Its
 constraints are of §16.4 form: a claim in section s is supported by evidence in a section at or before s, and
 a specialisation inherits its generalisation's locations.

 **That is an index in the sense of Chapter 14**, and the law applies to it.

### 32.1.1 What a referee would flag
 The chapter applies the law to the book. A referee applies something blunter, and seven things
 would be raised before any of the mathematics was read. They are stated in the form a referee would
 use, not the form the book would prefer.

 **First, the index of §32.1 is computed here, which it never was.** Harvesting every claim-bearing
 paragraph outside the register — §32.6's own test for what a claim is — gives 354 claims carrying a
 § citation and **138 distinct (location, support) cells**.

  resolution                          cells   box   density   E(book)
  chapter                               138   900     15.3%       578
  chapter, restricted to §32.1's own      80   465     17.2%       228
  **part**                               **25**  **25**  **100.0%**    **0**

 **And by §18.4.1 in its certificate form that number measures the drawing.** Coarsening to parts is
 an admitted operation and it reaches a fixed point of ℛ exactly — twenty-five cells filling a box of
 twenty-five. **A certificate exists, so E(book) at chapter resolution records how the chapters
 happen to cite one another, not a defect of the work.** This book is in the periodic table's
 category and not the measured nuclide chart's, and this is the first time its own law has been
 turned on it.

 **Second, §32.6's Condition 1 is named for a quantity it does not evaluate.** It is titled
 *E(book) > 0* and its test extracts headings, cross-references, section references and figures and
 checks that each target exists. **That is reference integrity.** It found a real defect — the
 missing Chapter 30 — and it has never computed E over the index §32.1 defines. The two are separated
 here: Condition 1 is a reference-integrity test, and E(book) is the figure above.

 **Third, §32.1's own constraint is violated fifty-eight times.** It states a claim in section s is
 supported by evidence at or before s. **Fifty-eight of 138 cells — 42% — cite forward**: the preface
 pointing at Chapter 25, Chapter 1 pointing at Chapter 30. Those are correct prose. **The constraint
 was written for an index and applied to a book**, and either it drops the ordering clause or this
 book carries fifty-eight admitted violations of a rule it states about itself. It is recorded rather
 than repaired, because deciding which is a decision in §2.18's sense. Registers 424–426.

 **1. The object does not contain the demonstration.** At the caps of §7.4, Λ has ℓ ∈ {0, 1} — s and
 p only, no d shell and no f shell, three principal shells, an object reaching argon. The bracket is
 tested on 1,442 cells across 35 systems spanning ℓ = 0 to 6 and *Z* = 1 to 83, and §12.11.2 computes
 the f-shell ceiling, 25 at f⁷, for a shell Λ cannot represent at the caps everything else is computed
 at. Chapter 6's indictment of the periodic table survives, since the thirty-six defects are in short
 periods Λ does cover; the tower's coupling results do not obviously transfer. **This is the flag the
 book answers worst.** §7.4 calls caps a presentation choice and §12.11.5's four-cap sweep shows the
 structural claims survive enlargement, but (5,5,2,6,2) is not the f shell either. The honest
 statement, which the book has never made in one sentence: Λ is a model of indexing demonstrated on a
 small closed fragment, and the bracket does not run on Λ at all — it runs on measured channels, and
 Λ supplies the ordering discipline, not the cells.

 **2. E = 0 is fibration-dependent, and two of the three are fibred.** §D.5.5 shows it for the
 mathematics: sixteen fibres give 0, one fibre gives 4. It holds for the ignorance too — E(Q) = 0 at
 four fibres and non-zero unfibred, the unfibred value being a build readout per §2.21. Only E(Λ) = 0 is computed unfibred, over 976 cells, and only that one
 is the strong claim. Every other *E = 0* here must be read with its fibration attached.

 **3. The central theorem is not first.** §29.11: A.2 is Bergman's Double-projection Theorem
 specialised to products of chains, from Baker and Pixley 1975. §29.12 U1: the property E(X) = 0 is
 2-decomposability, a named class in constraint satisfaction. A referee asks what remains, and the
 book must answer in a sentence rather than a chapter — the graded measure, the application, and the
 discipline of indexing one's own ignorance. Whether that is a book is not the author's call.

 **4. The register is longer than the results.** Three hundred and sixty-eight withdrawals, roughly
 thirty-seven of them from a single late session. A referee reads that two ways: unusual honesty, or
 a text not ready. The book has no answer to the fair form of the question — **is the withdrawal rate
 falling?** It is not tracked, and it should be.

 **5. There is no prediction.** §25.6.3 withdrew the one, correctly, and the preface says the recoding
 is deliberate. A physics referee will not accept a control-condition defence without one case where
 the method told a spectroscopist something they did not already have. The tripwire caught the book's
 own misidentified species; it has never run on anyone else's data.

 **6. S3 has never been tested.** §32.2 claims a reader given the claims and their evidence could
 reconstruct the chapter structure. Nobody has tried. It is the one self-reference claim with no
 computation behind it.

 **7. The 540 remain unverified.** §31.3.4's Kreuzer–Skarke count rests on a file this work could not
 read, and §12.11.6 supplies the calibration showing such proposals can be vetoed by structure the
 index does not carry. It is Q item D, and the largest unchecked number in the book.

### 32.1.2 Four of the seven, answered by computation
 The four that could be answered here were, and the answers are not all favourable.

 **Flag 1 — coverage — is substantially answered, and the answer was a computation not an argument.**
 Λ was rebuilt at caps that carry the shells the book had been discussing from outside its own object:

  caps (n, e, ℓ, k, f)     shells        |Λ|        |ℛ(Λ)|      E(Λ)
  (3, 3, 1, 3, 1)          s, p              976         976       0
  (4, 4, 1, 6, 1)          s, p            8,853       8,853       0
  (5, 5, 2, 10, 2)         + d            89,438      89,438       0
  (5, 5, 3, 14, 3)         + f           267,858     267,858       0
  (6, 6, 3, 14, 3)         + f, n ≤ 6    499,246     499,246       0

 **E(Λ) = 0 through the f shell at half a million cells.** The object is not confined to the fragment
 the caps of §7.4 present; the closure that Chapter 7 proves at 976 cells holds at 499,246, across
 every shell the periodic table uses. What the enlargement does not do is make the tower's density
 column transfer — those are computed at book caps and stay there — so the flag is answered for
 closure and remains open for the coupling results. Register 236.

 **Flag 2 — fibration — is answered by disclosure, not by repair.** The honest number for a fibred
 index is its unfibred one. E(Appendix D) = 4 at one fibre, E(Q) unfibred as the press reports it at each build,
 E(Λ) = 0 at one fibre = 0 at one
 fibre. Those are the comparable figures and the book now prints them beside the fibred ones wherever
 the fibred ones appear. Nothing was fixed; something was stopped being hidden.

 **Flag 4 — the register rate — is now measurable, and the measurement is the answer.** Of the
 forty-two entries made in the session that produced this section, thirty-four record defects already
 in the book and found by auditing it, and eight record errors made while working on it — 207, 213,
 215, 218, 220, 221, 227 and 203. The register is therefore not one quantity but two, and only the
 second is a rate that should fall. Conflating them is what makes one thousand one hundred and seventy-one look
 like an indictment rather than a measurement. **The book will track them separately from here.**

 **Flag 6 — S3 — is tested, and it fails in its strong form.** §32.2 claims a reader given the claims
 and their evidence could reconstruct the chapter structure. Operationalised as *can a section's
 chapter be recovered from the sections it cites*, the answer is 18 of 68 — 26% against a 4% baseline
 from uniform guessing over twenty-five chapters. Six and a half times chance is real signal and it is
 not reconstruction. **S3 as written is overstated**, and either it weakens to a claim about signal or
 it needs an operationalisation that succeeds; one test is not a refutation but it is the first
 evidence either way, and it points the wrong way for the book. Register 237.

 **Flags 3, 5 and 7 are not answerable here.** Three needs a sentence the author must write. Five
 needs a spectroscopist's data the tripwire has never seen. Seven needs a file this work cannot read.
 They stay flagged.

### 32.1.3 The frame has changed, and the change is measurable
 This book began as an exploration: an object built, examined and reported on. It is now a procedure —
 a set of instruments that finds its own defects — and the transition is not a matter of self-image. It
 has a signature in the register, read against the clock §F.4.3 identifies.

  entries          found by hand   found by an audit   found from outside
  165–239                    88%                  5%                  6%
  240–289                    73%                 23%                  2%
  290–319                    55%                 25%                 18%

 **Defects found by hand fall from 88% to 55% while defects found by instrument rise fivefold.** That
 is what becoming procedural looks like from inside: not fewer errors, but errors increasingly caught
 by something other than attention. The third column is the two companion papers — the method applied
 to electromagnetism and to muon-catalysed fusion, each returning corrections to this book — which is a
 procedure being *used* rather than a book being read.

 **The transition is real and it is not complete.** Three times in these sessions all twenty audits
 passed on a book that had a defect — registers 305, 310 and 318 — and each time the audit that owned
 the defect carried a literal: a hardcoded total, a fixed phrasing, a single named target. **An
 instrument with a literal in it is narrowed to the cases its author foresaw**, and 55% by hand is the
 measure of how much foresight is still doing the work.

 **What the change costs.** An exploratory book may report what it found in the order it found it. A
 procedural one owes a reader the procedure first and the findings as its output — and this book is
 still ordered by discovery: the register is a quarter of it and Part V carries the record. **The
 ordering was resolved after this section was written**: register 321 promoted the procedure to PART I,
 so the book now presents the method first and its findings as output. What remains disagreeing is
 proportion rather than order — the record is larger than the argument that produced it, which
 §F.4.1's withdrawal ratio measures and does not excuse. Registers 320, 321, 344.

### 32.1.4 What this book names before anyone tells it
 A closed index admits nothing it lacks, so a *closed* index names nothing unidentified. **This book is
 not one index but six, and only two of them are closed.** Asked what it can determine before an input
 is given, the answer is a count:

  index                            coordinates   E     cells named and unoccupied
  Λ, the object                              8   0     —
  Appendix D, fibred by kind × language     16   0     —
  Appendix D, unfibred                       3   4     four
  Appendix E (Q), fibred by domain           4   0     —
  Appendix E (Q), unfibred                   4   1     one
  Appendix F, the numbers                    3  33     thirty-three
  the audit set, at four coordinates         4  17     seventeen
                                                       **a build readout**

 **Fifty-five cells are named and nobody has put anything in them.** Each is a combination the
 structure admits, stated in coordinates already defined, arrived at by computation and not by
 suggestion. That is what E(X) *is* — §18.6 calls it the prediction budget, and this is the budget
 unspent.

 **It has been confirmed three times, which is the only evidence that matters here.** Register 285:
 audit 19 SEQUENCE occupied a cell E(audits) had listed as admitted and absent, found by a defect
 rather than proposed. Register 294: PROJECTION did the same. Register 278: E over the book's own
 decisions located a decision that had been deferred rather than made — the section-level locators for
 Appendices C and G. **In each case the cell was named first and the occupant arrived after.**

 **And there is a hard boundary, which is the honest half of the answer.** E names cells; it never
 names a coordinate. Every one of the fifty-five is a combination of values the index already carries,
 and §12's extension test requires a candidate axis to be *offered* before it can be judged. The book
 predicted the eleven missing audits and could not have predicted DEPTH, the coordinate that made them
 nineteen. **An index can tell you what it has not said in the language it has. It cannot tell you what
 it has no language for**, and that is §22.4's closing result read forward rather than back. Register
 332.

### 32.1.4.1 Which of this book's numbers hold still
 §32.1.4 counts fifty-five cells named and unoccupied. **Recomputed now the total is 48**, and the
 movement is the finding rather than the correction.

 Measured across three states of the book — before the third companion paper's intake, before the
 register was compressed, and now:

  figure                                  before intake   before compression   now
  E(Λ)                                                0                    0     0
  |Λ|                                              976                  976   976
  generators |J(Λ)|                                 17                   17    17
  surplus, bits per cell                           7.07                 7.07  7.07
  elements of Appendix D                          forty         forty-eight   forty-eight
  E over the numbers index                           33                   21    23
  E(Q), unfibred                                      1                    1     4

    **Every figure about Λ holds. Every figure about the book moves.**

 That is not a coincidence and it is not a defect. **Λ is a fixed mathematical object and the book is a
 text that changes**, so a measurement of Λ is a measurement and a measurement of the book is a reading
 taken at a moment. §12.11.3.1 divides bounds into those from a law and those from an extent; the same
 division sorts this book's own numbers, and **the sorting line falls exactly at the boundary between
 the object and the work about it.**

 **The consequence for a reader.** Every figure in Parts II and III can be checked against the
 construction and will agree forever. Every figure the book states about itself — the register's size,
 the ratios of F.4.1, the counts of Appendices D, E and F, the fifty-five of §32.1.4 — is true of a
 state, and the state is named. The press recomputes the two that move fastest at every build. **Three
 more are asserted here and should not be**, which is the work register 373 leaves open rather than
 does.

 Register 373.

### 32.2 Self-reference

 **S1 — alphabet.** The list of claims is recoverable by projection: it is the set of first coordinates, and the table of contents is its quotient by chapter.

 **S2 — order.** The specificity order among terms is a tree, and Chapter 15's propagation recovers it. **The book's sectioning is not arbitrary**; it is the order in which claims become supportable.

 **S3 — bounds.** Every claim's support locations are recoverable as the union over its specialisations. **A reader given only the claims and their evidence could reconstruct the chapter structure**, which is the content of E = 0.

### 32.3 Self-defence
 And this is where the book earns the claim rather than making it.

 ⅅ_def — two routes to every number. Each quantitative claim in this book carries both a value and
 a verification count. That is a redundancy: the value alone would be undetectable, but the pair can
 disagree.

      102 quantitative claims were extracted from the draft and re-derived independently from
      the data. Thirty were checkable in full. Two appeared to fail; both were traced to the
      auditor, not to the book.

 The two failures are the demonstration, not the exception. A claim about self-duality was flagged
 because the audit used the wrong involution. A defect was flagged because the audit sampled four levels
 of a twenty-member channel. In both cases the disagreement was visible, was pursued, and
 located the error. That is ⅅ_def operating on the text.

 ⅅ_phys — the claims must agree with the world. The book's physical claims are checked against
 measured levels from named compilations, and Chapter 28 lists one thousand one hundred and seventy-one that failed.

 **D3 — totality.** Every claim in this book is **supported, cited, or marked open**. There is no fourth state. Chapter 29 names the single open item; Chapter 28 names three documents and one section that could not be reached and states the consequence. **No claim is left in limbo, because limbo is not in the codomain.**

### 32.4 The recursion, and where it stops
      If the index is complete, then a book that comprehends it completely is itself an index —
      and is itself self-referencing and self-defending.

 This is not decoration. It is forced. A complete comprehension of a closed object must record the object's alphabet, order and bounds, or it is incomplete; and recording them makes the record itself closed under the same conditions.

 **And the recursion is stationary, which is not the same as terminating.** The distinction matters, and an earlier draft of this section got it wrong.

 Construct the claim-index of a closed index, then the claim-index of *that*, and so on. Measured at each level:

  level                                     cells     closed      E         occupancy        alphabets

  0 — the index                             10        yes         0         0.625            [4, 4]

  1 — claims about it                       10        yes         0         0.143            [10, 7]

  2 — claims about those                    10        yes         0         0.100            [10, 10]

  3                                         10        yes         0         0.100            [10, 10]

 **Closure, dimension and E are invariant from level 1.** But I²(Λ) ≠ I(Λ) as sets, and I³ ≠ I². **The content does not repeat.** What stabilises — by level 2, not level 1 — are the measured properties.

      The recursion is structurally stationary, not terminating.

 So the reason one need not write a book about this book is **not** that it would be identical. It is that it would exhibit **nothing new**: the same three mechanisms, verified again, on claims about claims. The tower is infinite and every storey above the second is furnished the same way.

### 32.4.1 Why the recursion is safe — logic without negation
 §32.4 shows the recursion is stationary. It does not say why it is not **paradoxical**, and a book
 that indexes its own mathematics, its own ignorance, its own errors, its own numbers, its own audits
 and its own decisions stands exactly where the classical paradoxes stand. E(X) is itself an element
 of the set §D.5.2 indexes: the operator measures a collection containing itself.

 **It is safe for two reasons, and both are computable.**

 **The language has no negation.** χ is a product of Heaviside steps of differences — seven factors,
 each a bound saying one coordinate is at most a function of another. Available: max, min, ≤. Absent:
 ¬, →, complement. **A paradox needs a negation to close its loop** — the liar needs *not true*,
 Russell needs *not a member of itself*, Gödel needs a negated provability predicate. This language
 cannot write the sentence. Self-reference in a negation-free monotone language is not a contradiction
 waiting to be found; it is an equation waiting to be solved.

 **And ℛ is a closure operator, so the equation has solutions.** Proved, not sampled. *Extensive:*
 every x ∈ X satisfies xᵢ ≤ φ̂ᵢⱼ(xⱼ) because φ̂ᵢⱼ(xⱼ) = max{yᵢ : y ∈ X, yⱼ ≤ xⱼ} ≥ xᵢ, x itself being
 one of the y; and each xᵢ lies in the value set Âᵢ(X). *Monotone:* X ⊆ Y makes every value set and
 every envelope of Y at least that of X, so any point admitted by X's bounds is admitted by Y's.
 *Idempotent:* ℛ(X) has the same value sets and the same envelopes as X — the maxima are attained at
 points of X, which ℛ(X) contains — so ℛ(ℛ(X)) is cut by the same inequalities and equals ℛ(X). ∎
 Corroborated on three thousand random subsets of a small ambient box with zero failures on each of
 the three, and the fixed points closed under intersection over one thousand one hundred and seventy-one pairs. A monotone operator on a complete lattice has fixed points — Knaster and Tarski — and
 this book's central theorem identifies them: *X is closed iff X = ℛ(X)*.

 **So the fixed point sits where the paradox would have been.** With negation, a self-referential
 operator yields a sentence true iff false. In a lattice it yields a set equal to its own
 reconstruction. **A closed index is what a liar sentence becomes when the negation is taken away**,
 and E(X) measures distance from that fixed point rather than the truth of a self-denial.

 That is why §32.4's recursion stabilises rather than exploding — and it is the limit of the result.
 **The safety is bought with expressive power.** §13 already prices it: no differences, no sums, no
 symmetric bounds. §12.11's envelope densities are the bill, and freedom from paradox is on the same
 invoice. Register 279.

### 32.4.2 Which of this book's figures survive their own inputs moving
 §32.1.4.1 sorts the book's numbers by whether they describe Λ or the book. **This is the second cut,
 and it runs inside Λ**: of the figures that describe the object, which survive the caps moving?

 **The values do not.** Every extensive figure moves with the caps, and moves a long way:

  figure                    (3,3,1,3,1)   (4,4,1,6,1)   (4,4,2,6,2)   (5,5,2,6,2)
  cells                             976         8,853        19,109        35,789
  generators                         17            31            33            35
  fill                          14.12%         6.72%         6.45%         7.73%
  rank levels                        18            32            34            36
  widest level                      122           704         1,497         2,742
  surplus, bits per cell           7.07         17.89         18.78         19.87
  reflection survivors                8            16            17            68

 **Nine of nine move.** That is not a defect — a bigger object has more cells — but it means every one
 of those figures is a reading at a setting, and the book states most of them once.

 **The identities do.** Tested at three cap settings, exhaustively where the pair count allowed:

  identity                                        holds
  E(Λ) = 0                                        at every setting
  \|J(Λ)\| = Σ(\|Aᵢ\| − 1)                            at every setting
  closed under join and meet                      at every setting tested by pairs
  rank(a∨b) + rank(a∧b) = rank(a) + rank(b)       at every setting tested by pairs
  the cylinder factorises over the transfer       at every setting

    **Six of six identities hold; nine of nine values move.** The book states both in the same
    voice, and a reader has no way to tell which is which from the sentence.

 **That is why the three figures recomputed under variation at registers 388, 391 and 392 all turned
 into findings.** They were values presented as though they were properties — the eleventh axis's
 density, the conservative share, containment across defects — and varying the input is what
